## Volume I / 第一卷
===Original===

> Is all our Life, then, but a dream
> Seen faintly in the golden gleam
> Athwart Time’s dark resistless stream?
> Bowed to the earth with bitter woe,
> Or laughing at some raree-show,
> We flutter idly to and fro.
> Man’s little Day in haste we spend,
> And, from its merry noontide, send
> No glance to meet the silent end.

===Chinese===

> 如此说来，我们的一生岂非一场梦
> 在金色微光里隐约映现
> 于时间黑暗而不息的洪流之上？
> 或因悲苦压弯了腰身，
> 或对着西洋景欢笑不己，
> 我们只是徒然来回飘飞。
> 匆匆挥霍着人短暂的白昼，
> 而在欢畅的正午时分，
> 不曾一瞥那寂静的终局。

### Preface / 序
===Original===

One little picture in this book, the Magic Locket, at chapter 6, was drawn by “Miss Alice Havers.” I did not state this on the titlepage, since it seemed only due, to the artist of all these (to my mind) *wonderful* pictures, that his name should stand there alone.
The descriptions, at chapter 25, of Sunday as spent by children of the last generation, are quoted verbatim from a speech made to me by a child-friend and a letter written to me by a lady-friend.
The Chapters, headed “Fairy Sylvie” and “Bruno’s Revenge,” are a reprint, with a few alterations, of a little fairytale which I wrote in the year 1867, at the request of the late Mrs. Gatty, for “Aunt Judy’s Magazine,” which she was then editing.
It was in 1874, I believe, that the idea first occurred to me of making it the nucleus of a longer story. As the years went on, I jotted down, at odd moments, all sorts of odd ideas, and fragments of dialogue, that occurred to me﻿—who knows how?﻿—with a transitory suddenness that left me no choice but either to record them then and there, or to abandon them to oblivion. Sometimes one could trace to their source these random flashes of thought﻿—as being suggested by the book one was reading, or struck out from the “flint” of one’s own mind by the “steel” of a friend’s chance remark﻿—but they had also a way of their own, of occurring, apropos of nothing﻿—specimens of that hopelessly illogical phenomenon, “an effect without a cause.” Such, for example, was the last line of “The Hunting of the Snark,” which came into my head (as I have already related in “The Theatre” for April, 1887) quite suddenly, during a solitary walk: and such, again, have been passages which occurred in *dreams*, and which I cannot trace to any antecedent cause whatever. There are at least *two* instances of such dream-suggestions in this book﻿—one, my Lady’s remark, “it often runs in families, just as a love for pastry does,” at chapter 7; the other, Eric Lindon’s *badinage* about having been in domestic service, at chapter 22.
And thus it came to pass that I found myself at last in possession of a huge unwieldy mass of litterature﻿—if the reader will kindly excuse the spelling﻿—which only needed stringing together, upon the thread of a consecutive story, to constitute the book I hoped to write. Only!

===Chinese===

本书中有一幅小插图，即第6章的「魔法挂坠盒」（the Magic Locket），出自「艾丽斯·哈弗斯小姐」（Miss Alice Havers）之手。我没有在扉页上注明这一点，因为在我看来，这些（在我看来）*绝妙*无比的插图全都出自一位画家之笔，扉页理应只署他一人的名字。
第25章关于上一代孩子如何度过礼拜日的描写，逐字引自一位小朋友对我讲的一番话，和一位女友人写给我的一封信。
题为「仙子西尔维（Sylvie）」和「布鲁诺（Bruno）的复仇」的两章，是我于1867年应已故加蒂夫人之请，为她当时主编的《朱迪姨母杂志》所写的一篇小童话的重刊，仅略作改动。
我想，正是在1874年，我头一回萌生了以它为一部长篇故事核心的念头。此后岁月流转，我在闲暇时刻随手记下各种古怪的念头和对话的片段——它们是谁知道怎么冒出来的？——来得突兀而短暂，容不得我选择：要么当场记下，要么任其湮灭。这些思想的零星火花，有时可以追溯出源头——或是受了手头所读之书的启发，或是被朋友一句无意之言的「钢」，从自己心头的「燧石」上击打出来的——但它们也有自己的路数，常常无缘无故、凭空而至，是那种全然不合逻辑的现象——「无因之果」——的标本。比如《猎蛇鲨》的最后一行，便是在一次独自散步时（我已在1887年4月的《戏剧》杂志上讲过）突如其来地闯进我脑中的；又如有些段落出现于*梦境*之中，我无论如何也追溯不出任何先前的缘由。本书中至少有*两处*这样的梦中启示——一处是第7章中夫人那句「这往往世代相传，就像爱吃糕点一样」；另一处是第22章中埃里克·林登（Eric Lindon）关于自己做过帮佣的*戏言*。
就这样，我最终发现自己手里握着一大堆臃肿杂乱的「litterature」——还请读者见谅这个拼法——只消用一条连贯故事的线索把它们串起来，便构成了我期望写成的这本书。仅此而已！

===Original===

The task, at first, seemed absolutely hopeless, and gave me a far clearer idea, than I ever had before, of the meaning of the word “chaos”: and I think it must have been ten years, or more, before I had succeeded in classifying these odds-and-ends sufficiently to see what sort of a story they indicated: for the story had to grow out of the incidents, not the incidents out of the story.
I am telling all this, in no spirit of egoism, but because I really believe that some of my readers will be interested in these details of the “genesis” of a book, which looks so simple and straightforward a matter, when completed, that they might suppose it to have been written straight off, page by page, as one would write a letter, beginning at the beginning and ending at the end.
It is, no doubt, *possible* to write a story in that way: and, if it be not vanity to say so, I believe that I could, myself﻿—if I were in the unfortunate position (for I do hold it to be a real misfortune) of being obliged to produce a given amount of fiction in a given time﻿—that I could “fulfil my task,” and produce my “tale of bricks,” as other slaves have done. One thing, at any rate, I could guarantee as to the story so produced﻿—that it should be utterly commonplace, should contain no new ideas whatever, and should be very very weary reading!
This species of literature has received the very appropriate name of “padding”﻿—which might fitly be defined as “that which all can write and none can read.” That the present volume contains *no* such writing I dare not avow: sometimes, in order to bring a picture into its proper place, it has been necessary to eke out a page with two or three extra lines: but I can honestly say I have put in no more than I was absolutely compelled to do.
My readers may perhaps like to amuse themselves by trying to detect, in a given passage, the one piece of “padding” it contains. While arranging the “slips” into pages, I found that the passage in chapter 3, was 3 lines too short. I supplied the deficiency, not by interpolating a word here and a word there, but by writing in 3 consecutive lines. Now can my readers guess *which* they are?
A harder puzzle﻿—if a harder be desired﻿—would be to determine, as to the Gardener’s Song, in *which* cases (if any) the stanza was adapted to the surrounding text, and in *which* (if any) the text was adapted to the stanza.

===Chinese===

这项任务起初看起来毫无希望，也让我比以往任何时候都更透彻地领会了「混沌」一词的含义。我想，大约过了十年，也许更久，我才把这些零七八碎归置得井然有序，足以看出它们指向一个什么样的故事：因为必须是故事从诸多情节中生长出来，而不是情节从故事中衍生出来。
我讲这一切，绝非出于自炫，而是因为我确实相信，有些读者会有兴趣了解一本书的「创生」细节——书一旦写成，看上去是那么简单顺当，人们说不定以为它是从头至尾一页页径直写下来的，就像写一封信那样，从开头开始，到结尾结束。
用那种方式写故事，无疑不是没有可能；倘若这样说不算自夸，我相信我自己也能做到——倘若我落入那种不幸的境地（我确实认为那是实实在在的不幸）：不得不在规定时间内产出规定数量的小说——我相信我也能「完成任务」，像其他奴隶一样交出我的「砖头故事」。
只是有一件事我敢担保：这样写出来的故事必定平庸至极，绝无任何新意，而且读起来令人昏昏欲睡、疲惫不堪！
这类文字得到过一个再贴切不过的名字——「填料」——不妨把它定义为「人人写得出来、无人读得下去的东西」。若说本卷中*绝无*此类文字，我可不敢夸口：有时为了让一幅插图落在合适的位置，不得不用两三行额外的文字把一页凑满；但我可以诚实地说明，凡非迫不得已，我绝没有多塞一句。
读者诸君也许愿意自娱自乐一番，试着在某个段落里找出其中那一处「填料」。我在把长条校样拼成书页时，发现第3章有一段短了三行。我便补足了缺额——不是东插一个词、西补一个词，而是续写了相连的三行。现在，读者们能猜出是哪三行吗？
若嫌不够、还想要更难的谜题，那便可以琢磨琢磨园丁（the Gardener）之歌：究竟有哪几处（如果有的话）是诗节迁就了前后的正文，又有哪几处（如果有的话）是正文迁就了诗节。

===Original===

Perhaps the hardest thing in all literature﻿—at least *I* have found it so: by no voluntary effort can I accomplish it: I have to take it as it comes﻿—is to write anything *original*. And perhaps the easiest is, when once an original line has been struck out, to follow it up, and to write any amount more to the same tune. I do not know if “Alice in Wonderland” was an *original* story﻿—I was, at least, no *conscious* imitator in writing it﻿—but I do know that, since it came out, something like a dozen storybooks have appeared, on identically the same pattern. The path I timidly explored﻿—believing myself to be “the first that ever burst into that silent sea”﻿—is now a beaten high road: all the wayside flowers have long ago been trampled into the dust: and it would be courting disaster for me to attempt that style again.
Hence it is that, in “Sylvie and Bruno,” I have striven﻿—with I know not what success﻿—to strike out yet another new path: be it bad or good, it is the best I can do. It is written, not for money, and not for fame, but in the hope of supplying, for the children whom I love, some thoughts that may suit those hours of innocent merriment which are the very life of Childhood; and also in the hope of suggesting, to them and to others, some thoughts that may prove, I would fain hope, not wholly out of harmony with the graver cadences of Life.
If I have not already exhausted the patience of my readers, I would like to seize this opportunity﻿—perhaps the last I shall have of addressing so many friends at once﻿—of putting on record some ideas that have occurred to me, as to books desirable to be written﻿—which I should much like to *attempt*, but may not ever have the time or power to carry through﻿—in the hope that, if *I* should fail (and the years are gliding away *very* fast) to finish the task I have set myself, other hands may take it up.
First, a Child’s Bible. The only real *essentials* of this would be, carefully selected passages, suitable for a child’s reading, and pictures. One principle of selection, which I would adopt, would be that Religion should be put before a child as a revelation of *love*﻿—no need to pain and puzzle the young mind with the history of crime and punishment. (On such a principle I should, for example, omit the history of the Flood.)

===Chinese===

在全部文学之中，最难的事也许莫过于写出*独创*的东西——至少我自己的体会是如此：任凭怎样自觉的努力，我都办不到；只能等它自己来。而最容易的，也许莫过于在独辟的蹊径一旦踏出之后，沿着它走下去，依着同一个调子想写多少便写多少。我不知道《爱丽丝漫游奇境》算不算一个*独创*的故事——至少我写它时并无*自觉*的模仿——但我确实知道，自它问世以来，已有大约一打故事书依着一模一样的路数相继出现。我当年怯生生探索的那条小径——当时还自以为是「第一个闯入那片静默之海的人」——如今已成车马通衢：路边的野花早被踩进了尘土；我若再试那种笔调，无异于自取其咎。
因此，在《西尔维与布鲁诺》中，我竭力——成败如何，不得而知——再辟一条新的路径：好也罢，坏也罢，这是我能尽的最大努力。我写这本书，不为金钱，不为名声，只希望为我所爱的孩子们提供一些想法，去契合那纯真欢乐的时光——那正是童年之所以为童年的生命所在；也希望向他们、也向其他人，提示一些想法——我衷心希望，它们不至于与人生较为庄重的节拍全然失谐。
倘若读者们的耐心尚未被我耗尽，我想借此机会——这也许是我最后一次能同时对这么许多朋友致辞——记录下一些我想到的、值得写成书的构想。这些书我很想*尝试*，却未必有时间或精力完成；我把它们记在这里，是希望倘若*我*未能（而年华正飞逝得*很*快）完成自己定下的任务，会有别人接手去做。
第一，一部《儿童圣经》。真正*必不可少*的只有两样：精心挑选、适合儿童阅读的经文章节，和插图。我采用的选材原则之一，是宗教应当作为一种*爱*的启示呈现给孩子——不必用罪恶与刑罚的历史去刺痛、困惑幼小的心灵。（依照这一原则，比如，我会略去大洪水的故事。）

===Original===

The supplying of the pictures would involve no great difficulty: no new ones would be needed: hundreds of excellent pictures already exist, the copyright of which has long ago expired, and which simply need photo-zincography, or some similar process, for their successful reproduction. The book should be handy in size﻿—with a pretty attractive-looking cover﻿—in a clear legible type﻿—and, above all, with abundance of pictures, pictures, pictures!
Secondly, a book of pieces selected from the Bible﻿—not single texts, but passages of from 10 to 20 verses each﻿—to be committed to memory. Such passages would be found useful, to repeat to one’s-self and to ponder over, on many occasions when reading is difficult, if not impossible: for instance, when lying awake at night﻿—on a railway-journey﻿—when taking a solitary walk﻿—in old age, when eyesight is failing or wholly lost﻿—and, best of all, when illness, while incapacitating us for reading or any other occupation, condemns us to lie awake through many weary silent hours: at such a time how keenly one may realise the truth of David’s rapturous cry “*O how sweet are thy words unto my throat: yea, sweeter than honey unto my mouth!*”
I have said “passages,” rather than single texts, because we have no means of *recalling* single texts: memory needs *links*, and here are none: one may have a hundred texts stored in the memory, and not be able to recall, at will, more than half-a-dozen﻿—and those by mere chance: whereas, once get hold of any portion of a *chapter* that has been committed to memory, and the whole can be recovered: all hangs together.
Thirdly, a collection of passages, both prose and verse, from books other than the Bible. There is not perhaps much, in what is called “uninspired” literature (a misnomer, I hold: if Shakespeare was not inspired, one may well doubt if any man ever was), that will bear the process of being pondered over, a hundred times: still there *are* such passages﻿—enough, I think, to make a goodly store for the memory.
These two books﻿—of sacred, and secular, passages for memory﻿—will serve other good purposes besides merely occupying vacant hours: they will help to keep at bay many anxious thoughts, worrying thoughts, uncharitable thoughts, unholy thoughts.

===Chinese===

插图并不难解决：无须新作：现成的佳作数以百计，版权早已过期，只消照相锌版术或类似工艺，便能成功复制。书要开本便巧——封面悦目——字体清晰易读——而最重要的是，要有大量的插图、插图、插图！
第二，一本从圣经中选辑成册的书——不是零散的句节，而是每段十节到二十节的段落——供人背诵。这类段落有许多用处：在阅读艰难乃至无法阅读的场合，可以默诵、可以细细回味——比如夜里失眠时——乘火车旅行时——独自散步时——到了晚年、目力衰退或完全失明时——而最有用的是在患病之时：疾病使我们无法阅读或做任何事，只得在许多疲惫的寂静时辰里醒着躺着；在那样的时候，人才会多么真切地体会到大卫那声欣喜若狂的呼喊是何等真实：「*你的言语在我喉中何等甘美，在我口中比蜜更甜！*」
我说的是「段落」，而非零散句节，因为零散的句节我们无从*追忆*：记忆需要*链条*，而句节没有链条：一个人脑子里可能存着一百条句节，能随意唤起的却不过半打——还是碰运气撞上的；然而，只要抓住已背诵的*章*的任何一部分，整章便能全部复原：环环相扣，浑然一体。
第三，一部选段集，散文与韵文兼收，选自圣经以外的书籍。在所谓「非受感而作」的文字中（我以为这称呼不当：倘若莎士比亚不算受感而作，那么谁曾受过感召都大可怀疑），经得起反复回味百遍的或许不多：但这样的段落*确乎*存在——我认为足以为记忆积下一宗可观的库藏。
这两部书——圣的与俗的背诵文选——除了消磨闲暇，还另有妙用：它们能帮助驱走许多忧思、烦虑、刻薄之念、不洁之念。

===Original===

Let me say this, in better words than my own, by copying a passage from that most interesting book, Robertson’s Lectures on the Epistles to the Corinthians, Lecture XLIX. “If a man finds himself haunted by evil desires and unholy images, which will generally be at periodical hours, let him commit to memory passages of Scripture, or passages from the best writers in verse or prose. Let him store his mind with these, as safeguards to repeat when he lies awake in some restless night, or when despairing imaginations, or gloomy, suicidal thoughts, beset him. Let these be to him the sword, turning everywhere to keep the way of the Garden of Life from the intrusion of profaner footsteps.”
Fourthly, a “Shakespeare” for girls: that is, an edition in which everything, not suitable for the perusal of girls of (say) from 10 to 17, should be omitted. Few children under 10 would be likely to understand or enjoy the greatest of poets: and those, who have passed out of girlhood, may safely be left to read Shakespeare, in any edition, “expurgated” or not, that they may prefer: but it seems a pity that so many children, in the intermediate stage, should be debarred from a great pleasure for want of an edition suitable to them. Neither Bowdler’s, Chambers’s, Brandram’s, nor Cundell’s “Boudoir” Shakespeare, seems to me to meet the want: they are not sufficiently “expurgated.” Bowdler’s is the most extraordinary of all: looking through it, I am filled with a deep sense of wonder, considering what he has left in, that he should have cut *anything* out! Besides relentlessly erasing all that is unsuitable on the score of reverence or decency, I should be inclined to omit also all that seems too difficult, or not likely to interest young readers. The resulting book might be slightly fragmentary: but it would be a real treasure to all British maidens who have any taste for poetry.
If it be needful to apologize to anyone for the new departure I have taken in this story﻿—by introducing, along with what will, I hope, prove to be acceptable nonsense for children, some of the graver thoughts of human life﻿—it must be to one who has learned the Art of keeping such thoughts wholly at a distance in hours of mirth and careless ease. To him such a mixture will seem, no doubt, ill-judged and repulsive.

===Chinese===

让我借用一段比我自己写得更好的文字来说明这一点——抄自那部极为动人的著作，罗伯逊的《哥林多前后书讲义》第四十九讲：「倘若一个人发觉自己被恶欲与不洁的幻象纠缠——它们多半在固定的时辰袭来——那就让他背诵经文，或最佳作家笔下的诗文段落。让他把这些存进心里，当作护身的凭仗，在某个辗转难眠的夜里默诵，在绝望的想象、阴郁的求死念头围攻他时念诵。让这些成为他的剑，四面转动，把守生命园的道路，不容亵慢的脚步闯入。」
第四，一部给女孩子们读的「莎士比亚」：即一个删去一切不适合十岁到十七岁左右女孩阅读之内容的版本。十岁以下的儿童能读懂或欣赏这位最伟大诗人的恐怕寥寥；已过少女之年的人，随她们高兴读哪个版本——「洁本」与否——都不会有妨害；可惜的是，夹在这中间的那么多孩子，竟因缺少一个适合她们的版本而被剥夺了这一大乐趣。鲍德勒版、钱伯斯版、布兰德拉姆版，还是坎德尔的「闺阁」版莎士比亚，在我看来都未能满足这一需要：它们删得不够「干净」。鲍德勒版是其中最令人称奇的：翻着它，想到他留下的那些东西，我真深深诧异他居然还删掉了*任何*东西！
除了毫不留情地删去一切在虔敬或体统上不宜的内容，我还倾向于把那些过于艰深、或未必能引起年轻读者兴趣的部分也一并删去。这样编出的书或许略显零碎：但对所有稍具诗心的英伦少女而言，它将是一件真正的珍宝。
倘若我在本书中的新尝试——在希望能为孩子们所接受的无稽之谈之外，掺入一些人生较为严肃的思考——需要向什么人致歉的话，那必定是向这样一位：他学会了那样一门艺术，能在欢笑与闲适的时光里把这些念头远远拒于门外。在他看来，这样的杂糅无疑失当而令人生厌。

===Original===

And that such an Art *exists* I do not dispute: with youth, good health, and sufficient money, it seems quite possible to lead, for years together, a life of unmixed gaiety﻿—with the exception of one solemn fact, with which we are liable to be confronted at *any* moment, even in the midst of the most brilliant company or the most sparkling entertainment. A man may fix his own times for admitting serious thought, for attending public worship, for prayer, for reading the Bible: all such matters he can defer to that “convenient season,” which is so apt never to occur at all: but he cannot defer, for one single moment, the necessity of attending to a message, which may come before he has finished reading this page, “*this night shall thy soul be required of thee*.”
The ever-present sense of this grim possibility has been, in all ages, an incubus that men have striven to shake off. Few more interesting subjects of enquiry could be found, by a student of history, than the various weapons that have been used against this shadowy foe. Saddest of all must have been the thoughts of those who saw indeed an *existence* beyond the grave, but an existence far more terrible than annihilation﻿—an existence as filmy, impalpable, all but invisible spectres, drifting about, through endless ages, in a world of shadows, with nothing to do, nothing to hope for, nothing to love! In the midst of the gay verses of that genial “bon vivant” Horace, there stands one dreary word whose utter sadness goes to one’s heart. It is the word “*exilium*” in the well-known passage

> Omnes eodem cogimur, omnium
> Versatur urnâ serius ocius
> Sors exitura et nos in æternum
> Exilium impositura cymbæ.

===Chinese===

这样一门艺术确实*存在*，我无意争辩：有青春、健康，加上充裕的金钱，一连多年过一种纯粹的欢愉生活似乎全然可能——只是有一桩冷峻的事实例外：我们随时可能与它打照面，哪怕正置身于最耀眼的宾客之间、最欢腾的盛宴之中。
一个人可以自己定下时辰来接纳严肃的思考、参加礼拜、祈祷、读经：这一切他都可以推迟到那个「方便的时候」——而那个时机偏偏永远不会来：但有一样他一刻也推迟不得，那就是倾听一个信息——它可能在他读完这一页之前便已到来：「*今夜必要你的灵魂。*」
对这阴森可能性的恒久意识，历来都是人们极力想甩脱的梦魇。对研治历史的人来说，几乎找不出比这更有意思的课题了：人类用来对抗这个幽冥之敌的种种武器。最可悲的，想必是那些人的念头：他们的确看见坟墓之外还有*存在*，可那存在却比寂灭可怕得多——化作薄雾般、无形无质、几乎看不见的幽灵，在无尽岁月里游荡于一个阴影的世界，无所事事，无所希冀，无所可爱！
在那位乐天的「饕客」贺拉斯欢快的诗行中间，矗立着一个凄凉的词，它彻骨的悲哀直刺人心。那就是人所熟知的这一段中的「流放」（exilium）一词：

> Omnes eodem cogimur, omnium
> Versatur urnâ serius ocius
> Sors exitura et nos in æternum
> Exilium impositura cymbæ.

===Original===

Yes, to him this present life﻿—spite of all its weariness and all its sorrow﻿—was the only life worth having: all else was “exile”! Does it not seem almost incredible that one, holding such a creed, should ever have smiled?
And many in this day, I fear, even though believing in an existence beyond the grave far more real than Horace ever dreamed of, yet regard it as a sort of “exile” from all the joys of life, and so adopt Horace’s theory, and say “let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die.”
We go to entertainments, such as the theatre﻿—I say “we,” for *I* also go to the play, whenever I get a chance of seeing a really good one﻿—and keep at arm’s length, if possible, the thought that we may not return alive. Yet how do you know﻿—dear friend, whose patience has carried you through this garrulous preface﻿—that it may not be *your* lot, when mirth is fastest and most furious, to feel the sharp pang, or the deadly faintness, which heralds the final crisis﻿—to see, with vague wonder, anxious friends bending over you﻿—to hear their troubled whispers﻿—perhaps yourself to shape the question, with trembling lips, “Is it serious?”, and to be told “Yes: the end is near” (and oh, how different all Life will look when those words are said!)﻿—how do you know, I say, that all this may not happen to *you*, this night?
And *dare* you, knowing this, say to yourself “Well, perhaps it *is* an immoral play: perhaps the situations *are* a little too ‘risky,’ the dialogue a little too strong, the ‘business’ a little too suggestive. I don’t say that conscience is *quite* easy: but the piece is so clever, I must see it this once! I’ll begin a stricter life tomorrow.” *Tomorrow, and tomorrow, and tomorrow!*

> “Who sins in hope, who, sinning, says,
> ‘Sorrow for sin God’s judgement stays!’
> Against God’s Spirit he lies; quite stops
> Mercy with insult; dares, and drops,
> Like a scorch’d fly, that spins in vain
> Upon the axis of its pain,
> Then takes its doom, to limp and crawl,
> Blind and forgot, from fall to fall.”

===Chinese===

是的，在他看来，今生——纵有千般疲惫、万般哀愁——是唯一值得拥有的生命：其余的一切都是「流放」！一个怀抱这种信条的人竟然还笑得出来，这岂非近乎不可思议？
而我担心，今日也有许多人，尽管相信坟墓之外有一种远比贺拉斯梦想的更为真实的存在，却仍把它看作对人生一切欢乐的一种「流放」，于是采纳贺拉斯的理论，说「我们就吃吃喝喝吧，因为明天要死了」。
我们去戏院之类的娱乐场所——我说「我们」，因为*我*自己每逢有机会看一出真正的好戏，也是要去的——并且尽可能远远推开那个念头：我们也许不能活着回来。可是，亲爱的朋友——多亏你的耐心，才读完了这篇絮叨的序言——你又怎么知道，命运不会轮到*你*头上：正当欢笑最是酣畅狂热之际，忽然感到那预示最后关头的剧痛，或那致命的眩晕——恍恍惚惚地看见焦急的朋友俯在你身畔——听见他们慌乱的低语——也许你自己还颤着嘴唇问出那句话：「严重吗？」然后听得回答：「是的，时候不远了」（啊，这句话一出，整个生命将会显得多么不同！）——我说，你又怎么知道，这一切不会就在今夜降临到*你*身上？
明知这一切，你还*敢*对自己说「好吧，这出戏也许确实不道德：情节或许确实有点太『冒险』，对白太重了一点，『桥段』太香艳了一点。我不敢说良心*全然*安然：可这戏实在精彩，我非看这一次不可！明天起我就过更严谨的日子」吗？*明天，明天，又一个明天！*

> 「心存侥幸而犯罪的人，一面犯罪一面说：
> 『悔罪能叫上帝的审判延宕！』
> 他便是向上帝的灵撒谎；用侮辱
> 挡住了怜悯；他胆敢一试，随即坠落，
> 像一只灼伤的苍蝇，徒然
> 围着自己的痛楚打转，
> 继而领受判决，从此踉跄爬行，
> 双目失明，被人遗忘，跌倒又跌倒。」

===Original===

Let me pause for a moment to say that I believe this thought, of the possibility of death﻿—if calmly realised, and steadily faced﻿—would be one of the best possible tests as to our going to any scene of amusement being right or wrong. If the thought of sudden death acquires, for *you*, a special horror when imagined as happening in a *theatre*, then be very sure the theatre is harmful for *you*, however harmless it may be for others; and that *you* are incurring a deadly peril in going. Be sure the safest rule is that we should not dare to *live* in any scene in which we dare not *die*.
But, once realise what the true object *is* in life﻿—that it is *not* pleasure, *not* knowledge, *not* even fame itself, “that last infirmity of noble minds”﻿—but that it *is* the development of *character*, the rising to a higher, nobler, purer standard, the building-up of the perfect *Man*﻿—and then, so long as we feel that this is going on, and will (we trust) go on forevermore, death has for us no terror; it is not a shadow, but a light; not an end, but a beginning!
One other matter may perhaps seem to call for apology﻿—that I should have treated with such entire want of sympathy the British passion for “Sport,” which no doubt has been in bygone days, and is still, in some forms of it, an excellent school for hardihood and for coolness in moments of danger. But I am not entirely without sympathy for *genuine* “Sport”: I can heartily admire the courage of the man who, with severe bodily toil, and at the risk of his life, hunts down some “man-eating” tiger: and I can heartily sympathize with him when he exults in the glorious excitement of the chase and the hand-to-hand struggle with the monster brought to bay. But I can but look with deep wonder and sorrow on the hunter who, at his ease and in safety, can find pleasure in what involves, for some defenceless creature, wild terror and a death of agony: deeper, if the hunter be one who has pledged himself to preach to men the Religion of universal Love: deepest of all, if it be one of those “*tender and delicate*” beings, whose very name serves as a symbol of Love﻿—“*thy love to me was wonderful, passing the love of women*’﻿—whose mission here is surely to help and comfort all that are in pain or sorrow!

===Chinese===

请容我停笔片刻说一句：我相信，死亡随时可能降临这一念头——倘若能冷静地体认、沉着地直面——便是检验我们去任何娱乐场所是对是错的最好试金石之一。倘若一想到猝然死去、特别是想象它发生在*戏院*里，便让你格外惊恐，那么你尽可以确信：戏院于*你*是有害的，无论它于别人多么无害；你去那儿，便是在招致致命的危险。请相信，最稳妥的准则莫过于：凡是我们不敢在其中*死*的场合，我们就不该胆敢在其中寻*生*。
然而，人一旦认清人生的真正目标*是什么*——它不是享乐，不是知识，甚至不是名声本身，那「高贵心灵最后的弱点」——而是*品格*的养成，是向更高贵、更纯洁之境界的攀升，是完美*之人*的造就——那么，只要我们感到这一进程正在继续，并且（我们相信）将永远继续下去，死亡于我们便再无恐怖；它不是阴影，而是光；不是终结，而是开始！
还有一件事也许需要道歉：我对英国人酷爱的「打猎」竟然表现得全无同情之感。诚然，这种爱好在往昔岁月里曾是一所绝佳的学校，其某些形式至今仍是磨砺胆魄、临危持定的好课堂。
但我对*真正的*「狩猎」并非全无同情：有人历尽艰辛、冒着生命危险追猎某只「食人」猛虎，我能由衷钦佩他的勇气；当他在追猎的壮烈兴奋中、与被逼入绝境的猛兽短兵相接时，我也能由衷地与他共感。可是，对一个安享自在、稳坐安全之地，却能从某种无自卫能力生灵的狂乱惊恐与痛苦死亡中寻得乐趣的猎手，我只能深感惊愕与悲哀；倘若那猎手还是立誓向世人传扬普世之爱的宗教的人，这惊愕与悲哀便更深一层；倘若猎者是那种「*柔弱娇嫩*」的生灵——她们的芳名本就是爱的象征——「*你向我发的爱情奇妙非常，过于妇女的爱情*」——她们在人世的使命分明是扶助、宽慰一切身处痛苦与忧患之人——那便惊愕与悲哀到了极点！

===Original===

> “Farewell, farewell! but this I tell
> To thee, thou Wedding-Guest!
> He prayeth well, who loveth well
> Both man and bird and beast.
> He prayeth best, who loveth best
> All things both great and small;
> For the dear God who loveth us,
> He made and loveth all.”

===Chinese===

> 「别了，别了！但这话我要
> 告诉你，赴婚宴的宾客！
> 谁爱人、爱鸟、爱兽，
> 谁的祷告才蒙垂听。
> 谁爱一切大小生灵
> 爱得最深，谁祷告最好；
> 因为爱我们的亲爱的神，
> 他创造万物，也爱着万物。」

## I Less Bread! More Taxes! / 一 少些面包！多些税！
===Original===

—and then all the people cheered again, and one man, who was more excited than the rest, flung his hat high into the air, and shouted (as well as I could make out) “Who roar for the Sub-Warden?” *Everybody* roared, but whether it was for the Sub-Warden, or not, did not clearly appear: some were shouting “Bread!” and some “Taxes!,” but no one seemed to know what it was they really wanted.
All this I saw from the open window of the Warden’s breakfast-saloon, looking across the shoulder of the Lord Chancellor, who had sprung to his feet the moment the shouting began, almost as if he had been expecting it, and had rushed to the window which commanded the best view of the marketplace.
“What *can* it all mean?” he kept repeating to himself, as, with his hands clasped behind him, and his gown floating in the air, he paced rapidly up and down the room. “I never heard such shouting before﻿—and at this time of the morning, too! And with such unanimity! Doesn’t it strike *you* as very remarkable?”
I represented, modestly, that to *my* ears it appeared that they were shouting for different things, but the Chancellor would not listen to my suggestion for a moment. “They all shout the same words, I assure you!” he said: then, leaning well out of the window, he whispered to a man who was standing close underneath, “Keep ’em together, can’t you? The Warden will be here directly. Give ’em the signal for the march up!” All this was evidently not meant for *my* ears, but I could scarcely help hearing it, considering that my chin was almost on the Chancellor’s shoulder.
The “march up” was a very curious sight: a straggling procession of men, marching two and two, began from the other side of the marketplace, and advanced in an irregular zigzag fashion towards the Palace, wildly tacking from side to side, like a sailing vessel making way against an unfavourable wind﻿—so that the head of the procession was often further from us at the end of one tack than it had been at the end of the previous one.
Yet it was evident that all was being done under orders, for I noticed that all eyes were fixed on the man who stood just under the window, and to whom the Chancellor was continually whispering.

===Chinese===

——接着人们又欢呼起来，其中一个比谁都激动的汉子把帽子高高抛到空中，喊道（我费了些劲才听清）：「谁拥护副守护人（the Sub-Warden），吼一声？」*人人*都吼了，可究竟是不是在拥护副守护人，却听不大分明：有些人喊「面包！」，有些人喊「税！」，但似乎谁也不清楚自己真正想要的是什么。
这一切，我是从守护人（the Warden）的早餐厅敞开的窗子里看见的，视线越过那位大法官（the Lord Chancellor）的肩头——喊声一起他便跳了起来，倒像是一直就在等着似的，径直冲向那扇能将市场广场尽收眼底的窗前。
「这到底都是什么意思？」他不停地自言自语，双手背在身后，袍袖飘飘，在屋里快步走来走去。「我从来没听过这样的喊声——还是在大清早！而且喊得这么齐心！你不觉得很不寻常吗？」
我怯怯地说，在我听来他们喊的好像是各不相同的事，可大法官半刻也不肯听我的意见。「我向你担保，他们喊的是同一句话！」他说：随即探身窗外，低声对正站在窗下的人说：「想法子把他们拢住，行不行？守护人马上就到。叫他们准备朝这边进发！」这些话分明不是说给*我*听的，可谁让我的下巴几乎贴着大法官的肩膀呢，想不听见也难。
这场「进发」可真是奇景：一支稀稀拉拉的队伍，两人一排，从市场广场的另一头列队出发，迂回曲折、七拐八绕地朝王宫推进，疯了似的左右乱转，活像一艘顶着逆风抢风航行的帆船——常常一趟转向走完，队首离我们反倒比上一趟还远。
然而明摆着，一切都是在号令之下进行的，因为我注意到所有的眼睛都盯着窗下那个人——正是大法官不停对他嘀嘀咕咕的那一位。

===Original===

This man held his hat in one hand and a little green flag in the other: whenever he waved the flag the procession advanced a little nearer, when he dipped it they sidled a little farther off, and whenever he waved his hat they all raised a hoarse cheer. “Hoo-roah!” they cried, carefully keeping time with the hat as it bobbed up and down. “Hoo-roah! Noo! Consti! Tooshun! Less! Bread! More! Taxes!”
“That’ll do, that’ll do!” the Chancellor whispered. “Let ’em rest a bit till I give you the word. He’s not here yet!” But at this moment the great folding-doors of the saloon were flung open, and he turned with a guilty start to receive His High Excellency. However it was only Bruno, and the Chancellor gave a little gasp of relieved anxiety.
“Morning!” said the little fellow, addressing the remark, in a general sort of way, to the Chancellor and the waiters. “Doos oo know where Sylvie is? I’s looking for Sylvie!”
“She’s with the Warden, I believe, y’reince!” the Chancellor replied with a low bow. There was, no doubt, a certain amount of absurdity in applying this title (which, as of course you see without my telling you, was nothing but “your Royal Highness” condensed into one syllable) to a small creature whose father was merely the Warden of Outland: still, large excuse must be made for a man who had passed several years at the Court of Fairyland, and had there acquired the almost impossible art of pronouncing five syllables as one.
But the bow was lost upon Bruno, who had run out of the room, even while the great feat of The Unpronounceable Monosyllable was being triumphantly performed.
Just then, a single voice in the distance was understood to shout “A speech from the Chancellor!” “Certainly, my friends!” the Chancellor replied with extraordinary promptitude. “You shall have a speech!” Here one of the waiters, who had been for some minutes busy making a queer-looking mixture of egg and sherry, respectfully presented it on a large silver salver. The Chancellor took it haughtily, drank it off thoughtfully, smiled benevolently on the happy waiter as he set down the empty glass, and began. To the best of my recollection this is what he said.
“Ahem! Ahem! Ahem! Fellow-sufferers, or rather suffering fellows﻿—” (“Don’t call ’em names!” muttered the man under the window. “I didn’t say *felons*!” the Chancellor explained.)

===Chinese===

那人一手拎着帽子，另一手握着一面小小的绿旗：旗子一挥，队伍便往前挪近些；旗子一压，他们便又悄悄蹭远些；而他一挥帽子，众人便齐声发出沙哑的欢呼。「嗬——啦！」他们喊着，还小心翼翼地跟着那顶一起一落的帽子打着拍子。「嗬——啦！宪——法！少——面包！多——税！」
「行了，行了！」大法官低声说。「让他们歇口气，等我的话头。他还没到呢！」可就在这时，大厅的折叠大门猛地敞开，他心虚地一惊，转过身去迎接大人。然而进来的只是布鲁诺（Bruno），大法官长舒一口气，悬着的心放了下来。
「早上好！」小家伙说道，把这句问候一股脑儿抛给大法官和侍者们。「你知道西尔维（Sylvie）在哪儿吗？我在找西尔维呢！」
「她跟守护人在一起，我想，殿下！」大法官深深一躬答道。把这样的称呼（当然不用我说你也看得出，那不过是把「your Royal Highness」压缩成了一个音节）加在一个小家伙身上——他父亲不过是外乡守护人（Warden of Outland）——无疑有几分荒唐：不过对一个在仙境（Fairyland）宫廷里待过好些年、并在那里练成了把五个音节念成一个音节这门近乎不可能的绝技的人，总得多多担待。
可惜这一躬白行了，就在那不可发音的单音节绝活儿正大功告成之际，布鲁诺早已跑出屋去了。
正在这时，远处一个孤零零的声音似乎在喊：「请大法官演讲！」「当然，朋友们！」大法官应答得出奇地利落。「这就给你们演讲！」于是，一个这几分钟里一直忙着调制一种蛋酒混合雪利酒的奇怪饮品的侍者，毕恭毕敬地把它用大银盘奉上。大法官派头十足地接过，若有所思地一饮而尽，放下空杯时慈祥地朝那个受宠若惊的侍者微微一笑，随即开了口。据我记忆所及，他是这么说的。
「咳咳！咳咳！咳咳！同受苦难的诸位，或者说，受苦的同胞们——」（「别骂他们！」窗下那人低声咕哝。「我说的可不是*重罪犯*！」大法官解释道。）

===Original===

“You may be sure that I always sympa﻿—” (“ ’Ear, ’ear!” shouted the crowd, so loudly as quite to drown the orator’s thin squeaky voice) “﻿—that I always sympa﻿—” he repeated. (“Don’t simper quite so much!” said the man under the window. “It makes yer look a hidiot!” And, all this time, “ ’Ear, ’ear!” went rumbling round the marketplace, like a peal of thunder.) “That I always *sympathise*!” yelled the Chancellor, the first moment there was silence. “But your *true* friend is the *Sub-Warden*! Day and night he is brooding on your wrongs﻿—I should say your *rights*﻿—that is to say your *wrongs*﻿—no, I mean your *rights*﻿—” (“Don’t talk no more!” growled the man under the window. “You’re making a mess of it!”) At this moment the Sub-Warden entered the saloon. He was a thin man, with a mean and crafty face, and a greenish-yellow complexion; and he crossed the room very slowly, looking suspiciously about him as if he thought there might be a savage dog hidden somewhere. “Bravo!” he cried, patting the Chancellor on the back. “You did that speech very well indeed. Why, you’re a born orator, man!”
“Oh, that’s nothing!” the Chancellor replied, modestly, with downcast eyes. “Most orators are *born*, you know.”
The Sub-Warden thoughtfully rubbed his chin. “Why, so they are!” he admitted. “I never considered it in that light. Still, you did it very well. A word in your ear!”
The rest of their conversation was all in whispers: so, as I could hear no more, I thought I would go and find Bruno.
I found the little fellow standing in the passage, and being addressed by one of the men in livery, who stood before him, nearly bent double from extreme respectfulness, with his hands hanging in front of him like the fins of a fish. “His High Excellency,” this respectful man was saying, “is in his Study, y’reince!” (He didn’t pronounce this quite so well as the Chancellor.) Thither Bruno trotted, and I thought it well to follow him.
The Warden, a tall dignified man with a grave but very pleasant face, was seated before a writing-table, which was covered with papers, and holding on his knee one of the sweetest and loveliest little maidens it has ever been my lot to see. She looked four or five years older than Bruno, but she had the same rosy cheeks and sparkling eyes, and the same wealth of curly brown hair.

===Chinese===

「你们可以相信，我一向是同——」（「好听！好听！」人群喊道，嗓门大得把演讲人尖细的嗓音完全淹没了）「——我一向是同——」他重复道。（「别笑得那么僵！」窗下那人说。「瞧着跟傻子似的！」而这一整段时间里，「好听！好听！」一直像滚雷一样在市场广场上幕噜噜地转。）「我一向是深表*同情*！」刚一安静下来，大法官就扯着嗓子喊。「但你们*真正*的朋友是副守护人！日日夜夜，他都在为你们所受的不公苦苦思虑——我是说你们的*权益*——也就是说你们所受的不公——不，我是说你们的*权益*——」（「别再说了！」窗下那人呛声道。「你越说越乱！」）这时，副守护人走进了大厅。他是个瘦子，一脸刻薄狡诈，面色蜡黄；他缓缓穿过房间，狐疑地四下张望，仿佛觉得哪里藏着条恶狗似的。「好极了！」他拍着大法官的后背嚷道，「这演讲你做得实在漂亮。呀，你天生就是演说家！」
「哦，那算不得什么！」大法官垂着眼睛，谦虚地答道。「大多数演说家都是*天生的*呢，这你知道。」
副守护人若有所思地摩挲着下巴。「呀，还真是！」他承认道。「我从未从这个角度想过。不过，你确实做得很好。附耳过来！」
他们余下的谈话全是耳语；反正我也听不见了，便想去把布鲁诺找回来。
我发现小家伙正站在过道里，被一个穿号衣的仆人恭恭敬敬地招呼着。那仆人站在他面前，因极度的恭谨几乎弯成了对折，双手垂在身前，活像鱼鳍。「大人此刻，」这位恭敬的人正说着，「正在书房里，殿下！」（他念这个词儿就不如大法官利索了。）布鲁诺小跑着往那边去了，我觉得还是跟上为好。
守护人——一位身材高扙、气度庄重的人，面容严肃却十分和蔼——正坐在堆满文件的书桌前，膝上坐着一位小姑娘，那是我此生所见最甜美可爱的小姑娘中的一位。她看上去比布鲁诺大四五岁，却同样有着红润的脸颊、亮晶晶的眼睛，和一头浓密的棕色鬈发。

===Original===

Her eager smiling face was turned upwards towards her father’s, and it was a pretty sight to see the mutual love with which the two faces﻿—one in the Spring of Life, the other in its late Autumn﻿—were gazing on each other.
“No, you’ve never seen him,” the old man was saying: “you couldn’t, you know, he’s been away so long﻿—traveling from land to land, and seeking for health, more years than you’ve been alive, little Sylvie!”
Here Bruno climbed upon his other knee, and a good deal of kissing, on a rather complicated system, was the result.
“He only came back last night,” said the Warden, when the kissing was over: “he’s been traveling posthaste, for the last thousand miles or so, in order to be here on Sylvie’s birthday. But he’s a very early riser, and I dare say he’s in the Library already. Come with me and see him. He’s always kind to children. You’ll be sure to like him.”
“Has the Other Professor come too?” Bruno asked in an awestruck voice.
“Yes, they arrived together. The Other Professor is﻿—well, you won’t like him quite so much, perhaps. He’s a little more *dreamy*, you know.”
“I wiss *Sylvie* was a little more dreamy,” said Bruno.
“What *do* you mean, Bruno?” said Sylvie.
Bruno went on addressing his father. “She says she *can’t*, oo know. But I thinks it isn’t *can’t*, it’s *won’t*.”
“Says she *can’t* dream!” the puzzled Warden repeated.
“She *do* say it,” Bruno persisted. “When I says to her ‘Let’s stop lessons!’, she says ‘Oh, I can’t *dream* of letting oo stop yet!’ ”
“He always wants to stop lessons,” Sylvie explained, “five minutes after we begin!”
“Five minutes’ lessons a day!” said the Warden. “You won’t learn much at *that* rate, little man!”
“That’s just what Sylvie says,” Bruno rejoined. “She says I *won’t* learn my lessons. And I tells her, over and over, I *can’t* learn ’em. And what doos oo think she says? She says ‘It isn’t *can’t*, it’s *won’t*!’ ”
“Let’s go and see the Professor,” the Warden said, wisely avoiding further discussion. The children got down off his knees, each secured a hand, and the happy trio set off for the Library﻿—followed by me. I had come to the conclusion by this time that none of the party (except, for a few moments, the Lord Chancellor) was in the least able to see me.
“What’s the matter with him?”

===Chinese===

她那张热切带笑的小脸仰起来朝着父亲的脸，那情景真是动人：两张脸——一张在生命的春天，一张在生命的暮秋——满含着彼此的爱意，默默相望。
「不，你们从未见过他，」老人正说着，「你们不可能见过，你是知道的，他离家太久了——逐国漫游，寻访健康，年头比你的岁数还多呢，小西尔维！」
布鲁诺一听便爬上了他的另一条膝头，于是一场按着相当复杂体系进行的亲吻随即展开。
「他昨晚才回来，」亲吻结束后守护人说，「最后那一千里左右的路，他是星夜兼程赶的，就为了赶上西尔维的生日。不过他起得极早，我敢说他这会儿已经在图书室了。跟我来见见他吧。他对孩子一向亲切。你们一定会喜欢他的。」
「另一位教授（the Other Professor）也来了吗？」布鲁诺做做做敬地轻声向。
「来了，他们一起到的。另一位教授嘛——嗯，你们未必会那么喜欢他。他有点儿更*爱幻想*，你知道的。」
「我希望*西尔维*也爱幻想点儿。」布鲁诺说。
「你这话什么意思，布鲁诺？」西尔维问。
布鲁诺只顾对他父亲说。「她总说她*不能*，你知道吧。可我觉得不是*不能*，是*不肯*。」
「她说她*不能*幻想！」守护人一头雾水地重复。
「她就是这么说的。」布鲁诺坚持道。「我跟她说『别上课啦！』，她就说『哦，现在让你停下课，我可是连*梦*都不敢做的！』」
「他老是想停课，」西尔维解释说，「一开课五分钟就想！」
「一天上五分钟的课！」守护人说。「照这个迷度，你可举不到多少东西，小家伙！」
「西尔维就是这么说的，」布鲁诺回嘴。「她说我*不肯*学功课。我就一遍又一遍地告诉她，我是*学不会*。你猜她怎么说？她说『不是学不会，是不肯学！』」
「我们去见教授吧。」守护人说，明智地避开了进一步讨论。孩子们从他膝头滑下来，一人占领一只手，快乐的三人组朝图书室出发了——我紧随其后。此时我已断定，这一行人中（除了那短短几分钟里的大法官）没有一个能看见我。
「他怎么了？」

===Original===

Sylvie asked, walking with a little extra sedateness, by way of example to Bruno at the other side, who never ceased jumping up and down.
“What *was* the matter﻿—but I hope he’s all right now﻿—was lumbago, and rheumatism, and that kind of thing. He’s been curing *himself*, you know: he’s a very learned doctor. Why, he’s actually *invented* three new diseases, besides a new way of breaking your collarbone!”
“Is it a nice way?” said Bruno.
“Well, hum, not *very*,” the Warden said, as we entered the Library. “And here *is* the Professor. Good morning, Professor! Hope you’re quite rested after your journey!”
A jolly-looking, fat little man, in a flowery dressing-gown, with a large book under each arm, came trotting in at the other end of the room, and was going straight across without taking any notice of the children. “I’m looking for Vol. Three,” he said. “Do you happen to have seen it?”
“You don’t see my *children*, Professor!” the Warden exclaimed, taking him by the shoulders and turning him round to face them.
The Professor laughed violently: then he gazed at them through his great spectacles, for a minute or two, without speaking.
At last he addressed Bruno. “I hope you have had a good night, my child?”
Bruno looked puzzled. “I’s had the same night *oo’ve* had,” he replied. “There’s only been *one* night since yesterday!”
It was the Professor’s turn to look puzzled now. He took off his spectacles, and rubbed them with his hankerchief. Then he gazed at them again. Then he turned to the Warden. “Are they bound?” he enquired.
“No, we aren’t,” said Bruno, who thought himself quite able to answer *this* question.
The Professor shook his head sadly. “Not even half-bound?”
“Why *would* we be half-bound?” said Bruno. “We’re not prisoners!”
But the Professor had forgotten all about them by this time, and was speaking to the Warden again. “You’ll be glad to hear,” he was saying, “that the Barometer’s beginning to move﻿—”
“Well, which way?” said the Warden﻿—adding to the children, “Not that *I* care, you know. Only *he* thinks it affects the weather. He’s a wonderfully clever man, you know. Sometimes he says things that only the Other Professor can understand. Sometimes he says things that *nobody* can understand! Which way is it, Professor? Up or down?”
“Neither!” said the Professor, gently clapping his hands. “It’s going sideways﻿—if I may so express myself.”

===Chinese===

西尔维问。她走路格外端庄了些，好给另一边蹦蹏不止的布鲁诺做个榜样。
「他从前不舒服——不过我希望他现在已经好了——是腰痛啦、风湿啦那一类毛病。他一直在给自己治病呢，你们知道：他是个非常博学的医生。呀，除了发明了一种新的锁骨骨折法，他还发明了三种新疾病呢！」
「那方法好玩吗？」布鲁诺问。
「嗯哼，不太好玩。」守护人说，这时我们进了图书室。「教授（the Professor）这不就来了。早安，教授！愿你旅途之后充分休息好了！」
一个乐呵呵的胖小个儿男人，穿着花卉图案的晨袍，左右胳膊各夹一本大书，从房间另一头小跑着进来，看也不看孩子们一眼就要径直穿过去。「我在找第三卷，」他说，「你们碰巧见过它吗？」
「你怎么看不见我的*孩子们*呢，教授！」守护人嚷道，抓住他的肩膀把他转过来面对孩子们。
教授卷帘大笑起来：然后透过他那副大眼镜盯着他们看了一两分钟，一言不发。
最后他终于开口对布鲁诺说话。「祝你昨晚过得愉快，孩子？」
布鲁诺一脸困惑。「我过的夜和*你*过的是同一个呀，」他答道。「从昨天到现在只过了一个晚上！」
这回轮到教授一脸困惑了。他摘下眼镜，用手绢擦拭。再看看两个孩子。然后转向守护人。「他们上没上装订？」他问。
「没上。」布鲁诺说，他觉得自己完全回答得了*这个*问题。
教授遗憾地摇头。「半皮面装订也没有？」
「我们为什么要被装订？」布鲁诺说。「我们又不是犯人！」
可教授这会儿早已把他们忘得一千二净，又跟守护人说起话来。「你会很高兴听到，」他正说着，「气压计开始动了——」
「哦，往哪边动？」守护人说——又对孩子们补了句：「倒不是我关心，你们知道。只是他相信它能影响天气。他是个聪明得不得了的人，你们知道。有时他说的话只有另一位教授听得懂。有时他说的话*谁也*听不懂！往哪边动，教授？上还是下？」
「都不是！」教授轻轻抬手拍了一下，说。「它在横向移动——如果我可以这么说的话。」

===Original===

“And what kind of weather does *that* produce?” said the Warden. “Listen, children! Now you’ll hear something worth knowing!”
“Horizontal weather,” said the Professor, and made straight for the door, very nearly trampling on Bruno, who had only just time to get out of his way.
“*Isn’t* he learned?” the Warden said, looking after him with admiring eyes. “Positively he runs over with learning!”
“But he needn’t run over *me*!” said Bruno.
The Professor was back in a moment: he had changed his dressing-gown for a frock-coat, and had put on a pair of very strange-looking boots, the tops of which were open umbrellas. “I thought you’d like to see them,” he said. “*These* are the boots for horizontal weather!”
“But what’s the use of wearing umbrellas round one’s knees?”
“In *ordinary* rain,” the Professor admitted, “they would *not* be of much use. But if ever it rained *horizontally*, you know, they would be invaluable﻿—simply invaluable!”
“Take the Professor to the breakfast-saloon, children,” said the Warden. “And tell them not to wait for me. I had breakfast early, as I’ve some business to attend to.” The children seized the Professor’s hands, as familiarly as if they had known him for years, and hurried him away. I followed respectfully behind.

===Chinese===

「那会产生什么样的天气呢？」守护人问。「听着，孩子们！这下你们可要听到值得知道的事了！」
「横向天气。」教授说完就径直朝门口走去，几乎踩到布鲁诺，小家伙勉强来得及闪开。
「他学问大不大？」守护人望着他的背影，眼神里满是钦佩。「他的学问简直满得溢出来了！」
「可他不必溢到*我*身上来呀！」布鲁诺说。
转眼间教授又回来了：晨袍换成了燕尾服，脚上蹬着一双样子古怪至极的靴子，靴口是一把把撑开的雨伞。「我想你们会愿意看看它们的，」他说。「*这种*靴子就是对付横向天气的！」
「可是把伞穿在膝盖上有什么用呢？」
「在*普通的*雨里，」教授承认道，「它们确实派不上多大用场。可一旦下起*横向的*雨来，你们知道，它们可就无价了——绝对无价！」
「孩子们，带教授去早餐厅吧，」守护人说。「告诉他们不用等我。我早点过餐了，还有点事要办。」孩子们拉住教授的手，熟稔得仿佛已认识了他多年，簇拥着他匆匆去了。我恭恭敬敬地跟在后面。

## II L’Amie Inconnue / 二 陌生的女友
===Original===

As we entered the breakfast-saloon, the Professor was saying “﻿—and he had breakfast by himself, early: so he begged you wouldn’t wait for him, my Lady. This way, my Lady,” he added, “this way!” And then, with (as it seemed to me) most superfluous politeness, he flung open the door of my compartment, and ushered in “﻿—a young and lovely lady!” I muttered to myself with some bitterness. “And this is, of course, the opening scene of Vol. I. *She* is the Heroine. And *I* am one of those subordinate characters that only turn up when needed for the development of her destiny, and whose final appearance is outside the church, waiting to greet the Happy Pair!”
“Yes, my Lady, change at Fayfield,” were the next words I heard (oh that too obsequious Guard!), “next station but one.” And the door closed, and the lady settled down into her corner, and the monotonous throb of the engine (making one feel as if the train were some gigantic monster, whose very circulation we could feel) proclaimed that we were once more speeding on our way. “The lady had a perfectly formed nose,” I caught myself saying to myself, “hazel eyes, and lips﻿—” and here it occurred to me that to see, for myself, what “the lady” was really like, would be more satisfactory than much speculation.
I looked round cautiously, and﻿—was entirely disappointed of my hope. The veil, which shrouded her whole face, was too thick for me to see more than the glitter of bright eyes and the hazy outline of what *might* be a lovely oval face, but might also, unfortunately, be an equally *un*lovely one. I closed my eyes again, saying to myself “﻿—couldn’t have a better chance for an experiment in Telepathy! I’ll *think out* her face, and afterwards test the portrait with the original.”
At first, no result at all crowned my efforts, though I “divided my swift mind,” now hither, now thither, in a way that I felt sure would have made Aeneas green with envy: but the dimly-seen oval remained as provokingly blank as ever﻿—a mere Ellipse, as if in some mathematical diagram, without even the Foci that might be made to do duty as a nose and a mouth. Gradually, however, the conviction came upon me that I could, by a certain concentration of thought, *think the veil away*, and so get a glimpse of the mysterious face﻿—as to which the two questions, “is she pretty?” and “is she plain?”

===Chinese===

我们走进早餐厅时，教授正说着「——他一个人早早用过早餐了，所以他请您不必等他，夫人。这边请，夫人，」他又补上一句，「这边请！」接着，以一种（在我看来）全然多余的殷勤，猛地拉开我那间包厢的门，引进来一位「——年轻貌美的女士！」我暗自酸溜溜地喃囊。「这自然便是第一卷的开场戏了。*她*是女主角。而我呢，是那种次要角色，只在剧情需要推进她的命运时才出场，最后一面是在教堂外面，等着向那对幸福的新人道喜！」
「是的，夫人，在费菲尔德换车，」这是我听到的下一句话（哼，那个过分谄媚的乘务员！），「再过一站就到。」车门关上了，女士在角落里安顿下来，机车单调的震响（让人觉得这火车仿佛是头巨兽，连它的血液循环都能感觉到）宣告我们又上路疾驰了。「这位女士鼻型完美无瑕，」我发觉自己正在心里描画，「榛色的眼睛，还有嘴唇——」说到这儿我忽然想到，与其这样凭空猜测，倒不如亲眼看看这位「女士」究竟什么模样，岂不更叫人满意。
我小心地侧目张望——希望完全落了空。罩住她整张脸的面纱太厚，我只看得见明亮眼睛的闪光，和一个朦胧的轮廓——*也许*是张可爱的鹅蛋脸，可不幸也可能同样*不*可爱。我重新跑上眼，暗自说道：「——这不是做心灵感应实验的绝佳机会嘛！我要用意念把她的脸想出来，然后再拿原版检验这幅肖像。」
起初，我的种种努力一无所获，尽管我把「敏捷的心智」忽而抛向这头、忽而抛向那头——我确信这阵仗足以让埃涅阿斯嫉妒得脸色发青：可那隐约可见的鹅蛋脸依旧空空如也，恼人得很——只剩一个椭圆，像数学图上画的，连能权充鼻子和嘴巴的两个焦点都没有。渐渐地，我却生出一种确信：只要意念足够集中，我准能*把面纱想掉*，从而一窥那张神秘的脸——关于它，「她漂亮吗」和「她难看吗」这两个问题……

===Original===

, still hung suspended, in my mind, in beautiful equipoise.
Success was partial﻿—and fitful﻿—still there *was* a result: ever and anon, the veil seemed to vanish, in a sudden flash of light: but, before I could fully realise the face, all was dark again. In each such glimpse, the face seemed to grow more childish and more innocent: and, when I had at last *thought* the veil entirely away, it was, unmistakeably, the sweet face of little Sylvie!
“So, either I’ve been dreaming about Sylvie,” I said to myself, “and this is the reality. Or else I’ve really been with Sylvie, and this is a dream! Is Life itself a dream, I wonder?”
To occupy the time, I got out the letter, which had caused me to take this sudden railway-journey from my London home down to a strange fishing-town on the North coast, and read it over again:﻿—

> “Dear old Friend,
> “I’m sure it will be as great a pleasure to me, as it can possibly be to you, to meet once more after so many years: and of course I shall be ready to give you all the benefit of such medical skill as I have: only, you know, one mustn’t violate professional etiquette! And you are already in the hands of a first-rate London doctor, with whom it would be utter affectation for me to pretend to compete. (I make no doubt he is right in saying the heart is affected: all your symptoms point that way.) One thing, at any rate, I have already done in my doctorial capacity﻿—secured you a bedroom on the ground-floor, so that you will not need to ascend the stairs at all.
> “I shall expect you by last train on Friday, in accordance with your letter: and, till then, I shall say, in the words of the old song, ‘Oh for Friday nicht! Friday’s lang a-coming!’
> “Yours always,
> “Arthur Forester.
> “P.S. Do you believe in Fate?”

===Chinese===

……在我心里仍悬而未决，两边不分高下，妙不可言。
成功是一星半点、断断续续的——但总算有了结果：那面纱不时地仿佛在一道骤亮的闪光中消失了：可不等我完全看清那张脸，一切又归于黑暗。每这样一瞥，那张脸都显得更孩子气、更天真：而当我终于把面纱整个*想*掉时，那张脸分明、无疑，是小西尔维那张甜美的脸！
「这么说，要么我一直在梦见西尔维，」我自言自语，「而眼下才是现实。要么我方才真的和西尔维在一起，而此刻才是一场梦！人生本身会不会也是一场梦呢？」
为了打发时光，我掏出那封信——正是它害我从伦敦家中突然起程，坐火车北上这座陌生的海滨渔镇——又从头读了一遍：——

> 「亲爱的老友：
> 「阔别多年之后重逢，这于我的欣喜，想必与于你的一般无二：我自然愿以我所有的医术为你效劳：只是，你知道，总不能坏了行里的规矩！何况你已在一位伦敦一流医生的手上，我若装模作样地跟他争个高下，那未免太矫情了。（他说心脏受了累，我毫不怀疑他是对的：你的种种症状都指向这一点。）有一件事，我倒是已经以医生的身份办妥了——给你订下了一间底层的卧室，这样你完全不必上楼梯。
> 「依照你的信，我将恭候你周五的末班车：在那之前，且让我借老歌里的词儿说一句：『啊，盼着礼拜五的夜！礼拜五可真来得慢！』
> 「永远是你的，
> 「阿瑟·福雷斯特（Arthur Forester）
> 「又及：你信命运吗？」

===Original===

This Postscript puzzled me sorely. “He is far too sensible a man,” I thought, “to have become a Fatalist. And yet what else can he mean by it?” And, as I folded up the letter and put it away, I inadvertently repeated the words aloud. “Do you believe in Fate?”
The fair “Incognita” turned her head quickly at the sudden question. “No, I don’t!” she said with a smile. “Do you?”
“I﻿—I didn’t mean to ask the question!” I stammered, a little taken aback at having begun a conversation in so unconventional a fashion.
The lady’s smile became a laugh﻿—not a mocking laugh, but the laugh of a happy child who is perfectly at her ease. “Didn’t you?” she said. “Then it was a case of what you Doctors call ‘unconscious cerebration’?”
“I am no Doctor,” I replied. “Do I look so like one? Or what makes you think it?”
She pointed to the book I had been reading, which was so lying that its title, “Diseases of the Heart,” was plainly visible.
“One needn’t be a *Doctor*,” I said, “to take an interest in medical books. There’s another class of readers, who are yet more deeply interested﻿—”
“You mean the *Patients*?” she interrupted, while a look of tender pity gave new sweetness to her face. “But,” with an evident wish to avoid a possibly painful topic, “one needn’t be *either*, to take an interest in books of *Science*. Which contain the greatest amount of Science, do you think, the books, or the minds?”
“Rather a profound question for a lady!” I said to myself, holding, with the conceit so natural to Man, that Woman’s intellect is essentially shallow. And I considered a minute before replying. “If you mean *living* minds, I don’t think it’s possible to decide. There is so much *written* Science that no living person has ever *read*: and there is so much *thought-out* Science that hasn’t yet been *written*. But, if you mean the whole human race, then I think the *minds* have it: everything, recorded in *books*, must have once been in some *mind*, you know.”
“Isn’t that rather like one of the Rules in Algebra?” my Lady enquired. (“*Algebra* too!” I thought with increasing wonder.) “I mean, if we consider thoughts as *factors*, may we not say that the Least Common Multiple of all the *minds* contains that of all the *books*; but not the other way?”
“Certainly we may!” I replied, delighted with the illustration.

===Chinese===

这则附言把我难住了。「他是个头脑清醒透顶的人，」我想，「断不至于变成了宿命论者。可不然这话是什么意思呢？」我把信折好收起时，不觉把这句话念出了声：「你信命运吗？」
那位美丽的「无名女士」被这突如其来的一问惊得转过头来。「不，我不信！」她微笑着说。「你呢？」
「我——我并不是有意要发问的！」我结结巴巴地说，以如此不合常轨的方式开了场，未免有些发窘。
女士的微笑化作了笑声——不是嘲弄的笑，而是一个悠然自得的快乐孩子般的笑。「不是有意的？」她说。「那这就是你们医生所说的『无意识思考』的一个病例了？」
「我不是医生，」我答道。「我看上去很像个医生吗？还是什么让您这么想？」
她指了指我方才在读的那本书——它正好摆着，《心脏病症》的书名一目了然。
「对*医学*书籍感兴趣，」我说，「未必就得是*医生*。还有一类读者，兴趣反倒更深厚——」
「您是指*病人*吧？」她插话道，一抹温柔的怜惜给她的脸平添了几分甜美。「不过，」她显然有意避开这个也许令人伤感的话题，「不论做哪一种人，都可以对*科学*书籍感兴趣呀。您说，哪一边包含的科学更多呢，是书，还是头脑？」
「对一位女士来说，这问题可够深的！」我暗自想——怀着人类天生的自负，认定女人的智力本质上是浅的。我斟酌了一分钟才回答。「如果您说的是*活着的*头脑，我想无从评判。世上*写成了*的科学浩如烟海，没有一个活人*读过*；而*想出来*的科学也有许多，尚未*写成*文字。可如果您指的是全人类，那我认为*头脑*更胜一筹：凡*记录在书里*的东西，必定都曾在某个*头脑里*待过，您知道的。」
「这岂不像代数里的一条法则？」女士问道。（居然还有*代数*！我越发惊异了。）「我是说，倘若我们把思想看作*因数*，我们可不可以说，所有*头脑*的最小公倍数包含了所有*书籍*的最小公倍数，反过来却不行呢？」
「当然可以！」我答道，这个类比让我心花怒放。

===Original===

“And what a grand thing it would be,” I went on dreamily, thinking aloud rather than talking, “if we could only *apply* that Rule to books! You know, in finding the Least Common Multiple, we strike out a quantity wherever it occurs, except in the term where it is raised to its highest power. So we should have to erase every recorded thought, except in the sentence where it is expressed with the greatest intensity.”
My Lady laughed merrily. “*Some* books would be reduced to blank paper, I’m afraid!” she said.
“They would. Most libraries would be terribly diminished in *bulk*. But just think what they would gain in *quality*!”
“When will it be done?” she eagerly asked. “If there’s any chance of it in *my* time, I think I’ll leave off reading, and wait for it!”
“Well, perhaps in another thousand years or so﻿—”
“Then there’s no use waiting!” said my Lady. “Let’s sit down. Uggug, my pet, come and sit by me!”
“Anywhere but by *me*!” growled the Sub-Warden. “The little wretch always manages to upset his coffee!”
I guessed at once (as perhaps the reader will also have guessed, if, like myself, he is *very* clever at drawing conclusions) that my Lady was the Sub-Warden’s wife, and that Uggug (a hideous fat boy, about the same age as Sylvie, with the expression of a prize-pig) was their son. Sylvie and Bruno, with the Lord Chancellor, made up a party of seven.
“And you actually got a plunge-bath every morning?” said the Sub-Warden, seemingly in continuation of a conversation with the Professor. “Even at the little roadside-inns?”
“Oh, certainly, certainly!” the Professor replied with a smile on his jolly face. “Allow me to explain. It is, in fact, a very simple problem in Hydrodynamics. (That means a combination of Water and Strength.) If we take a plunge-bath, and a man of great strength (such as myself) about to plunge into it, we have a perfect example of this science. I am bound to admit,” the Professor continued, in a lower tone and with downcast eyes, “that we need a man of *remarkable* strength. He must be able to spring from the floor to about twice his own height, gradually turning over as he rises, so as to come down again head first.”
“Why, you need a *flea*, not a *man*!” exclaimed the Sub-Warden.
“Pardon me,” said the Professor. “This particular kind of bath is *not* adapted for a flea.

===Chinese===

「而且，倘若能把这条法则*应用*到书上，那该是多么美妙的事啊！」我心不在焉地接着说，与其说是谈话，不如说是自言自语。「您知道，求最小公倍数时，同一个量无论在哪里出现都要划掉，唯独它幂次最高的那一项保留。那么我们也就得抹去每一条被记录下来的思想，唯独它在哪句话里表现得最强烈，才把那句保留。」
女士欢快地笑了。「恐怕*有些*书要缩成一沓白纸了！」她说。
「会的。大多数图书馆的*篇幅*都要大大缩水。可您想想，它们的*品质*将得到多大的提升！」
「什么时候能做到呢？」她急切地问。「要是*我*这辈子还赶得上，我想我就此搁下书不读了，专等那一天！」
「嗯，也许再过一千年左右吧——」
「那就等也无用啦！」女士说。「我们坐下吧。乌格格（Uggug），小宝贝，过来坐在妈妈身边！」
「坐哪儿都行，就是别坐*我*旁边！」副守护人吼道。「这小孺障回回都能把咖啡打翻！」
我立刻猜到（倘若读者也像我一样*极*善于下结论，恐怕早已猜到）：这位夫人是副守护人的妻子，而乌格格（一个面目可憎的胖小子，年纪与西尔维相仿，长着一副得奖肉猪的神情）是他们的儿子。加上西尔维、布鲁诺和大法官，凑成了一桌七人。
「这么说，你当真每天早上都能洗个浸浴？」副守护人说，像是在继续先前与教授的谈话。「哪怕在路边的小客栈里？」
「哦，当然，当然！」教授乐呵呵的脸上挂着笑回答。「请允许我解释。这其实是流体动力学里一个非常简单的问题。（意思就是水和力气的结合。）取一个浸浴桶，再取一个力大无穷的人（比如在下），正要往桶里跳——这门科学便有了一个完美的例证。不过我必须承认，」教授压低嗓音、垂下眼帘继续说，「我们需要一个力气*非同小可*的人。他得能从地板上跃起约莫自己身高两倍的高度，边升边慢慢翻转，好头朝下落回来。」
「哎呀，你要的是只*跳蚤*，根本不是*人*！」副守护人叫道。
「恕我直言，」教授说，「这种特殊的浴法*并不*适合跳蚤。

===Original===

Let us suppose,” he continued, folding his table-napkin into a graceful festoon, “that this represents what is perhaps *the* necessity of this Age﻿—the Active Tourist’s Portable Bath. You may describe it briefly, if you like,” looking at the Chancellor, “by the letters A.T.P.B.”
The Chancellor, much disconcerted at finding everybody looking at him, could only murmur, in a shy whisper, “Precisely so!”
“One great advantage of this plunge-bath,” continued the Professor, “is that it requires only half-a-gallon of water﻿—”
“I don’t call it a *plunge*-bath,” His Sub-Excellency remarked, “unless your Active Tourist goes *right under*!”
“But he *does* go right under,” the old man gently replied. “The A.T. hangs up the P.B. on a nail﻿—*thus*. He then empties the water-jug into it﻿—places the empty jug below the bag﻿—leaps into the air﻿—descends headfirst into the bag﻿—the water rises round him to the top of the bag﻿—and there you are!” he triumphantly concluded. “The A.T. is as much under water as if he’d gone a mile or two down into the Atlantic!”
“And he’s drowned, let us say, in about four minutes﻿—”
“By no means!” the Professor answered with a proud smile. “After about a minute, he quietly turns a tap at the lower end of the P.B.﻿—all the water runs back into the jug﻿—and there you are again!”
“But how in the world is he to get *out* of the bag again?”
“*That*, I take it,” said the Professor, “is the most beautiful part of the whole invention. All the way up the P.B., inside, are loops for the thumbs; so it’s something like going upstairs, only perhaps less comfortable; and, by the time the A.T. has risen out of the bag, all but his head, he’s sure to topple over, one way or the other﻿—the Law of Gravity secures *that*. And there he is on the floor again!”
“A little bruised, perhaps?”
“Well, yes, a little bruised; but *having had his plunge-bath*: that’s the great thing.”
“Wonderful! It’s almost beyond belief!” murmured the Sub-Warden. The Professor took it as a compliment, and bowed with a gratified smile.
“*Quite* beyond belief!” my Lady added﻿—meaning, no doubt, to be more complimentary still. The Professor bowed, but he didn’t smile *this* time.
“I can assure you,” he said earnestly, “that, *provided the bath was made*, I used it every morning. I certainly *ordered* it﻿—*that* I am clear about﻿—my only doubt is, whether the man ever finished making it.

===Chinese===

「我们不妨设想，」他继续说着，把餐巾叠成一道雅致的垂花形，「它代表着这个时代也许*首屈一指*的必需品——活跃旅行者的便携浴缸。您若愿意，」他望着大法官，「可以简称为 A.T.P.B. 四个字母。」
大法官发现人人都望着自己，窘迫不已，只怯生生地低声道：「正是如此！」
「这种浸浴的一大妙处，」教授接着说，「是它只需半加仑水——」
「这我可不敢称作*浸*浴，」副大人评了一句，「除非您那位活跃旅行者能*整个儿没入*水中！」
「可他*确实*整个儿没入呀，」老人温和地答道。「活跃旅行者把便携浴缸往钉子上一挂——*就像这样*。再把水罐里的水倒进去——空罐放在袋子底下——纵身跃入空中——头朝下落进袋里——水便在他四周涨到袋顶——这不就成了嘛！」他得意洋洋地总结道。「这位活跃旅行者没入水中的程度，跟沉到大西洋一两里深处没什么两样！」
「那么，比方说，四分钟之后他就淹死了——」
「绝非如此！」教授带着自豪的微笑回答。「约莫一分钟之后，他不慌不忙拧开便携浴缸下端的一个龙头——水便尽数流回罐中——这不又成了嘛！」
「可他究竟要怎么从袋子里*出*来呢？」
「*这个*嘛，依我看，」教授说，「是整项发明中最精彩的部分。便携浴缸内侧一路都缝着供大拇指抠住的环扣；所以有点像上楼梯，只是或许没那么舒服；等活跃旅行者从袋里升出来、只剩脑袋还留在袋中时，他必定会朝这边或那边翻倒——万有引力定律*管保*这一点。于是他又回到地板上了！」
「兴许会摔出几块瘀伤？」
「嗯，是的，瘀伤是有一点儿；可*浸浴是洗过了*：这才是要紧的。」
「神了！简直难以置信！」副守护人喃喃道。教授把这当作恭维，心满意足地微笑还礼。
「*完全*超乎置信！」夫人补了一句——无疑想奉承得更进一层。教授躬身还礼，可这回却笑不出来了。
「我可以向您担保，」他一本正经地说，「*只要那浴缸做成了*，我天天早上都用它的。订做这事我*肯定*是办了的——*这一点*我记得清楚——我唯一拿不准的，是那工匠到底有没有把它做完。隔了这么多年，实在难以记清——」

===Original===

It’s difficult to remember, after so many years﻿—”
At this moment the door, very slowly and creakingly, began to open, and Sylvie and Bruno jumped up, and ran to meet the well-known footstep.

===Chinese===

正在这时，房门缓缓地、吱吱呀呀地开了，西尔维和布鲁诺跳起来，奔向那阵熟悉的脚步声。

## III Birthday-Presents / 三 生日礼物
===Original===

“It’s my brother!” the Sub-Warden exclaimed, in a warning whisper. “Speak out, and be quick about it!”
The appeal was evidently addressed to the Lord Chancellor, who instantly replied, in a shrill monotone, like a little boy repeating the alphabet, “As I was remarking, your Sub-Excellency, this portentous movement﻿—”
“You began too soon!” the other interrupted, scarcely able to restrain himself to a whisper, so great was his excitement. “He couldn’t have heard you. Begin again!”
“As I was remarking,” chanted the obedient Lord Chancellor, “this portentous movement has already assumed the dimensions of a Revolution!”
“And what *are* the dimensions of a Revolution?” The voice was genial and mellow, and the face of the tall dignified old man, who had just entered the room, leading Sylvie by the hand, and with Bruno riding triumphantly on his shoulder, was too noble and gentle to have scared a less guilty man: but the Lord Chancellor turned pale instantly, and could hardly articulate the words “The dimensions﻿—your﻿—your High Excellency? I﻿—I﻿—scarcely comprehend!”
“Well, the length, breadth, and thickness, if you like it better!” And the old man smiled, half-contemptuously.
The Lord Chancellor recovered himself with a great effort, and pointed to the open window. “If your High Excellency will listen for a moment to the shouts of the exasperated populace﻿—” (“of the exasperated populace!” the Sub-Warden repeated in a louder tone, as the Lord Chancellor, being in a state of abject terror, had dropped almost into a whisper)“﻿—you will understand what it is they want.”
And at that moment there surged into the room a hoarse confused cry, in which the only clearly audible words were “Less﻿—bread﻿—More﻿—taxes!” The old man laughed heartily. “What in the world﻿—” he was beginning: but the Chancellor heard him not. “Some mistake!” he muttered, hurrying to the window, from which he shortly returned with an air of relief. “*Now* listen!” he exclaimed, holding up his hand impressively. And now the words came quite distinctly, and with the regularity of the ticking of a clock, “More﻿—bread﻿—Less﻿—taxes!”
“More bread!” the Warden repeated in astonishment. “Why, the new Government Bakery was opened only last week, and I gave orders to sell the bread at cost-price during the present scarcity! What *can* they expect more?”
“The Bakery’s closed, y’reince!”

===Chinese===

「是我哥哥来了！」副守护人警告似的低呼。「大声讲，快！」
这话显然是说给大法官听的，他立刻答了腔，尖着嗓子、平板单调，活像背字母表的小学生：「方才我正说到，副大人，这场不祥的动向——」
「你起头起得太早了！」对方打断他，激动得几乎压不住嗓门。「他不可能听见的。重来！」
「方才我正说到，」听话的大法官吟诵般地念道，「这场不祥的动向，其规模已经堪比一场革命！」
「那么，一场革命的*规模*究竟是怎样的呢？」嗓音温厚圆润。那位高扙威严的老人刚进屋来，一手牵着西尔维，肩上得意洋洋地骑着布鲁诺；他的面容太高贵、太温和了，换一个心虚没这么重的人根本不会被吓着：可大法官顿时面无人色，几乎语不成句：「规模——大——大人？我——我——不甚明白！」
「唔，说长、宽、高，您要是乐意也行！」老人微笑着，带三分轻蔑。
大法官费了好大劲才定下神来，指向敞开的窗子。「倘若大人肯听一听愤怒民众的呼喊——」（「愤怒民众的呼喊！」副守护人提高了嗓门替他复述，因为大法官已陷于极度惶恐，声音几乎低成了耳语）「——您就会明白他们要的是什么了。」
正在这时，一阵嘶哑杂乱的喊声涌进屋来，其中听得清的只有「少——面包——多——税！」老人开怀大笑。「这到底——」他刚开口：大法官却没听见。「一定是听岔了！」他咕哝着，急忙奔到窗前，不多时便如释重负地回来。「*现在*您听！」他郑重其事地举起手。这一回，词句听得清清楚楚，还像钟摆一样规整：「多——面包——少——税！」
「还要更多面包！」守护人惊愕地重复道。「咦，新的官办面包坊上礼拜才开张，我还下过令，眼下缺粮期间按成本价售卖！他们还能指望*再*多要什么呢？」
「面包坊关门了，殿下！」

===Original===

the Chancellor said, more loudly and clearly than he had spoken yet. He was emboldened by the consciousness that *here*, at least, he had evidence to produce: and he placed in the Warden’s hands a few printed notices, that were lying ready, with some open ledgers, on a side-table.
“Yes, yes, *I* see!” the Warden muttered, glancing carelessly through them. “Order countermanded by my brother, and supposed to be *my* doing! Rather sharp practice! It’s all right!” he added in a louder tone. “My name is signed to it: so I take it on myself. But what do they mean by ‘Less Taxes’? How *can* they be less? I abolished the last of them a month ago!”
“It’s been put on again, y’reince, and by y’reince’s own orders!”, and other printed notices were submitted for inspection.
The Warden, whilst looking them over, glanced once or twice at the Sub-Warden, who had seated himself before one of the open ledgers, and was quite absorbed in adding it up; but he merely repeated “It’s all right. I accept it as my doing.”
“And they do say,” the Chancellor went on sheepishly﻿—looking much more like a convicted thief than an Officer of State, “that a change of Government, by the abolition of the Sub-Warden﻿—I mean,” he hastily added, on seeing the Warden’s look of astonishment, “the abolition of the *office* of Sub-Warden, and giving the present holder the right to act as *Vice*-Warden whenever the Warden is absent﻿—would appease all this seedling discontent. I mean,” he added, glancing at a paper he held in his hand, “all this *seething* discontent!”
“For fifteen years,” put in a deep but very harsh voice, “my husband has been acting as Sub-Warden. It is too long! It is much too long!” My Lady was a vast creature at all times: but, when she frowned and folded her arms, as now, she looked more gigantic than ever, and made one try to fancy what a haystack would look like, if out of temper.
“He would distinguish himself as a Vice!” my Lady proceeded, being far too stupid to see the double meaning of her words. “There has been no such Vice in Outland for many a long year, as he would be!”
“What course would *you* suggest, Sister?” the Warden mildly enquired.
My Lady stamped, which was undignified: and snorted, which was ungraceful. “This is no *jesting* matter!” she bellowed.
“I will consult my brother,” said the Warden. “Brother!”

===Chinese===

大法官说，嗓门比先前任何时候都响亮清晰。他心里有底——*这回*至少拿得出凭据——胆气便壮了：他把几张印好的通告放到守护人手里，那原是同一摞打开的账本一起搁在一张边桌上的。
「是，是，*我*看见了！」守护人喃喃道，随手翻检着。「命令被我弟弟撤销了，人家还只当是*我*干的！这一手可真够利落！没关系！」他提高嗓门补了一句。「上面签的是我的名字：那就算在我头上好了。可他们喊『少些税』是什么意思？还能怎么个*少*法？最后一批税一个月前就被我废除了！」
「税又征上了，殿下，还是奉殿下自己的命令征的！」又有几张印好的通告呈上来查验。
守护人翻看时，朝副守护人瞟了一两眼——他早在摊开的账本前坐定，正专心致志地加总；但守护人只是重复道：「没关系。就算是我干的，我认了。」
「外头还有人说，」大法官讪讪地往下讲——那模样活像个定了罪的窃贼，全无国务官的体统——「说政府若能变一变，把副守护人裁掉——我是说，」他见守护人面露惊愕，慌忙补上，「把副守护人这个*职位*裁掉，并授予现任者以*代*守护人之衔，凡守护人不在时皆可代行——便可平息这场萌芽中的不满。我是说，」他瞥了一眼手里的纸片，「这场*沸腾的*不满！」
「十五年了，」一个低沉却极为刺耳的声音插进来，「我丈夫做副守护人都做了十五年了。太久了！实在是太久太久！」夫人生来便是个庞然大物：而当她皱起眉头、抱起胳膊，像此刻这样，便显得愈发巍峨，不由得让人琢磨：一座干草堆若是发起脾气来，该是何等光景。
「他当起代守护人来，必能显赫一时！」夫人接着说，她蠢得根本听不出自己话里的双关。「外乡多少年了，都不曾有过他这样的一位恶——呃，一位*代*守护人！」
「那*你*的意思该如何呢，弟妹？」守护人温和地问。
夫人跺了跺脚——有失体统；又哼了一声——殊为不雅。「这可不是*闹着玩*的事！」她吼道。
「我要同我弟弟商量商量，」守护人说。「弟弟！」

===Original===

“﻿—and seven makes a hundred and ninety-four, which is sixteen and twopence,” the Sub-Warden replied. “Put down two and carry sixteen.”
The Chancellor raised his hands and eyebrows, lost in admiration. “*Such* a man of business!” he murmured.
“Brother, could I have a word with you in my Study?” the Warden said in a louder tone. The Sub-Warden rose with alacrity, and the two left the room together.
My Lady turned to the Professor, who had uncovered the urn, and was taking its temperature with his pocket-thermometer. “Professor!” she began, so loudly and suddenly that even Uggug, who had gone to sleep in his chair, left off snoring and opened one eye. The Professor pocketed his thermometer in a moment, clasped his hands, and put his head on one side with a meek smile.
“You were teaching my son before breakfast, I believe?” my Lady loftily remarked. “I hope he strikes you as having talent?”
“Oh, very much so indeed, my Lady!” the Professor hastily replied, unconsciously rubbing his ear, while some painful recollection seemed to cross his mind. “I was very forcibly struck by His Magnificence, I assure you!”
“He is a charming boy!” my Lady exclaimed. “Even his snores are more musical than those of other boys!”
If that *were* so, the Professor seemed to think, the snores of *other* boys must be something too awful to be endured: but he was a cautious man, and he said nothing.
“And he’s so clever!” my Lady continued. “No one will enjoy your Lecture more﻿—by the way, have you fixed the time for it yet? You’ve never given one, you know: and it was promised years ago, before you﻿—”
“Yes, yes, my Lady, *I* know! Perhaps next Tuesday﻿—or Tuesday week﻿—”
“That will do very well,” said my Lady, graciously. “Of course you will let the Other Professor lecture as well?”
“I think *not*, my Lady,” the Professor said with some hesitation. “You see, he always stands with his back to the audience. It does very well for *reciting*; but for *lecturing*﻿—”
“You are quite right,” said my Lady. “And, now I come to think of it, there would hardly be time for more than *one* Lecture. And it will go off all the better, if we begin with a Banquet, and a Fancy-dress Ball﻿—”
“It will indeed!” the Professor cried, with enthusiasm.
“I shall come as a Grasshopper,” my Lady calmly proceeded. “What shall *you* come as, Professor?”
The Professor smiled feebly.

===Chinese===

「——加七得一百九十四，合十六先令两便士，」副守护人答道。「落下二，进十六。」
大法官双手和眉毛一齐扬起，钦佩得五体投地。「多么*了不起*的理财能手！」他喃喃道。
「弟弟，可否到我书房说句话？」守护人提高了嗓音。副守护人一骨碌站起来，两人一同出了屋。
夫人转向教授——他正揭开茶壶的盖子，用随身温度计量它的温度。「教授！」她开口便喊，嗓门又大又突兀，连椅子里睡着的乌格格都停止了打呼，睁开一只眼。教授一转眼便收起温度计，双手交叠，头一歪，温顺地微笑着。
「早餐前是您在教导我儿子吧，我没记错的话？」夫人居高临下地说。「他给您的印象，想必是有天分的喽？」
「哦，可不是嘛，天分大得很，夫人！」教授急忙答道，下意识地摩挲着自己的耳朵，某种痛苦的回忆似乎掠过心头。「大人的威仪给了我极深的*撞击*，我向您担保！」
「他是个讨人喜爱的孩子！」夫人嚷道。「连他的鼾声都比别的孩子的更悦耳！」
倘若*真*是那样，教授似乎在想，*别的*孩子的鼾声只怕就可怕得叫人没法忍受了：但他是个谨慎人，什么也没说。
「他还聪明得很呢！」夫人继续说。「没有人会比他更欣赏您的演讲了——对了，演讲的日子定下了吗？您可还一次都没讲过呢，您知道：这可是多少年前就答应下的，在您——」
「是，是，夫人，*我*记得！兴许下礼拜二——要不就下下礼拜二——」
「那也很好，」夫人和颜悦色地说。「您自然也会让另一位教授讲一场喽？」
「我想*不必*了，夫人，」教授面带迟疑地说。「您知道，他总是背对着听众。*朗诵*倒还使得；可*演讲*嘛——」
「您说得极是，」夫人说。「况且我想起来了，工夫也只够*一场*演讲。再说，若能先来一场宴会、再开一场化装舞会，散场时必更尽兴——」
「那是自然！」教授热切地喊道。
「我要扮一只蚱蜢，」夫人从容不迫地继续说。「*您*打算扮什么呢，教授？」
教授凄然一笑。

===Original===

“I shall come as﻿—as early as I can, my Lady!”
“You mustn’t come in before the doors are opened,” said my Lady.
“I can’t,” said the Professor. “Excuse me a moment. As this is Lady Sylvie’s birthday, I would like to﻿—” and he rushed away.
Bruno began feeling in his pockets, looking more and more melancholy as he did so: then he put his thumb in his mouth, and considered for a minute: then he quietly left the room.
He had hardly done so before the Professor was back again, quite out of breath. “Wishing you many happy returns of the day, my dear child!” he went on, addressing the smiling little girl, who had run to meet him. “Allow me to give you a birthday-present. It’s a secondhand pincushion, my dear. And it only cost fourpence-halfpenny!”
“Thank you, it’s *very* pretty!” And Sylvie rewarded the old man with a hearty kiss.
“And the *pins* they gave me for nothing!” the Professor added in high glee. “Fifteen of em, and only *one* bent!”
“I’ll make the bent one into a *hook*!” said Sylvie. “To catch Bruno with, when he runs away from his lessons!”
“You can’t guess what *my* present is!” said Uggug, who had taken the butter-dish from the table, and was standing behind her, with a wicked leer on his face.
“No, I can’t guess,” Sylvie said without looking up. She was still examining the Professor’s pincushion.
“It’s *this*!” cried the bad boy, exultingly, as he emptied the dish over her, and then, with a grin of delight at his own cleverness, looked round for applause.
Sylvie coloured crimson, as she shook off the butter from her frock: but she kept her lips tight shut, and walked away to the window, where she stood looking out and trying to recover her temper.
Uggug’s triumph was a very short one: the Sub-Warden had returned, just in time to be a witness of his dear child’s playfulness, and in another moment a skilfully-applied box on the ear had changed the grin of delight into a howl of pain.
“My darling!” cried his mother, enfolding him in her fat arms. “Did they box his ears for nothing? A precious pet!”
“It’s not for *nothing*!” growled the angry father. “Are you aware, Madam, that *I* pay the house-bills, out of a fixed annual sum? The loss of all that wasted butter falls on *me*! Do you hear, Madam!”
“Hold your tongue, Sir!” My Lady spoke very quietly﻿—almost in a whisper. But there was something in her *look* which silenced him.

===Chinese===

「我打算扮——扮个来得早的，夫人！」
「门没开之前您可不能进来。」夫人说。
「我进不来，」教授说。「恕我失陪片刻。今天是西尔维小姐的生日，我想去——」说着他便冲了出去。
布鲁诺开始翻自己的口袋，越翻越愁：随后把大拇指塞进嘴里，琢磨了一分钟：然后悄悄地出了屋。
他前脚刚走，教授便气喘吁吁地回来了。「祝亲爱的孩子生日快乐、年年有今日！」他对着笑吟吟迎上来的小姑娘说道。「请允许我送您一件生日礼物。是个二手的针插，亲爱的。统共才花了四便士半！」
「谢谢您，它*真*好看！」西尔维给了老人一个热乎乎的吻作报答。
「还有那些*大头针*，是白送的！」教授喜滋滋地补充。「十五根呢，只弯了*一根*！」
「我要把弯的那根做成*钩子*！」西尔维说。「布鲁诺从功课跟前逃跑的时候，好用它来钩他！」
「你猜不出*我的*礼物是什么！」乌格格说。他从桌上端起黄油碟，站在她背后，脸上挂着恶毒的斜睨。
「嗯，我猜不出。」西尔维头也不抬地说。她还在端详教授的针插。
「就是*这个*！」坏小子得意忘形地喊道，把一碟黄油尽数扣在她身上，随即为自己这份聪明咧嘴直乐，环顾四周等着喝彩。
西尔维的脸涨得通红，一面抖落裙子上的黄油：但她紧闭着嘴唇，走到窗前，站在那儿望着窗外，努力平心静气。
乌格格的得意没维持多久：副守护人回来了，恰好看清了爱子的这场顽皮，转眼间一记手法娴熟的耳光，便把得意的咧嘴笑变成了疼痛的嚎叫。
「我的宝贝！」他母亲嚷道，把他搂进肥胖的怀抱。「他们凭什么平白无故打他耳光？多金贵的小乖乖！」
「才不是平白无故！」怒气冲冲的父亲咆哮道。「您可知道，夫人，*我*是用一笔固定的年银应付全家开销的？糟蹋的那些黄油，损失全落在*我*头上！听见没有，夫人！」
「住口，先生！」夫人说得极轻——几乎像耳语。可她那*眼神*里有种东西，叫他哑了声。

===Original===

“Don’t you see it was only a *joke*? And a very clever one, too! He only meant that he loved nobody *but* her! And, instead of being pleased with the compliment, the spiteful little thing has gone away in a huff!”
The Sub-Warden was a very good hand at changing a subject. He walked across to the window. “My dear,” he said, “is that a *pig* that I see down below, rooting about among your flowerbeds?”
“A *pig*!” shrieked my Lady, rushing madly to the window, and almost pushing her husband out, in her anxiety to see for herself. “Whose pig is it? How did it get in? Where’s that crazy Gardener gone?”
At this moment Bruno reentered the room, and passing Uggug (who was blubbering his loudest, in the hope of attracting notice) as if he was quite used to that sort of thing, he ran up to Sylvie and threw his arms round her. “I went to my toy-cupboard,” he said with a very sorrowful face, “to see if there were *somefin* fit for a present for oo! And there isn’t *nuffin*! They’s *all* broken, everyone! And I haven’t got *no* money left, to buy oo a birthday-present! And I can’t give oo nuffin but *this*!” (“*This*” was a very earnest hug and a kiss.)
“Oh, thank you, darling!” cried Sylvie. “I like *your* present best of all!” (But if so, why did she give it back so quickly?)
His Sub-Excellency turned and patted the two children on the head with his long lean hands. “Go away, dears!” he said. “There’s business to talk over.”
Sylvie and Bruno went away hand in hand: but, on reaching the door, Sylvie came back again and went up to Uggug timidly. “I don’t mind about the butter,” she said, “and I﻿—I’m sorry he hurt you!” And she tried to shake hands with the little ruffian: but Uggug only blubbered louder, and wouldn’t make friends. Sylvie left the room with a sigh.
The Sub-Warden glared angrily at his weeping son. “Leave the room, Sirrah!” he said, as loud as he dared. His wife was still leaning out of the window, and kept repeating “I *can’t* see that pig! Where *is* it?”
“It’s moved to the right﻿—now it’s gone a little to the left,” said the Sub-Warden: but he had his back to the window, and was making signals to the Lord Chancellor, pointing to Uggug and the door, with many a cunning nod and wink.

===Chinese===

「您没看出来吗，那不过是个*玩笑*！还是个顶聪明的玩笑！他无非是想说，除了她他谁也不爱！可这小东西不领情道谢，反倒怀恨走开了！」
副守护人是岔开话题的好手。他踱到窗前。「亲爱的，」他说，「底下那头是不是*猪*啊，在您的花坛里乱拱？」
「*猪*！」夫人尖声叫道，发疯似的冲向窗口，急于一见，差点把她丈夫挤了出去。「谁家的猪？怎么进来的？那个疯园丁死哪儿去了？」
这时布鲁诺回到屋里，路过哭得震天响、巴望引人注意的乌格格身边时，仿佛全然习惯了这一套似的，径直跑到西尔维跟前，张开手臂抱住她。「我去我的玩具柜里找了，」他哭丧着脸说，「看看有没有*什嘛*东西配当送你的礼物！可是啥也*没有*！它们*全*碎了，一个不剩！我一分钱也*没*剩下，没法给你买生日礼物！我除了*这个*，啥也送不了你！」（「*这个*」是一个极其诚挚的拥抱和一个吻。）
「啊，谢谢你，亲爱的！」西尔维喊道。「*你的*礼物我最最喜欢！」（可既然如此，她怎么这么快就把它还回去了呢？）
副大人转过身，用他瘦长的手拍拍两个孩子的头。「去一边玩吧，亲们！」他说。「大人有事要商量。」
西尔维和布鲁诺手拉手走开了：可到了门口，西尔维又折回来，怯生生地走到乌格格跟前。「黄油的事我不介意，」她说，「还——还有，他打了你，我很抱歉！」她还想同这个小恶棍握手：可乌格格只是哭得更响，不肯言归于好。西尔维叹了口气，出了屋。
副守护人怒冲冲地瞪着哭哭啼啼的儿子。「滚出去，小子！」他扯着敢扯的最大嗓门说。他妻子还伏在窗口，一个劲儿念叨「我*就是*看不见那头猪！它*到底*在哪儿？」
「它挪到右边去了——现在又往左边挪了一点，」副守护人说：可他背朝着窗，正朝大法官打暗号，指着乌格格和房门，不住地狡黠点头眨眼。

===Original===

The Chancellor caught his meaning at last, and, crossing the room, took that interesting child by the ear﻿—the next moment he and Uggug were out of the room, and the door shut behind them: but not before one piercing yell had rung through the room, and reached the ears of the fond mother.
“What *is* that hideous noise?” she fiercely asked, turning upon her startled husband.
“It’s some hyena﻿—or other,” replied the Sub-Warden, looking vaguely up to the ceiling, as if that was where they usually were to be found. “Let us to business, my dear. Here comes the Warden.” And he picked up from the floor a wandering scrap of manuscript, on which I just caught the words “after which Election duly holden the said Sibimet and Tabikat his wife may at their pleasure assume Imperial﻿—” before, with a guilty look, he crumpled it up in his hand.

===Chinese===

大法官终于明白了他的意思，穿过屋子，一把馥住那位有趣孩子的耳朵——下一刻他和乌格格已出了门，房门在身后关上：只是先有一声刺耳的尖叫响彻全屋，钻进了慈母的耳朵。
「那难听的动静*到底*是什么？」她凶巴巴地质问，猛地转向她那吓了一跳的丈夫。
「许是哪只鬣狗——反正是种什么东西，」副守护人答道，茫然地望着天花板，仿佛那类东西平时都待在那儿似的。「说正事吧，亲爱的。守护人来了。」说着他从地板上捾起一页流落的手稿，我恰好磎见上面写着「选举依法如期举行之后，上述西比梅特（Sibimet）及其妻塔比卡特（Tabikat）可随意即皇帝——」他就心虚地把它一团攥进了手心。

## IV A Cunning Conspiracy / 一个狡猾的阴谋
===Original===

The Warden entered at this moment: and close behind him came the Lord Chancellor, a little flushed and out of breath, and adjusting his wig, which appeared to have been dragged partly off his head.
“But where is my precious child?” my Lady enquired, as the four took their seats at the small side-table devoted to ledgers and bundles and bills.
“He left the room a few minutes ago﻿—with the Lord Chancellor,” the Sub-Warden briefly explained.
“Ah!” said my Lady, graciously smiling on that high official. “Your Lordship has a very *taking* way with children! I doubt if anyone could *gain the ear* of my darling Uggug so quickly as *you* can!” For an entirely stupid woman, my Lady’s remarks were curiously full of meaning, of which she herself was wholly unconscious.
The Chancellor bowed, but with a very uneasy air. “I think the Warden was about to speak,” he remarked, evidently anxious to change the subject.
But my Lady would not be checked. “He is a clever boy,” she continued with enthusiasm, “but he needs a man like your Lordship to *draw him out*!”
The Chancellor bit his lip, and was silent. He evidently feared that, stupid as she looked, she understood what she said *this* time, and was having a joke at his expense. He might have spared himself all anxiety: whatever accidental meaning her *words* might have, she *herself* never meant anything at all.
“It is all settled!” the Warden announced, wasting no time over preliminaries. “The Sub-Wardenship is abolished, and my brother is appointed to act as Vice-Warden whenever I am absent. So, as I am going abroad for a while, he will enter on his new duties at once.”
“And there will really be a Vice after all?” my Lady enquired.
“I hope so!” the Warden smilingly replied.
My Lady looked much pleased, and tried to clap her hands: but you might as well have knocked two featherbeds together, for any noise it made. “When my husband is Vice,” she said, “it will be the same as if we had a *hundred* Vices!”
“Hear, hear!” cried the Sub-Warden.
“You seem to think it very remarkable,” my Lady remarked with some severity, “that your wife should speak the truth!”
“No, not *remarkable* at all!” her husband anxiously explained. “*Nothing* is remarkable that *you* say, sweet one!”
My Lady smiled approval of the sentiment, and went on. “And am I Vice-Wardeness?”

===Chinese===

就在这时，守护人走了进来，紧随其后的是大法官——他微微涨红了脸，气喘吁吁，正扶着自己的假发，那假发看来是被扯得半离了头顶。
「我那宝贵的孩子呢？」夫人问道，此时四人已在那张专放账本、纸包和单据的小边桌旁落了座。
「几分钟前离开这屋子了——跟大法官一块儿，」副守护人简短地解释道。
「啊！」夫人说着，和颜悦色地朝那位高官微笑。「大人您哄孩子可真有*一套*！我看谁也没法像*您*这么快就*赢得*我心爱的乌格格的欢心！」作为一个彻头彻尾的蠢女人，夫人的话里却出奇地满是深意，只是她自己全然没有察觉。
大法官鞠了一躬，神色却十分不安。「我想守护人正要讲话，」他说，显然急于岔开话题。
可夫人不肯被打断。「他是个聪明的孩子，」她兴冲冲地接着说，「不过他需要大人您这样的男子来*开导开导*他！」
大法官咬住嘴唇，不作声了。他显然在担心：她看上去虽蠢，这回却听懂了自己话里的意思，正在拿他打趣呢。他大可不必这样焦虑：无论她的*话*碰巧有什么含义，她*本人*是从来不带任何意思的。
「都定下来了！」守护人宣布道，在寒暄上毫不耽搁。「副守护人之职已废除，我不在时，由我兄弟出任代守护人行事。我既然要出国一段时间，他即刻便可履新。」
「这么说，到底真要有『代』了？」夫人问。
「但愿如此！」守护人含笑答道。
夫人显得大为欢喜，想拍拍手：可那声音，还不如把两床羽绒被撞在一起来得响。「等我家老爷做了代守护人，」她说，「那就跟我们有一*百*个代守护人一样啦！」
「好哇，好哇！」副守护人喝彩道。
「你倒觉得稀奇得很，」夫人颇为严厉地说，「好像你老婆说的不是真话似的！」
「不，一点儿也*不*稀奇！」她丈夫急忙解释。「*你*说什么都不稀奇，心肝儿！」
夫人对这句情话点头称许，继续说道：「那我呢，就是代守护人夫人了？」

===Original===

“If you choose to use that title,” said the Warden: “but ‘Your Excellency’ will be the proper style of address. And I trust that both ‘*His* Excellency’ and ‘*Her* Excellency’ will observe the Agreement I have drawn up. The provision I am *most* anxious about is this.” He unrolled a large parchment scroll, and read aloud the words “ ‘*item*, that we will be kind to the poor.’ The Chancellor worded it for me,” he added, glancing at that great Functionary. “I suppose, now, that word ‘*item*’ has some deep legal meaning?”
“Undoubtedly!” replied the Chancellor, as articulately as he could with a pen between his lips. He was nervously rolling and unrolling several other scrolls, and making room among them for the one the Warden had just handed to him. “These are merely the rough copies,” he explained: “and, as soon as I have put in the final corrections﻿—” making a great commotion among the different parchments, “﻿—a semicolon or two that I have accidentally omitted﻿—” here he darted about, pen in hand, from one part of the scroll to another, spreading sheets of blotting-paper over his corrections, “all will be ready for signing.”
“Should it not be read out, first?” my Lady enquired.
“No need, no need!” the Sub-Warden and the Chancellor exclaimed at the same moment, with feverish eagerness.
“No need at all,” the Warden gently assented. “Your husband and I have gone through it together. It provides that he shall exercise the full authority of Warden, and shall have the disposal of the annual revenue attached to the office, until my return, or, failing that, until Bruno comes of age: and that he shall then hand over, to myself or to Bruno as the case may be, the Wardenship, the unspent revenue, and the contents of the Treasury, which are to be preserved, intact, under his guardianship.”
All this time the Sub-Warden was busy, with the Chancellor’s help, shifting the papers from side to side, and pointing out to the Warden the place where he was to sign. He then signed it himself, and my Lady and the Chancellor added their names as witnesses.
“Short partings are best,” said the Warden. “All is ready for my journey. My children are waiting below to see me off.” He gravely kissed my Lady, shook hands with his brother and the Chancellor, and left the room.

===Chinese===

「你愿意用那个头衔也无妨，」守护人说，「不过合乎礼制的称呼是『阁下』。我也相信『*男*阁下』和『*女*阁下』都会遵守我拟定的这份协定。我最*最*挂心的是这一条。」他展开一大幅羊皮纸卷，高声念道：「『又，我等须善待穷人。』这一条是大法官替我措的辞，」他补充说，瞥了那位显贵一眼。「我想，这个『又』字总该有什么深奥的法律含义吧？」
「那是自然！」大法官答道，嘴里还叼着一支笔，能说多清楚就说了多清楚。他正紧张地把另外几卷羊皮纸卷起又展开，在其中腾出地方，好搁下守护人刚递给他的那一卷。「这些不过是草稿，」他解释道，「等我添上最后几处修正——」他在那堆羊皮纸间折腾出一阵大动静，「——一两个我无意间漏掉的分号——」说着他手执笔，飞快地从纸卷的这一头窜到那一头，在改过的地方覆上一张张吸墨纸，「一切就都可以签字了。」
「不该先把它宣读一遍吗？」夫人问。
「不必了，不必了！」副守护人和大法官同一瞬间叫起来，急得近乎发狂。
「完全不必，」守护人温和地表示同意。「你丈夫和我已经一起从头到尾过了一遍。协定规定：在我回来之前——若回不来，则到布鲁诺成年为止——他可行使守护人的全部职权，并支配该职位的岁入；届时他须视情形将守护人之职、未用完的岁入，以及国库中的全部财物移交给我本人或布鲁诺，这些财物在他保管期间须完好无损。」
这工夫，副守护人一直在趁着大法官帮忙，把纸卷挪来挪去，指给守护人看该签字的地方。随后他本人签了字，夫人和大法官也以见证人的身份签上了各自的名字。
「分别以短暂为最好，」守护人说。「行装都已齐备。孩子们正在楼下等着送我启程呢。」他郑重地吻了吻夫人，同兄弟和大法官握了握手，便离开了房间。

===Original===
