“Fading, with the Night, the memory of a dead love, and the withered leaves of a blighted hope, and the sickly repinings and moody regrets that numb the best energies of the soul: and rising, broadening, rolling upward like a living flood, the manly resolve, and the dauntless will, and the heavenward gaze of faith﻿—*the substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen*!
“Look Eastward! Aye, look Eastward!”

===Chinese===

*听到*你的消息，这是肯定的！我热烈地应道，并引了那首奇诗《沃林》的结尾几句：——

> “哦，星辰从不会
> 在此陨灭，它只是在远方升起！
> 看东方，崭新的万千星辰正在涌现！
> 在毗湿奴之邦，将有何种化身降世？”

是啊，看东方！亚瑟热切地应和，在楼梯窗前驻足——那窗正望得见大海与东方的地平线。西方，是安葬过去一切哀伤与叹息、一切错误与愚行的合适坟茔：安葬一切枯萎的希望与埋葬了的爱！从东方，涌来新的力量、新的抱负、新的希望、新的生命、新的爱！看东方！是啊，看东方！
走进房间、拉开窗帘时，他最后的话仍在耳畔回响；正当其时，我看见太阳以它的荣光冲破海上囚笼，为世界披上新一天的光。
愿他也如此，我也如此，我们都如此！我冥想。一切邪恶的、死灭的、无望的，随昨夜消退！一切善良的、鲜活的、有望的，随黎明升起！
随夜消退的，是凛冽的雾、有毒的瘴、沉沉的影、呜咽的风、猫头鹰哀愁的啼；随昼升起的，是疾射的光箭、清新的晨风、初生生命的暖意、云雀疯狂的乐音！看东方！
随夜消退的，是无知的阴云、罪的致命枯萎、悲伤的无声泪；随昼升起的，越升越高，是知识的璀璨黎明、纯洁的甜美气息、一个世界狂喜的搏动！看东方！
随夜消退的，是一段死去之爱的记忆、一个夭折希望的枯叶、那麻痹灵魂最好精力的病态哀怨与阴郁悔恨；升起的，拓宽着，如活水的洪流滚滚向上，是男儿的决断、无畏的意志、信仰之仰望苍天的目光——*是所望之事的实底，是未见之事的确据*！
看东方！是啊，看东方！

## Volume II / 第二卷
===Original===

> Dreams, that elude the Waker’s frenzied grasp﻿–﻿
> Hands, stark and still, on a dead Mother’s breast,
> Which nevermore shall render clasp for clasp,
> Or deftly soothe a weeping Child to rest﻿–﻿
> In suchlike forms me listeth to portray
> My Tale, here ended. Thou delicious Fay﻿–﻿
> The guardian of a Sprite that lives to tease thee﻿–﻿
> Loving in earnest, chiding but in play
> The merry mocking Bruno! Who, that sees thee,
> Can fail to love thee, Darling, even as I?﻿–﻿
> My sweetest Sylvie, we must say “Goodbye!”

> 梦，逃避着醒来之人疯狂的抓握——
> 手，僵冷而静止，搁在亡母的胸前，
> 再不能以拥抱回抱，
> 再不能灵巧地哄哭孩入睡——
> 我便以这般意象，描绘
> 我这已完结的故事。你这甜美的精灵——
> 一位生来就爱逗你的小精怪的守护者——
> 认真爱恋，嗔怪只为嬉戏，
> 那快活促狭的布鲁诺！谁见了你，
> 能不爱你，宝贝，像我一样？——

### Preface / 序
===Original===

I must begin with the same announcement as in the previous Volume (which I shall henceforward refer to as “Vol. I,” calling the present Volume “Vol. II”), viz. that the Locket, at chapter 25, was drawn by “Miss Alice Havers.” And my reason, for not stating this on the titlepage﻿—that it seems only due, to the artist of these wonderful pictures, that his name should stand there alone﻿—has, I think, even greater weight in Vol. II than it had in Vol. I. Let me call especial attention to the three “Little Birds” borders, at chapter 23. The way, in which he has managed to introduce the most minute details of the stanzas to be illustrated, seems to me a triumph of artistic ingenuity.
Let me here express my sincere gratitude to the many Reviewers who have noticed, whether favorably or unfavorably, the previous Volume. Their unfavorable remarks were, most probably, well-deserved; the favorable ones less probably so. Both kinds have no doubt served to make the book known, and have helped the reading Public to form their opinions of it. Let me also here assure them that it is not from any want of respect for their criticisms, that I have carefully forborne from reading *any* of them. I am strongly of opinion that an author had far better *not* read any reviews of his books: the unfavorable ones are almost certain to make him cross, and the favorable ones conceited; and *neither* of these results is desirable.
Criticisms have, however, reached me from private sources, to some of which I propose to offer a reply.
One such critic complains that Arthur’s strictures, on sermons and on choristers, are too severe. Let me say, in reply, that I do *not* hold myself responsible for *any* of the opinions expressed by the characters in my book. They are simply opinions which, it seemed to me, might probably be held by the persons into whose mouths I put them, and which were worth consideration.
Other critics have objected to certain innovations in spelling, such as “ca’n’t,” “wo’n’t,” “traveler.” In reply, I can only plead my firm conviction that the popular usage is *wrong*. As to “ca’n’t,” it will not be disputed that, in all *other* words ending in “n’t,” these letters are an abbreviation of “not”; and it is surely absurd to suppose that, in this solitary instance, “not” is represented by “ ’t”!

===Chinese===

我必须像上一卷那样开个头（以下称前卷为“第一卷”，称本卷为“第二卷”），即：第25章的挂坠盒插图出自“艾丽斯·哈弗斯小姐”之手。我未在扉页署明此节——因为依我之见，这些美妙图画的画师理当独享其名——这一理由在第二卷比在第一卷更有分量。请允许我特别提请注意第23章的三道“小鸟”花边。他竟能把所配诗节中最细微的细节都纳入画中，在我看来，这是艺术匠心的凯旋。
在此，我谨向关注过上卷的众多书评家——无论褒贬——致以诚挚的谢意。他们的贬语多半是应得的；褒语则多半未必。两类无疑都让本书为人所知，也帮助读者大众形成了自己的判断。也请容我向他们保证：我刻意不读其中*任何*一篇，绝非出于对批评的不敬。我坚决认为，作家最好不要读任何关于自己著作的书评：贬评几乎必定叫他恼火，褒评几乎必定叫他自满；而这两种结果*无一*可取。
不过，私信中也有批评到达我处，对其中的几条，我打算作答。
有位批评家抱怨，亚瑟对布道与唱诗班的苛评过于严厉。作为回答，我要说：书中人物发表的*任何*观点，我都*不*自任其责。它们只是在我看来、由我代言的那些人很可能持有的观点，而且值得斟酌。
另一些批评家不满某些拼写上的革新，如“ca'n't”“wo'n't”“traveler”。作为回答，我只能申明我坚定的信念：通行的用法是*错的*。至于“ca'n't”：所有*其他*以“n't”结尾的词，这几处字母都是“not”的缩写，这一点无可争辩；偏偏在这一个词里，“not”竟被缩成了“'t”——这岂不荒唐！

===Original===

In fact “ca’n’t” is the *proper* abbreviation for “can it,” just as “is’t” is for “is it.” Again, in “wo’n’t,” the first apostrophe is needed, because the word “would” is here *abridged* into “wo”: but I hold it proper to spell “don’t” with only *one* apostrophe, because the word “do” is here *complete*. As to such words as “traveler,” I hold the correct principle to be, to *double* the consonant when the accent falls on that syllable; otherwise to leave it *single*. This rule is observed in most cases (e.g. we double the *r* in “preferred,” but leave it single in “offered”), so that I am only extending, to other cases, an existing rule. I admit, however, that I do not spell “parallel,” as the rule would have it; but here we are constrained, by the etymology, to insert the double *l*.
In the Preface to Vol. I were two puzzles, on which my readers might exercise their ingenuity. One was, to detect the three lines of “padding,” which I had found it necessary to supply in the passage at chapter 3. The other puzzle was, to determine which (if any) of the 8 stanzas of the Gardener’s Song (see stanza number 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 and 8) were adapted to the context, and which (if any) had the context adapted to them. The last of them is the only one that was adapted to the context, the “Garden-Door that opened with a key” having been substituted for some creature (a Cormorant, I think) “that nestled in a tree.” In stanzas 2, 5 and 7, the context was adapted to the stanza. In the fourth, neither stanza nor context was altered: the connection between them was simply a piece of good luck.
In the Preface to Vol. I, I gave an account of the making-up of the story of “Sylvie and Bruno.” A few more details may perhaps be acceptable to my Readers.
It was in 1873, as I *now* believe, that the idea first occurred to me that a little fairytale (written, in 1867, for “Aunt Judy’s Magazine,” under the title “Bruno’s Revenge”) might serve as the nucleus of a longer story. This I surmise, from having found the original draft of the last paragraph of Vol. II, dated 1873. So that this paragraph has been waiting 20 years for its chance of emerging into print﻿—more than twice the period so cautiously recommended by Horace for “repressing” one’s literary efforts!
It was in February, 1885, that I entered into negotiations, with Mr. Harry Furniss, for illustrating the book.

===Chinese===

其实，“ca'n't”是“can it”的*正*缩写，正如“is't”之于“is it”。再说“wo'n't”：第一个擇号是必需的，因为“would”在此被*缩*成了“wo”：而“don't”我认为只须*一个*擇号，因为“do”在此是*完整*的。至于“traveler”一类词，我认为正确的原则是：重音落在此音节上时，辅音*双*写；否则*单*写。这条规则多数情况下都被遵守（例如“preferred”中*r*双写，“offered”中*r*单写），所以我不过是把一条既有规则推广到别的情形。不过我承认，“parallel”一词我未按规则拼写；但此处出于词源，不得不双写*l*。
第一卷序言里有两道谜题，供读者施展巧思。一是找出第3章那段诗里我不得已补入的三行“衬垫”。二是判定园丁之歌的八节诗（见第1、2、3、4、5、6、7、8节）中，哪几节（若有）是迁就上下文的，哪几节（若有）是上下文迁就它们的。其中只有末一节是迁就上下文的：“用钥匙打开的园门”替换了原本的某种生物（我记得是鸬鹚）“栖在树上”。第2、5、7节，则是上下文迁就了诗节。第四节，诗与上下文都未改动：两者之间的关联纯属运气。
在第一卷序言中，我讲述过《西尔维与布鲁诺》故事的成书过程。再补几件细事，读者诸君或许愿闻。
我*如今*认为，是在1873年，我头一回起念：一篇小童话（1867年为《朱迪阿姨杂志》所写，题为《布鲁诺的报复》）或可充作一个更长故事的内核。此念何来？因为我发现了第二卷末段的原始草稿，日期正是1873年。也就是说，这一段等了二十年才等到付梓的机会——比贺拉斯谨慎建议的“按压”自己文思的期限，长出了一倍还多！
1885年2月，我开始与哈里·弗尼斯先生商谈为本书绘制插图。

===Original===

Most of the substance of *both* Volumes was then in existence in manuscript: and my original intention was to publish the *whole* story at once. In September, 1885, I received from Mr. Furniss the first set of drawings﻿—the four which illustrate “Peter and Paul” (see the incidents 1, 2, 3 and 4): in November, 1886, I received the second set﻿—the three which illustrate the Professor’s song about the “little man” who had “a little gun” (In Vol. II chapter 17, drawings 1, 2, 3): and in January, 1887, I received the third set﻿—the four which illustrate the “Pig-Tale.”
So we went on, illustrating first one bit of the story, and then another, without any idea of sequence. And it was not till March, 1889, that, having calculated the number of pages the story would occupy, I decided on dividing it into *two* portions, and publishing it half at a time. This necessitated the writing of a *sort* of conclusion for the first Volume: and *most* of my Readers, I fancy, regarded this as the *actual* conclusion, when that Volume appeared in December, 1889. At any rate, among all the letters I received about it, there was only *one* which expressed *any* suspicion that it was not a *final* conclusion. This letter was from a child. She wrote “we were so glad, when we came to the end of the book, to find that there was no ending-up, for that shows us that you are going to write a sequel.”
It may interest some of my Readers to know the *theory* on which this story is constructed. It is an attempt to show what might *possibly* happen, supposing that Fairies really existed; and that they were sometimes visible to us, and we to them; and that they were sometimes able to assume human form: and supposing, also, that human beings might sometimes become conscious of what goes on in the Fairy-world﻿—by actual transference of their immaterial essence, such as we meet with in “Esoteric Buddhism.”
I have supposed a Human being to be capable of various psychical states, with varying degrees of consciousness, as follows:﻿—
(*a*) the ordinary state, with no consciousness of the presence of Fairies;
(*b*) the “eerie” state, in which, while conscious of actual surroundings, he is *also* conscious of the presence of Fairies;
(*c*) a form of trance, in which, while *un*conscious of actual surroundings, and apparently asleep, he (i.e.

===Chinese===

那时*两*卷的大部分内容已有手稿在案：我原本打算把*全*部故事一次出齐。1885年9月，我收到弗尼斯先生的第一批画——为《彼得与保罗》所作的四幅（见情节1、2、3、4）：1886年11月，收到第二批——为教授那支“小小人”“小小枪”之歌所作的三幅（见第二卷第17章，画1、2、3）：1887年1月，收到第三批——为“猪的故事”所作的四幅。
我们就这样东一段西一段地画下去，毫无次序可言。直到1889年3月，我估算出故事的页数，才决定把它分成*两*半，分两次出版。这就不得不为第一卷写一个*勉强算是*结局的东西：我想，*大多数*读者在该卷于1889年12月问世时，都把它当成了*真正的*结局。至少，在我收到的所有来信中，只有*一*封流露出*些许*怀疑，觉得它不是*最终*结局。这封信出自一个孩子。她写道：“我们读到书的末尾，发现它没有收尾，都高兴极了——因为这说明您还要写续集呢。”
本故事据以构建的*理论*，也许有读者愿闻。它试图展示：倘若精灵当真存在，倘若他们有时为我们所见、我们为他们所见，倘若他们有时能化为人形，又倘若人类有时能意识到精灵世界中的动静——通过其非物质本质的实际转移，如我们在《密传佛教》中见到的那样——那么*可能*发生什么。
我假设一个人能处于几种心理状态，意识程度各异，如下：——
（a）寻常状态，不觉得精灵在场；
（b）“灵异”状态：此状态下，人既意识到现实环境，*同时*也意识到精灵在场；
（c）一种入定状态：此状态下，人对现实环境*失去*意识，貌似睡去，而他（即——

===Original===

his immaterial essence) migrates to other scenes, in the actual world, or in Fairyland, and is conscious of the presence of Fairies.
I have also supposed a Fairy to be capable of migrating from Fairyland into the actual world, and of assuming, at pleasure, a Human form; and also to be capable of various psychical states, viz.
(*a*) the ordinary state, with no consciousness of the presence of Human beings;
(*b*) a sort of “eerie” state, in which he is conscious, if in the actual world, of the presence of actual Human beings; if in Fairyland, of the presence of the immaterial essences of Human beings.
I will here tabulate the passages, in both Volumes, where abnormal states occur.
In the Preface to Vol. I., I gave an account of the *origination* of some of the ideas embodied in the book. A few more such details may perhaps interest my Readers:﻿—
I. The very peculiar use, here made of a dead mouse, comes from real life. I once found two very small boys, in a garden, playing a microscopic game of “Single-Wicket.” The bat was, I think, about the size of a tablespoon; and the utmost distance attained by the ball, in its most daring flights, was some 4 or 5 yards. The *exact* length was of course a matter of *supreme* importance; and it was always carefully measured out (the batsman and the bowler amicably sharing the toil) with a dead mouse!
I. The two quasi-mathematical Axioms, quoted by Arthur at chapter 18 of Vol. I, (“Things that are greater than the same are greater than one another,” and “All angles are equal”) were actually enunciated, in all seriousness, by undergraduates at a University situated not 100 miles from Ely.
II. Bruno’s remark (“I can, if I like, etc.”) was actually made by a little boy.
II. So also was his remark (“I know what it *doesn’t* spell.”) And his remark (“I just twiddled my eyes, etc.”) I heard from the lips of a little girl, who had just solved a puzzle I had set her.
II. Bruno’s soliloquy (“For its father, etc.”) was actually spoken by a little girl, looking out of the window of a railway-carriage.
II. The remark, made by a guest at the dinner-party, when asking for a dish of fruit (“I’ve been wishing for them, etc.”) I heard made by the great Poet-Laureate, whose loss the whole reading-world has so lately had to deplore.
II.

===Chinese===

他的非物质本质）转移到别的场景——或现实世界、或仙境——并意识到精灵在场。
我还假设精灵能从仙境迁入现实世界，并能随意化为人形；也能处于几种心理状态，即：
（a）寻常状态，不觉得人类在场；
（b）一种“灵异”状态：此状态下，他若在现实世界，便意识到现实的人类在场；若在仙境，便意识到人类的非物质本质在场。
两卷之中出现异常状态之处，我列表于此。
第一卷序言中，我交代过书中某些构思的*来历*。再补几件，读者或感兴趣：——
一、书中对死老鼠的那番奇用，出自真实生活。我曾见两个很小的男孩在花园里玩一场微型板球。球拍大约只有汤匙大小；球最勇猛的飞行，至多达到四五码。*精确*的长度当然事关*至高*的利害；于是总是（击球手与投球手友善地分担劳作）用一只死老鼠仔细量出来！
一、第一卷第18章亚瑟引的那两条准数学公理（“大于同一个东西的东西，彼此也分大小”与“凡角皆相等”），确曾被一所大学的大学生——该校距伊利不足一百英里——一本正经地宣讲过。
二、布鲁诺的话（“只要我愿意，我就……能”云云）确出自一个小男孩之口。
二、他那句“我知道它*不*拼成什么”亦然。还有“我只不过把眼睛转了转”云云，是我亲耳听一个小女孩说的——她刚解出我给她出的一道谜题。
二、布鲁诺的独白（“它爸爸……”云云）确实出自一个小女孩，她当时正从火车车厢的窗口望出去。
二、宴席上客人讨果盘时说的那句话（“我一直在盼它们……”云云），我听自伟大的桂冠诗人之口——整个读书界前不久才痛失此人。
二、

===Original===

Bruno’s speech, on the subject of the age of “Mein Herr,” embodies the reply of a little girl to the question “Is your grandmother an *old* lady?” “I don’t know if she’s an *old* lady,” said this cautious young person; “she’s *eighty-three*.”
II. The speech about “Obstruction” is no mere creature of my imagination! It is copied *verbatim* from the columns of the Standard, and was spoken by Sir William Harcourt, who was, at the time, a member of the “Opposition,” at the “National Liberal Club,” on July the 16th, 1890.
II. The Professor’s remark, about a dog’s tail, that “it doesn’t bite at *that* end,” was actually made by a child, when warned of the danger he was incurring by pulling the dog’s tail.
II. The dialogue between Sylvie and Bruno, which occupies lines 6 to 15, is a *verbatim* report (merely substituting “cake” for “penny”) of a dialogue overheard between two children.
One story in this Volume﻿—“Bruno’s Picnic”﻿—I can vouch for as suitable for telling to children, having tested it again and again; and, whether my audience has been a dozen little girls in a village-school, or some thirty or forty in a London drawing-room, or a hundred in a High School, I have always found them earnestly attentive, and keenly appreciative of such fun as the story supplied.
May I take this opportunity of calling attention to what I flatter myself was a successful piece of name-coining, at chapter 4 of Vol. I. Does not the name “Sibimet” fairly embody the character of the Sub-Warden? The gentle Reader has no doubt observed what a singularly useless article in a house a brazen trumpet is, if you simply leave it lying about, and never blow it!
Readers of the first Volume, who have amused themselves by trying to solve the two puzzles propounded at the Preface, may perhaps like to exercise their ingenuity in discovering which (if any) of the following parallelisms were intentional, and which (if any) accidental.
I will publish the answer to this puzzle in the Preface to a little book of “Original Games and Puzzles,” now in course of preparation.
I have reserved, for the last, one or two rather more serious topics.

===Chinese===

布鲁诺关于“我先生”年纪的那番话，化自一个小女孩对“你祖母是*老*太太吗”的回答。“我不知道她算不算*老*太太，”这位审慎的小人儿说，“她*八十三岁*了。”
二、关于“阻挠议事”的那番话，绝非我想象的产物！它*一字不差*地抄自《旗帜报》，为威廉·哈考特爵士1890年7月16日在“全国自由俱乐部”所言——他当时属于“反对党”。
二、教授关于狗尾巴“它*那*头不咬人”的妙语，确出自一个孩子——当时有人警告他拽狗尾巴有危险。
二、西尔维与布鲁诺在第6至15行的对话，是一段*逐字*实录（仅把“便士”换成了“蛋糕”），原话是我在两个孩子那里偷听来的。
本卷中有一篇故事——《布鲁诺的野餐》——我可以担保适合讲给孩子听，因为它我一试再试；无论听众是村校里的十几个小女孩、伦敦客厅里的三四十人，还是中学里的一百人，他们无不全神贯注，对故事提供的乐趣心领神会。
可否借此机会，请诸位留意我自鸣得意的一处造名妙笔——第一卷第4章。“Sibimet（西比梅特）”这个名字，岂不正是副守护人（Sub-Warden）品格的化身？温柔的读者想必早已看出：黄铜喇叭这种东西，若只管把它撂在家里、从不吹响，是何等奇特的无用之物！
读过第一卷、且在序言两谜中自娱过的读者，或许还愿意动动脑筋：下列各处对应，哪几处（若有）是有意为之，哪几处（若有）纯属巧合。
此谜的答案，我将在一本正在编写的小书《原创游戏与谜题》的序言中公布。
我把一两件较为严肃的话题，留在了最后。

===Original===

I had intended, in this Preface, to discuss more fully, than I had done in the previous Volume, the “Morality of Sport,” with special reference to letters I have received from lovers of Sport, in which they point out the many great advantages which men get from it, and try to prove that the suffering, which it inflicts on animals, is too trivial to be regarded.
But, when I came to think the subject out, and to arrange the whole of the arguments “pro” and “con,” I found it much too large for treatment here. Some day, I hope to publish an essay on this subject. At present, I will content myself with stating the net result I have arrived at.
It is, that God has given to Man an absolute right to take the *lives* of other animals, for *any* reasonable cause, such as the supply of food: but that He has *not* given to Man the right to inflict *pain*, unless when *necessary*: that mere pleasure, or advantage, does not constitute such a necessity: and, consequently, that pain, inflicted for the purposes of *Sport*, is cruel, and therefore wrong. But I find it a far more complex question than I had supposed; and that the “case,” on the side of the Sportsman, is a much stronger one than I had supposed. So, for the present, I say no more about it.
Objections have been raised to the severe language I have put into the mouth of “Arthur,” at chapter 19, Vol. II, on the subject of “Sermons,” earlier in the chapter, on the subjects of Choral Services and “Choristers.”
I have already protested against the assumption that I am ready to endorse the opinions of characters in my story. But, in these two instances, I admit that I am much in sympathy with “Arthur.” In my opinion, far too many sermons are expected from our preachers; and, as a consequence, a great many are preached, which are not worth listening to; and, as a consequence of *that*, we are very apt *not* to listen. The reader of this paragraph probably heard a sermon last Sunday morning? Well, let him, if he can, name the text, and state how the preacher treated it!
Then, as to “Choristers,” and all the other accessories﻿—of music, vestments, processions, etc.

===Chinese===

我本打算在这篇序言里，比上一卷更充分地讨论“游猎的道德”，特别针对狩猎爱好者们的来信——他们在信中历数狩猎带给人的种种巨大益处，并力图证明它加于动物的苦痛微不足道、无足挂齿。
可待我把题目想透、把正反论据一一排开，才发现它远非此处所能容纳。我希望有朝一日就此专文发表。眼下，只把我的最终结论陈于下。
结论是：上帝赋予人一种绝对的权利，可为*任何*正当缘由——如供给食物——取走其他动物的*性命*：但上帝*没有*赋予人施加*痛苦*的权利，除非出于*必要*：单纯的快乐或利益构不成这种必要：因此，为*游猎*之目的而施加的痛苦是残忍的，故而是错的。不过我发现，这个问题远比我预想的复杂；狩猎者一方的“案情”也远比我预想的有力。所以眼下，我就说到这里。
有人对我借“亚瑟”之口说出的激烈言辞提出异议——第二卷第19章关于“布道”的一节，及更前关于合唱礼拜与“唱诗班”的两节。
我已申明过，不要以为我随时背书书中人物的观点。但在这两处，我承认我与“亚瑟”大有同感。依我之见，我们对传道人索要的布道实在太多；于是，许许多多不值一听的布道被讲了出听；再于是，我们十有八九*根本*没听。读到这里的人，上礼拜天早晨多半听过一场布道吧？好——请他试试：说出经文，讲讲传道人如何阐发它！
再说“唱诗班”，以及随之而来的一切附属物——音乐、祭衣、列队行进等等——

===Original===

﻿—which have come, along with them, into fashion﻿—while freely admitting that the “Ritual” movement was sorely needed, and that it has effected a vast improvement in our Church-Services, which had become dead and dry to the last degree, I hold that, like many other desirable movements, it has gone too far in the opposite direction, and has introduced many new dangers.
For the Congregation this new movement involves the danger of learning to think that the Services are done *for* them; and that their bodily *presence* is all they need contribute. And, for Clergy and Congregation alike, it involves the danger of regarding these elaborate Services as *ends in themselves*, and of forgetting that they are simply *means*, and the very hollowest of mockeries, unless they bear fruit in our *lives*.
For the Choristers it seems to involve the danger of self-conceit, as described at chapter 19, Vol. I, the danger of regarding those parts of the Service, where their help is not required, as not worth attending to, the danger of coming to regard the Service as a mere outward form﻿—a series of postures to be assumed, and of words to be said or sung, while the *thoughts* are elsewhere﻿—and the danger of “familiarity” breeding “contempt” for sacred things.
Let me illustrate these last two forms of danger, from my own experience. Not long ago, I attended a Cathedral-Service, and was placed immediately behind a row of men, members of the Choir; and I could not help noticing that they treated the *Lessons* as a part of the Service to which they needed not to give *any* attention, and as affording them a convenient opportunity for arranging music-books, etc., etc. Also I have frequently seen a row of little choristers, after marching in procession to their places, kneel down, as if about to pray, and rise from their knees after a minute spent in looking about them, it being but too evident that the attitude was a mere mockery. Surely it is very dangerous, for these children, to thus accustom them to *pretend* to pray? As an instance of irreverent treatment of holy things, I will mention a custom, which no doubt many of my readers have noticed in Churches where the Clergy and Choir enter in procession, viz.

===Chinese===

——这些如今都随唱诗班一起成了时尚——我坦然承认，“礼仪”运动曾大有必要，它也确实让我们的礼拜有了巨大改观——此前礼拜已死板干枯到极点；但我认为，同许多可取的运动一样，它又矫枉过正，带来了许多新的危险。
对会众而言，这场新运动的危险在于：他们渐渐以为礼拜是*替他们*办的；自己只须*到场*便尽了本分。而对教牧与会众双方，危险在于把这些繁复的礼拜当作*目的本身*，忘了它们只是*手段*——若不在我们的*生命*中结出果实，便是最空洞的嘲弄。
对唱诗班的孩子，危险似乎在于自负（第一卷第19章已述）；在于把礼拜中用不着他们帮忙的部分视若无物；在于渐渐把礼拜当作纯粹的外形——摆出一系列姿势、念唱一系列词句，而*心思*云游天外；还在于“熟稔”滋生对神圣之物的“轻慢”。
请允许我以亲身经历说明末两种危险。不久前我参加一场主教座堂礼拜，座位恰在一排唱诗班成员身后；我不能不注意到，他们把*经文诵读*当成礼拜中无须*丝毫*留意的部分，正好用来整理乐谱等等。我还屡屡见到一列小唱诗童列队入席后跪下，一副将要祈祷的样子，一分钟后起身——那一分钟全用在东张西望上，姿势纯属敷衍，再明显不过。让孩子们如此习惯*假装*祈祷，岂不十分危险？再举一个轻慢圣物的例子——在教牧与唱诗班列队入场的教堂里，许多读者想必见过这习俗，即：

===Original===

that, at the end of the private devotions, which are carried on in the vestry, and which are of course inaudible to the Congregation, the final “Amen” is *shouted*, loud enough to be heard all through the Church. This serves as a signal, to the Congregation, to prepare to rise when the procession appears: and it admits of no dispute that it is for this purpose that it is thus shouted. When we remember to Whom that “Amen” is *really* addressed, and consider that it is here *used* for the same purpose as one of the Church-bells, we must surely admit that it is a piece of gross irreverence? To *me* it is much as if I were to see a Bible used as a footstool.
As an instance of the dangers, for the Clergy themselves, introduced by this new movement, let me mention the fact that, according to *my* experience, Clergymen of this school are *specially* apt to retail comic anecdotes, in which the most sacred names and words﻿—sometimes actual texts from the Bible﻿—are used as themes for jesting. Many such things are repeated as having been originally said by *children*, whose utter ignorance of evil must no doubt acquit *them*, in the sight of God, of all blame; but it must be otherwise for those who *consciously* use such innocent utterances as material for their unholy mirth.
Let me add, however, *most* earnestly, that I fully believe that this profanity is, in many cases, *un*conscious: the “environment” (as I have tried to explain at chapter 8, Vol. II) makes all the difference between man and man; and I rejoice to think that many of these profane stories﻿—which *I* find so painful to listen to, and should feel it a sin to repeat﻿—give to *their* ears no pain, and to *their* consciences no shock; and that *they* can utter, not less sincerely than myself, the two prayers, “*Hallowed be Thy Name*” and “*from hardness of heart, and contempt of Thy Word and Commandment, Good Lord, deliver us!*” To which I would desire to add, for their sake and for my own, Keble’s beautiful petition, “*help us, this and every day, To live more nearly as we pray!*” It is, in fact, for its *consequences*﻿—for the grave dangers, both to speaker and to hearer, which it involves﻿—rather than for what it is *in itself*, that I mourn over this clerical habit of profanity in social talk.

===Chinese===

更衣室里进行的私人祈祷——会众当然听不见——末了那声“阿们”要*高喊*出来，响彻全堂。它向会众发信号，好待队列出现时预备起立：而这声喊正是为此目的，毫无争辩余地。当我们记起那声“阿们”*真正*是说给谁听的，再想想它在此竟被*派作*教堂钟一样的用场，我们岂不必须承认，这是十足的轻慢？在*我*看来，这几乎无异于眼看一部《圣经》被当脚凳使。
至于这场新运动给教牧自身带来的危险，请容我举一事实：依*我*的经验，这一派的教士*特别*爱传播滑稽轶事，其中最神圣的名号与言词——有时竟是圣经经文——被当作笑料。许多这类段子都说是*孩子*们当初说的；孩子全然不知恶为何物，在上帝眼中自然全然无罪；但那些*有意*拿这些天真言语当材料、供其不洁之乐的人，就另当别论了。
不过，请容我*极其*诚恳地补充：我深信这亵渎在许多情形下是*无意*的：“环境”（我在第二卷第8章试图解释过）造成了人与人之间的全部差别；而且我一想到便觉欣慰：这些在*我*听来刺心、复述便是罪的故事，在*他们*耳中并不刺痛，在*他们*良心上也不震撼；*他们*能同我一样真诚地念诵那两段祷文——“*愿人都尊你的名为圣*”与“*主啊，求你拯救我们脱离心里的刚硬，轻慢你的圣言与诫命！*”——并为他们的缘故、也为我自己的缘故，我愿再添上基布勒那美好的祈求：“*助我们，今日并每日，活得愈近我们所祷！*”事实上，我为教士社交言谈中这亵渎之习而悲，与其是为它*本身*，毋宁是为它的*后果*——它给说者与听者双方带来的深重危险。

===Original===

To the *believing* hearer it brings the danger of loss of reverence for holy things, by the mere act of listening to, and enjoying, such jests; and also the temptation to retail them for the amusement of others. To the *unbelieving* hearer it brings a welcome confirmation of his theory that religion is a fable, in the spectacle of its accredited champions thus betraying their trust. And to the speaker himself it must surely bring the danger of *loss of faith*. For surely such jests, if uttered with no consciousness of harm, must necessarily be also uttered with no consciousness, at the moment, of the *reality* of God, as a *living being*, who hears all we say. And he, who allows himself the habit of thus uttering holy words, with no thought of their meaning, is but too likely to find that, for him, God has become a myth, and heaven a poetic fancy﻿—that, for him, the light of life is gone, and that he is at heart an atheist, lost in “*a darkness that may be felt*.”
There is, I fear, at the present time, an increasing tendency to irreverent treatment of the name of God and of subjects connected with religion. Some of our theatres are helping this downward movement by the gross caricatures of clergymen which they put upon the stage: some of our clergy are themselves helping it, by showing that they can lay aside the spirit of reverence, along with their surplices, and can treat as jests, when *outside* their churches, names and things to which they pay an almost superstitious veneration when *inside*: the “Salvation Army” has, I fear, with the best intentions, done much to help it, by the coarse familiarity with which they treat holy things: and surely everyone, who desires to *live* in the spirit of the prayer “*Hallowed be thy Name*,” ought to do what he can, however little that may be, to check it. So I have gladly taken this unique opportunity, however unfit the topic may seem for the Preface to a book of this kind, to express some thoughts which have weighed on my mind for a long time. I did not expect, when I wrote the Preface to Vol.

===Chinese===

对*信*的听者，它带来失去对圣物敬畏的危险——仅仅因听这类笑话并乐在其中；还带来转述给他人取乐的试探。对*不信*的听者，眼见宗教公认的卫道者如此辜负其所托，恰好为他“宗教乃寓言”的高论添了欢迎的佐证。而对说者本人，它必带来*丧失信仰*的危险。因为这类笑话若说时不知其害，则说时也必定不知——那一刻不知上帝作为*活物*的*真实*，他听得见我们的一切言语。而惯于这样口吐圣言、心中全无其意的人，十有八九会发现：于他，上帝已成神话，天堂已成诗意的空想——于他，生命之光已经熄灭，他骨子里是个无神论者，迷失在“*可以感觉到的黑暗*”之中。
我担心，当今对上帝之名及宗教话题的轻慢，正呈上升趋势。我们的一些剧院把教士的粗俗漫画搬上舞台，在给这股下坡推力；我们的一些教士也在亲自推波助澜——他们表明自己能把敬畏之心连同白袍一起脱下，在教堂*外*把他们在教堂*内*近乎迷信地尊崇的名号与事物当作笑谈；“救世军”呢，我担心，虽出于至善之意，也以其对待圣物的粗鄙狎昵大力助长了它：凡愿活在“*愿人都尊你的名为圣*”这祷文之灵中的人，无论力所能及何等微薄，都当尽力遏止它。于是我欣然抓住这个独一无二的机会——哪怕这话题看似不宜出现在这类书的序言里——倾吐几件压在我心头已久的想法。当初写第一卷序言时，我并不指望——

===Original===

I, that it would be read to any appreciable extent: but I rejoice to believe, from evidence that has reached me, that it *has* been read by many, and to hope that this Preface will also be so: and I think that, among them, some will be found ready to sympathise with the views I have put forwards, and ready to help, with their prayers and their example, the revival, in Society, of the waning spirit of reverence.
Christmas, 1893.

===Chinese===

它会被多少人认真读到：但已有证据传来，令我欣然相信它*确实*被许多人读了，并令我期望这篇序言也如此：而且我想，他们之中，必有愿意同情我所持见解者，愿以祈祷和榜样，助那在社交中日渐式微的敬畏之心重新振作。
1893年，圣诞节。

## I Bruno’s Lessons / I 布鲁诺的功课
===Original===

During the next month or two my solitary town-life seemed, by contrast, unusually dull and tedious. I missed the pleasant friends I had left behind at Elveston﻿—the genial interchange of thought﻿—the sympathy which gave to one’s ideas a new and vivid reality: but, perhaps more than all, I missed the companionship of the two Fairies﻿—or Dream-Children, for I had not yet solved the problem as to who or what they were﻿—whose sweet playfulness had shed a magic radiance over my life.
In office-hours﻿—which I suppose reduce most men to the mental condition of a coffee-mill or a mangle﻿—time sped along much as usual: it was in the pauses of life, the desolate hours when books and newspapers palled on the sated appetite, and when, thrown back upon one’s own dreary musings, one strove﻿—all in vain﻿—to people the vacant air with the dear faces of absent friends, that the real bitterness of solitude made itself felt.
One evening, feeling my life a little more wearisome than usual, I strolled down to my Club, not so much with the hope of meeting any friend there, for London was now “out of town,” as with the feeling that here, at least, I should hear “sweet words of human speech,” and come into contact with human thought.
However, almost the first face I saw there *was* that of a friend. Eric Lindon was lounging, with rather a “bored” expression of face, over a newspaper; and we fell into conversation with a mutual satisfaction which neither of us tried to conceal.
After a while I ventured to introduce what was just then the main subject of my thoughts. “And so the Doctor” (a name we had adopted by a tacit agreement, as a convenient compromise between the formality of “Doctor Forester” and the intimacy﻿—to which Eric Lindon hardly seemed entitled﻿—of “Arthur”) “has gone abroad by this time, I suppose? Can you give me his present address?”
“He is still at Elveston﻿—I believe,” was the reply. “But I have not been there since I last met you.”
I did not know which part of this intelligence to wonder at most. “And might I ask﻿—if it isn’t taking too much of a liberty﻿—when your wedding-bells are to﻿—or perhaps they *have* rung, already?”
“No,” said Eric, in a steady voice, which betrayed scarcely a trace of emotion: “*that* engagement is at an end. I am still ‘Benedick the *un*married man.’

===Chinese===

此后一两个月，相形之下，我独居城中的生活显得格外沉闷乏味。我思念留在埃尔夫斯顿的愉快朋友们——那亲切的思想交流——那赋予一个人的观念以崭新生动实在感的共鸣：但思念得最切的，也许还是那两个精灵——或者“梦之子”，因为他们究竟是谁、是什么，我尚未参透——他们甜美的嬉闹，曾为我的生活洒上一层魔幻的光辉。
办公时间里——我想那总把多数人的心智磨成一架咖啡磨或轧衣机——时间照常飞逝：是在生活的间隙，在那书报都已餍足生腻的凄凉时辰，在人退回自身阴郁的沉思里、竭力——全然徒劳地——想用缺席挚友的可爱面孔充满虚空时，孤独真正的苦涩才显出分量。
一天晚上，觉得生活比平时更难挨，我便踱到俱乐部去；倒不指望在那里遇上朋友——伦敦这时正“倾城外出”——而是觉得，至少在这里能听见“人语的甘美词句”，能触到人的思想。
然而，我在那里最先看见的面孔之一，*恰恰*是一位朋友的。埃里克·林登正歪在椅子里看报，一脸百无聊赖；我们攀谈起来，彼此的满意谁也不想掩饰。
过了一会儿，我斗胆引入了当时盘踞我心头的主要话题。这么说，“医生”（这个称呼是我们心照不宣地采用的，是“福雷斯特医生”的拘礼与“亚瑟”的亲昵之间的一个折中——后一种亲昵，埃里克·林登似乎还不够格）这会儿已经出国了吧？能给我他现在的地址吗？
他还在埃尔夫斯顿——我想是的。他答道。可自上次遇见你之后，我没去过那里。
这条消息里，我不知该对哪一部分更惊讶。那么可否容我问一句——若不算太冒昧——你们的喜钟几时敲响——或者，已经敲过了？
没有，埃里克说，声调平稳，几乎不露一丝情感：*那*桩婚约已经了结。我依旧是“未婚的班奈狄克”。

===Original===

”
After this, the thick-coming fancies﻿—all radiant with new possibilities of happiness for Arthur﻿—were far too bewildering to admit of any further conversation, and I was only too glad to avail myself of the first decent excuse, that offered itself, for retiring into silence.
The next day I wrote to Arthur, with as much of a reprimand for his long silence as I could bring myself to put into words, begging him to tell me how the world went with him.
Needs must that three or four days﻿—possibly more﻿—should elapse before I could receive his reply; and never had I known days drag their slow length along with a more tedious indolence.
To while away the time, I strolled, one afternoon, into Kensington Gardens, and, wandering aimlessly along any path that presented itself, I soon became aware that I had somehow strayed into one that was wholly new to me. Still, my elfish experiences seemed to have so completely faded out of my life that nothing was further from my thoughts than the idea of again meeting my fairy-friends, when I chanced to notice a small creature, moving among the grass that fringed the path, that did not seem to be an insect, or a frog, or any other living thing that I could think of. Cautiously kneeling down, and making an extempore cage of my two hands, I imprisoned the little wanderer, and felt a sudden thrill of surprise and delight on discovering that my prisoner was no other than *Bruno* himself!
Bruno took the matter *very* coolly, and, when I had replaced him on the ground, where he would be within easy conversational distance, he began talking, just as if it were only a few minutes since last we had met.
“Doos oo know what the *Rule* is,” he enquired, “when oo catches a Fairy, withouten its having tolded oo where it was?” (Bruno’s notions of English Grammar had certainly *not* improved since our last meeting.)
“No,” I said. “I didn’t know there was any Rule about it.”
“I *think* oo’ve got a right to *eat* me,” said the little fellow, looking up into my face with a winning smile. “But I’m not pruffickly sure. Oo’d better not do it wizout asking.”
It did indeed seem reasonable not to take so irrevocable a step as *that*, without due enquiry. “I’ll certainly *ask* about it, first,” I said. “Besides, I don’t know yet whether you would be *worth* eating!”

===Chinese===

此后，纷至沓来的遐想——每一缕都闪耀着亚瑟新的幸福可能——搅得我心头眩晕，谈话再也进行不下去；正好有个说得过去的借口送上门，我乐得借它退入沉默。
第二天我给亚瑟写信，尽量把对他久久不来信的责备写进字句——以我下得了笔为度——恳请他告诉我他的近况。
要等三四天——或许更久——他的回信才会到；这些日子拖着迟缓的身子挪动，那份拖沓慵懒，我从未领教过。
为了消磨时光，一天下午我踱进肯辛顿花园，信步沿着一道道呈现眼前的路径乱走；不多时便发觉，自己不知怎么拐进了一条全然陌生的路。不过，那些精灵奇遇似乎已彻底从我生活中褪去，“再遇精灵朋友”的念头离我的心比什么都远；恰在此时，我偶然注意到路旁草丛里有个小东西在动——它不像昆虫，不像青蛙，也不像我想得出的任何活物。我小心跪下，双手拢成一个临时笼子，把那小游荡者罩住；随即一阵惊喜的战栗——我的囚徒，不是别人，正是*布鲁诺*！
布鲁诺对这桩事*极为*冷静；我把他放回地上——放到方便交谈的距离——他便开口聊起来，仿佛我们上次分手不过几分钟。
奥知道那条*规矩*吗，他问，就是奥捉住一个精灵、而它事先没告过诉奥它在哪儿的规矩？（自上次见面，布鲁诺的英语语法显然*毫无*长进。）
不知道，我说。我不知道还有这么一条规矩。
我*想*奥有权*吃*掉我，小家伙仰望我的脸，带着迷人的微笑说。不过我不百分百确定。奥最好先问问再吃。
不先问个清楚，就迈出*那样*无可挽回的一步，确实不合情理。我一定先*问*明白，我说。再说，你还值不值得吃，我都还不知道呢！

===Original===

“I guess I’m *deliciously* good to eat,” Bruno remarked in a satisfied tone, as if it were something to be rather proud of.
“And what are you doing here, Bruno?”
“*That’s* not my name!” said my cunning little friend. “Don’t oo know my name’s ‘Oh Bruno!’? That’s what Sylvie always calls me, when I says mine lessons.”
“Well then, what are you doing here, oh Bruno?”
“Doing mine lessons, a-course!” With that roguish twinkle in his eye, that always came when he knew he was talking nonsense.
“Oh, *that’s* the way you do your lessons, is it? And do you remember them well?”
“Always can ’member *mine* lessons,” said Bruno. “It’s *Sylvie’s* lessons that’s so *dreffully* hard to ’member!” He frowned, as if in agonies of thought, and tapped his forehead with his knuckles. “I *can’t* think enough to understand them!” he said despairingly. “It wants *double* thinking, I believe!”
“But where’s Sylvie gone?”
“That’s just what *I* want to know!” said Bruno disconsolately. “What ever’s the good of setting me lessons, when she isn’t here to ’splain the hard bits?”
“*I’ll* find her for you!” I volunteered; and, getting up, I wandered round the tree under whose shade I had been reclining, looking on all sides for Sylvie. In another minute I *again* noticed some strange thing moving among the grass, and, kneeling down, was immediately confronted with Sylvie’s innocent face, lighted up with a joyful surprise at seeing me, and was accosted, in the sweet voice I knew so well, with what seemed to be the *end* of a sentence whose beginning I had failed to catch.
“﻿—and I think he ought to have *finished* them by this time. So I’m going back to him. Will you come too? It’s only just round at the other side of this tree.”
It was but a few steps for *me*; but it was a great many for Sylvie; and I had to be very careful to walk slowly, in order not to leave the little creature so far behind as to lose sight of her.

===Chinese===

我猜我吃起来*美味得很*，布鲁诺以满意的口气评点道，仿佛这还挺值得夸耀。
那你在这儿干什么呢，布鲁诺？
那不是我的名字！我这位狡黠的小朋友说。奥不知道我的名字叫“哦布鲁诺”吗？我念功课的时候，西尔维总是这么叫我。
那么，你在这儿干什么呢，哦布鲁诺？
做功课呗，那还用说！眼里闪着他明知自己在胡说时总会闪起的那点狡黠。
哦，*原来*你的功课是这么做的！那你记得牢吗？
*我自己的*功课我向来记得住，布鲁诺说。难记的是*西尔维的*功课，难得*要命*！他皱起眉，仿佛思绪痛楚，用指节敲着前额。我*想*不够多，想不懂它们！他绝望地说。那需要*双倍*的想，我看！
可西尔维去哪儿了？
这*我*也正想知道呢！布鲁诺凄凄地说。她不在这儿讲解难的地方，给我布置功课还有什么用？
我帮你找她去！我自告奋勇；起身绕着我方才倚歇的那棵树寻视，四下张望西尔维。不多时，我*又*注意到草丛里有什么奇怪的东西在动；跪下一看，迎面正对上西尔维那张天真无邪的脸——见到我，她惊喜得满脸放光；随即那个我再熟悉不过的甜美嗓音向我搭话，说的仿佛是某句话的*结尾*，而开头我没赶上听。
——我想这会儿他总该*做*完了。所以我正要回去找他。您也一起来吗？就在这棵树那一边，没几步。
那几步路对*我*不过咫尺；对西尔维却是漫漫长途；我只得刻意放慢脚步，免得把这小人儿落下太远、看不见她。

===Original===

To find Bruno’s *lessons* was easy enough: they appeared to be neatly written out on large smooth ivy-leaves, which were scattered in some confusion over a little patch of ground where the grass had been worn away; but the pale student, who ought by rights to have been bending over them, was nowhere to be seen: we looked in all directions, for some time, in vain; but at last Sylvie’s sharp eyes detected him, swinging on a tendril of ivy, and Sylvie’s stern voice commanded his instant return to *terra firma* and to the business of Life.
“Pleasure first and business afterwards” seemed to be the motto of these tiny folk, so many hugs and kisses had to be interchanged before anything else could be done.
“Now, Bruno,” Sylvie said reproachfully, “didn’t I tell you you were to go on with your lessons, unless you heard to the contrary?”
“But I *did* heard to the contrary!” Bruno insisted, with a mischievous twinkle in his eye.
“*What* did you hear, you wicked boy?”
“It were a sort of noise in the air,” said Bruno: “a sort of a scrambling noise. Didn’t *oo* hear it, Mister Sir?”
“Well, anyhow, you needn’t go to *sleep* over them, you lazy-lazy!” For Bruno had curled himself up, on the largest “lesson,” and was arranging another as a pillow.
“I *wasn’t* asleep!” said Bruno, in a deeply-injured tone. “When I shuts mine eyes, it’s to show that I’m *awake*!”
“Well, how much have you learned, then?”
“I’ve learned a little tiny bit,” said Bruno, modestly, being evidently afraid of overstating his achievement. “*can’t* learn no more!”
“Oh Bruno! You know you *can*, if you like.”
“Course I can, if I *like*,” the pale student replied; “but I can’t if I *don’t* like!”
Sylvie had a way﻿—which I could not too highly admire﻿—of evading Bruno’s logical perplexities by suddenly striking into a new line of thought; and this masterly stratagem she now adopted.
“Well, I must say *one* thing﻿—”
“Did oo know, Mister Sir,” Bruno thoughtfully remarked, “that Sylvie can’t count? Whenever she says ‘I must say *one* thing,’ I *know* quite well she’ll say *two* things! And she always doos.”
“Two heads are better than one, Bruno,” I said, but with no very distinct idea as to what I meant by it.

===Chinese===

找布鲁诺的*功课*倒容易：它们工整地写在大片光滑的常春藤叶上，有些散乱地铺在一小块草磨秃了的地上；可那位理应伏案苦读的苍白学生却踪影全无：我们四下找了半天，一无所获；终于西尔维的锐眼把他逮住了——正吊在一根藤须上打秋千——西尔维严厉的嗓音勒令他即刻回到*坚实大地*、回到人生的正务上来。
对这两个小人儿来说，格言仿佛是“先享乐，后办事”：干正事之前，必得先互赠无数的搜抱与亲吻。
听着，布鲁诺，西尔维责备地说，我不是吩咐过你继续做功课，除非听到别的指示吗？
可我*就是*听到别的指示了！布鲁诺坚持道，眼里闪着淘气的光。
你听见*什么*了，你这个坏孩子？
是一种空中的声响，布鲁诺说：一种窸窸窣窣的动静。*奥*没听见吗，先生先生？
好吧，反正你总不致*睡*到功课上去吧，你这懒懒鬼！——布鲁诺已在最大的那片“功课”上蜷成一团，正把另一片抽出来当枕头。
我*没*睡！布鲁诺的口气深受冤枉。我闭上眼睛，正是表示我*醒着*！
那你学了多少了？
我学了一小丁点儿，布鲁诺谦虚地说，显然怕夸大了成绩。*再也*学不动了！
哦布鲁诺！你知道只要*愿意*，你就*能*学。
我*愿意*当然能学，苍白的学生答道：可我*不*愿意就不能学！
西尔维有个法子——我佩服得无以复加——面对布鲁诺的逻辑难题，她突然拔脚拐进一条新思路；此刻她便施展了这一高招。
好吧，我得说*一*件事——
奥知道吗，先生先生，布鲁诺若有所思地评点，西尔维不会数数？每当她说“我得说*一*件事”，我*心里*雪亮，她准要说*两*件！她回回如此。
三个臭皮匠顶个诸葛亮，布鲁诺，我说——不过我自己也不大清楚这话是什么意思。

===Original===

“I shouldn’t mind having two *heads*,” Bruno said softly to himself: “one head to eat mine dinner, and one head to argue wiz Sylvie﻿—doos oo think oo’d look prettier if oo’d got *two* heads, Mister Sir?”
The case did not, I assured him, admit of a doubt.
“The reason why Sylvie’s so cross﻿—” Bruno went on very seriously, almost sadly.
Sylvie’s eyes grew large and round with surprise at this new line of enquiry﻿—her rosy face being perfectly radiant with good humour. But she said nothing.
“Wouldn’t it be better to tell me after the lessons are over?” I suggested.
“Very well,” Bruno said with a resigned air: “only she won’t be cross then.”
“There’s only three lessons to do,” said Sylvie. “Spelling, and Geography, and Singing.”
“Not *Arithmetic*?” I said.
“No, he hasn’t a head for Arithmetic﻿—”
“Course I haven’t!” said Bruno. “Mine head’s for *hair*. I haven’t got a *lot* of heads!”
“﻿—and he can’t learn his Multiplication-table﻿—”
“I like *History* ever so much better,” Bruno remarked. “Oo has to *repeat* that Muddlecome table﻿—”
“Well, and you have to repeat﻿—”
“No, oo hasn’t!” Bruno interrupted. “History repeats itself. The Professor said so!”
Sylvie was arranging some letters on a board﻿—**E**-**V**-**I**-**L**. “Now, Bruno,” she said, “what does *that* spell?”
Bruno looked at it, in solemn silence, for a minute. “I knows what it *doosn’t* spell!” he said at last.
“That’s no good,” said Sylvie. “What *does* it spell?”
Bruno took another look at the mysterious letters. “Why, it’s ‘**live**,’ backwards!” he exclaimed. (I thought it was, indeed.)
“How *did* you manage to see that?” said Sylvie.
“I just twiddled my eyes,” said Bruno, “and then I saw it directly. Now may I sing the Kingfisher Song?”
“Geography next,” said Sylvie. “Don’t you know the Rules?”
“I thinks there oughtn’t to be such a lot of Rules, Sylvie! I thinks﻿—”
“Yes, there *ought* to be such a lot of Rules, you wicked, wicked boy! And how dare you *think* at all about it? And shut up that mouth directly!”
So, as “that mouth” didn’t seem inclined to shut up of itself, Sylvie shut it for him﻿—with both hands﻿—and sealed it with a kiss, just as you would fasten up a letter.
“Now that Bruno is fastened up from talking,” she went on, turning to me, “I’ll show you the Map he does his lessons on.”
And there it was, a large Map of the World, spread out on the ground.

===Chinese===

长两个*脑袋*我倒不反对，布鲁诺轻声自语：一个脑袋吃我的饭，一个脑袋同西尔维抬杠——奥觉得要是自己长*两*个脑袋，会不会更好看，先生先生？
我向他保证，此案无可怀疑。
西尔维所以这么凶——布鲁诺一本正经、几乎悲戚地往下说。
对这条新思路，西尔维惊得眼睛又大又圆——红润的小脸漾满好脾气的光彩。可她一言不发。
功课做完再告诉我，是不是更好？我提议。
也好，布鲁诺一副听天由命的神气：只是到那时她就不凶了。
只有三门功课要做，西尔维说。拼写、地理，还有唱歌。
没有*算术*？我说。
没有，他学算术的脑子不济——
当然不济！布鲁诺说。我的脑袋是长*头发*的。我可没有*一大堆*脑袋！
——乘法口诀他也背不下来——
我*顶*喜欢历史，布鲁诺说。那张乱七八糟的口诀非得*背*——
呢，历史你不也得背——
不，不用！布鲁诺打断。历史自己会重演。教授说的！
西尔维正往一块板上摆字母——E-V-I-L。来，布鲁诺，她说，*这*拼成什么？
布鲁诺庄重地默默看了它一分钟。我知道它*不*拼成什么！他终于说。
那可没用，西尔维说。它*到底*拼成什么？
布鲁诺又看了那串神秘的字母一眼。咦，这是“**live**”倒过来写嘛！他喊道。（我想，确实如此。）
你是*怎么*看出来的？西尔维说。
我把眼睛转了转，布鲁诺说，一下就看出来了。现在我可以唱翠鸟之歌了吗？
接下来是地理，西尔维说。规矩你不知道吗？
我觉得规矩不该这么多，西尔维！我觉得——
就该有这么多规矩，你这个坏呀坏孩子！这事你也敢*想*？快把嘴闭上！
于是，既然“那张嘴”看来无意自行闭上，西尔维便亲自动手替他闭——用两只手——又用一个吻封了口，就像封一封信那样。
现在布鲁诺被封住说不了话了，她转向我接着说，我带您看他做功课用的地图。
地图就在那里，一幅巨大的世界地图，铺展在地上。

===Original===

It was so large that Bruno had to crawl about on it, to point out the places named in the “Kingfisher Lesson.”
“When a Kingfisher sees a Ladybird flying away, he says ‘*Ceylon*, if you *Candia*!’ And when he catches it, he says ‘Come to *Media*! And if you’re *Hungary* or thirsty, I’ll give you some *Nubia*!’ When he takes it in his claws, he says ‘*Europe!*’ When he puts it into his beak, he says ‘*India!*’ When he’s swallowed it, he says ‘*Eton!*’ That’s all.”
“That’s *quite* perfect,” said Sylvie. “Now you may sing the Kingfisher Song.”
“Will *oo* sing the chorus?” Bruno said to me.
I was just beginning to say “I’m afraid I don’t know the *words*,” when Sylvie silently turned the map over, and I found the words were all written on the back. In one respect it was a *very* peculiar song: the chorus to each verse came in the *middle*, instead of at the *end* of it. However, the tune was so easy that I soon picked it up, and managed the chorus as well, perhaps, as it is possible for *one* person to manage such a thing. It was in vain that I signed to Sylvie to help me: she only smiled sweetly and shook her head.

> “King Fisher courted Lady Bird﻿—
> *Sing Beans, sing Bones, sing Butterflies!*
> ‘Find me my match,’ he said,
> ‘With such a noble head﻿—
> With such a beard, as white as curd﻿—
> With such expressive eyes!’
> “ ‘Yet pins have heads,’ said Lady Bird﻿—
> *Sing Prunes, sing Prawns, sing Primrose-Hill!*
> ‘And, where you stick them in,
> They stay, and thus a pin
> Is very much to be preferred
> To one that’s never still!’
> “ ‘Oysters have beards,’ said Lady Bird﻿—
> *Sing Flies, sing Frogs, sing Fiddle-strings!*
> ‘I love them, for I know
> *They* never chatter so:
> They would not say one single word﻿—
> Not if you crowned them Kings!’
> “ ‘Needles have eyes,’ said Lady Bird﻿—
> *Sing Cats, sing Corks, sing Cowslip-tea!*
> ‘And they are sharp﻿—just what
> Your Majesty is *not:*
> So get you gone﻿—’tis too absurd
> To come a-courting *me*!’ ”

===Chinese===

它大得布鲁诺得在上面爬来爬去，才能指到“翠鸟课”里点到的地名。
翠鸟看见一只瓢虫飞走，就说“*锡兰*（Ceylon）小姐，你要是*肯飞*（Candia）就好啦！”逮住它，就说“到*米底*（Media）来！你要是*饿*（Hungary）了、渴了，我给你点*尝的*（Nubia）！”用爪子抓住它，就喊“*欧罗巴*（Europe）！”塞进嘴里，就说“*印度*（India）！”咽下肚，就说“*伊顿*（Eton）！”完啦。
*非常*完美，西尔维说。现在可以唱翠鸟之歌了。
*奥*来唱副歌好吗？布鲁诺对我说。
我刚要开口说“恐怕我不记得*歌词*”，西尔维默默把地图翻了过来——歌词原来全写在背面。这歌有*一处*非常别致：每节的副歌插在*中间*，而不在*末尾*。好在曲调极容易，我很快上口；副歌嘛，大约也唱出了*一个*人所能达到的最好水平。我朝西尔维打眼色求援：徒劳——她只甜甜一笑，摇了摇头。

> “翠鸟先生向瓢虫小姐求婚——
> *唱豆子，唱骨头，唱蝴蝶！*
> “给我找个般配的，”他说，
> “配这高贵的头颅——
> 配这把凝乳般雪白的胡须——
> 配这对深情的眼睛！”
> ““可大头针也有头哇，”瓢虫小姐说——
> *唱梅子，唱虾子，唱樱草山！*
> “而且，往哪儿一插，
> 它就乖乖不动；这样的大头针，
> 可比一个一刻不安分的
> 强出太多啦！”
> ““牡蛎也有胡须，”瓢虫小姐说——
> *唱苍蝇，唱青蛙，唱琴弦！*
> “我爱它们，因为我知道
> *它们*从不这样聒噪：
> 给它们戴上王冠，
> 它们也一声不吭！”
> ““缝衣针也有眼睛，”瓢虫小姐说——
> *唱猫儿，唱软木塞，唱黄花茶！*
> “而且它们尖得很——恰恰
> 是陛下您*不*具备的：
> 请您走吧——向我*求爱*，
> 这实在太荒唐啦！”

===Original===

“So he went away,” Bruno added as a kind of postscript, when the last note of the song had died away. “Just like he always did.”
“Oh, my *dear* Bruno!” Sylvie exclaimed, with her hands over her ears. “You shouldn’t say ‘like’: you should say ‘*what*.’ ”
To which Bruno replied, doggedly, “I only says ‘what!’ when oo doosn’t speak loud, so as I can hear oo.”
“Where did he go to?” I asked, hoping to prevent an argument.
“He went more far than he’d never been before,” said Bruno.
“You should never say ‘more far,’ ” Sylvie corrected him: “you should say ‘*farther*.’ ”
“Then *oo* shouldn’t say ‘more broth,’ when we’re at dinner,” Bruno retorted: “oo should say ‘*brother*’!”
This time Sylvie evaded an argument by turning away, and beginning to roll up the Map. “Lessons are over!” she proclaimed in her sweetest tones.
“And has there been no *crying* over them?” I enquired. “Little boys *always* cry over their lessons, don’t they?”
“I never cries after twelve o’clock,” said Bruno: “ ’cause then it’s getting so near to dinnertime.”
“Sometimes, in the morning,” Sylvie said in a low voice; “when it’s Geography-day, and when he’s been disobe﻿—”
“*What* a fellow you are to talk, Sylvie!” Bruno hastily interposed. “Doos oo think the world was *made* for oo to talk in?”
“Why, where would you *have* me talk, then?” Sylvie said, evidently quite ready for an argument.
But Bruno answered resolutely. “I’m not going to argue about it, ’cause it’s getting late, and there won’t be time﻿—but oo’s as ’ong as ever oo can be!” And he rubbed the back of his hand across his eyes, in which tears were beginning to glitter.
*Sylvie’s* eyes filled with tears in a moment. “I didn’t mean it, Bruno, *darling*!” she whispered; and the rest of the argument was lost “amid the tangles of Neaera’s hair,” while the two disputants hugged and kissed each other.
But this new form of argument was brought to a sudden end by a flash of lightning, which was closely followed by a peal of thunder, and by a torrent of raindrops, which came hissing and spitting, almost like live creatures, through the leaves of the tree that sheltered us.
“Why, it’s raining cats and dogs!” I said.
“And all the *dogs* has come down *first*,” said Bruno: “there’s nothing but *cats* coming down now!”
In another minute the pattering ceased, as suddenly as it had begun.

===Chinese===

于是他走掉了，歌声的最后一个音符消散后，布鲁诺又补了句类似附言的话。就像他一贯的那样。
哎呀，我*亲爱的*布鲁诺！西尔维双手捫耳喊道。不该说“就像他”：该说“照着他*干的什么*说”。
布鲁诺犟头犟脑地答：奥只有在您说话声太小、奥听不清的时候，才说“什么！”
他去了哪儿？我问，想止住一场争论。
他去到了比他从来没到过的更远的地方，布鲁诺说。
永远不该说“更远”，西尔维纠正他：该说“*更遥*远”。
那*您*吃饭时也永远不该喊“再来点儿汤”，布鲁诺回敬：*您*该喊“再来点儿*糖*”！
这一回，西尔维以转身卷地图避开了争论。功课结束啦！她用最甜的嗓音宣布。
那做功课没*哭*过吧？我问。小男孩做功课*总是*要哭的，对不对？
十二点以后我从不哭，布鲁诺说：因为那之后就快挨到饭点儿了。
有时候，在上午，西尔维低声说；碰上是地理课的日子，又赶上他不听话——
*你*可真能说，西尔维！布鲁诺急忙插进来。奥以为这世界是*造*出来给奥说话的吗？
嗨，那你要*我*到哪儿去说？西尔维说，看来完全准备好吵一架了。
可布鲁诺斩钉截铁地答：我不打算吵这个，因为天不早了，来不及了——不过奥真是错得没边儿了！他用手背抹了抹眼睛，眼里已开始闪泪光。
*西尔维*的眼里霎时也充满了泪。我不是那个意思，布鲁诺，*宝贝*！她悄声说；这场争论的余下部分，都“消失在尼埃拉的发绺间”了——两位论敌紧紧搜抱着，互相亲吻。
可这场新式争论被一道闪电骤然打断——雷声紧随而至，接着是倾盆的雨点，嘶嘶喷溅着，几乎像活物一般，穿过荫蔽我们的树叶。
哎呀，天上在下猫下狗啦！我说。
*狗*都先落下来啦，布鲁诺说：现在落下来的全是*猫*！
又过了一分钟，噼啪声骤然止息，同它开始时一样突然。

===Original===

I stepped out from under the tree, and found that the storm was over; but I looked in vain, on my return, for my tiny companions. They had vanished with the storm, and there was nothing for it but to make the best of my way home.
On the table lay, awaiting my return, an envelope of that peculiar yellow tint which always announces a telegram, and which must be, in the memories of so many of us, inseparably linked with some great and sudden sorrow﻿—something that has cast a shadow, never in this world to be wholly lifted off, on the brightness of Life. No doubt it has *also* heralded﻿—for many of us﻿—some sudden news of joy; but this, I think, is less common: human life seems, on the whole, to contain more of sorrow than of joy. And yet the world goes on. Who knows why?
This time, however, there was no shock of sorrow to be faced: in fact, the few words it contained (“Could not bring myself to write. Come soon. Always welcome. A letter follows this. Arthur.”) seemed so like Arthur himself speaking, that it gave me quite a thrill of pleasure, and I at once began the preparations needed for the journey.

===Chinese===

我从树下走出，雷雨已过；可归途中我再找那两位小小同伴，遍寻不见。他们随雷雨一同消散了；我只得悻悻然尽力赶回家去。
桌上躺着一只等我归来的信封，那种特有的黄色，永远宣告电报的到来；在多少人记忆里，它必与某场巨大而突然的悲恸密不可分——那种在生命的光明上投下阴影、此生再不能全然抹去的东西。无疑，它也曾为我们中许多人*预告*过突至的喜讯；但我想那较少见：人生在世，总的说来悲多于喜。然而世界照常运转。谁知道为什么？
不过这一次，无须面对悲恸的打击：实际上，那寥寥数字（“实在提不起笔。速来。永远欢迎。随后有信。亚瑟。”）简直像亚瑟本人在开口说话，令我心头掠过一阵欢愉的震颤；我当即着手打点行装。

## II Love’s Curfew / II 爱的晚钟
===Original===

“Fayfield Junction! Change for Elveston!”
What subtle memory could there be, linked to these commonplace words, that caused such a flood of happy thoughts to fill my brain? I dismounted from the carriage in a state of joyful excitement for which I could not at first account. True, I had taken this very journey, and at the same hour of the day, six months ago; but many things had happened since then, and an old man’s memory has but a slender hold on recent events: I sought “the missing link” in vain. Suddenly I caught sight of a bench﻿—the only one provided on the cheerless platform﻿—with a lady seated on it, and the whole forgotten scene flashed upon me as vividly as if it were happening over again.
“Yes,” I thought. “This bare platform is, for me, rich with the memory of a dear friend! She was sitting on that very bench, and invited me to share it, with some quotation from Shakespeare﻿—I forget what. I’ll try the Earl’s plan for the Dramatisation of Life, and fancy that figure to be Lady Muriel; and I won’t undeceive myself too soon!”
So I strolled along the platform, resolutely “making-believe” (as children say) that the casual passenger, seated on that bench, was the Lady Muriel I remembered so well. She was facing away from me, which aided the elaborate cheatery I was practising on myself: but, though I was careful, in passing the spot, to look the other way, in order to prolong the pleasant illusion, it was inevitable that, when I turned to walk back again, I should see who it was. It was Lady Muriel herself!
The whole scene now returned vividly to my memory; and, to make this repetition of it stranger still, there was the same old man, whom I remembered seeing so roughly ordered off, by the Stationmaster, to make room for his titled passenger. The same, but “with a difference”: no longer tottering feebly along the platform, but actually seated at Lady Muriel’s side, and in conversation with her! “Yes, put it in your purse,” she was saying, “and remember you’re to spend it all for *Minnie*. And mind you bring her something nice, that’ll do her real good! And give her my love!” So intent was she on saying these words, that, although the sound of my footstep had made her lift her head and look at me, she did not at first recognise me.

===Chinese===

费菲尔德枢纽站！去埃尔夫斯顿的换车！
这几句平常话里，能拴着什么微妙的记忆，竟引得一股幸福的思绪涌满脑海？我带着一阵自己也说不清的欢喜激动下了车。不错，半年前，正是在这趟旅程、正是一天中的这个时辰；但此后发生了太多事，老人的记忆对近事最留不住：那“缺失的一环”我怎么也找不着。忽然，我望见一条长椅——这凄冷的站台上仅有的那条——椅上坐着一位女士，整幅忘却的景象霎时鲜活地闪回眼前，仿佛正在重演。
是啊，我想。这光秃秃的站台，于我却盈满一位挚友的记忆！她就坐在那条椅上，还引了一句莎士比亚请我同坐——引的什么我忘了。我要试试伯爵那套“把人生戏剧化”的办法，把那身影就当作穆丽尔小姐；而且不急着拆穿自己！
于是我沿站台踱去，坚决地“假装”（孩子们的说法）——假装坐在长椅上的那位偶然旅客，就是我记忆犹新的穆丽尔小姐。她背对着我，正方便我对自己施展这场精心自欺：不过，经过那里时，我虽小心地望着另一边，想延长这愉快的幻觉，可等我转身往回走，终究要看清那是谁。正是穆丽尔小姐本人！
整个场景如今生动地重回记忆；而令这重现愈发奇诡的是：那位老人还在——我记得他曾被站长粗声喝斥着赶开、给有头衔的旅客让位。还是他，却“有所不同”：不再在站台上步履蹒跚，而是真真切切坐在穆丽尔小姐身边，同她说话！好，把它收进钱包，她正说着，记住，这钱要全花在*明妮*身上。记着给她带样好东西，要真正对她有益的！替我问她好！她说这些话说得太专注，我的脚步声虽使她抬头望了我一眼，一时却没认出我来。

===Original===

I raised my hat as I approached, and then there flashed across her face a genuine look of joy, which so exactly recalled the sweet face of Sylvie, when last we met in Kensington Gardens, that I felt quite bewildered.
Rather than disturb the poor old man at her side, she rose from her seat, and joined me in my walk up and down the platform, and for a minute or two our conversation was as utterly trivial and commonplace as if we were merely two casual guests in a London drawing-room. Each of us seemed to shrink, just at first, from touching on the deeper interests which linked our lives together.
The Elveston train had drawn up at the platform, while we talked; and, in obedience to the Stationmaster’s obsequious hint of “This way, my Lady! Time’s up!”, we were making the best of our way towards the end which contained the sole first-class carriage, and were just passing the now-empty bench, when Lady Muriel noticed, lying on it, the purse in which her gift had just been so carefully bestowed, the owner of which, all unconscious of his loss, was being helped into a carriage at the other end of the train. She pounced on it instantly. “Poor old man!” she cried. “He mustn’t go off, and think he’s lost it!”
“Let *me* run with it! I can go quicker than you!” I said. But she was already halfway down the platform, flying (“running” is much too mundane a word for such fairy-like motion) at a pace that left all possible efforts of *mine* hopelessly in the rear.
She was back again before I had well completed my audacious boast of speed in running, and was saying, quite demurely, as we entered our carriage, “and you really think *you* could have done it quicker?”
“No indeed!” I replied. “I plead ‘Guilty’ of gross exaggeration, and throw myself on the mercy of the Court!”
“The Court will overlook it﻿—for this once!” Then her manner suddenly changed from playfulness to an anxious gravity.
“You are not looking your best!” she said with an anxious glance. “In fact, I think you look *more* of an invalid than when you left us. I very much doubt if London agrees with you?”
“It *may* be the London air,” I said, “or it may be the hard work﻿—or my rather lonely life: anyhow, I’ve *not* been feeling very well, lately. But Elveston will soon set me up again.

===Chinese===

我走近时举帽致意，她脸上霎时掠过一缕真切的欢喜——同上次我们在肯辛顿花园分手时西尔维那甜美的脸如此一模一样，我竟怔住了。
她不愿惊扰身旁那可怜的老人，便起身离座，随我在站台上来回踱步；有一两分钟，我们的谈话琐屑平常至极，仿佛只是伦敦客厅里两位萍水相逢的宾客。起初，谁似乎都缩着，不敢碰那把我们生命连在一起的更深的关切。
谈话间，埃尔夫斯顿的列车已靠上站台；站长谄媚地提醒“这边请，小姐！时间到了！”，我们便赶紧朝列车那头唯一的一节头等车厢赶去，正经过此刻已空的长椅，穆丽尔小姐瞥见椅上躺着那只钱包——方才她那番馈赠还郑重其事地放进去过——而失主对此全然不觉，正被人搭扶着上另一头的车厢。她一把抓起钱包。可怜的老人！她喊道。可不能让他走了，还以为自己丢了它！
让*我*跑去送！我比你快！我说。可她已经奔下半个站台——“奔跑”这个字眼，对那样仙子般的身法实在太过平庸——那速度把我一切可能的努力遥遥抛在身后。
我那句关于跑步速度的大话还没吹完，她已经回来了；进车厢时她规规矩矩地问：您真觉得自己能比我快？
不，快不了！我答。我承认“严重夸大”之罪，听凭法庭发落！
法庭姑且宽宥——就这一次！随即她的神情从顽皮骤然转为焦虑的郑重。
您的气色可不太好！她担心地看了一眼说。说实话，我觉得您比离开我们时更像*病人*了。我很怀疑，伦敦是不是对您不相宜？
也许是伦敦的空气，我说，也许是劳碌——也许是我过于孤单的日子：总之，近来我*一直*不大舒服。但埃尔夫斯顿很快会让我缓过来。

===Original===

Arthur’s prescription﻿—he’s my doctor, you know, and I heard from him this morning﻿—is ‘plenty of ozone, and new milk, and *pleasant society*’!”
“Pleasant society?” said Lady Muriel, with a pretty make-believe of considering the question. “Well, really I don’t know where we can find *that* for you! We have so few neighbours. But new milk we *can* manage. Do get it of my old friend Mrs. Hunter, up there, on the hillside. You may rely upon the *quality*. And her little Bessie comes to school every day, and passes your lodgings. So it would be very easy to send it.”
“I’ll follow your advice, with pleasure,” I said; “and I’ll go and arrange about it tomorrow. I know Arthur will want a walk.”
“You’ll find it quite an easy walk﻿—under three miles, I think.”
“Well, now that we’ve settled that point, let me retort your own remark upon yourself. I don’t think *you’re* looking quite your best!”
“I daresay not,” she replied in a low voice; and a sudden shadow seemed to overspread her face. “I’ve had some troubles lately. It’s a matter about which I’ve been long wishing to consult you, but I couldn’t easily write about it. I’m *so* glad to have this opportunity!”
“Do you think,” she began again, after a minute’s silence, and with a visible embarrassment of manner most unusual in her, “that a promise, deliberately and solemnly given, is *always* binding﻿—except, of course, where its fulfilment would involve some actual *sin*?”
“I can’t think of any other exception at this moment,” I said. “That branch of casuistry is usually, I believe, treated as a question of truth and untruth﻿—”
“Surely that *is* the principle?” she eagerly interrupted. “I always thought the Bible-teaching about it consisted of such texts as ‘*lie not one to another*’?”
“I have thought about that point,” I replied; “and it seems to me that the essence of *lying* is the intention of *deceiving*. If you give a promise, fully *intending* to fulfil it, you are certainly acting truthfully *then*; and, if you afterwards break it, that does not involve any *deception*. I cannot call it *untruthful*.”
Another pause of silence ensued. Lady Muriel’s face was hard to read: she looked pleased, I thought, but also puzzled; and I felt curious to know whether her question had, as I began to suspect, some bearing on the breaking off of her engagement with Captain (now Major) Lindon.

===Chinese===

亚瑟开的方子——您知道，他就是我的医生，我今天早上还收到他的信——是“多吸臭氧，喝鲜奶，外加*愉快的社交*”！
愉快的社交？穆丽尔小姐说，装出一副认真思索这问题的可爱模样。这个，实在不知道上哪儿给您找这个去！我们这儿邻里寥寥。不过鲜奶倒*办*得到。您就找我的老朋友亨特太太买吧，就在那边山坡上。*品质*您尽可放心。她家的小贝西每天上学，都打您的住处门前过。捎起奶来方便得很。
我乐于遵命，我说；明天我就去把这事安排妥当。我知道亚瑟正想散散步。
您会发现路很好走——我想，不到三英里。
好，这一点既已议定，容我把您的话回敬您自己。我看*您*的气色也不大好呢！
恐怕是吧，她低声答道；一片阴影似乎倏然罩上她的脸。我近来有些烦难事。这件事我早就想向您讨教，却又难以下笔。能有这个机会，真是*太*好了！
沉默了一分钟后她又开口，神情里带着一种她素来罕有的明显的局促：您认为，一个经过深思熟虑、郑重许下的承诺，是不是*永远*有约束力——当然，履行它会构成真正的*罪孽*时除外？
此刻我想不出别的例外，我说。决疑学的这一支，我相信，通常被当作真话与假话的问题来处理——
这不正是那条原则吗？她急切地打断我。我一向以为，圣经关于这问题的教导，尽在“不可彼此说谎”这一类经文里了？
这一点我想过，我答道；依我看，*说谎*的本质在于存心*欺骗*。许诺时若真心实意*打算*履行，那么在彼时彼刻，你无疑是在说真话；倘若事后失约，那并不包含任何*欺骗*。我不能称之为*不真*。
接着又是一阵沉默。穆丽尔小姐的脸色难以捉摸：我想，她看上去既高兴，又困惑；我开始怀疑，她的问题与她同林登上尉（如今已是少校）解除婚约一事有关，很想知道是否如此。

===Original===

“You have relieved me from a great fear,” she said; “but the thing is of course *wrong*, somehow. What texts would *you* quote, to prove it wrong?”
“Any that enforce the payment of *debts*. If *A* promises something to *B*, *B* has a claim upon *A*. And *A*’s sin, if he breaks his promise, seems to me more analogous to *stealing* than to *lying*.”
“It’s a new way of looking at it﻿—to me,” she said; “but it seems a *true* way, also. However, I won’t deal in generalities, with an old friend like you! For we *are* old friends, somehow. Do you know, I think we *began* as old friends?” she said with a playfulness of tone that ill accorded with the tears that glistened in her eyes.
“Thank you very much for saying so,” I replied. “I like to think of you as an *old* friend,” (“﻿—though you don’t look it!” would have been the almost necessary sequence, with any other lady; but she and I seemed to have long passed out of the time when compliments, or any such trivialities, were possible.)
Here the train paused at a station, where two or three passengers entered the carriage; so no more was said till we had reached our journey’s end.
On our arrival at Elveston, she readily adopted my suggestion that we should walk up together; so, as soon as our luggage had been duly taken charge of﻿—hers by the servant who met her at the station, and mine by one of the porters﻿—we set out together along the familiar lanes, now linked in my memory with so many delightful associations. Lady Muriel at once recommenced the conversation at the point where it had been interrupted.
“You knew of my engagement to my cousin Eric. Did you also hear﻿—”
“Yes,” I interrupted, anxious to spare her the pain of giving any details. “I heard it had all come to an end.”
“I would like to tell you how it happened,” she said; “as that is the very point I want your advice about. I had long realised that we were not in sympathy in religious belief. His ideas of Christianity are very shadowy; and even as to the existence of a God he lives in a sort of dreamland. But it has not affected his life! I feel sure, now, that the most absolute Atheist *may* be leading, though walking blindfold, a pure and noble life. And if you knew half the good deeds﻿—” she broke off suddenly, and turned away her head.
“I entirely agree with you,” I said.

===Chinese===

您为我卸下了一桩大恐惧，她说；可这件事总归是*错*的，不知怎么。您会引哪些经文，来证明它是错的呢？
凡敦促人偿还*债务*的都可以。倘若*甲*向*乙*许诺了什么，*乙*就对*甲*享有一份权利。*甲*若食言，其罪在我看来与其近于*说谎*，不如说更近于*偷窃*。
这看法对我倒是新鲜的，她说；不过似乎也是个*真切*的看法。可是，对您这样的老朋友，我不打算空谈大道理！因为我们到底*是*老朋友，说不上为什么。您知道吗，我觉得我们*一开始*就是老朋友？她说这话时语调轻快，却与她眼中闪动的泪光很不相称。
多谢您这样说，我答道。我乐于把您当作一位*老*朋友，（“——虽然您看上去一点也不老！”对任何别的女士，这话几乎是必不可少的下文；可她和我似乎早已过了能说恭维话、或任何这类虚文客套的时候。）
火车在一处车站停了下来，两三位乘客走进车厢；于是直到旅程终点，我们再没说什么。
到了埃尔夫斯顿，我提议一同步行前往，她欣然同意；行李各有妥当照管——她的交给到站迎接的仆人，我的交给一位搬运工——我们便沿着熟悉的巷子一路同行，这些巷子如今在我的记忆里系着多少愉快的联想。穆丽尔小姐立刻从方才打断的地方接着谈了下去。
您知道我与我表兄埃里克订过婚。您可也听说——
是的，我打断她，急欲免她说出细节的苦楚。我听说一切都已告终。
我想告诉您事情的原委，她说；因为这正是我要向您讨教的地方。我早就明白，我们在宗教信仰上并不投契。他对基督教的观念十分朦胧；甚至对神是否存在，他也活在一种梦境里。可这并没有影响他的为人！如今我确信，最彻底的无神论者也*可能*过着——虽是蒙着眼行走——一种纯洁高尚的生活。况且，只要您知道他行过的一半善事——她突然收住话头，把脸扭向一旁。
我完全同意您，我说。况且，救主亲口应许过，这样的生命终必走向光明，不是吗？

===Original===

“And have we not our Saviour’s own promise that such a life shall surely lead to the light?”
“Yes, I know it,” she said in a broken voice, still keeping her head turned away. “And so I told him. He said he would believe, for *my* sake, if he could. And he wished, for *my* sake, he could see things as I did. But that is all wrong!” she went on passionately. “God *cannot* approve such low motives as that! Still it was not *I* that broke it off. I knew he loved me; and I had *promised*; and﻿—”
“Then it was *he* that broke it off?”
“He released me unconditionally.” She faced me again now, having quite recovered her usual calmness of manner.
“Then what difficulty remains?”
“It is *this*, that I don’t believe he did it of his own free will. Now, supposing he did it *against* his will, merely to satisfy my scruples, would not his claim on me remain just as strong as ever? And would not my promise be as binding as ever? My father says ‘no’; but I can’t help fearing he is biased by his love for me. And I’ve asked no one else. I have many friends﻿—friends for the bright sunny weather; not friends for the clouds and storms of life; not *old* friends like you!”
“Let me think a little,” I said: and for some minutes we walked on in silence, while, pained to the heart at seeing the bitter trial that had come upon this pure and gentle soul, I strove in vain to see my way through the tangled skein of conflicting motives.
“If she loves him truly,” (I seemed at last to grasp the clue to the problem) “is not *that*, for her, the voice of God? May she not hope that she is sent to him, even as Ananias was sent to Saul in his blindness, that he may receive his sight?” Once more I seemed to hear Arthur whispering “*What knowest thou, O wife, whether thou shalt save thy husband?*” and I broke the silence with the words “If you still love him truly﻿—”
“I do *not*!” she hastily interrupted. “At least﻿—not in *that* way. I *believe* I loved him when I promised; but I was very young: it is hard to know. But, whatever the feeling was, it is dead *now*. The motive on *his* side is Love: on *mine* it is﻿—Duty!”
Again there was a long silence. The whole skein of thought was tangled worse than ever. This time *she* broke the silence. “Don’t misunderstand me!” she said. “When I said my heart was not *his*, I did not mean it was anyone else’s!

===Chinese===

是的，我知道，她声音哽咽地说，头依然扭向一旁。我也是这么对他说的。他说，若能做到，他愿为*我*而信。他说，但愿为*我*之故，他能同我一样看事物。可这全错了！她激动地接着说。神*断不能*称许那样低的动机！不过，破坏婚约的并不是*我*。我知道他爱我；我也*许诺*过；而且——
那么，是*他*解除的婚约？
他无条件地放了我。这时她又面向我，神态已完全恢复平日的沉静。
那么还有什么为难之处呢？
难就难在，我不相信他是出于自愿。喏，倘若他是*违*心这样做的，只为宽解我的顾忌，那么他对我的权利，岂不依旧一如既往？我的承诺岂不依旧有约束力？我父亲说“不”；可我不由得担心，他对我的偏爱让他偏了心。旁的人我谁也没问过。我有许多朋友——都是晴日丽天的朋友；不是人生风雨阴霾里的朋友；不是像您这样的*老*朋友！
容我想一想，我说：此后几分钟我们默默走着，我眼见这桩苦涩的试炼降临在这样纯洁温柔的灵魂上，心痛已极，却徒然在那团乱麻般相互缠绕的动机里寻不出一条路来。
倘若她真正爱他，（我似乎终于抓住了这难题的头绪）这*岂不*就是神对她的声音？她岂不可望自己之受遣于他，正如亚拿尼亚受遣往失明的扫罗那里去，好叫他重见光明？我仿佛又一次听见亚瑟低声说：“*你这作妻子的，怎么知道不能救你的丈夫呢？*”于是我打破沉默，说道：倘若您至今还真正爱他——
我*不*爱！她急忙打断我。至少——不是*那种*爱。我*相信*许诺时我是爱他的；可那时我太年轻：实在难说。但无论那份感情是什么，如今它已经死了。*他*那边的动机是爱：*我*这边却是——责任！
又是一场长久的沉默。思绪的乱麻缠得比先前更紧了。这回是*她*打破沉默。别误会我！她说。我说我的心不属于*他*，并没有说它属于任何别的人！

===Original===

At present I feel bound to *him*; and, till I know I am absolutely free, in the sight of God, to love any other than him, I’ll never even *think* of anyone else﻿—in *that* way, I mean. I would die sooner!” I had never imagined my gentle friend capable of such passionate utterances.
I ventured on no further remark until we had nearly arrived at the Hall-gate; but, the longer I reflected, the clearer it became to me that no call of Duty demanded the sacrifice﻿—possibly of the happiness of a life﻿—which she seemed ready to make. I tried to make this clear to *her* also, adding some warnings on the dangers that surely awaited a union in which mutual love was wanting. “The only argument for it, worth considering,” I said in conclusion, “seems to be his supposed *reluctance* in releasing you from your promise. I have tried to give to that argument its *full* weight, and my conclusion is that it does *not* affect the rights of the case, or invalidate the release he has given you. My belief is that you are *entirely* free to act as *now* seems right.”
“I am *very* grateful to you,” she said earnestly. “Believe it, please! I can’t put it into proper words!” and the subject was dropped by mutual consent: and I only learned, long afterwards, that our discussion had really served to dispel the doubts that had harassed her so long.
We parted at the Hall-gate, and I found Arthur eagerly awaiting my arrival; and, before we parted for the night, I had heard the whole story﻿—how he had put off his journey from day to day, feeling that he *could* not go away from the place till his fate had been irrevocably settled by the wedding taking place: how the preparations for the wedding, and the excitement in the neighbourhood, had suddenly come to an end, and he had learned (from Major Lindon, who called to wish him goodbye) that the engagement had been broken off by mutual consent: how he had instantly abandoned all his plans for going abroad, and had decided to stay on at Elveston, for a year or two at any rate, till his newly-awakened hopes should prove true or false; and how, since that memorable day, he had avoided all meetings with Lady Muriel, fearing to betray his feelings before he had had any sufficient evidence as to how she regarded him.

===Chinese===

眼下我觉得自己同*他*拴在一起；而且，在确知自己在神眼中已绝对自由、可以爱他之外的任何人之前，我连*想*都绝不会去想别人——我是说，往*那种*意义上想。我宁死不肯！我从未想到我这位温柔的朋友能说出这样激烈的话。
直到我们快到府邸门前，我再没敢多说一句；可是我越想越明白：无论责任的哪一种召唤，都不曾要求她做出她似乎准备做出的牺牲——那可能是牺牲一生的幸福。我也努力向*她*说明这一点，并告诫她，缺乏相互爱情的结合，必有大危险在等候。总结起来我说：此事值得一提的理由，只有一条，就是他解除您的承诺时想必*不甚情愿*。我已尽力给这理由以*十足*的分量，而我的结论是：它并*不*影响这桩事情的是非，也不使他已经给您的解除失效。我相信，您*完全*自由，尽可照*如今*看来正当的去做。
我*万分*感激您，她恳切地说。请相信这一点！我找不出妥帖的话来表达了！这话题便由双方默契地放下了：直到很久以后我才知道，我们那番讨论，当真驱散了缠扰她许久的疑虑。
我们在府邸门前分手，我发现亚瑟正热切地等我到来；当夜分手之前，我已听完了全部原委——他如何一天又一天推迟行程，觉得在自己的命运随着婚礼的举行而尘埃落定之前，他*决*不能离开这个地方：婚礼的筹备和四邻的轰动如何突然戛然而止，他如何（从前来道别的林登少校口中）得知婚约已由双方同意解除：他如何当即放弃了出国的全部计划，决定继续留在埃尔夫斯顿——至少一两年——直到他新萌的希望被证明是真是假；还有，自那难忘的一天起，他如何回避与穆丽尔小姐的一切相见，唯恐在自己尚未充分确知她的心意之前，便泄露了自己的感情。

===Original===

“But it is nearly six weeks since all that happened,” he said in conclusion, “and we can meet in the ordinary way, now, with no need for any painful allusions. I would have written to tell you all this: only I kept hoping from day to day, that﻿—that there would be *more* to tell!”
“And how should there be *more*, you foolish fellow,” I fondly urged, “if you never even go near her? Do you expect the offer to come from *her*?”
Arthur was betrayed into a smile. “No,” he said, “I hardly expect *that*. But I’m a desperate coward. There’s no doubt about it!”
“And what *reasons* have you heard of for breaking off the engagement?”
“A good many,” Arthur replied, and proceeded to count them on his fingers. “First, it was found that she was dying of﻿—something; so *he* broke it off. Then it was found that *he* was dying of﻿—some other thing; so *she* broke it off. Then the Major turned out to be a confirmed gamester; so the *Earl* broke it off. Then the Earl insulted him; so the *Major* broke it off. It got a good deal broken off, all things considered!”
“You have all this on the very best authority, of course?”
“Oh, certainly! And communicated in the strictest confidence! Whatever defects Elveston society suffers from, *want of information* isn’t one of them!”
“Nor *reticence*, either, it seems. But, seriously, do you know the real reason?”
“No, I’m quite in the dark.”
I did not feel that I had any right to enlighten him; so I changed the subject, to the less engrossing one of “new milk,” and we agreed that I should walk over, next day, to Hunter’s farm, Arthur undertaking to set me part of the way, after which he had to return to keep a business-engagement.

===Chinese===

不过，那一切到如今已过去将近六个星期了，他最后说道，现在我们可以按平常的方式相见，无须作任何令人难过的提起了。这些我本想写信告诉您：只是我一天天指望——指望会有*更多*可讲的！
哪来的*更多*呢，你这傻家伙，我慈爱地劝他，你连她的边都不去沾？你总不至于指望先由*她*开口吧？
亚瑟不由得笑了。不，他说，*这*我倒不指望。可我是个不折不扣的胆小鬼。这没什么可怀疑的！
那解除婚约的*缘由*，你都听说些什么？
听过好些呢，亚瑟答道，随即扳着指头数起来。起初，说是她得了——什么绝症，快死了；于是*他*解除了婚约。接着，又说是*他*得了——另一种什么病，快死了；于是*她*解除了婚约。后来，又查出少校是个积习难改的赌棍；于是*伯爵*解除了婚约。再后来，伯爵侮辱了他；于是*少校*解除了婚约。通盘算下来，这婚约还真解除得不少！
这些你自然都得自最可靠的权威吧？
哦，那当然！而且是以最严格的机密相告的！埃尔夫斯顿社交界的毛病无论有多少，*消息不灵通*可算不上一条！
看来*口风不严*也算不上，我又说。不过说正经的，真实的原因你可知道？
不知道，我完全蒙在鼓里。
我觉得自己无权向他点破；便把话头岔开，转到“鲜奶”这个不那么揪心的话题上。我们商定，第二天我步行去亨特农场，亚瑟答应陪我走上一程，之后他还得回去赴一个事务上的约。

## III Streaks of Dawn / 三 曙光初露
===Original===

Next day proved warm and sunny, and we started early, to enjoy the luxury of a good long chat before he would be obliged to leave me.
“This neighbourhood has more than its due proportion of the *very* poor,” I remarked, as we passed a group of hovels, too dilapidated to deserve the name of “cottages.”
“But the few rich,” Arthur replied, “give more than their due proportion of help in charity. So the balance is kept.”
“I suppose the *Earl* does a good deal?”
“He *gives* liberally; but he has not the health or strength to do more. Lady Muriel does more in the way of school-teaching and cottage-visiting than she would like me to reveal.”
“Then *she*, at least, is not one of the ‘idle mouths’ one so often meets with among the upper classes. I have sometimes thought they would have a hard time of it, if suddenly called on to give their raison d’être, and to show cause why they should be allowed to live any longer!”
“The whole subject,” said Arthur, “of what we may call ‘idle mouths’ (I mean persons who absorb some of the material *wealth* of a community﻿—in the form of food, clothes, and so on﻿—without contributing its equivalent in the form of productive *labour*) is a complicated one, no doubt. I’ve tried to think it out. And it seemed to me that the simplest form of the problem, to start with, is a community without *money*, who buy and sell by *barter* only; and it makes it yet simpler to suppose the food and other things to be capable of *keeping* for many years without spoiling.”
“Yours is an excellent plan,” I said. “What is your solution of the problem?”
“The commonest type of ‘idle mouths,’ ” said Arthur, “is no doubt due to money being left by parents to their own children. So I imagined a man﻿—either exceptionally clever, or exceptionally strong and industrious﻿—who had contributed so much valuable labour to the needs of the community that its equivalent, in clothes, etc., was (say) five times as much as he needed for himself. We cannot deny his *absolute* right to give the superfluous wealth as he chooses. So, if he leaves *four* children behind him (say two sons and two daughters), with enough of all the necessaries of life to last them a lifetime, I cannot see that the *community* is in any way wronged if they choose to do nothing in life but to ‘eat, drink, and be merry.’

===Chinese===

第二天天气暖和晴朗，我们一早便动身，好趁他不得不离开我之前，享受一场畅谈的奢侈。
“这一带*赤贫*之人的比例，远超了应有的份额。”经过一片破烂得连“村舍”二字都担当不起的窝棚时，我说道。
“可那寥寥的富人，”亚瑟答道，“在慈善上出的力，也远超了他们应有的份额。天平就这样摆平了。”
“我想*伯爵*出力不少吧？”
“他*捐*得慷慨；只是健康和体力都不容他做得更多。穆丽尔小姐在教书授课、走访农户上做的事，比她愿意让我透露的还要多。”
“那么至少*她*不是上层阶级里常见的那种‘吃闲饭的嘴’。我有时想，若是突然要他们交代自己存在的理由（raison d'être），说明凭什么还许他们活下去，他们可就要日子难过了！”
“‘吃闲饭的嘴’（我是指这样的人：他们取用社会物质*财富*的一部分——食物、衣物等等——却不以生产性*劳动*付出等价的贡献）这个题目，”亚瑟说，“无疑相当复杂。我曾试着把它想个通透。依我看，这问题最简单的形式，不妨从这样一个社会入手：没有*货币*，买卖全凭*以物易物*；倘若再假设食物之类可以存放多年不坏，问题便愈发简单了。”
“你这方案妙极了，”我说，“那么你的解法是什么？”
“‘吃闲饭的嘴’最常见的类型，”亚瑟说，“无疑源于父母把钱产留给亲生儿女。于是我设想一个人——或聪明绝顶，或体魄过人、勤勉过人——他为社会所需付出的宝贵劳动如此之多，以致其等价物，衣物等等，且算作他自己所需的五倍。他愿把这份多余财富随意赠谁，这份*绝对*权利我们无从否认。那么，倘若他身后留下*四*个儿女（就说是两儿两女），留给他们的生活必需品尽够一世受用，我便看不出*社会*受了什么委屈——哪怕他们一辈子偏要‘吃吃喝喝，及时行乐’。”

===Original===

Most certainly, the community could not fairly say, in reference to *them*, ‘*if a man will not work, neither let him eat*.’ Their reply would be crushing. ‘The labour has already been *done*, which is a fair equivalent for the food we are eating; and you have had the benefit of it. On what principle of justice can you demand *two* quotas of work for *one* quota of food?’ ”
“Yet surely,” I said, “there is something wrong *somewhere*, if these four people are well able to do useful work, and if that work is actually *needed* by the community, and they elect to sit idle?”
“I think there *is*,” said Arthur: “but it seems to me to arise from a Law of God﻿—that everyone shall do as much as he can to help others﻿—and not from any *rights*, on the part of the community, to exact labour as an equivalent for food that has already been fairly earned.”
“I suppose the *second* form of the problem is where the ‘idle mouths’ possess *money* instead of *material* wealth?”
“Yes,” replied Arthur: “and I think the simplest case is that of *paper*-money. *Gold* is itself a form of material wealth; but a banknote is merely a *promise* to hand over so much *material* wealth when called upon to do so. The father of these four ‘idle mouths,’ had done (let us say) five thousand pounds’ worth of useful work for the community. In return for this, the community had given him what amounted to a written promise to hand over, whenever called upon to do so, five thousand pounds’ worth of food, etc. Then, if he only uses *one* thousand pounds’ worth himself, and leaves the rest of the notes to his children, surely they have a full right to *present* these written promises, and to say ‘hand over the food, for which the equivalent labour has been already done.’ Now I think *this* case well worth stating, publicly and clearly. I should like to drive it into the heads of those Socialists who are priming our ignorant paupers with such sentiments as ‘Look at them bloated haristocrats! Doing not a stroke o’ work for theirselves, and living on the sweat of *our* brows!’ I should like to *force* them to see that the *money*, which those ‘haristocrats’ are spending, represents so much labour *already done* for the community, and whose equivalent, in *material* wealth, is *due from the community*.”
“Might not the Socialists reply, ‘Much of this money does not represent *honest* labour *at all*.

===Chinese===

当然，对*他们*，社会无法公道地说出‘*不肯做工的，也不可让他吃饭*’这样的话。他们的回答将是压倒性的。‘我们所吃粮食的等价劳动*早已*做完；好处你们已经领了。凭哪一条公义的原则，你们能拿*一*份口粮，索*两*份工？’”
“然而，”我说，“倘若这四个人明明做得来有用的工作，那工作又确实为社会所*需*，他们却甘愿闲坐，那总该是*哪儿*出了岔子吧？”
“我想是出了岔子，”亚瑟说：“但依我看，毛病出自上帝的一条律法——人人当竭尽所能帮助他人——而不是社会拥有什么*权利*，可以对公道挣来的口粮强索劳动作抵。”
“我猜问题的*第二*种形式，是‘吃闲饭的嘴’手里握的是*钱*，而非*物质*财富？”
“正是，”亚瑟答道：“而最简单的情形，我想是*纸*币。*黄金*本身就是物质财富的一种；钞票却不过是*一纸承诺*：见票即付若干*物质*财富。这四个‘吃闲饭的嘴’的父亲，曾为社会做过（姑且算作）五千英镑的有用工作。作为回报，社会给了他一样等于书面承诺的东西：何时索取，何时便交付五千英镑价值的粮食等等。那么，倘若他自己只用了*一*千英镑之数，余下的钞票留给儿女，他们无疑有充分的权利*出示*这些书面承诺，说‘把粮食交出来，等价的劳动早已做过’。我认为*这*个道理很值得公之于众、讲个明白。那些社会主义者正拿这类话给我们无知的贫民灌迷汤——‘瞧瞧那些脑满肠肥的“贵主”老爷们！自个儿一丁点儿活不干，全靠俺们眉毛上的汗过活！’——我真想把道理钉进这些人脑袋里。我要*迫使他们*看见：那些‘老爷们’花的*钱*，代表的是早已为社会*做过*的劳动，其*物质*财富的等价物，是*社会欠下的*。”
“社会主义者岂不是可以回一句：‘这钱里头，有许多压根儿不代表*老实的*劳动。”

===Original===

If you could trace it back, from owner to owner, though you might begin with several legitimate steps, such as gift, or bequeathing by will, or “value received,” you would soon reach an owner who had no moral right to it, but had got it by fraud or other crimes; and of course his successors in the line would have no better right to it than *he* had.’ ”
“No doubt, no doubt,” Arthur replied. “But surely that involves the logical fallacy of *proving too much*? It is *quite* as applicable to *material* wealth, as it is to *money*. If we once begin to go back beyond the fact that the *present* owner of certain property came by it honestly, and to ask whether any previous owner, in past ages, got it by fraud, would *any* property be secure?”
After a minute’s thought, I felt obliged to admit the truth of this.
“My general conclusion,” Arthur continued, “from the mere standpoint of human *rights*, man against man, was this﻿—that if some wealthy ‘idle mouth,’ who has come by his money in a lawful way, even though not one atom of the labour it represents has been his own doing, chooses to spend it on his own needs, without contributing any labour to the community from whom he buys his food and clothes, that community has no *right* to interfere with him. But it’s quite another thing, when we come to consider the *divine* law. Measured by *that* standard, such a man is undoubtedly doing wrong, if he fails to use, for the good of those in need, the strength or the skill, that God has given him. That strength and skill do *not* belong to the community, to be paid to *them* as a *debt*: they do *not* belong to the man *himself*, to be used for his *own* enjoyment: they *do* belong to God, to be used according to *His* will; and we are not left in doubt as to what that will is. ‘*Do good, and lend, hoping for nothing again.*’ ”
“Anyhow,” I said, “an ‘idle mouth’ very often gives away a great deal in charity.”
“In *so-called* ‘charity,’ ” he corrected me. “Excuse me if I seem to speak *un*charitably. I would not dream of *applying* the term to any *individual*. But I would say, *generally*, that a man who gratifies every fancy that occurs to him﻿—denying himself in *nothing*﻿—and merely gives to the poor some part, or even *all*, of his *superfluous* wealth, is only deceiving himself if he calls it *charity*.”

===Chinese===

你若能顺着一任任物主往上追溯，纵然起头几步或许名正言顺——或馈赠，或遗嘱相遗，或“对价收讫”——你很快便会追到一任物主，他对它并无道义上的权利，而是凭欺诈或别的罪行到手；当然，这条链上的后继者，权利也决不比*他*更硬。’”
“的确，的确，”亚瑟答道。“可这岂不犯了‘证明过当’的逻辑谬误？这一套用在*物质*财富上，同用在*钱*上*完全*一样使得。一旦我们越过‘某项财产的*现今*主人来路清白’这一事实，去追问前代哪个主人是否凭欺诈得手，那天下还有*什么*财产是稳妥的？”
思索了一分钟，我只得承认这话在理。
