“单单从人*权*的立场——人对人——来看，我的总结论是这样，”亚瑟继续道：“某个阔气的‘吃闲饭的嘴’，钱财来路合法，哪怕它所代表的劳动没有一星半点出诸他手，只要他愿意把钱花在自己身上，不为卖他衣食的社会贡献半分劳动，社会也无*权*干涉。可一谈到*神*圣律法，便是另一回事了。拿*那*把尺子量，这样的人无疑在作恶——倘若他不肯把上帝赐他的气力或才干，用于困苦之人的益处。那气力和才干*不*属于社会，不欠*他们*的*债*；也*不*属于他*本人*，不归他*自己*享用；它们*是*属于上帝的，当遵*祂*的旨意使用；而那旨意为何，我们并无疑惑：‘*行善，借贷，不指望偿还。*’”
“不管怎样，”我说，“‘吃闲饭的嘴’往往也施舍不少呢。”
“是*所谓的*‘慈善’吧，”他纠正我。“若我显得*没有*爱心，还请包涵。我做梦也不肯把这字眼扣到哪个*个人*头上。但*大体*而论，我要说：一个人随心所欲、样样都依着自己——于己*毫无*克扣——只把*多余*财富的一部分、乃至*全部*施与穷人，还管这叫*慈善*，那不过是自欺。”

===Original===

“But, even in giving away *superfluous* wealth, he *may* be denying himself the miser’s pleasure in hoarding?”
“I grant you that, gladly,” said Arthur. “Given that he *has* that morbid craving, he is doing a good deed in restraining it.”
“But, even in spending on *himself*,” I persisted, “our typical rich man often does good, by employing people who would otherwise be out of work: and that is often better than pauperising them by *giving* the money.”
“I’m glad you’ve said that!” said Arthur. “I would not like to quit the subject without exposing the *two* fallacies of that statement﻿—which have gone so long uncontradicted that Society now accepts it as an axiom!”
“What are they?” I said. “I don’t even see *one*, myself.”
“One is merely the fallacy of *ambiguity*﻿—the assumption that ‘*doing good*’ (that is, benefiting somebody) is necessarily *a good thing to do* (that is, a *right* thing). The other is the assumption that, if one of two specified acts is *better* than another, it is necessarily a *good* act in itself. I should like to call this the fallacy of *comparison*﻿—meaning that it assumes that what is *comparatively* good is therefore *positively* good.”
“Then what is *your* test of a good act?”
“That it shall be *our best*,” Arthur confidently replied. “And even *then* ‘*we are unprofitable servants*.’ But let me illustrate the two fallacies. Nothing illustrates a fallacy so well as an extreme case, which fairly comes under it. Suppose I find two children drowning in a pond. I rush in, and save one of the children, and then walk away, leaving the other to drown. Clearly I have ‘*done good*,’ in saving a child’s life? But﻿—. Again, supposing I meet an inoffensive stranger, and knock him down, and walk on. Clearly that is ‘*better*’ than if I had proceeded to jump upon him and break his ribs? But﻿—”
“Those ‘buts’ are quite unanswerable,” I said. “But I should like an instance from *real* life.”
“Well, let us take one of those abominations of modern Society, a Charity-Bazaar. It’s an interesting question to think out﻿—how much of the money, that reaches the object in view, is *genuine* charity; and whether even *that* is spent in the *best* way. But the subject needs regular classification, and analysis, to understand it properly.”
“I should be glad to *have* it analysed,” I said: “it has often puzzled me.”
“Well, if I am really not boring you.

===Chinese===

“可就算散去的是*多余*财富，他*或许*也克制了守财奴囤积的乐子？”
“这一点，我欣然认了，”亚瑟说。“既然他*有*那份病态的贪瘾，能按住它，便是善举。”
“可就算花在*他自己*身上，”我坚持道，“我们这位典型的富人也常常行了善——雇用了本要失业的人；这往往胜过把钱*白送*，养出他们的贫民习气。”
“亏得你说出这话！”亚瑟道。“不揭穿这句话里的*两*重谬误，我不愿就此撇下这题目——它们旷日持久无人驳斥，如今社会竟奉为公理了！”
“哪两重？”我说。“我自己连*一*重也没瞧出来。”
“一重不过是*含混*谬误——以为‘*与人以惠*’（即让人得了好处）就必然*是件该做的好事*（即*正当*的事）。另一重是以为：指定的两事之中，一件既比另一件*较好*，它自身便必然是*好*事。我愿称之为*比较*谬误——即把*相对*的好，当作了*绝对*的好。”
“那么，*你*检验善行的标准是什么？”
“标准是：它须是*我们所能拿出的最好*，”亚瑟胸有成竹地答道。“即便如此，‘*我们是无用的仆人*。’且让我把这两重谬误各举一例。极端的例子最能说明谬误，只要它确在谬误的涵盖之内。假设我看见两个孩子溺在池中，我冲进去救起一个，随即转身走开，任另一个淹死。救下一命，我显然‘*与人以惠*’了吧？可是——。再假设我遇上一个无害的生人，一拳撂倒他，扬长而去。这显然比接着跳上去踩断他的肋骨要‘*好*’吧？可是——”
“这两个‘可是’，实在无从招架，”我说。“可我想要个*真实*生活的例子。”
“好吧，那就拿现代社会的一桩可憎之物——慈善义卖会来说。有个问题颇堪玩味——送抵目标之手的钱里，有多少算*真正的*慈善；就连这笔钱，是否又花在了*最好*的去处。只是这题目须正经分类、条分缕析，方能看得透彻。”
“我很乐意*听*你剖析，”我说：“它常叫我纳闷。”
“好吧，只要你真不嫌我啰嗦。”

===Original===

Let us suppose our Charity-Bazaar to have been organised to aid the funds of some Hospital: and that A, B, C *give* their services in making articles to sell, and in acting as salesmen, while X, Y, Z buy the articles, and the money so paid goes to the Hospital.
“There are two distinct species of such Bazaars: one, where the payment exacted is merely the *market-value* of the goods supplied, that is, exactly what you would have to pay at a shop: the other, where *fancy-prices* are asked. We must take these separately.
“First, the ‘market-value’ case. Here A, B, C are exactly in the same position as ordinary shopkeepers; the only difference being that they give the proceeds to the Hospital. Practically, they are *giving their skilled labour* for the benefit of the Hospital. This seems to me to be genuine charity. And I don’t see how they could use it better. But X, Y, Z, are exactly in the same position as any ordinary purchasers of goods. To talk of ‘charity’ in connection with *their* share of the business, is sheer nonsense. Yet they are very likely to do so.
“Secondly, the case of ‘fancy-prices.’ Here I think the simplest plan is to divide the payment into two parts, the ‘market-value’ and the excess over that. The ‘market-value’ part is on the same footing as in the first case: the *excess* is all we have to consider. Well, A, B, C do not *earn* it; so we may put *them* out of the question: it is a *gift*, from X, Y, Z, to the Hospital. And my opinion is that it is not given in the best way: far better buy what they choose to *buy*, and give what they choose to *give*, as two *separate* transactions: then there is *some* chance that their motive in giving may be real charity, instead of a mixed motive﻿—half charity, half self-pleasing. ‘The trail of the serpent is over it all.’ And *therefore* it is that I hold all such spurious ‘Charities’ in *utter* abomination!” He ended with unusual energy, and savagely beheaded, with his stick, a tall thistle at the roadside, behind which I was startled to see Sylvie and Bruno standing. I caught at his arm, but too late to stop him. Whether the stick reached them, or not, I could not feel sure: at any rate they took not the smallest notice of it, but smiled gaily, and nodded to me; and I saw at once that they were only visible to *me*: the “eerie” influence had not reached to *Arthur*.
“Why did you try to save it?”

===Chinese===

不妨假设我们的慈善义卖会是为资助某家医院的经费而办：甲、乙、丙*义务*制作售卖品、充任店员；子、丑、寅购买货品，所付之钱归于医院。
“这类义卖会分明有两属：一属，索取的不过是所供货物的*市价*，即在铺子里购物要付的那份钱：另一属，则讨*花头价*。两属得分开来看。
“先说‘市价’一属。此处甲、乙、丙与寻常店主处境全然相同；唯一分别是收益捐给了医院。实际上，他们是把自己的*熟稔劳作*捐给了医院。依我看，这是真正的慈善，而且我想不出更好的用法。可子、丑、寅呢，与寻常买主毫无二致。要把‘慈善’二字安到*他们*那一份上，纯属瞎说。可他们多半真会这么安。
“再说‘花头价’一属。此处最简便之法，是把付款一分为二：‘市价’，及超出市价之数。‘市价’那份，与第一属同理：须斟酌的只是*超出*之数。那么，甲、乙、丙并不*挣*它，*他们*可以撇开不论：这是子、丑、寅予医院的*馈赠*。而我的见解是，这赠予的方式并不最佳：远不如想*买*什么便买什么，愿*赠*多少便赠多少，分作*两笔*交易——这样，他们捐赠的动机才有*几分*可能是纯粹的慈善，而非一半慈善、一半自娱的杂拌动机。‘蛇的踪迹爬满其上。’正因如此，我才把一切这般假冒的‘慈善’恨入*骨髓*！”他收尾时一反常态地激昂，抡起手杖狠狠斩落路边一株高蓟——蓟后竟立着西尔维和布鲁诺，唬了我一跳。我伸手去挽他的臂，已然迟了。手杖究竟及没及着他们，我无从确知：反正他们毫不在意，只朝我欢快地微笑、点头；我当即明白，他们唯*我*得见：那“灵异”之感未及于*亚瑟*。

===Original===

he said. “*That’s* not the wheedling Secretary of a Charity-Bazaar! I only wish it were!” he added grimly.
“Doos oo know, that stick went right froo my head!” said Bruno. (They had run round to me by this time, and each had secured a hand.) “Just under my chin! I *are* glad I aren’t a thistle!”
“Well, we’ve threshed *that* subject out, anyhow!” Arthur resumed. “I’m afraid I’ve been talking too much, for *your* patience and for my strength. I must be turning soon. This is about the end of my tether.”

> “Take, O boatman, thrice thy fee;
> Take, I give it willingly;
> For, invisible to thee,
> Spirits twain have crossed with me!”

===Chinese===

他说。“那可不是慈善义卖会里千哄万哄的干事！我倒巴不得它是！”他冷冷地补了一句。
“咚可知道，那根棍儿正好从俺脑袋瓜里穿过去啦！”布鲁诺说。（这时他们俩已绕到我身旁，一人攥了我一只手。）“就打俺下巴底下过！俺*真*高兴俺不是棵蓟草！”
“好啦，*那*个题目总算嚼个透了！”亚瑟续道。“只怕我话讲太多，费了*你*的耐心，也费了我的气力。我得赶紧回头了。我这气力差不多使到头了。

> “收下吧，船夫，三倍的渡资；
> 收下吧，我心甘情愿；
> 因为，你看不见，
> 两个精灵随我渡了河！”

===Original===

I quoted, involuntarily.
“For utterly inappropriate and irrelevant quotations,” laughed Arthur, “you are ‘ekalled by few, and excelled by none’!” And we strolled on.
As we passed the head of the lane that led down to the beach, I noticed a single figure, moving slowly along it, seawards. She was a good way off, and had her back to us: but it was Lady Muriel, unmistakably. Knowing that Arthur had not seen her, as he had been looking, in the other direction, at a gathering rain-cloud, I made no remark, but tried to think of some plausible pretext for sending him back by the sea.
The opportunity instantly presented itself. “I’m getting tired,” he said. “I don’t think it would be prudent to go further. I had better turn here.”
I turned with him, for a few steps, and as we again approached the head of the lane, I said, as carelessly as I could, “Don’t go back by the road. It’s too hot and dusty. Down this lane, and along the beach, is nearly as short; and you’ll get a breeze off the sea.”
“Yes, I think I will,” Arthur began; but at that moment we came into sight of Lady Muriel, and he checked himself. “No, it’s too far round. Yet it certainly *would* be cooler﻿—” He stood, hesitating, looking first one way and then the other﻿—a melancholy picture of utter infirmity of purpose!
How long this humiliating scene would have continued, if *I* had been the only external influence, it is impossible to say; for at this moment Sylvie, with a swift decision worthy of Napoleon himself, took the matter into her own hands. “You go and drive *her*, up this way,” she said to Bruno. “I’ll get *him* along!” And she took hold of the stick that Arthur was carrying, and gently pulled him down the lane.
He was totally unconscious that any will but his own was acting on the stick, and appeared to think it had taken a horizontal position simply because he was pointing with it. “Are not those *orchises* under the hedge there?” he said. “I think that decides me. I’ll gather some as I go along.”
Meanwhile Bruno had run on beyond Lady Muriel, and, with much jumping about and shouting (shouts audible to no one but Sylvie and myself), much as if he were driving sheep, he managed to turn her round and make her walk, with eyes demurely cast upon the ground, in our direction.
The victory was ours!

===Chinese===

我不由自主地吟出了口。
“论引诗引得全然文不对题、风马牛不相及，”亚瑟笑道，“‘你可谓少有人及、更无人超’！”于是我们继续闲步。
行经那条通往海滩的小径径口时，我瞥见一个人影，正独自沿径缓缓向海而去。她隔得老远，又背对我们：可那分明是穆丽尔小姐。我知道亚瑟不曾见她——他正向另一边张望一团聚拢的雨云——我便一声不响，只想寻个体面的由头，让他取道海边回去。
机会旋即自至。“我乏了，”他说。“再走下去怕是不审慎。不如就在此处折返。”
我陪他转回走了几步，待我们再度走近径口，我装作漫不经心地说：“别打大路回去。又热又尘。下这条小径，沿海滩走，远近差不多，还能兜着海风。”
“好，就这么走。”亚瑟刚起话头，却在此时望见了穆丽尔小姐，话便咽了回去。“不，绕得太远。不过凉快倒是*真*会凉快些——”他立在当地，踌躇不决，一时望这头，一时望那头——一幅优柔寡断到家的愁人画！
若说*我*是唯一的外力，这难堪的场面还要僵多久，无从知晓；因为此时西尔维以堪与拿破仑本人媲美的迅捷果决，把事揽到了自己手里。“你去把*她*往这边赶，”她对布鲁诺说，“*他*交给我搞定！”说罢便握住亚瑟携着的手杖，轻轻把他往小径里引。
他浑然不觉手杖上还作用着自家意志以外的意志，只道它横了过来，是因为自己正拿它指点方向。“那边树篱底下的可是*兰花*？”他说。“我想这下定了。一路走一路采几枝。”
另一头，布鲁诺早已越过穆丽尔小姐跑上前去，又蹦又跳、大呼小叫（那叫声唯西尔维与我听得见），活脱脱一副赶羊架势，竟真让她转过身来，垂目敛神、娴静地望着地面，朝我们这边走来。
胜利属于我们！

===Original===

And, since it was evident that the lovers, thus urged together, *must* meet in another minute, I turned and walked on, hoping that Sylvie and Bruno would follow my example, as I felt sure that the fewer the spectators the better it would be for Arthur and his good angel.
“And what sort of meeting was it?” I wondered, as I paced dreamily on.

===Chinese===

这对恋人被如此催到一处，显然再过一分钟*必定*碰面，我便转身自去，但愿西尔维和布鲁诺也学我的样——我深信，围观者越少，于亚瑟和他的好天使越是有利。
“那将是怎样一场相逢呢？”我梦悠悠踱着步，暗自寻思。

## IV The Dog-King / IV 狗中之王
===Original===

“They shooked hands,” said Bruno, who was trotting at my side, in answer to the unspoken question.
“And they looked *ever* so pleased!” Sylvie added from the other side.
“Well, we must get on, now, as quick as we can,” I said. “If only I knew the best way to Hunter’s farm!”
“They’ll be sure to know in this cottage,” said Sylvie.
“Yes, I suppose they will. Bruno, would you run in and ask?”
Sylvie stopped him, laughingly, as he ran off. “Wait a minute,” she said. “I must make you *visible* first, you know.”
“And *audible* too, I suppose?” I said, as she took the jewel, that hung round her neck, and waved it over his head, and touched his eyes and lips with it.
“Yes,” said Sylvie: “and *once*, do you know, I made him *audible*, and forgot to make him *visible*! And he went to buy some sweeties in a shop. And the man *was* so frightened! A voice seemed to come out of the air, ‘Please, I want two ounces of barley-sugar drops!’ And a shilling came *bang* down upon the counter! And the man said ‘I can’t *see* you!’ And Bruno said ‘It doosn’t sinnify seeing *me*, so long as oo can see the *shilling*!’ But the man said he never sold barley-sugar drops to people he couldn’t *see*. So we had to﻿—*Now*, Bruno, you’re ready!” And away he trotted.
Sylvie spent the time, while we were waiting for him, in making *herself* visible also. “It’s rather awkward, you know,” she explained to me, “when we meet people, and they can see *one* of us, and can’t see the *other*!”
In a minute or two Bruno returned, looking rather disconsolate. “He’d got friends with him, and he were *cross*!” he said. “He asked me who I were. And I said ‘I’m Bruno: who is *these* peoples?’ And he said ‘One’s my half-brother, and t’other’s my half-sister: and I don’t want no more company! Go along with yer!’ And I said ‘I can’t go along *wizout* mine self!’ And I said ‘Oo shouldn’t have *bits* of peoples lying about like that! It’s welly untidy!’ And he said ‘Oh, don’t talk to *me*!’ And he pushted me outside! And he shutted the door!”
“And you never asked where Hunter’s farm was?” queried Sylvie.
“Hadn’t room for any questions,” said Bruno. “The room were so crowded.”
“Three people *couldn’t* crowd a room,” said Sylvie.
“They *did*, though,” Bruno persisted. “*He* crowded it most. He’s such a welly *thick* man﻿—so as oo couldn’t knock him down.”
I failed to see the drift of Bruno’s argument.

===Chinese===

「他们握过手手啦，」布鲁诺说，他正小跑在我身旁，回答了那没说出口的疑问。
「他们看上去*可*高兴啦！」西尔维从另一边补充道。
「好，咱们现在得尽快赶路，」我说。「要是知道去亨特农场最近的路就好了！」
「这间村舍里的人准知道，」西尔维说。
「是啊，想来是知道。布鲁诺，你跑进去问问好不好？」
他刚要跑，西尔维笑着把他拦住。「等一等，」她说。「我得先让你能被*看见*呀。」
「还得能被*听见*吧，我猜？」我说。她正取下挂在脖子上的宝石，在他头顶上挥了挥，又用它碰了碰他的眼睛和嘴唇。
「对，」西尔维说：「有一回，你知道吗，我光让他能被*听见*，忘了让他能被*看见*！他跑到铺子里买糖吃。那店主可吓坏啦！半空中像是冒出一个声音：『劳驾，我要二两麦芽糖球！』一枚先令*啪*地落在柜台上！店主说：『我看*不见*你呀！』布鲁诺说：『看不见*我*不碍事，只要看得见那*先令*就行啦！』可店主说，他从来不把麦芽糖球卖给看不见的人。所以我们只好——*好了*，布鲁诺，你这就绪啦！」于是他小跑着去了。
等他的时候，西尔维把*自己*也变回了看得见的模样。「这挺尷尬的，你知道，」她向我解释，「咱们碰上人，人家看得见*这个*、却看不见*那个*的时候！」
一两分钟后布鲁诺回来了，一脸怏怏不乐。「他家来了客人，那人还*凶巴巴*的！」他说。「他问我是谁。我说『我是布鲁诺：这些*人*又是什么人呀？』他说『一个是我半个兄弟，另一个是我半个姐妹：我不要再来客了！快走开！』我说『我不能撇下*我自己*走哇！』我又说『你不该把人家*半个*半个地这么乱摆一地！太不整齐啦！』他说『噢，别跟我*啰嗦*！』他把我*推*了出去！他把门*关*上了！」
「那亨特农场怎么走你就没问？」西尔维问。
「挤不出地方来放问题呀，」布鲁诺说。「那屋子挤极了。」
「三个人可挤不满一间屋，」西尔维说。
「可他们*就*是挤满了，」布鲁诺坚持道。「*他*一个人挤得最满。他是个好——好*厚*的人——厚得你根本撞他不倒。」
我没弄明白布鲁诺这套论证的路数。

===Original===

“Surely *anybody* could be knocked down,” I said: “thick or thin wouldn’t matter.”
“Oo couldn’t knock *him* down,” said Bruno. “He’s more wider than he’s high: so, when he’s lying down, he’s more higher than when he’s standing: so a-course oo couldn’t knock him *down*!”
“Here’s another cottage,” I said: “*I’ll* ask the way, *this* time.”
There was no need to go in, this time, as the woman was standing in the doorway, with a baby in her arms, talking to a respectably dressed man﻿—a farmer, as I guessed﻿—who seemed to be on his way to the town.
“﻿—and when there’s *drink* to be had,” he was saying, “he’s just the worst o’ the lot, is your Willie. So they tell me. He gets fairly mad wi’ it!”
“I’d have given ’em the lie to their faces, a twelvemonth back!” the woman said in a broken voice. “But a’ canna noo! A’ canna noo!” She checked herself, on catching sight of us, and hastily retreated into the house, shutting the door after her.
“Perhaps you can tell me where Hunter’s farm is?” I said to the man, as he turned away from the house.
“I can *that*, Sir!” he replied with a smile. “I’m John Hunter hissel, at your sarvice. It’s nobbut half a mile further﻿—the only house in sight, when you get round bend o’ the road yonder. You’ll find my good woman within, if so be you’ve business wi’ *her*. Or mebbe I’ll do as well?”
“Thanks,” I said. “I want to order some milk. Perhaps I had better arrange it with your wife?”
“Aye,” said the man. “*She* minds all *that*. Good day t’ye, Master﻿—and to your bonnie childer, as well!” And he trudged on.
“He should have said ‘*child*,’ not ‘*childer*,’ ” said Bruno. “Sylvie’s not a *childer*!”
“He meant *both* of us,” said Sylvie.
“No, he didn’t!” Bruno persisted. “ ’cause he said ‘bonnie,’ oo know!”
“Well, at any rate he *looked* at us both,” Sylvie maintained.
“Well, then he *must* have seen we’re not *both* bonnie!” Bruno retorted. “A-*course* I’m much uglier than *oo*! Didn’t he mean *Sylvie*, Mister Sir?” he shouted over his shoulder, as he ran off.
But there was no use in replying, as he had already vanished round the bend of the road. When we overtook him he was climbing a gate, and was gazing earnestly into the field, where a horse, a cow, and a kid were browsing amicably together. “For its father, a *Horse*,” he murmured to himself. “For its mother, a *Cow*.

===Chinese===

「随便*谁*都能被撞倒呀，」我说：「厚啊薄啊不碍事。」
「你就是撞不倒*他*，」布鲁诺说。「他横着比竖着宽：所以他躺下的时候，比站着的时候还高：这么着，你当然就撞他不*倒*啦！」
「这儿又有一间村舍，」我说：「这回*我*去问路。」
这回不必进屋了，那妇人正抱着个娃娃站在门口，同一位衣著体面的男人说话——我猜是个庄稼汉——看样子正要进城去。
「——可有*酒喝*的时候，你们家威利就是那帮人里头顶坏的，人家都这么说。一灌上黄汤他就跟疯了似的！」他正这么说。
「要搁一年前，俺当面就骂他们瞎扯了！」妇人哽咽着说。「可如今不成啦！不成啦！」她一眼瞥见我们，便猛地收住话头，慌忙退回屋里，随手关上了门。
那人从屋前转过身来，我便问他：「请问去亨特农场怎么走，您可知道？」
「咋不知道，先生！」他含笑答道。「俺就是约翰·亨特本人，听您吩咐。前头不过半英里——您绕过那边路弯，一眼望得见的独一处就是。您要有事找*俺家那口子*，她就在家里。要不俺代劳也成？」
「谢谢，」我说。「我想订些牛奶。兴许同您太太面商更好？」
「着啊，」那人说。「*那些*事全归*她*管。回见了您呐，先生——还有您这两个俊娃儿！」说完他便吃力地赶路去了。
「他该说『娃』，不该说『娃们』，」布鲁诺说。「西尔维不是什么『娃们』！」
「他是把咱们*俩*都算了，」西尔维说。
「不，才没有呢！」布鲁诺坚持。「因为他说了『俊』，你知道吧！」
「可不管怎么说，他*看*了咱们俩，」西尔维一口咬定。
「那他准是看出来了，咱们俩不是*都*俊！」布鲁诺反驳。「我当然比*你*丑多啦！他说的『俊』不就是*西尔维*嘛，先生先生（Mister Sir）？」他一边跑开一边回头喊道。
不过回答也白搭，他早拐过路弯不见了。等我们追上他，他正往一扇栅门上爬，热切地朝田里张望——田里一匹马、一头母牛和一只小山羊正亲亲热热地一块儿吃草。「它的爸爸呢，是一匹*马*，」他喃喃自语。「它的妈妈呢，是一头*母牛*。」

===Original===

For their dear little child, a *little* Goat, is the most curiousest thing I ever seen in my world!”
“Bruno’s World!” I pondered. “Yes, I suppose every child has a world of his own﻿—and every man, too, for the matter of that. I wonder if *that’s* the cause for all the misunderstanding there is in Life?”
“That *must* be Hunter’s farm!” said Sylvie, pointing to a house on the brow of the hill, led up to by a cart-road. “There’s no other farm in sight, *this* way; and you *said* we must be nearly there by this time.”
I had *thought* it, while Bruno was climbing the gate, but I couldn’t remember having *said* it. However, Sylvie was evidently in the right. “Get down, Bruno,” I said, “and open the gate for us.”
“It’s a good thing we’s with oo, *isn’t* it, Mister Sir?” said Bruno, as we entered the field. “That big dog might have bited oo, if oo’d been alone! Oo needn’t be *flightened* of it!” he whispered, clinging tight to my hand to encourage me. “It aren’t fierce!”
“Fierce!” Sylvie scornfully echoed, as the dog﻿—a magnificent Newfoundland﻿—that had come galloping down the field to meet us, began curveting round us, in gambols full of graceful beauty, and welcoming us with short joyful barks. “Fierce! Why, it’s as gentle as a lamb! It’s﻿—why, Bruno, don’t you know it? It’s﻿—”
“So it *are*!” cried Bruno, rushing forwards and throwing his arms round its neck. “Oh, you *dear* dog!” And it seemed as if the two children would never have done hugging and stroking it.
“And how *ever* did he get *here*?” said Bruno. “Ask him, Sylvie. I doosn’t know how.”
And then began an eager talk in Doggee, which of course was lost upon *me*; and I could only *guess*, when the beautiful creature, with a sly glance at me, whispered something in Sylvie’s ear, that *I* was now the subject of conversation. Sylvie looked round laughingly.
“He asked me who you are,” she explained. “And I said ‘He’s our *friend*.’ And he said ‘What’s his name?’ And I said ‘It’s *Mister Sir*.’ And he said ‘Bosh!’ ”
“What is ‘Bosh!’ in Doggee?” I enquired.
“It’s the same as in English,” said Sylvie. “Only, when a *dog* says it, it’s a sort of a whisper, that’s half a *cough* and half a *bark*. Nero, say ‘*Bosh!*’ ”
And Nero, who had now begun gamboling round us again, said “*Bosh!*” several times; and I found that Sylvie’s description of the sound was perfectly accurate.

===Chinese===

「它们那亲爱的娃娃呢，一只*小*山羊，是我这世界里见过的最最稀奇的事儿！」
「布鲁诺的世界！」我暗自思忖。「是啊，我料想每个孩子都有一个自己的世界——说来，每个大人也各有各的。人生里那一切的误会，莫不莫不是打这儿来的？」
「那准是亨特农场了！」西尔维说，指着山脊上一所房子，一条大车道直通门前。「这一路望不见别的农庄；您*说*过的，这时候咱们也差不多该到了。」
布鲁诺爬栅门那会儿，我*心里*确实这么想过，可我不记得自己*说*出来过。不过，西尔维显然没说错。「下来吧，布鲁诺，」我说，「替咱们把门打开。」
「有咱们陪着您真好，*是不是*呀，先生先生？」进田的时候布鲁诺说。「您要是独个儿来，那条大狗没准儿咬您哪！您可别*怕*它！」他悄悄说，紧紧攥住我的手给我打气。「它不凶！」
「凶！」西尔维轻蔑地学了一遍这句话——那条狗，一只威武的纽芬兰犬，已经从田那头飞奔下来迎我们，此刻正围着我们腾跃，姿态优雅漂亮，还用短促欢快的吠声相迎。「凶！它温顺得跟羔羊似的！它是——咦，布鲁诺，你不认识它了吗？它是——」
「真的是*它*呀！」布鲁诺喊着冲上前去，一把捾住它的脖子。「噢，你这*亲爱*的狗！」两个抱着它、抚着它，仿佛永远也亲不够、摸不够。
「它到底*怎么*会到*这儿*来的呢？」布鲁诺说。「问问它，西尔维。我不知道咋来的。」
接着是一场热切的狗语交谈，我自然一句也听不懂；只等那漂亮的生灵斜眼瞟了我一下，对着西尔维的耳朵低声说了句什么，我才能*猜*出，如今谈话的题目正是*我*。西尔维笑着回过头来。
「它问您是谁，」她解释道。「我说『他是咱们的*朋友*。』它说『他叫什么名字？』我说『他叫*先生先生*。』它说『呸！』」
「『呸』在狗语里是什么意思？」我问。
「跟英语里一个意思，」西尔维说。「只不过*狗*说的时候，是一种耳语似的动静，一半像*咳嗽*，一半像*吠*。尼禄（Nero），说声『*呸*！』」
尼禄这时又已在我们周围欢腾起来，便「*呸*」了好几声；我发现西尔维对那声音的描述分毫不差。

===Original===

“I wonder what’s behind this long wall?” I said, as we walked on.
“It’s the *Orchard*,” Sylvie replied, after a consultation with Nero. “See, there’s a boy getting down off the wall, at that far corner. And now he’s running away across the field. I do believe he’s been stealing the apples!”
Bruno set off after him, but returned to us in a few moments, as he had evidently no chance of overtaking the young rascal.
“I couldn’t catch him!” he said. “I wiss I’d started a little sooner. His pockets *was* full of apples!”
The Dog-King looked up at Sylvie, and said something in Doggee.
“Why, of *course* you can!” Sylvie exclaimed. “How stupid not to think of it! *Nero*’ll hold him for us, Bruno! But I’d better make him invisible, first.” And she hastily got out the Magic Jewel, and began waving it over Nero’s head, and down along his back.
“That’ll do!” cried Bruno, impatiently. “After him, good Doggie!”
“Oh, Bruno!” Sylvie exclaimed reproachfully. “You shouldn’t have sent him off so quick! I hadn’t done the tail!”
Meanwhile Nero was coursing like a greyhound down the field: so at least I concluded from all *I* could see of him﻿—the long feathery tail, which floated like a meteor through the air﻿—and in a very few seconds he had come up with the little thief.
“He’s got him safe, by one foot!” cried Sylvie, who was eagerly watching the chase. “Now there’s no hurry, Bruno!”
So we walked, quite leisurely, down the field, to where the frightened lad stood. A more curious sight I had seldom seen, in all my “eerie” experiences. Every bit of him was in violent action, except the left foot, which was apparently glued to the ground﻿—there being nothing visibly holding it: while, at some little distance, the long feathery tail was waving gracefully from side to side, showing that Nero, at least, regarded the whole affair as nothing but a magnificent game of play.
“What’s the matter with you?” I said, as gravely as I could.
“Got the crahmp in me ahnkle!” the thief groaned in reply. “An’ me fut’s gone to sleep!” And he began to blubber aloud.
“Now, look here!” Bruno said in a commanding tone, getting in front of him. “Oo’ve got to give up those apples!”
The lad glanced at me, but didn’t seem to reckon *my* interference as worth anything. Then he glanced at Sylvie: *she* clearly didn’t count for very much, either. Then he took courage.

===Chinese===

「这道长墙后头是什么呀？」我们往前走时我说。
「是*果园*，」西尔维同尼禄商量过后答道。「瞧，那头拐角上有个男孩正从墙上下来。这会儿他往田那头跑啦。我说什么来着，他准是偷苹果来着！」
布鲁诺拔腿追去，可不多会儿就回到我们身边，显然是追上那小滑头的指望不大。
「追不上他！」他说。「真希望自己早点儿动身。他兜里*全*是苹果！」
狗中之王抬头望望西尔维，用狗语说了句什么。
「咦，你*当然*能行啦！」西尔维喊道。「怎么就没想到呢！尼禄会替咱们拽住他，布鲁诺！不过我最好先让它隐了形。」说着她急忙取出魔宝，在尼禄头顶上挥动，顺着它的脊背一路挥下去。
「够啦！」布鲁诺不耐烦地喊。「上，好狗狗！」
「噢，布鲁诺！」西尔维责备地叫起来。「你不该这么快就放它走！尾巴我还没挥完呢！」
这时尼禄已经像灵缇似的沿田飞掠——至少从*我*能看见的它那部分——那条羽状长尾，流星一般在半空飘掠——我是这么推断的；不出几秒它已追上那小贼。
「它稳稳捾住他一只脚啦！」热切观战的西尔维喊道。「这下不用急了，布鲁诺！」
于是我们好整以暇地缓步走向田头那吓坏了的孩子站立的地方。我平生种种「灵异」见识里，比这更古怪的景象也难得一见。他浑身每一处都在拼命扭动，唯独左脚，分明钉死在了地上——又看不见任何东西捾着它：而不远处，那条羽状长尾正左右优雅地摇摆，可见至少尼禄是把这整桩事只当一场痛快的游戏。
「你怎么啦？」我尽量板着脸说。
「脚脖子抽筋喽！」小贼哼哼着答。「脚都麻掉喽！」说着他放声哭闹起来。
「喂，听好！」布鲁诺用发号施令的口气说，挡到他跟前。「你得把那些苹果交出来！」
那孩子瞟了我一眼，似乎没把*我*的干涉当回事。又瞟了瞟西尔维：*她*显然也不怎么算数。于是他壮起胆来。

===Original===

“It’ll take a better man than any of *yer* to get ’em!” he retorted defiantly.
Sylvie stooped and patted the invisible Nero. “A *little* tighter!” she whispered. And a sharp yell from the ragged boy showed how promptly the Dog-King had taken the hint.
“What’s the matter *now*?” I said. “Is your ankle worse?”
“And it’ll get worse, and worse, and worse,” Bruno solemnly assured him, “till oo gives up those apples!”
Apparently the thief was convinced of this at last, and he sulkily began emptying his pockets of the apples. The children watched from a little distance, Bruno dancing with delight at every fresh yell extracted from Nero’s terrified prisoner.
“That’s all,” the boy said at last.
“It *isn’t* all!” cried Bruno. “There’s three more in that pocket!”
Another hint from Sylvie to the Dog-King﻿—another sharp yell from the thief, now convicted of lying also﻿—and the remaining three apples were surrendered.
“Let him go, please,” Sylvie said in Doggee, and the lad limped away at a great pace, stooping now and then to rub the ailing ankle, in fear, seemingly, that the “crahmp” might attack it again.
Bruno ran back, with his booty, to the orchard wall, and pitched the apples over it one by one. “I’s welly afraid *some* of them’s gone under the wrong trees!” he panted, on overtaking us again.
“The *wrong* trees!” laughed Sylvie. “Trees *can’t* do wrong! There’s no such things as *wrong* trees!”
“Then there’s no such things as *right* trees, neither!” cried Bruno. And Sylvie gave up the point.
“Wait a minute, please!” she said to me. “I must make Nero *visible*, you know!”
“No, *please* don’t!” cried Bruno, who had by this time mounted on the Royal back, and was twisting the Royal hair into a bridle. “It’ll be *such* fun to have him like this!”
“Well, it *does* look funny,” Sylvie admitted, and led the way to the farmhouse, where the farmer’s wife stood, evidently much perplexed at the weird procession now approaching her. “It’s summat gone wrong wi’ my spectacles, I doubt!” she murmured, as she took them off, and began diligently rubbing them with a corner of her apron.
Meanwhile Sylvie had hastily pulled Bruno down from his steed, and had just time to make His Majesty wholly visible before the spectacles were resumed.
All was natural, now; but the good woman still looked a little uneasy about it.

===Chinese===

「想拿回去？得比你们这帮*人*厉害的才行呢！」他挑衅地顶嘴。
西尔维弯下腰，拍了拍隐形的尼禄。「再*紧*一点儿！」她悄声说。那衣衫褴褛的男孩一声尖利的嚎叫，表明狗中之王多么从善如流。
「这会儿*又*怎么啦？」我说。「脚脖子更疼了？」
「还会越来越疼、越来越疼、越来越疼，」布鲁诺郑重其事地向他保证，「直到你把苹果交出来为止！」
小贼看来终于信了，赌着气开始把兜里的苹果一个个往外掏。孩子们在稍远处看着，尼禄那吓破胆的俘虏每嚎一声，布鲁诺就乐得直蹦。
「没啦，」男孩最后说。
「*没*完！」布鲁诺喊。「那只兜里还有仨呢！」
西尔维又对狗中之王递了个话——小贼又一声尖叫，这回撒谎也定了罪——剩下的三个苹果也缴了出来。
「请放开他吧，」西尔维用狗语说，那孩子便一瘸一拐、飞也似的逃了，还不时弯腰揉揉那只作痛的脚脖子，像是生怕那「抽筋」再扑上来。
布鲁诺带着缴获的战利品跑回果园墙边，把苹果一个个扔过墙去。「我怕有*几只*滚错树底下了！」重新赶上我们时他喘着气说。
「*错*的树！」西尔维笑道。「树*不会*做错事！世上根本没有*错*的树！」
「那世上也没有*对*的树！」布鲁诺喊。西尔维便不再争了。
「请稍等一下！」她对我说。「我得把尼禄变回*看得见*的呀！」
「不，*千万别*！」布鲁诺喊道，他这时已骑上御背，正把御鬃拧成缰绳。「就这么着才*好玩*呢！」
「呃，这么瞧着是挺滑稽，」西尔维承认，便领路朝农舍走去。农妇正站在舍前，眼见这支古怪的队伍向她逼近，显然大为惶惑。「俺琢磨是俺的眼镜出毛病啦！」她喃喃说着，摘下眼镜，用围裙角卖力地擦起来。
趁这工夫，西尔维已急忙把布鲁诺从坐骑上拽下来，赶在眼镜重新戴上之前，恰好让陛下整个儿现了形。
这下一切都合乎自然了；可这位好心的大娘瞧着，仍不免有几分不自在。

===Original===

“My eyesight’s getting bad,” she said, “but I see you *now*, my darlings! You’ll give me a kiss, won’t you?”
Bruno got behind me, in a moment: however Sylvie put up *her* face, to be kissed, as representative of *both*, and we all went in together.

===Chinese===

「俺这眼神是一年不如一年喽，」她说，「不过这会儿俺可看清你们啦，小宝贝们！亲俺一个，好不好呀？」
布鲁诺一转眼就躲到了我背后：不过西尔维倒是仰起*她的*小脸儿，代表*他们俩*受了这一吻，我们便一齐进了屋。

## V Matilda Jane / 五 玛蒂尔达·珍妮
===Original===

“Come to me, my little gentleman,” said our hostess, lifting Bruno into her lap, “and tell me everything.”
“I can’t,” said Bruno. “There wouldn’t be time. Besides, I don’t *know* everything.”
The good woman looked a little puzzled, and turned to Sylvie for help. “Does he like *riding*?” she asked.
“Yes, I *think* so,” Sylvie gently replied. “He’s just had a ride on *Nero*.”
“Ah, Nero’s a grand dog, isn’t he? Were you ever outside a *horse*, my little man?”
“*Always!*” Bruno said with great decision. “Never was *inside* one. Was *oo*?”
Here I thought it well to interpose, and to mention the business on which we had come, and so relieved her, for a few minutes, from Bruno’s perplexing questions.
“And those dear children will like a bit of cake, *I’ll* warrant!” said the farmer’s hospitable wife, when the business was concluded, as she opened her cupboard, and brought out a cake. “And don’t you waste the crust, little gentleman!” she added, as she handed a good slice of it to Bruno. “You know what the poetry-book says about wilful waste?”
“No, I don’t,” said Bruno. “What doos he say about it?”
“Tell him, Bessie!” And the mother looked down, proudly and lovingly, on a rosy little maiden, who had just crept shyly into the room, and was leaning against her knee. “What’s that your poetry-book says about wilful waste?”
“*For wilful waste makes woeful want*,” Bessie recited, in an almost inaudible whisper: “*and you may live to say ‘How much I wish I had the crust that then I threw away!’*”
“Now try if *you* can say it, my dear! *For wilful*﻿—”
“*For wifful*﻿—sumfinoruvver﻿—” Bruno began, readily enough; and then there came a dead pause. “Can’t remember no more!”
“Well, what do you *learn* from it, then? You can tell us *that*, at any rate?”
Bruno ate a little more cake, and considered: but the moral did not seem to him to be a very obvious one.
“Always to﻿—” Sylvie prompted him in a whisper.
“Always to﻿—” Bruno softly repeated: and then, with sudden inspiration, “always to look where it goes to!”
“Where *what* goes to, darling?”
“Why the *crust*, a course!” said Bruno. “Then, if I lived to say ‘*How much I wiss I had the crust*﻿—’ (and all that), I’d know where I frew it to!”
This new interpretation quite puzzled the good woman. She returned to the subject of “Bessie.” “Wouldn’t you like to see Bessie’s doll, my dears!

===Chinese===

“到我这儿来，小绅士，”女主人说着把布鲁诺抱到膝上，“把什么都讲给我听。”
“讲不了，”布鲁诺说。“时间不够。再说，我也不是什么都*懂*。”
好心的妇人露出一点困惑，转头向西尔维求援。“他喜欢*骑马*吗？”她问。
“嗯，我想是喜欢的，”西尔维柔声答道。“他方才在*尼禄*背上骑了一程。”
“啊，尼禄是条神气的狗，可不是嘛！你可曾到过一匹*马*的外头来着，小家伙？”
“*一直*都在外头呀！”布鲁诺斩钉截铁地说。“从没到过一匹马的*里头*。*你*到过吗？”
这时我觉得最好插一句话，把来意说明，也好让她暂且从布鲁诺那些令人发懵的问题里解脱几分钟。
“这几位小宝贝准愿意来点儿蛋糕，*我*敢保！”事情谈完，好客的农妇太太一面开碗柜取蛋糕一面说。“皮儿可别糟蹋了，小绅士！”她把厚厚一块递给布鲁诺，又添了一句。“诗集上说糟蹋东西怎么说，你可知道？”
“不知道，”布鲁诺说。“他说了啥呀？”
“背给他听，贝西！”母亲又疼又得意地低头望着一个红扑扑的小姑娘——她刚怯生生地蹭进屋来，倚在妈妈膝边。“你的诗集上关于糟蹋东西是怎么说的？”
“*任性糟蹋，招来穷苦*，”贝西用几乎听不见的细声背诵道：“*你迟早会叹：“多么希望留着那时扔掉的面包皮！”*”
“现在看*你*能不能说，宝贝！*任性糟蹋*——”
“‘任信糟蹋’——个啥玩意儿——”布鲁诺起头起得倒爽快，随即来了个彻底的冷场。“后头记不住啦！”
“那这诗教你什么道理呢？这个你总讲得上来吧？”
布鲁诺又吃了口蛋糕，想了想：可这诗的教训在他看来实在不算浅显。
“总是要——”西尔维悄声提他。
“总是要——”布鲁诺轻声跟着念，忽然灵光一现：“总是要看清它扔到哪儿去！”
“*什么*扔到哪儿去呀，宝贝？”
“当然是*面包皮*呀！”布鲁诺说。“这样，等我哪天叹‘*多希望我留着那块皮*——’（什么的），我就知道它扔哪儿啦！”
这番新解把好心的妇人彻底弄懵了。她回到“贝西”的话题上。“想不想看看贝西的娃娃呀，宝贝们？

===Original===

Bessie, take the little lady and gentleman to see Matilda Jane!”
Bessie’s shyness thawed away in a moment. “Matilda Jane has just woke up,” she stated, confidentially, to Sylvie. “Won’t you help me on with her frock? Them strings *is* such a bother to tie!”
“I can tie *strings*,” we heard, in Sylvie’s gentle voice, as the two little girls left the room together. Bruno ignored the whole proceeding, and strolled to the window, quite with the air of a fashionable gentleman. Little girls, and dolls, were not at all in his line.
And forthwith the fond mother proceeded to tell me (as what mother is not ready to do?) of all Bessie’s virtues (and vices too, for the matter of that) and of the many fearful maladies which, notwithstanding those ruddy cheeks and that plump little figure, had nearly, time and again, swept her from the face of the earth.
When the full stream of loving memories had nearly run itself out, I began to question her about the working men of that neighbourhood, and specially the “Willie,” whom we had heard of at his cottage. “He was a good fellow once,” said my kind hostess: “but it’s the drink has ruined him! Not that I’d rob them of the drink﻿—it’s good for the most of them﻿—but there’s some as is too weak to stand agin’ temptations: it’s a thousand pities, for *them*, as they ever built the Golden Lion at the corner there!”
“The Golden Lion?” I repeated.
“It’s the new Public,” my hostess explained. “And it stands right in the way, and handy for the workmen, as they come back from the brickfields, as it might be today, with their week’s wages. A deal of money gets wasted that way. And some of ’em gets drunk.”
“If only they could have it in their own houses﻿—” I mused, hardly knowing I had said the words out loud.
“That’s it!” she eagerly exclaimed. It was evidently a solution, of the problem, that she had already thought out. “If only you could manage, so’s each man to have his own little barrel in his own house﻿—there’d hardly be a drunken man in the length and breadth of the land!”

===Chinese===

贝西，带小小姐、小绅士去看玛蒂尔达·珍妮！”
贝西的羞怯霎时消融。“玛蒂尔达·珍妮刚睡醒，”她跟西尔维咬耳朵似的说。“帮我给她穿衣裳好不好？那些带子*真是*麻烦死啦！”
“我会系*带子*。”两个小姑娘一同出屋时，我们听见西尔维温软的声音。布鲁诺对这一切全不理会，踱到窗边，一副十足时髦绅士的做派。小姑娘和洋娃娃，根本不合他的行当。
紧接着，这位慈母便对我讲起（哪位母亲不乐于这样呢？）贝西的种种美德（老实说，连缺点也一并讲了），还有那些吓人的重症——别看她红扑扑的脸蛋、圆滚滚的身材，那些病可一次又一次险些把她从这世上卷走。
等这条爱意潺潺的回忆之河将流未尽，我便问起这一带的做工人家，尤其是我们在他家小屋里听说过的那位“威利”。“他原是个好后生，”好心的女主人说：“是酒把他毁了！倒不是我要夺他们的酒——对大多数人酒是好的——可有些人就是太软弱，抵不住诱惑：为他们着想，街角上盖起那家‘金狮’，真真是千不该万不该！”
“金狮？”我重复道。
“是新开的酒馆，”女主人解释道。“正好挡在道边上，做工的从砖窑场回来——就说今儿吧——揣着一礼拜的工钱，顺脚就进去了。多少钞票就这么糟蹋掉。有的人还喝得烂醉。”
“要是他们能在自家屋里喝酒就好了——”我喃喃自语，几乎不知道自己说出了声。
“着啊！”她热切地叫起来。这显然是她早已想出的解法。“要是能想个法子，让每个男人自己家里都摆上一小桶——普天之下，怕就难找一个醉汉喽！”

===Original===

And then I told her the old story﻿—about a certain cottager who bought himself a little barrel of beer, and installed his wife as barkeeper: and how, every time he wanted his mug of beer, he regularly paid her over the counter for it: and how she never would let him go on “tick,” and was a perfectly inflexible barkeeper in never letting him have more than his proper allowance: and how, every time the barrel needed refilling, she had plenty to do it with, and something over for her money-box: and how, at the end of the year, he not only found himself in first-rate health and spirits, with that undefinable but quite unmistakeable air which always distinguishes the sober man from the one who takes “a drop too much,” but had quite a box full of money, all saved out of his own pence!
“If only they’d all do like that!” said the good woman, wiping her eyes, which were overflowing with kindly sympathy. “Drink hadn’t need to be the curse it is to some﻿—”
“Only a *curse*,” I said, “when it is used wrongly. Any of God’s gifts may be turned into a curse, unless we use it wisely. But we must be getting home. Would you call the little girls? Matilda Jane has seen enough of company, for *one* day, I’m sure!”
“I’ll find ’em in a minute,” said my hostess, as she rose to leave the room. “Maybe that young gentleman saw which way they went?”
“Where are they, Bruno?” I said.
“They ain’t in the field,” was Bruno’s rather evasive reply, “ ’cause there’s nothing but *pigs* there, and Sylvie isn’t a pig. Now don’t imperrupt me any more, ’cause I’m telling a story to this fly; and it won’t attend!”
“They’re among the apples, I’ll warrant ’em!” said the Farmer’s wife. So we left Bruno to finish his story, and went out into the orchard, where we soon came upon the children, walking sedately side by side, Sylvie carrying the doll, while little Bess carefully shaded its face, with a large cabbage-leaf for a parasol.
As soon as they caught sight of us, little Bess dropped her cabbage-leaf and came running to meet us, Sylvie following more slowly, as her precious charge evidently needed great care and attention.
“I’m its Mamma, and Sylvie’s the Head-Nurse,” Bessie explained: “and Sylvie’s taught me ever such a pretty song, for me to sing to Matilda Jane!”
“Let’s hear it once more, Sylvie,” I said, delighted at getting the chance I had long wished for, of hearing her sing.

===Chinese===

接着我给她讲了那个老故事——说的是某个庄稼汉给自己买了一小桶啤酒，请自家婆娘充任掌柜：他每回要喝一大杯，都规规矩矩隔着柜台付钱；她从不许他赊账，是个铁面无私的掌柜，绝不让他多喝一口超出定量的酒；每回酒桶该添酒了，她手头总有钱添，还剩些放进钱盒；到了年底，他不但发现自己身子骨硬朗、精神头十足，带着那股说不清却错不了的气色——凡是滴酒不沾的人同“多贪一杯”的人，一望便知的分别——还攒下了满满一盒钱，全是从自己一个一个便士里省出来的！
“他们要都这么办该多好！”好心的妇人说，抹着满含温情的眼睛。“酒本不必成为有些人的祸害——”
“只有用得不对时才是*祸害*，”我说，“上帝的任何恩赐，若不智慧地用，都可能变成祸害。不过我们该回家了。请把小姑娘们叫来好吗？我敢说，玛蒂尔达·珍妮今天应酬客人，应酬得够多啦！”
“我一忽儿就找着她们，”女主人起身出屋道。“兴许那位小绅士瞧见她们往哪儿去了？”
“她们在哪儿，布鲁诺？”我问。
“她们不在田里，”布鲁诺的回答颇有躲闪，“因为田里除了*猪*什么也没有，西尔维又不是猪。别再打搅我啦，我正给这只苍蝇讲故事呢；它还不肯听！”
“保险在苹果树里头呢！”农妇太太说。于是我们把布鲁诺留下讲完他的故事，自己走进果园，不多时就撞见两个孩子，并着肩斯斯文文地走，西尔维抱着娃娃，小贝西拿一片大白菜叶替它遮着脸，权当阳伞。
一瞧见我们，小贝西撒下白菜叶便跑过来迎，西尔维跟得慢些——她那金贵的宝贝显然需要悉心照料。
“我是它的妈妈，西尔维是总保姆，”贝西解释道：“西尔维还教了我一支顶好听的歌，好让我唱给玛蒂尔达·珍妮听！”
“再唱一遍给我们听听吧，西尔维，”我说，得偿久愿，喜出望外——我一直盼着听她唱歌。

===Original===

But Sylvie turned shy and frightened in a moment. “No, *please* not!” she said, in an earnest “aside” to me. “Bessie knows it quite perfect now. Bessie can sing it!”
“Aye, aye! Let Bessie sing it!” said the proud mother. “Bessie has a bonny voice of her own,” (this again was an “aside” to me) “though I say it as shouldn’t!”
Bessie was only too happy to accept the “encore.” So the plump little Mamma sat down at our feet, with her hideous daughter reclining stiffly across her lap (it was one of a kind that won’t sit down, under *any* amount of persuasion), and, with a face simply beaming with delight, began the lullaby, in a shout that *ought* to have frightened the poor baby into fits. The Head-Nurse crouched down behind her, keeping herself respectfully in the background, with her hands on the shoulders of her little mistress, so as to be ready to act as Prompter, if required, and to supply “*each gap in faithless memory void*.”
The shout, with which she began, proved to be only a momentary effort. After a very few notes, Bessie toned down, and sang on in a small but very sweet voice. At first her great black eyes were fixed on her mother, but soon her gaze wandered upwards, among the apples, and she seemed to have quite forgotten that she had any other audience than her Baby, and her Head-Nurse, who once or twice supplied, almost inaudibly, the right note, when the singer was getting a little “flat.”

> “Matilda Jane, you never look
> At any toy or picture-book:
> I show you pretty things in vain﻿—
> You must be blind, Matilda Jane!
> “I ask you riddles, tell you tales,
> But *all* our conversation fails:
> You *never* answer me again﻿—
> I fear you’re dumb, Matilda Jane!
> “Matilda, darling, when I call,
> You never seem to hear at all:
> I shout with all my might and main﻿—
> But you’re *so* deaf, Matilda Jane!
> “Matilda Jane, you needn’t mind;
> For, though you’re deaf, and dumb, and blind,
> There’s *someone* loves you, it is plain﻿—
> And that is *me*, Matilda Jane!”

===Chinese===

可西尔维霎时羞怯慌张起来。“不，*求您*别让我唱！”她恳切地对我“打旁白”说。“贝西这会儿背得顶熟了。贝西会唱！”
“哎，哎！就让贝西唱吧！”自豪的母亲说。“贝西有副自家天生的好嗓门，”（这又是对我的“旁白”）“虽说这话轮不到我自夸！”
贝西巴不得接受这声“再来一个”。于是圆滚滚的小妈妈在我们脚边坐定，她那丑得吓人的女儿直挺挺横躺在她膝上（那是再怎么劝也死活不肯坐下的品种），她满脸放光，扯开一嗓子唱起摇篮曲——那嗓门*理应*把可怜的娃娃吓得抽风。
总保姆蹲在她身后，恭恭敬敬地退居背景，两手搭在小主人的肩上，以便随时充任提词人，补上“*善忘的记忆留下的每处空白*”。
起头那一嗓子，原来只是昙花一现的气力。没唱几个音，贝西便收敛了，改用细小却十分甜美的嗓音唱下去。起初她乌黑的大眼睛望着妈妈，可不久目光便悠悠飘向上方的苹果间，她似乎全然忘了除了她的宝宝和总保姆，还有别的听众——总保姆有一两回在唱的人走了点“调”时，几不可闻地递上正确的音。

> “玛蒂尔达·珍妮，你从不瞧
> 什么玩具、什么画报：
> 好东西给你看也白看——
> 你眼睛准是瞎，玛蒂尔达·珍妮！
> “我给你猜谜、讲故事，
> 可咱们的话*全*说不到一块儿：
> 你*从来不*答我的话——
> 恐怕你是哑巴，玛蒂尔达·珍妮！
> “玛蒂尔达，宝贝，我叫你，
> 你像是压根儿听不见：
> 我扯足了嗓门喊——
> 可你耳朵*真*聋，玛蒂尔达·珍妮！
> “玛蒂尔达·珍妮，你别介意；
> 虽说你又聋又哑又瞎，
> 有*一个人*爱你，这明摆着——
> 那就是*我*呀，玛蒂尔达·珍妮！”

===Original===

She sang three of the verses in a rather perfunctory style, but the last stanza evidently excited the little maiden. Her voice rose, ever clearer and louder: she had a rapt look on her face, as if suddenly inspired, and, as she sang the last few words, she clasped to her heart the inattentive Matilda Jane.
“Kiss it now!” prompted the Head-Nurse. And in a moment the simpering meaningless face of the Baby was covered with a shower of passionate kisses.
“What a bonny song!” cried the Farmer’s wife. “Who made the words, dearie?”
“I﻿—I think I’ll look for Bruno,” Sylvie said demurely, and left us hastily. The curious child seemed always afraid of being praised, or even noticed.
“Sylvie planned the words,” Bessie informed us, proud of her superior information: “and Bruno planned the music﻿—and *I* sang it!” (this last circumstance, by the way, we did not need to be told).
So we followed Sylvie, and all entered the parlour together. Bruno was still standing at the window, with his elbows on the sill. He had, apparently, finished the story that he was telling to the fly, and had found a new occupation. “Don’t imperrupt!” he said as we came in. “I’m counting the Pigs in the field!”
“How many are there?” I enquired.
“About a thousand and four,” said Bruno.
“You mean ‘about a thousand,’ ” Sylvie corrected him. “There’s no good saying ‘*and four*’: you *can’t* be sure about the four!”
“And you’re as wrong as ever!” Bruno exclaimed triumphantly. “It’s just the *four* I *can* be sure about; ’cause they’re here, grubbling under the window! It’s the *thousand* I isn’t pruffickly sure about!”
“But some of them have gone into the sty,” Sylvie said, leaning over him to look out of the window.
“Yes,” said Bruno; “but they went so slowly and so fewly, I didn’t care to count *them*.”
“We must be going, children,” I said. “Wish Bessie goodbye.” Sylvie flung her arms round the little maiden’s neck, and kissed her: but Bruno stood aloof, looking unusually shy. (“I never kiss *nobody* but Sylvie!” he explained to me afterwards.) The farmer’s wife showed us out: and we were soon on our way back to Elveston.
“And that’s the new public-house that we were talking about, I suppose?” I said, as we came in sight of a long low building, with the words “**The Golden Lion**” over the door.
“Yes, that’s it,” said Sylvie. “I wonder if *her* Willie’s inside? Run in, Bruno, and see if he’s there.”

===Chinese===

头三节她唱得有点敷衍，末一节却显然让小姑娘来了劲。嗓音越唱越亮、越唱越响：她脸上一副入迷的神情，仿佛骤然得了灵感，唱到末几个字时，把那全不理会的玛蒂尔达·珍妮紧紧搂在心口。
“快亲亲它！”总保姆提词道。转眼间，娃娃那咧着嘴、没心没肺的脸便落满了密密的香吻。
“多好听的歌呀！”农妇太太嚷道。“词儿是谁编的，宝贝？”
“我——我想我去找找布鲁诺，”西尔维端端正正地说，匆匆走开了。这古怪的孩子似乎总怕人夸，连被人注意都怕。
“词儿是西尔维编的，”贝西通报我们，为自己消息灵通而得意：“曲儿是布鲁诺编的——*唱歌的是我*！”（这最后一节，说句题外话，倒不必她告诉我们。）
于是我们跟上西尔维，一道进了客厅。布鲁诺还站在窗前，胳膊肘支在窗台上。给苍蝇讲的故事显然已经讲完，他又找了新营生。“别打搅！”我们进来时他说。“我在数田里的猪呢！”
“有多少头呀？”我问。
“差不多一千零四头，”布鲁诺说。
“你是说‘差不多一千’，”西尔维纠正他。“说‘*零四*’没用：那*四*头你又拿不准！”
“你才又错啦！”布鲁诺得意洋洋地嚷。“恰恰是那*四*头我*拿得准*；因为它们就在这儿，窗底下拱土呢！倒是那*一千*头，我不老肯定！”
“可有几头已经进猪圈了，”西尔维俯身从他头上望向窗外说。
“是的，”布鲁诺说；“可它们走得那么慢、又一星星一滴滴的，我才懒得数*它们*。”
“我们该走了，孩子们，”我说。“跟贝西道个别。”西尔维搂住小姑娘的脖子亲了亲；布鲁诺却远远站着，出奇地羞涩。（“我从来*谁也*不亲，只亲西尔维！”他后来向我解释。）农妇太太送我们出门：我们很快便踏上回埃尔夫斯顿的路。
“那大概就是咱们说的新酒馆吧？”望见一幢低矮的长屋、门楣上写着“**金狮**”两个大字时，我说。
“对，就是它，”西尔维说。“不知道*她家*威利在不在里头？跑进去瞧瞧，布鲁诺，看他在不在。”

===Original===

I interposed, feeling that Bruno was, in a sort of way, in *my* care. “That’s not a place to send a child into.” For already the revelers were getting noisy: and a wild discord of singing, shouting, and meaningless laughter came to us through the open windows.
“They won’t *see* him, you know,” Sylvie explained. “Wait a minute, Bruno!” She clasped the jewel, that always hung round her neck, between the palms of her hands, and muttered a few words to herself. What they were I could not at all make out, but some mysterious change seemed instantly to pass over us. My feet seemed to me no longer to press the ground, and the dreamlike feeling came upon me, that I was suddenly endowed with the power of floating in the air. I could still just *see* the children: but their forms were shadowy and unsubstantial, and their voices sounded as if they came from some distant place and time, they were so unreal. However, I offered no further opposition to Bruno’s going into the house. He was back again in a few moments. “No, he isn’t come yet,” he said. “They’re talking about him inside, and saying how drunk he was last week.”
While he was speaking, one of the men lounged out through the door, a pipe in one hand and a mug of beer in the other, and crossed to where we were standing, so as to get a better view along the road. Two or three others leaned out through the open window, each holding his mug of beer, with red faces and sleepy eyes. “Canst see him, lad?” one of them asked.
“I dunnot know,” the man said, taking a step forwards, which brought us nearly face to face. Sylvie hastily pulled me out of his way. “Thanks, child,” I said. “I had forgotten he couldn’t see us. What would have happened if I had stayed in his way?”
“I don’t know,” Sylvie said gravely. “It wouldn’t matter to *us*; but *you* may be different.” She said this in her usual voice, but the man took no sort of notice, though she was standing close in front of him, and looking up into his face as she spoke.
“He’s coming now!” cried Bruno, pointing down the road.
“He be a-coomin noo!” echoed the man, stretching out his arm exactly over Bruno’s head, and pointing with his pipe.
“Then *chorus* agin!” was shouted out by one of the red-faced men in the window: and forthwith a dozen voices yelled, to a harsh discordant melody, the refrain:﻿—

===Chinese===

我出言拦住，觉得布鲁诺多多少少算在*我*照管之下。“那不是派孩子进去的地方。”酒客们已经闹腾起来了：敞开的窗口里传出一阵狂乱的聒噪，又是唱又是吼，还有无意义的哄笑。
“他们*看不见*他的，你知道，”西尔维解释道。“等一等，布鲁诺！”她双掌合拢，捧住那枚常年挂在她颈间的宝石，喃喃念了几句。念的是什么我全然听不出，可某种神秘的变化似乎霎时罩住了我们。我觉得双脚不再踏地，一种梦一般的感觉涌上心头——我忽然有了浮在空中的本领。孩子们我还能勉强*望见*：只是身影朦胧虚浮，话音听着像是从遥远的时空传来，虚幻得很。于是布鲁诺进屋一事，我不再阻拦。不多时他便回来了。“没，他还没来，”他说。“他们在里头正说起他呢，说他上礼拜喝得有多醉。”
他说话的当儿，一个男人趿拉着晃出门来，一手烟斗、一手啤酒杯，踱到我们站的地方，好沿大路望得更远。另有两三个从敞开的窗口探出身，人人举着啤酒杯，红着脸，眯着眼。“瞧见他了吗，伙计？”其中一个问。
“瞅不着哇，”那男人说着上前一步，几乎与我们迎面相对。西尔维急忙把我拽开，让出道来。“多谢，孩子，”我说。“我忘了他看不见咱们。我若挡在他道上，会怎么样呢？”
“我不知道，”西尔维正色道。“对*我们*不要紧；可*你*说不定不一样。”她说这话用的还是平常的嗓音，那男人却浑然无觉——尽管她就近站在他跟前，仰着他的脸说话。
“他来啦！”布鲁诺指着大路那头喊。
“俺瞅他来喽！”那男人应声附和，一条胳膊恰好从布鲁诺头顶伸出去，拿烟斗指着。
“那再*合唱*一遍！”窗口一个红脸汉子嚷道：于是十几条嗓子立刻就着刺耳走调的旋律，吼起了那支副歌：——

===Original===

> “There’s him, an’ yo’ an’ me,
> Roarin’ laddies!
> We loves a bit o’ spree,
> Roarin’ laddies we,
> Roarin’ laddies
> Roarin’ laddies!”

The man lounged back again to the house, joining lustily in the chorus as he went: so that only the children and I were in the road when “Willie” came up.

===Chinese===

> “有他，有俺，有你，
> 吼叫的哥儿们！
> 咱就爱撒个欢儿，
> 咱是吼叫的哥儿们，
> 吼叫的哥儿们，
> 吼叫的哥儿们！”

那男人又趿拉回屋里去，一路扯着嗓门跟着合唱：等“威利”走近时，路上只剩孩子们和我了。

## VI Willie’s Wife / 六 威利的妻子
===Original===

He made for the door of the public-house, but the children intercepted him. Sylvie clung to one arm; while Bruno, on the opposite side, was pushing him with all his strength, with many inarticulate cries of “Gee-up! Gee-back! Woah then!” which he had picked up from the wagoners.
“Willie” took not the least notice of them: he was simply conscious that *something* had checked him: and, for want of any other way of accounting for it, he seemed to regard it as his own act.
“I wunnut coom in,” he said: “not today.”
“A mug o’ beer wunnut hurt ’ee!” his friends shouted in chorus. “*Two* mugs wunnut hurt ’ee! Nor a dozen mugs!”
“Nay,” said Willie. “I’m agoan whoam.”
“What, withouten thy drink, Willie man?” shouted the others. But “Willie man” would have no more discussion, and turned doggedly away, the children keeping one on each side of him, to guard him against any change in his sudden resolution.
For a while he walked on stoutly enough, keeping his hands in his pockets, and softly whistling a tune, in time to his heavy tread: his success, in appearing entirely at his ease, was *almost* complete; but a careful observer would have noted that he had forgotten the second part of the air, and that, when it broke down, he instantly began it again, being too nervous to think of another, and too restless to endure silence.
It was not the old fear that possessed him now﻿—the old fear, that had been his dreary companion every Saturday night he could remember, as he had reeled along, steadying himself against gates and garden-palings, and when the shrill reproaches of his wife had seemed to his dazed brain only the echo of a yet more piercing voice within, the intolerable wail of a hopeless remorse: it was a wholly new fear that had come to him now: life had taken on itself a new set of colours, and was lighted up with a new and dazzling radiance, and he did not see, as yet, how his home-life, and his wife and child, would fit into the new order of things: the very novelty of it all was, to his simple mind, a perplexity and an overwhelming terror.

===Chinese===

他直奔酒馆大门，孩子们却把他截住了。西尔维吊住他一条胳膊；布鲁诺在另一边使出全身力气推他，嘴里发出一串含混不清的吆喝：“驾！回！吁——！”这都是从赶车汉那儿学来的。
“威利”对他们浑然不觉：他只意识到*有什么东西*拦住了自己：又找不出别的解释，便当是自己停下了脚。
“俺不进去了，”他说：“今儿个不。”
“一杯啤酒伤不着你！”他的朋友们齐声嚷。“*两*杯也伤不着！一打也伤不着！”
“不咧，”威利说。“俺回家去。”
“啥？酒都不喝啦，威利老弟？”旁人嚷道。可“威利老弟”再不容多说，倯头倯脑地转身走了，孩子们一左一右护着他，防着他这突起的决心再变卦。
有一程他走得相当硬朗，双手插兜，随着沉重的脚步轻轻地吹着口哨：他那副全然自若的样子装得*几乎*天衣无缝；可细心的旁观者会发现，那曲子的后半段他忘了，而曲子一断，他就立刻从头再吹——他紧张得想不出换一支曲子，又烦躁得受不了寂静。
此刻缠住他的，已不是那老恐惧——那老恐惧，在他记得起的每个星期六夜里都是他阴郁的伙伴：那时他一路趔趄，扶着栅栏门和花园篱桩稳住身子，妻子尖利的责骂在他昏沉的脑子里，不过是内心一个更刺耳的声音的回响——一种无可救药的悔恨发出的、难以忍受的哀号：如今缠上他的，是一种全然崭新的恐惧：生活换上了一整套新的色彩，被一种崭新的、眩目的光辉照亮，而他还看不出来，他的家庭生活、他的妻儿，将如何安放进这新的秩序：这一切的新奇，对头脑简单的他来说，既是惶惑，又是压倒一切的惊骇。

===Original===

And now the tune died into sudden silence on the trembling lips, as he turned a sharp corner, and came in sight of his own cottage, where his wife stood, leaning with folded arms on the wicket-gate, and looking up the road with a pale face, that had in it no glimmer of the light of hope﻿—only the heavy shadow of a deep stony despair.
“Fine an’ early, lad! Fine an’ early!” The words might have been words of welcoming, but oh, the bitterness of the tone in which she said it! “What brings thee from thy merry mates, and all the fiddling and the jigging? Pockets empty, I doubt? Or thou’st come, mebbe, for to see thy little one die? The bairnie’s clemmed, and I’ve nor bite nor sup to gie her. But what does *thou* care?” She flung the gate open, and met him with blazing eyes of fury.
The man said no word. Slowly, and with downcast eyes, he passed into the house, while she, half terrified at his strange silence, followed him in without another word; and it was not till he had sunk into a chair, with his arms crossed on the table and with drooping head, that she found her voice again.
It seemed entirely natural for us to go in with them: at another time one would have asked leave for this, but I felt, I knew not why, that we were in some mysterious way invisible, and as free to come and to go as disembodied spirits.
The child in the cradle woke up, and raised a piteous cry, which in a moment brought the children to its side: Bruno rocked the cradle, while Sylvie tenderly replaced the little head on the pillow from which it had slipped. But the mother took no heed of the cry, nor yet of the satisfied “coo” that it set up when Sylvie had made it happy again: she only stood gazing at her husband, and vainly trying, with white quivering lips (I believe she thought he was mad), to speak in the old tones of shrill upbraiding that he knew so well.
“And thou’st spent all thy wages﻿—I’ll swear thou hast﻿—on the devil’s own drink﻿—and thou’st been and made thysen a beast again﻿—as thou allus dost﻿—”
“Hasna!” the man muttered, his voice hardly rising above a whisper, as he slowly emptied his pockets on the table. “There’s th’ wage, Missus, every penny on’t.”
The woman gasped, and put one hand to her heart, as if under some great shock of surprise. “Then *how*’s thee gotten th’ drink?”
“*Hasna* gotten it,” he answered her, in a tone more sad than sullen.

===Chinese===

这时曲子在颤抖的唇上戛然而止——他拐过一道急弯，望见了自己的小屋：妻子抱着胳膊倚在栅栏小门上，苍白着脸朝大路这头张望，脸上没有一丝希望的光——只有一层深重的、石头般死寂的绝望的暗影。
“回来得可真早哇，汉子！真早哇！”话本可以是迎人的话，可那腔调里的苦味哟！“是什么把你从快活的哥们儿那儿拽回来了，撞下那提琴和蹦跳？口袋空了，俺猜？要不，你是回来瞧你那小的断气？娃儿饿得慌，俺连一口吃的喝的都给不了她。可你*在乎*吗？”她猛地推开栅栏门，眼里烧着怒火迎上来。
男人一言不发。他垂着眼缓缓进了屋；她被他这反常的沉默吓得半怔，也一声不响跟了进去；直到他跌坐进椅子，双臂交叠在桌上、辛拉着头，她才找回自己的声音。
我们随他们一同进屋，仿佛再自然不过：换在平时，这样总得先讨个允许，可我感到——不知为何——我们正以某种神秘的方式隐形，来去自如，如无形的魂灵。
摇篮里的孩子醒了，发出一声凄惶的啼哭，霎时把两个孩子引到摇篮边：布鲁诺摇起摇篮，西尔维轻轻把那滑落枕外的小脑袋摆回枕上。可母亲对哭声不理不睬，连西尔维哄好娃娃后那满足的“咕咕”声也充耳不闻：她只立在那里死盯着丈夫，惨白哆嗦的嘴唇徒劳地禽动着（我相信她当他是疯了），想说出那他早已听惯的尖声数落，却说不出来。
“你准是把工钱全造光了——俺敢赌你造光了——全灌了那魔鬼的酒——你又把自己作践成牲口——像你*回回*那样——”
“没造！”男人嗫嘴道，声音低得几乎耳语，一面缓缓把口袋里的东西掏在桌上。“工钱在这儿呢，孩儿他娘，一个子儿不少。”
女人倒抽一口气，一只手按住心口，像遭了大大的惊骇。“那你的酒*打*哪儿来的？”
“*压根没*喝酒。”他答她，语气与其说犟，不如说凄然。

===Original===

“I hanna touched a drop this blessed day. No!” he cried aloud, bringing his clenched fist heavily down upon the table, and looking up at her with gleaming eyes, “nor I’ll never touch another drop o’ the cursed drink﻿—till I die﻿—so help me God my Maker!” His voice, which had suddenly risen to a hoarse shout, dropped again as suddenly: and once more he bowed his head, and buried his face in his folded arms.
The woman had dropped upon her knees by the cradle, while he was speaking. She neither looked at him nor seemed to hear him. With hands clasped above her head, she rocked herself wildly to and fro. “Oh my God! Oh my God!” was all she said, over and over again.
Sylvie and Bruno gently unclasped her hands and drew them down﻿—till she had an arm round each of them, though she took no notice of them, but knelt on with eyes gazing upwards, and lips that moved as if in silent thanksgiving. The man kept his face hidden, and uttered no sound: but one could *see* the sobs that shook him from head to foot.
After a while he raised his head﻿—his face all wet with tears. “Polly!” he said softly; and then, louder, “Old Poll!”
Then she rose from her knees and came to him, with a dazed look, as if she were walking in her sleep. “Who was it called me old Poll?” she asked: her voice took on it a tender playfulness: her eyes sparkled; and the rosy light of Youth flushed her pale cheeks, till she looked more like a happy girl of seventeen than a worn woman of forty. “Was that my own lad, my Willie, awaiting for me at the stile?”
His face too was transformed, in the same magic light, to the likeness of a bashful boy: and boy and girl they seemed, as he wound an arm about her, and drew her to his side, while with the other hand he thrust from him the heap of money, as though it were something hateful to the touch. “Tak it, lass,” he said, “tak it all! An’ fetch us summat to eat: but get a sup o’ milk, first, for t’ bairn.”
“My *little* bairn!” she murmured as she gathered up the coins. “My own little lassie!” Then she moved to the door, and was passing out, but a sudden thought seemed to arrest her: she hastily returned﻿—first to kneel down and kiss the sleeping child, and then to throw herself into her husband’s arms and be strained to his heart. The next moment she was on her way, taking with her a jug that hung on a peg near the door: we followed close behind.

===Chinese===

“今儿个一整天，俺一滴没沾。没有！”他扬声喊道，攋紧的拳头重重砸在桌上，抬眼望着她，目光灼灼：“往后那该死的酒，俺到死也一滴不沾了——上帝、俺的造主，给俺作证！”那陡然拔高成了嘶哑呐喊的嗓音，又陡然低了下去：他再度垂下头，把脸埋进交叠的臂弯里。
他说话时，女人已在摇篮边跪倒。她不看他，也不像听他。双手举过头顶合十，她狂乱地前后摇晃着身子。“哦上帝！哦上帝！”她翻来覆去只有这一句。
西尔维与布鲁诺轻轻掰开她合十的手，引下来——直到她一条胳膊搭住他们一个，虽然她对他们毫无察觉，只顾跪着，双眼仰望，嘴唇禽动，似在无声地感恩。男人始终埋着脸，不出一点声：可那从头到脚摇撞他的呜咽，人人*看得见*。
过了一阵他抬起头——满脸泪湿。“波莉！”他轻轻唤道；随即又唤，响些：“老波儿！”
她这才从膝上起身，向他走来，神情恍惚，像在梦游。“刚才是谁叫我老波儿来着？”她问：嗓音里透出一缕温柔的娇嗔；眼睛亮了；青春的红晕涌上她苍白的双颊，她看上去哪像四十岁操劳的妇人，分明是个十七岁的快活姑娘。“是我自己的小伙子，我的威利，在栅栏阶那儿等我么？”
在同一片魔光里，他的脸也变了个样，变作一个腼腆少年的模样：他们俨然成了一对少年情侣——他一条胳膊搜住她，把她拉到身边，另一只手却把那堆钱推远，仿佛那东西碰着都嫌脏。“拿着，丫头，”他说，“全拿着！去弄点吃的来：先给孩子弄口奶。”
“我的*小*娃儿！”她一面拢起铜钱一面喃喃。“我自己的小丫头！”说着她向门口走去，眼看要出门，一个念头又把她拽住：她匆匆折回来——先跪下去亲了亲熟睡的孩子，再一头扎进丈夫怀里，被他紧紧搜在心口。下一刻她便上了路，拎走挂在门边木钉上的瓦罐：我们紧随其后。

===Original===

We had not gone far before we came in sight of a swinging signboard bearing the word “**dairy**” on it, and here she went in, welcomed by a little curly white dog, who, not being under the “eerie” influence, saw the children, and received them with the most effusive affection. When I got inside, the dairyman was in the act of taking the money. “Is’t for thysen, Missus, or for t’ bairn?” he asked, when he had filled the jug, pausing with it in his hand.
“For t’ *bairn*!” she said, almost reproachfully. “Think’st tha I’d touch a drop *mysen*, while as *she* hadna got her fill?”
“All right, Missus,” the man replied, turning away with the jug in his hand. “Let’s just mak sure it’s good measure.” He went back among his shelves of milk-bowls, carefully keeping his back towards her while he emptied a little measure of cream into the jug, muttering to himself “mebbe it’ll hearten her up a bit, the little lassie!”
The woman never noticed the kind deed, but took back the jug with a simple “Good evening, Master,” and went her way: but the children had been more observant, and, as we followed her out, Bruno remarked “That were *welly* kind: and I loves that man: and if I was welly rich I’d give him a hundred pounds﻿—and a bun. That little grummeling dog doosn’t know its business!” He referred to the dairyman’s little dog, who had apparently quite forgotten the affectionate welcome he had given us on our arrival, and was now following at a respectful distance, doing his best to “*speed the parting guest*” with a shower of little shrill barks, that seemed to tread on one another’s heels.
“What *is* a dog’s business?” laughed Sylvie. “Dogs can’t keep shops and give change!”
“Sisters’ businesses *isn’t* to laugh at their brothers,” Bruno replied with perfect gravity. “And dogs’ businesses is to *bark*﻿—not like that: it should finish one bark before it begins another: and it should﻿—Oh Sylvie, there’s some dindledums!”
And in another moment the happy children were flying across the common, racing for the patch of dandelions.

===Chinese===

没走多远，便望见一块悬晃的招牌，上头写着“**奶坊**”两个大字；她便走了进去，一条白色卷毛小狗迎上来——它不受那“灵异”影响，看得见孩子们，对他们亲热得了不得。我进到里面时，卖奶的正接过钱。“是给*您自己*打的，孩儿他娘，还是给娃儿的？”罐子灌满了，他提在手里，顿了顿问。
“给*娃儿*的！”她说，语气近乎嗔怪。“她自己还没喝够，你想俺*自个儿*会先沾一口？”
“好嘞，孩儿他娘，”男人应着，提罐转身。“俺这就给足您分量。”他走回那一排排奶碗架间，小心背对着她，往罐里又兑了一小盅奶油，口中喃喃：“兴许能给她添点力气，这小丫头！”
女人全然没有察觉这番好意，只接回罐子，道了声“晚上好，掌柜的”，便上了路：孩子们却看得仔细，我们随她出门时，布鲁诺评论道：“那可*真*是好心：俺喜欢那个人：俺要是真有钱，就给他一百镑——外加一个面包。那条小浑球狗不会干自己的本行！”他指的是奶坊那条小狗——它显然把我们进门时那番亲热劲儿忘了个精光，这会儿正隔着老远的“恭敬距离”跟着，拼了命地要“*送走将别的客人*”——一阵尖细的狂吹，一声踩着一声的跟。
“狗的本行*是*什么呀？”西尔维笑了。“狗又不会看铺子找零钱！”
“姐姐们的本行*不是*笑话自家弟弟，”布鲁诺一本正经地答。“狗的本行是*汪汪叫*——不是那样叫：应该叫完一声再叫下一声：还应该——哦西尔维，那儿有些登登花！”
转眼间，两个快活的孩子已飞奔过公地，朝着那一片蒲公英赛起跑来。

===Original===

While I stood watching them, a strange dreamy feeling came upon me: a railway-platform seemed to take the place of the green sward, and, instead of the light figure of Sylvie bounding along, I seemed to see the flying form of Lady Muriel; but whether Bruno had also undergone a transformation, and had become the old man whom she was running to overtake, I was unable to judge, so instantaneously did the feeling come and go.
When I reentered the little sitting-room which I shared with Arthur, he was standing with his back to me, looking out of the open window, and evidently had not heard me enter. A cup of tea, apparently just tasted and pushed aside, stood on the table, on the opposite side of which was a letter, just begun, with the pen lying across it: an open book lay on the sofa: the London paper occupied the easy chair; and on the little table, which stood by it, I noticed an unlighted cigar and an open box of cigar-lights: all things betokened that the Doctor, usually so methodical and so self-contained, had been trying every form of occupation, and could settle to none!
“This is very unlike *you*, Doctor!” I was beginning, but checked myself, as he turned at the sound of my voice, in sheer amazement at the wonderful change that had taken place in his appearance. Never had I seen a face so radiant with happiness, or eyes that sparkled with such unearthly light! “Even thus,” I thought, “must the herald-angel have looked, who brought to the shepherds, watching over their flocks by night, that sweet message of ‘*peace on earth, goodwill to men*’!”
“Yes, dear friend!” he said, as if in answer to the question that I suppose he read in my face. “It is true! It is true!”
No need to ask *what* was true. “God bless you both!” I said, as I felt the happy tears brimming to my eyes. “You were made for each other!”
“Yes,” he said, simply, “I believe we were. And *what* a change it makes in one’s Life! This isn’t the same world! That isn’t the sky I saw yesterday! Those clouds﻿—I never saw such clouds in all my life before! They look like troops of hovering angels!”
To *me* they looked very ordinary clouds indeed: but then *I* had not fed “*on honeydew, And drunk the milk of Paradise*”!
“She wants to see you﻿—at once,” he continued, descending suddenly to the things of earth. “She says *that* is the *one* drop yet wanting in her cup of happiness!”

===Chinese===

我立在那里望着他们，一阵奇异的如梦之感袭来：绿草坪仿佛化作了一座火车站台，轻盈跳跃的西尔维不见了，我恍若看见穆丽尔小姐飞奔的身影；至于布鲁诺是否也变了身，变成了她追赶的那位老人，我却无从判断——那感觉来得快，去得也快。
回到我与亚瑟合用的那间小客厅时，他正背对我站着，望着敞开的窗外，显然没听见我进来。一杯茶搁在桌上，像是才呷了一口就推开了；桌子那头有一封才起了头的信，笔横搁在信纸上：沙发上摊着一本打开的书：安乐椅里躺着伦敦报纸；旁边小几上，我注意到一支未点的雪茄和一盒敞开的火柴：种种迹象表明，这位平日有条不素、沉稳自持的医生，已经把样样营生都试了一遍，却一样也安不下心！
“这可真不像*你*，医生！”我刚开口便住了口——他闻声转过身来，那容颜发生的惊人变化让我怔住了。我从没见过一张脸焕着这样的幸福之光，也没见过一双眼睛闪着这般超凡的光彩！“报信的天使向夜里看守羊群的牧人传来‘*地上平安，人间喜悦*’的甜美讯息时，”我想，“想必就是这副容颜！”
“是的，亲爱的朋友！”他说，像是回答了我猜他从我脸上读出的问题。“是真的！是真的！”
不必问*什么*是真的。“上帝保佑你们俩！”我说，幸福的泪水已涌到眼眶。“你们本是天造地设的一对！”
“是啊，”他简单地说，“我相信是的。它给一个人的生命带来*多么*大的变化！这已不是原来那个世界！那也不是我昨天望见的天！那些云——我活了一辈子没见过这样的云！它们像一队队盘旋的天使！”
在*我*看来，那分明是些再平常不过的云：可话说回来，*我*又没“以甘露为食，饮过乐园的乳汁”！
“她要见你——立刻，”他继续说，忽然落回人间的事上。“她说*那*是她幸福之杯里尚缺的*唯一*一滴！"

===Original===

“I’ll go at once,” I said, as I turned to leave the room. “Won’t you come with me?”
“No, Sir!” said the Doctor, with a sudden effort﻿—which proved an utter failure﻿—to resume his professional manner. “Do I *look* like coming with you? Have you never heard that two is company, and﻿—”
“Yes,” I said, “I *have* heard it: and I’m painfully aware that*I* am *Number Three*! But, *when* shall we three meet again?”
“*When the hurly-burly’s done!*” he answered with a happy laugh, such as I had not heard from him for many a year.

===Chinese===

“我这就去，”我说着转身要出屋。“你不一起来吗？”
“不，先生！”医生说，猛地想摆回他那执业医生的架势——结果一败涂地。“我*像*是要跟去的样子吗？你没听说过两人成伴，三人——”
“听过，”我说，“我确实听过：而且我正苦涩地自觉，*我*就是那*第三位*！不过，咱们三个*几时*再相聚呢？”
“*等这场喧鐴闹腾完了！*”他答道，发出一阵幸福的笑声——这样的笑，我已多年没听他笑过。

## VII Mein Herr / VII 我先生
===Original===

So I went on my lonely way, and, on reaching the Hall, I found Lady Muriel standing at the garden-gate waiting for me.
“No need to *give* you joy, or to *wish* you joy?” I began.
“None *whatever*!” she replied, with the joyous laugh of a child. “We *give* people what they haven’t got: we *wish* for something that is yet to come. For *me*, it’s all *here*! It’s all *mine*! Dear friend,” she suddenly broke off, “do you think Heaven ever begins on *Earth*, for any of us?”
“For *some*,” I said. “For some, perhaps, who are simple and childlike. You know He said ‘of such is the Kingdom of Heaven.’ ”
Lady Muriel clasped her hands, and gazed up into the cloudless sky, with a look I had often seen in Sylvie’s eyes. “I feel as if it had begun for *me*,” she almost whispered. “I feel as if *I* were one of the happy children, whom He bid them bring near to Him, though the people would have kept them back. Yes, He has seen me in the throng. He has read the wistful longing in my eyes. He has beckoned me to Him. They have *had* to make way for me. He has taken me up in His arms. He has put His hands upon me and blessed me!” She paused, breathless in her perfect happiness.
“Yes,” I said. “I think He has!”
“You must come and speak to my father,” she went on, as we stood side by side at the gate, looking down the shady lane. But, even as she said the words, the “eerie” sensation came over me like a flood: I saw the dear old Professor approaching us, and also saw, what was stranger still, that he was visible to *Lady Muriel*!
What was to be done? Had the fairy-life been merged in the real life? Or was Lady Muriel “eerie” also, and thus able to enter into the fairy-world along with me? The words were on my lips (“I see an old friend of mine in the lane: if you don’t know him, may I introduce him to you?”) when the strangest thing of all happened: Lady Muriel spoke.
“I see an old friend of mine in the lane,” she said: “if you don’t know him, may I introduce him to you?”
I seemed to wake out of a dream: for the “eerie” feeling was still strong upon me, and the figure outside seemed to be changing at every moment, like one of the shapes in a kaleidoscope: now he was the *Professor*, and now he was somebody else! By the time he had reached the gate, he certainly was somebody else: and I felt that the proper course was for *Lady Muriel*, not for *me*, to introduce him.

===Chinese===

于是我独自上路，到了府邸，只见穆丽尔小姐正站在园门口等我。
「不用*送*您喜、也不必*祝*您喜了吧？」我开场道。
「一概不用！」她答道，发出孩子般欢快的笑声。「*送*人东西，是送人家没有的：*祝*愿，是盼还没来的。而于我，喜*就在眼前*！全是*我的*！亲爱的朋友，」她突然收住话头，「您说，天堂可在人*间*就开始了，对谁会这样？」
「对*某些人*，」我说。「或许对那些单纯、有童心的人。您知道祂说过：『天国正是属于这样的人。』」
穆丽尔小姐双手交握，仰望着无云的天空，那神情我常在西尔维眼中见到。「我觉得它已为我开始了，」她几乎是耳语。「我觉得自己就是那些幸福的孩子中的一个——祂吩咐人把他们带到祂跟前，尽管众人想拦着不放。是的，祂在人群中看见了我。祂读出了我眼中恳切的渴慕。祂向我招了手。众人*不得不*给我让路。祂把我抱在怀中。祂按手在我身上，为我祝福！」她顿住了，在圆满的幸福里屏住了呼吸。
「是的，」我说。「我想祂正是这样！」
「您一定要来跟我父亲说说，」她接着说。我们并肩站在门口，望着树影婆娑的巷子。可就在她说话的当儿，那种「灵异」之感如潮水般漫过我全身：我看见亲爱的老教授正向我们走来，还看见一件更奇怪的事——穆丽尔小姐也*看得见*他！
这可如何是好？难道仙界的生活已融进了现实生活？还是穆丽尔小姐也有这份「灵异」，因而能随我一同进入仙界？话已到我唇边（「我看见巷子里有位我的老朋友：您若不认识，可否容我引见？」）——就在这时，最奇怪的事发生了：穆丽尔小姐开了口。
「我看见巷子里有位我的老朋友，」她说，「您若不认识，可否容我引见？」
我仿佛从梦中醒来：因为「灵异」之感仍重重笼罩着我，门外那个身影似乎每一刻都在变，像万花筒里的图案：这一刻他是*教授*，那一刻又是别人！待他走到门前，他确实是别人了：我觉得理应由*穆丽尔小姐*、而不是由*我*来引见。

===Original===

She greeted him kindly, and, opening the gate, admitted the venerable old man﻿—a German, obviously﻿—who looked about him with dazed eyes, as if *he*, too, had but just awaked from a dream!
No, it was certainly *not* the Professor! My old friend *could* not have grown that magnificent beard since last we met: moreover, he would have recognised *me*, for I was certain that *I* had not changed much in the time.
As it was, he simply looked at me vaguely, and took off his hat in response to Lady Muriel’s words “Let me introduce Mein Herr to you”; while in the words, spoken in a strong German accent, “proud to make your acquaintance, Sir!” I could detect no trace of an idea that we had ever met before.
Lady Muriel led us to the well-known shady nook, where preparations for afternoon tea had already been made, and, while she went in to look for the Earl, we seated ourselves in two easy-chairs, and “Mein Herr” took up Lady Muriel’s work, and examined it through his large spectacles (one of the adjuncts that made him so provokingly like the Professor). “Hemming pocket-handkerchiefs?” he said, musingly. “So *that* is what the English miladies occupy themselves with, is it?”
“It is the one accomplishment,” I said, “in which Man has never yet rivaled Woman!”
Here Lady Muriel returned with her father; and, after he had exchanged some friendly words with “Mein Herr,” and we had all been supplied with the needful “creature-comforts,” the newcomer returned to the suggestive subject of Pocket-handkerchiefs.
“You have heard of Fortunatus’s Purse, Miladi? Ah, so! Would you be surprised to hear that, with three of these leetle handkerchiefs, you shall make the Purse of Fortunatus, quite soon, quite easily?”
“Shall I indeed?” Lady Muriel eagerly replied, as she took a heap of them into her lap, and threaded her needle. “*Please* tell me how, Mein Herr! I’ll make one before I touch another drop of tea!”
“You shall first,” said Mein Herr, possessing himself of two of the handkerchiefs, spreading one upon the other, and holding them up by two corners, “you shall first join together these upper corners, the right to the right, the left to the left; and the opening between them shall be the *mouth* of the Purse.”
A very few stitches sufficed to carry out *this* direction. “Now, if I sew the other three edges together,” she suggested, “the bag is complete?”

===Chinese===

她和善地招呼他，打开园门，请进这位可敬的老人——一看便知是位德国人——他两眼茫然地四下张望，仿佛*他*也才从梦中醒来！
不，这肯定*不是*教授！我的老朋友不可能在上次见面之后长出这么一部漂亮的大胡子：况且，他也该认出*我*来，因为我确信自己这些年没怎么变。
可他只是朝我漠然望了一眼，听了穆丽尔小姐那句「容我给您引见『我先生』（Mein Herr）」，便脱帽为礼；而那句带着浓重德国口音的「幸会幸会，先生！」里，我察觉不出一丝一毫「我们从前见过」的意思。
穆丽尔小姐领我们到那处大家熟识的树荫角落，午后茶的茶具已经摆好；她进屋去找伯爵，我们便在两把安乐椅里落座，「我先生」拿起穆丽尔小姐的针线活，透过他的大眼镜细看（这正是他气人的地方——处处像极了教授）。「给手帕缝边？」他若有所思地说。「原来*英国*的贵夫人们忙的就是这个呀，是不是？」
「这可是绝无仅有的一门本事，」我说，「男人至今没能同女人较量过的，独此一样！」
这时穆丽尔小姐同她父亲回来了；伯爵同「我先生」寒暄了几句，我们也都领到了必要的「口腹之慰」，新来的客人便又回到了手帕这个启人遐思的话题上。
「您听说过福图纳图斯的钱袋吧，夫人？啊，是的！要是听说用三条这小小的手帕，您就能做成一只福图纳图斯的钱袋，又快又容易，您会吃惊吗？」
「当真？」穆丽尔小姐急切地应道，一面把一小堆手帕拢到膝上，一面穿针。「*快*教教我怎么做，我先生！不喝下一滴茶，我就先做出一只来！」
「您先这样，」我先生说着，取过两条手帕，一条覆在另一条上，捏住两只角提起来，「您先把这些上角缝到一起，右边对右边，左边对左边；中间的开口呢，就算钱袋的*袋口*。」
依言缝了几针便成了。「这会儿只要把另外三条边缝上，」她提议道，「袋子就算完工了吧？

===Original===

“Not so, Miladi: the *lower* edges shall *first* be joined﻿—ah, not so!” (as she was beginning to sew them together). “Turn one of them over, and join the *right* lower corner of the one to the *left* lower corner of the other, and sew the lower edges together in what you would call *the wrong way*.”
“*I* see!” said Lady Muriel, as she deftly executed the order. “And a very twisted, uncomfortable, uncanny-looking bag it makes! But the *moral* is a lovely one. Unlimited wealth can only be attained by doing things *in the wrong way*! And how are we to join up these mysterious﻿—no, I mean *this* mysterious opening?” (twisting the thing round and round with a puzzled air.) “Yes, it *is* one opening. I thought it was *two*, at first.”
“You have seen the puzzle of the Paper Ring?” Mein Herr said, addressing the Earl. “Where you take a slip of paper, and join its ends together, first twisting one, so as to join the *upper* corner of *one* end to the *lower* corner of the *other*?”
“I saw one made, only yesterday,” the Earl replied. “Muriel, my child, were you not making one, to amuse those children you had to tea?”
“Yes, I know that Puzzle,” said Lady Muriel. “The Ring has only *one* surface, and only *one* edge. It’s very mysterious!”
“The *bag* is just like that, isn’t it?” I suggested. “Is not the *outer* surface of one side of it continuous with the *inner* surface of the other side?”
“So it is!” she exclaimed. “Only it *isn’t* a bag, just yet. How shall we fill up this opening, Mein Herr?”
“Thus!” said the old man impressively, taking the bag from her, and rising to his feet in the excitement of the explanation. “The edge of the opening consists of *four* handkerchief-edges, and you can trace it continuously, round and round the opening: down the right edge of *one* handkerchief, up the left edge of the *other*, and then down the left edge of the *one*, and up the right edge of the *other*!”
“So you can!” Lady Muriel murmured thoughtfully, leaning her head on her hand, and earnestly watching the old man. “And that *proves* it to be only *one* opening!”
She looked so strangely like a child, puzzling over a difficult lesson, and Mein Herr had become, for the moment, so strangely like the old Professor, that I felt utterly bewildered: the “eerie” feeling was on me in its full force, and I felt almost *impelled* to say “Do you understand it, Sylvie?”

===Chinese===

「不是这样，夫人：要先缝*下*边——啊，不是那样！」（她刚开始把两边往一块儿缝。）「把其中一条翻个面，把这条的*右*下角同那条的*左*下角对上，再照您们的说法，*反着*把两条下边缝到一起。」
「*我*明白了！」穆丽尔小姐一面利落地照办，一面说。「缝出来这么一条扭扭歪歪、别别扭扭、透着邪气的袋子！可这*寓意*妙极了：无边的财富，唯有*倒着做事*才能到手！那么这几个神秘的——不对，我是说*这一个*神秘的口子，该怎么缝上呢？」（她一脸困惑，把那东西翻来覆去地转。）「对，它*是*一个口子。起初我还当是*两个*呢。」
「您见过纸环那个戏法吧？」我先生转而对伯爵说。「取一条纸带，把两端接起来，先捾转其中一端，让这端的*上*角对上那端的*下*角，您见过吧？」
「昨天还见人做过一个呢，」伯爵答道。「穆丽尔，我的孩子，你不就是做了一个，逗那些来喝茶的孩子们吗？」
「对，我知道那个戏法，」穆丽尔小姐说。「那环只有*一个*面，也只有*一条*边。神秘得很！」
「这*袋子*正是一理，对不对？」我插言道。「它这一侧的*外*面，不就是同那一侧的*里*面连成一体的吗？」
「可不是嘛！」她喊道。「只可惜眼下还*算不得*一只袋子。这个口子可怎么补上呢，我先生？」
「这样！」老人郑重其事地说，从她手里接过袋子，讲到兴头，竟站起身来。「开口的边由手帕的*四条*边组成，您可以顺着它绕着口子一路追下去：沿*这条*手帕的右边下去，沿*那条*的左边上来，再沿*这条*的左边下去，沿*那条*的右边上来！」
「真的呢！」穆丽尔小姐一手托腮，若有所思地喃喃道，热切地注视着老人。「这就*证明*了它只有*一个*口子！」
她那模样活脱脱像个对着难题发愁的孩子，而「我先生」这一刻又活脱脱像极了老教授，我简直晕头转向：「灵异」之感将我彻底淹没，我几乎*忍不住*要脱口说：「你听得懂吗，西尔维？」

===Original===

However I checked myself by a great effort, and let the dream (if indeed it *was* a dream) go on to its end.
“Now, this *third* handkerchief,” Mein Herr proceeded, “has *also* four edges, which you can trace continuously round and round: all you need do is to join its four edges to the four edges of the opening. The Purse is then complete, and its outer surface﻿—”
“*I* see!” Lady Muriel eagerly interrupted. “Its *outer* surface will be continuous with its *inner* surface! But it will take time. I’ll sew it up after tea.” She laid aside the bag and resumed her cup of tea. “But why do you call it Fortunatus’s Purse, Mein Herr?”
The dear old man beamed upon her, with a jolly smile, looking more exactly like the Professor than ever. “Don’t you see, my child﻿—I should say Miladi? Whatever is *inside* that Purse, is *outside* it; and whatever is *outside* it, is *inside* it. So you have all the wealth of the world in that leetle Purse!”
His pupil clapped her hands, in unrestrained delight. “I’ll certainly sew the third handkerchief in﻿—*some* time,” she said: “but I won’t take up your time by trying it now. Tell us some more wonderful things, please!” And her face and her voice so *exactly* recalled Sylvie, that I could not help glancing round, half-expecting to see *Bruno* also!
Mein Herr began thoughtfully balancing his spoon on the edge of his teacup, while he pondered over this request. “Something wonderful﻿—like Fortunatus’s Purse? *That* will give you﻿—when it is made﻿—wealth beyond your wildest dreams: but it will not give you *Time*!”
A pause of silence ensued﻿—utilised by Lady Muriel for the very practical purpose of refilling the teacups.
“In *your* country,” Mein Herr began with a startling abruptness, “what becomes of all the wasted Time?”
Lady Muriel looked grave. “Who can tell?” she half-whispered to herself. “All one knows is that it is gone﻿—past recall!”
“Well, in *my*﻿—I mean in a country *I* have visited,” said the old man, “they store it up: and it comes in *very* useful, years afterwards! For example, suppose you have a long tedious evening before you: nobody to talk to: nothing you care to do: and yet hours too soon to go to bed. How do *you* behave then?”
“I get *very* cross,” she frankly admitted: “and I want to throw things about the room!”
“When that happens to﻿—to the people I have visited, they never act *so*.
