但我竭力忍住了，听任这场梦（倘若它*当真*是梦）走到它的尽头。
「喏，这*第三*条手帕，」我先生接着说，「也*同样*有四条边，可以一圈一圈连续追下去：您只须把它的四条边同开口的四条边一一缝上。钱袋便大功告成，而它的外面——」
「*我*懂了！」穆丽尔小姐急切地插嘴。「它的*外*面会同它的*里*面连成一体！不过这得费些工夫。我喝完茶再缝。」她把袋子放到一边，重新端起茶杯。「可您为什么管它叫福图纳图斯的钱袋呢，我先生？」
亲爱的老人含着快活的笑意望着她，比先前更像教授了。「您还不明白吗，我的孩子——我该说夫人？凡在钱袋*里*面的东西，就都在它*外*面；凡在它*外*面的，又都在它*里*面。如此一来，全世界的财富您都装进这小小的钱袋里了！」
他的学生喜不自胜，拍起手来。「第三条手帕我*一定*会缝进去的——找机会，」她说，「可现在再试就占用您的时间了。请再给我们讲些妙事儿吧！」她的脸、她的嗓音同西尔维像得*分毫不差*，我忍不住四下张望，半以为还会看见*布鲁诺*呢！
我先生若有所思地把茶匙竖在茶杯边上玩着平衡，一边掂量这个请求。「妙事儿——像福图纳图斯钱袋那样的？*那*东西——做成之后——会给您做梦也不敢想的财富：可它给不了您*时间*！」
接着一阵沉默——穆丽尔小姐趁这工夫干了件顶实际的事：给各人续茶。
「在*贵*国，」我先生突兀得吓人地开了口，「浪费掉的时间都上哪儿去了？」
穆丽尔小姐神色凝重。「谁能说清呢？」她半是自语地低声道。「人只知道它去了——再也唤不回了！」
「唔，在*我*的——我是说，在我*到过*的一个国家，」老人说，「人们把它贮存起来：多年之后，*大*有用场！譬如说，假定您面前横着一个漫长无聊的夜晚：没人可说话：没有想干的事：可离睡觉又还早得很。那时*您*会怎么样？」
「我会*非常*烦躁，」她坦率地承认：「还想满屋子摔东西！」
「遇上这种时候，我到过的那些人呀，从来不*这么*办。」

===Original===

By a short and simple process﻿—which I cannot explain to you﻿—they store up the useless hours: and, on some *other* occasion, when they happen to *need* extra time, they get them out again!”
The Earl was listening with a slightly incredulous smile. “Why cannot you *explain* the process?” he enquired.
Mein Herr was ready with a quite unanswerable reason. “Because you have no *words*, in *your* language, to convey the ideas which are needed. I could explain it in﻿—in﻿—but you would not understand it!”
“No indeed!” said Lady Muriel, graciously dispensing with the *name* of the unknown language. “I never learnt it﻿—at least, not to speak it *fluently*, you know. *Please* tell us some more wonderful things!”
“They run their railway-trains without any engines﻿—nothing is needed but machinery to *stop* them with. Is *that* wonderful enough, Miladi?”
“But where does the *force* come from?” I ventured to ask.
Mein Herr turned quickly round, to look at the new speaker. Then he took off his spectacles, and polished them, and looked at me again, in evident bewilderment. I could see he was thinking﻿—as indeed *I* was also﻿—that we *must* have met before.
“They use the force of *gravity*,” he said. “It is a force known also in *your* country, I believe?”
“But that would need a railway going *downhill*,” the Earl remarked. “You can’t have *all* your railways going downhill?”
“They *all* do,” said Mein Herr.
“Not from *both* ends?”
“From *both* ends.”
“Then I give it up!” said the Earl.
“Can you explain the process?” said Lady Muriel. “Without using that language, that I can’t speak fluently?”
“Easily,” said Mein Herr. “Each railway is in a long tunnel, perfectly straight: so of course the *middle* of it is nearer the centre of the globe than the two ends: so every train runs halfway *down*-hill, and that gives it force enough to run the *other* half *up*-hill.”
“Thank you. I understand that perfectly,” said Lady Muriel. “But the velocity, in the *middle* of the tunnel, must be something *fearful*!”
“Mein Herr” was evidently much gratified at the intelligent interest Lady Muriel took in his remarks. At every moment the old man seemed to grow more chatty and more fluent. “You would like to know our methods of *driving*?” he smilingly enquired. “To us, a runaway horse is of no import at all!”
Lady Muriel slightly shuddered. “To *us* it is a very real danger,” she said.

===Chinese===

「靠一道简便的工序——这工序我没法向您解释——他们把无用的钟点贮存起来：等到哪一回碰巧*需要*多余的时间，再取出来用！」
伯爵带着一丝将信将疑的微笑听着。「您为什么*不能*解释这工序呢？」他问。
我先生早备好了一条全然无从反驳的理由。「因为*贵*国的语言里没有*词汇*，能传达所需的意思。我可以用——用——可说了你们也听不懂！」
「那自然！」穆丽尔小姐说，宽宏大量地免去了那门未知语言的*名称*。「我从没学过——至少没能把它说得*流利*，您知道。*请*再给我们讲些妙事儿吧！」
「他们的火车不用机车——只需要配备*刹车*的机械。这够妙的了吧，夫人？」
「可*动力*打哪儿来呢？」我冒昧问了一句。
我先生矍地转过身来打量这位新发言的人。接着他摘下眼镜擦了擦，再看我，一脸的困惑。我看得出他在想——其实*我*也在想——咱们*必定*从前见过。
「他们用的是*重力*，」他说。「这力量我想*贵*国也有吧？」
「可那就得铁路一路*下坡*呀，」伯爵说。「总不能*所有*铁路都下坡吧？」
「*全都*下坡，」我先生说。
「总不能*两头*都下坡吧？」
「*两头*都下坡。」
「那我就认输了！」伯爵说。
「您能把原理讲讲吗？」穆丽尔小姐说。「不用那种我说不流利的语言。」
「容易，」我先生说。「每条铁路都藏在一条笔直的长隧道里：如此一来，隧道*中段*自然比两端更接近地心：于是每列火车都是半程*下*坡，这半程给它的力量，足够它跑完另外半程的*上*坡。」
「谢谢。我完全听懂了，」穆丽尔小姐说。「可是隧道*正中*的速度，一定*吓死人*吧！」
穆丽尔小姐对他的话听得这样入耳动心，「我先生」显然大为受用。老人每一刻都显得更健谈、更滔滔不绝。「您想听听我们*赶车*的法子吗？」他笑吟吟地问。「在我们那儿，马惊了根本不算一回事！」
穆丽尔小姐轻轻打了个寒噤。「在*我们*这儿，那可是千真万确的危险，」她说。

===Original===

“That is because your carriage is wholly *behind* your horse. Your horse runs. Your carriage follows. Perhaps your horse has the bit in his teeth. Who shall stop him? You fly, ever faster and faster! Finally comes the inevitable upset!”
“But suppose *your* horse manages to get the bit in his teeth?”
“No matter! We would not concern ourselves. Our horse is harnessed in the very centre of our carriage. Two wheels are in front of him, and two behind. To the roof is attached one end of a broad belt. This goes under the horse’s body, and the other end is attached to a leetle﻿—what you call a ‘windlass,’ I think. The horse takes the bit in his teeth. He runs away. We are flying at ten miles an hour! We turn our little windlass, five turns, six turns, seven turns, and﻿—poof! Our horse is off the ground! *Now* let him gallop in the air, as much as he pleases: our *carriage* stands still. We sit round him, and watch him till he is tired. Then we let him down. Our horse is glad, very much glad, when his feet once more touch the ground!”
“Capital!” said the Earl, who had been listening attentively. “Are there any other peculiarities in your carriages?”
“In the *wheels*, sometimes, my Lord. For your health, *you* go to sea: to be pitched, to be rolled, occasionally to be drowned. *We* do all that on land: we are pitched, as you; we are rolled, as you; but *drowned*, no! There is no water!”
“What are the wheels like, then?”
“They are *oval*, my Lord. Therefore the carriages rise and fall.”
“Yes, and pitch the carriage backwards and forwards: but how do they make it *roll*?”
“They do not match, my Lord. The *end* of one wheel answers to the *side* of the opposite wheel. So first one side of the carriage rises, then the other. And it pitches all the while. Ah, you must be a good sailor, to drive in our boat-carriages!”
“I can easily believe it,” said the Earl.
Mein Herr rose to his feet. “I must leave you now, Miladi,” he said, consulting his watch. “I have another engagement.”
“I only wish we had stored up some extra time!” Lady Muriel said, as she shook hands with him. “Then we could have kept you a little longer!”
“In *that* case I would gladly stay,” replied Mein Herr. “As it is﻿—I fear I must say goodbye!”
“Where did you first meet him?” I asked Lady Muriel, when Mein Herr had left us. “And where does he live? And what is his real name?”

===Chinese===

「那是因为贵国的车厢整个儿*拖*在马*后*。马跑。车随。也许马咬住了嚼子。谁来拦它？您飞驰，越飞越快！最后免不了翻车完事！」
「可要是*贵国*的马也咬住了嚼子呢？」
「不要紧！我们毫不在意。我们的马套在车厢的*正中央*。它前头两只轮子，后头也是两只。车顶上系着一条宽皮带的一端。皮带从马身子底下穿过，另一端绕上一只小小的——按贵国的叫法，是『绞盘』吧。马咬住了嚼子。它狂奔起来。我们正以每小时十英里的速度飞驰！我们摇起小绞盘，摇五圈，六圈，七圈——呼！马离了地！*这会儿*随它在半空中撒开四蹄跑个痛快：咱们的*车厢*纹丝不动。我们围着它坐，看着它直到跑累。然后把它放下来。四蹄重新落地时，我们的马呀，高兴，非常非常高兴！」
「妙极了！」一直凝神听的伯爵说。「贵国的马车还有旁的妙处吗？」
「有时妙在*轮子*上，大人。贵国人为养生，*坐船*出海：颠簸，摇晃，偶尔淹死。这些事*我们*在陆地上全办了：颠簸，同你们一样；摇晃，同你们一样；只是淹死——不会！没有水！」
「那轮子是什么样的呢？」
「是*椭圆形*的，大人。所以车身一起一伏。」
「是啊，还会把车前后颠簸：可它怎么让车*摇晃*呢？」
「四只轮子并不一样，大人。这边一只轮子的*一头*，对着对面一只轮子的*一侧*。于是车身先这边翘起，再那边翘起。同时一直颠个不停。啊，要在我们的船车里赶路，您可得先做个好水手！」
「这我完全相信，」伯爵说。
我先生站起身来。「我得告辞了，夫人，」他看看表说。「另有一个约。」
「真希望我们也存下了些多余的时间！」穆丽尔小姐同他握手时说。「那样就能多留您一会儿了！」
「*若是*那样，我乐意奉陪，」我先生答道。「如今这样——恕我只好告辞了！」
「您最初是在哪儿遇见他的？」等他走后我问穆丽尔小姐。「他住在哪儿？他到底姓甚名谁？」

===Original===

“We first﻿—met﻿—him﻿—” she musingly replied, “really, I can’t remember *where*! And I’ve no idea where he lives! And I never heard any other name! It’s very curious. It never occurred to me before to consider what a mystery he is!”
“I hope we shall meet again,” I said: “he interests me very much.”
“He will be at our farewell-party, this day fortnight,” said the Earl. “Of course you will come? Muriel is anxious to gather all our friends around us once more, before we leave the place.”
And then he explained to me﻿—as Lady Muriel had left us together﻿—that he was so anxious to get his daughter away from a place full of so many painful memories connected with the now-canceled engagement with Major Lindon, that they had arranged to have the wedding in a months time, after which Arthur and his wife were to go on a foreign tour.
“Don’t forget Tuesday week!” he said as we shook hands at parting. “I only wish you could bring with you those charming children, that you introduced to us in the summer. Talk of the mystery of Mein Herr! That’s *nothing* to the mystery that seems to attend *them*! I shall never forget those marvellous flowers!”
“I will bring them if I possibly can,” I said. But how to *fulfil* such a promise, I mused to myself on my way back to our lodgings, was a problem entirely beyond my skill!

===Chinese===

「我们最初——是在哪儿——遇见他的，」她若有所思地答道，「说真的，我想不起*在哪儿*了！他住在哪儿，我也毫无所知！旁的名字，我更是从没听人叫过！真是怪事。我以前竟从没想过，他是个多大的谜！」
「但愿我们后会有期，」我说：「他令我很感兴趣。」
「两周后的今天，他要来参加我们的告别会，」伯爵说。「您自然会来的吧？穆丽尔盼着在离开此地之前，把我们的朋友再齐聚一堂。」
接着他向我解释——这时穆丽尔小姐已撇下我们自去了——他一心想让女儿离开这个处处勾起伤心回忆的地方：那都是同林登少校那桩如今已作罔的婚约连着的；他们已安排一个月后完婚，随后亚瑟携妻子出国游历。
「下星期二可别忘了！」分手握手时他说。「只盼您能把夏天您引见给我们的那两个可爱的孩子也带来。说到『我先生』之神秘——同缠绕着*他们*的那团神秘相比，简直*不算什么*！那些奇妙的花，我一辈子也忘不了！」
「只要办得到，我一定带来，」我说。可这样一句承诺*如何兑现*——回寓所的路上我暗自思忖——实在是一道全然超出我本领的难题！

## VIII In a Shady Place / 八 绿荫之下
===Original===

The ten days glided swiftly away: and, the day before the great party was to take place, Arthur proposed that we should stroll down to the Hall, in time for afternoon-tea.
“Hadn’t you better go *alone*?” I suggested. “Surely *I* shall be very much *de trop*?”
“Well, it’ll be a kind of *experiment*,” he said. “*Fiat experimentum in corpore vili!*” he added, with a graceful bow of mock politeness towards the unfortunate victim. “You see I shall have to bear the sight, tomorrow night, of my ladylove making herself agreable to everybody *except* the right person, and I shall bear the agony all the better if we have a dress-rehearsal beforehand!”
“*My* part in the play being, apparently, that of the sample *wrong* person?”
“Well, no,” Arthur said musingly, as we set forth: “there’s no such part in a regular company. ‘Heavy Father’? *That* won’t do: that’s filled already. ‘Singing Chambermaid’? Well, the ‘First Lady’ doubles *that* part. ‘Comic Old Man’? You’re not comic enough. After all, I’m afraid there’s no part for you but the ‘Well-dressed Villain’: only,” with a critical side-glance, “I’m a *leetle* uncertain about the dress!”
We found Lady Muriel alone, the Earl having gone out to make a call, and at once resumed old terms of intimacy, in the shady arbour where the tea-things seemed to be always waiting. The only novelty in the arrangements (one which Lady Muriel seemed to regard as *entirely* a matter of course), was that two of the chairs were placed *quite* close together, side by side. Strange to say, *I* was not invited to occupy *either* of them!
“We have been arranging, as we came along, about letter-writing,” Arthur began. “He will want to know how we’re enjoying our Swiss tour: and of course we must pretend we *are*?”
“Of course,” she meekly assented.
“And the skeleton-in-the-cupboard﻿—” I suggested.
“﻿—is always a difficulty,” she quickly put in, “when you’re traveling about, and when there are no cupboards in the hotels. However, *ours* is a *very* portable one; and will be neatly packed, in a nice leather case﻿—”
“But please don’t think about *writing*,” I said, “when you’ve anything more attractive on hand. I delight in *reading* letters, but I know well how tiring it is to *write* them.”
“It *is*, sometimes,” Arthur assented. “For instance, when you’re very shy of the person you have to write to.”
“Does that show itself in the *letter*?”

===Chinese===

十天光阴倏忽而过：盛大的宴会前一日，亚瑟提议我们散步去府邸，赶下午茶。
“你*一个人*去不是更好？”我提议道。“有*我*在场，岂不十分*碍事*？”
“呃，这算是种*实验*吧，”他说。“*就让实验做在贱体上吧！*”他补了一句，朝那不幸的牺牲品风度翩翩、佯装恭敬地一躬身。“你想，明晚我得眼睁睁看着我我的心上人对人人献殷勤，唯独对那个正主儿不——事先彩排一回，正式受刑时也好受些！”
“而*我*在戏里的角色，显然就是‘错误人选’的样板啦？”
“呃，倒也不是，”动身时亚瑟若有所思地说：“正经戏班子里没这个角色。‘严厉老父’？*那*不行：已有人当了。‘唱歌的小丫鬟’？嗯，‘女主角’兼着*那*角儿呢。‘滑稽老头’？你滑稽得不够。说到底，怕是只剩‘衣冠楚楚的反派’给你当了：只是，”他挑剔地斜睨一眼，“那身衣裳，我有点儿*拿不准*！”
穆丽尔小姐独自在家——伯爵出门拜客去了——在那座凉荫习习的藤架下，我们立刻重拾旧日的亲昵，茶具仿佛永远在那里候着。布置上唯一的新鲜之处（穆丽尔小姐似乎*全然*视之为理所当然），是有两把椅子*紧紧*相并、双双揸在一处。说来奇怪，*我*可没受邀坐*其中任何一把*！
“来的一路上，我们正商量写信的事，”亚瑟起头道。“他会想知道我们瑞士之游玩得如何：当然，咱们得装作*玩得*尽兴？”
“当然，”她温顺地应道。
“还有柜中髅骸——”我提了一句。
“——向来是件难事，”她赶紧接上，“人在旅途，旅馆里连柜子都没有。好在*我们*那具*非常*轻便；可以妥妥帖帖装进一只漂亮的皮箱——”
“不过，手上若有更要紧的好事，请千万别惦记*写信*，”我说。“我*读*信读得快活，可*写*信有多累人，我是深知的。”
“有时*确实*累人，”亚瑟附和。“比如，你对收信的人羞于启齿的时候。”
“这种羞怯会在*信*里露出来吗？”

===Original===

Lady Muriel enquired. “Of course, when I hear anyone *talking*﻿—*you*, for instance﻿—I can see how *desperately* shy he is! But can you see that in a *letter*?”
“Well, of course, when you hear anyone talk *fluently*﻿—*you*, for instance﻿—you can see how desperately *un*-shy she is﻿—not to say saucy! But the shyest and most intermittent talker must *seem* fluent in letter-writing. He may have taken half-an-hour to *compose* his second sentence; but there it is, close after the first!”
“Then letters don’t express all that they *might* express?”
“That’s merely because our system of letter-writing is incomplete. A shy writer *ought* to be able to show that he is so. Why shouldn’t he make *pauses* in writing, just as he would do in speaking? He might leave blank spaces﻿—say half a page at a time. And a *very* shy girl﻿—if there *is* such a thing﻿—might write a sentence on the *first* sheet of her letter﻿—then put in a couple of *blank* sheets﻿—then a sentence on the *fourth* sheet: and so on.”
“I quite foresee that *we*﻿—I mean this clever little boy and myself﻿—” Lady Muriel said to me, evidently with the kind wish to bring me into the conversation, “﻿—are going to become famous﻿—of course all our inventions are common property now﻿—for a new Code of Rules for Letter-writing! Please invent some more, little boy!”
“Well, another thing *greatly* needed, little girl, is some way of expressing that we *don’t* mean anything.”
“Explain yourself, little boy! Surely *you* can find no difficulty in expressing a *total* absence of meaning?”
“I mean that you should be able, when you *don’t* mean a thing to be taken seriously, to express that wish. For human nature is so constituted that whatever you write seriously is taken as a joke, and whatever you mean as a joke is taken seriously! At any rate, it is so in writing to a *lady*!”
“Ah! you’re not used to writing to ladies!” Lady Muriel remarked, leaning back in her chair, and gazing thoughtfully into the sky. “You should try.”
“Very good,” said Arthur. “How many ladies may I begin writing to? As many as I can count on the fingers of both hands?”
“As many as you can count on the *thumbs* of *one* hand!” his ladylove replied with much severity. “What a *very* naughty little boy he is! *Isn’t* he?” (with an appealing glance at me).
“He’s a little fractious,” I said. “Perhaps he’s cutting a tooth.”

===Chinese===

穆丽尔小姐问道。“听人*说话*时——比如听*你*——我自然看得出他羞得多么*厉害*！可在*信*里也看得出来吗？”
“呃，当然，听人说话*流利*时——比如听*你*——也看得出她多么*一*点儿也不羞——简直称得上泼辣！可再羞怯、再断断续续的说话人，写起信来必定*显得*流利。他的第二句也许*琢磿*了半个钟头；可落在纸上，紧挨着第一句！”
“这么说，信表达不出它*本可以*表达的一切？”
“那只是因为我们写信的体系尚不完善。羞怯的写信人*理应*能表明自己是羞怯的。他何不照说话那样，在信里留*停顿*？他可以留出空白——比如一次半页。而一个*非常*羞怯的姑娘——倘若世上*真*有这号人——可以只在信的*第一*页写一句话——随后夹进两页*白*纸——再到*第四*页写上一句：如此以往。”
“我完全预见到*我们*——我是说这位聪明的小男孩和我自己——”穆丽尔小姐对我说，显然存心好意要把我拉进谈话，“——将要成名了——当然，我们的一切发明如今已属公共财产——凭一部全新的《书信写作守则》！再发明几条嘛，小男孩！”
“呢，还有一样东西*大大*地缺着呢，小女孩：一种法子，表明我们*并不*含什么深意。”
“讲讲清楚，小男孩！*你*要表达*完全没有*含义，想必不会有什么困难吧？”
“我是说，当*不*希望一句话被当真时，你应当能把这个愿望表达出来。因为人性天生如此：你正正经经写下的，被当作玩笑；你存心玩笑的，倒被当了真！至少，给*女士*写信时就是这样！”
“啊哈！你是不惯给女士写信！”穆丽尔小姐说着，仰进椅背，若有所思地望天。“该练练。”
“很好，”亚瑟说。“我可以开始给多少位女士写信？两只手的手指头数得过来的那么多位？”
“*一只*手的*大拇指*数得过来的那么些位！”他的心上人极严厉地回答。“这小男孩多*淘气*呀！*是不是*？”（说着向我投来求援的一瞥。）
“他有点闹觉，”我说。“许是在出牙呢。”

===Original===

While to myself I said “How *exactly* like Sylvie talking to Bruno!”
“He wants his tea.” (The naughty little boy volunteered the information.) “He’s getting very tired, at the mere *prospect* of the great party tomorrow!”
“Then he shall have a good rest beforehand!” she soothingly replied. “The tea isn’t made yet. Come, little boy, lean well back in your chair, and think about nothing﻿—or about *me*, whichever you prefer!”
“All the same, all the same!” Arthur sleepily murmured, watching her with loving eyes, as she moved her chair away to the tea table, and began to make the tea. “Then he’ll wait for his tea, like a good, patient little boy!”
“Shall I bring you the London Papers?” said Lady Muriel. “I saw them lying on the table as I came out, but my father said there was nothing in them, except that horrid murder-trial.” (Society was just then enjoying its daily thrill of excitement in studying the details of a specially sensational murder in a thieves’ den in the East of London.)
“I have no appetite for horrors,” Arthur replied. “But I hope we have learned the lesson they should teach us﻿—though we are very apt to read it backwards!”
“You speak in riddles,” said Lady Muriel. “Please explain yourself. See now,” suiting the action to the word, “I am sitting at your feet, just as if you were a second Gamaliel! Thanks, no.” (This was to me, who had risen to bring her chair back to its former place.) “Pray don’t disturb yourself. This tree and the grass make a very nice easy-chair. *What* is the lesson that one always reads wrong?”
Arthur was silent for a minute. “I would like to be clear what it *is* I mean,” he said, slowly and thoughtfully, “before I say anything to *you*﻿—because you *think* about it.”
Anything approaching to a compliment was so unusual an utterance for Arthur, that it brought a flush of pleasure to her cheek, as she replied “It is *you*, that give me the ideas to think about.”
“One’s first thought,” Arthur proceeded, “in reading of anything specially vile or barbarous, as done by a fellow-creature, is apt to be that we see a new depth of Sin revealed *beneath* us: and we seem to gaze down into that abyss from some higher ground, far apart from it.”
“I think I understand you now.

===Chinese===

心里我却暗道：“这多*像*西尔维同布鲁诺说话呀！”
“他要喝茶了。”（那淘气的小男孩自告奋勇地通报。）“光是想到明天的盛会，他就累得不行了！”
“那先让他好好歇歇！”她温言抚慰道。“茶还没沏呢。来，小男孩，在椅子上好好靠牢，什么都别想——或者想*我*，随你挑！”
“都一样，都一样！”亚瑟睡意蒙眬地喃喃道，一双含情的眼睛望着她把椅子挪到茶桌旁，动手沏茶。“那他就乖乖等茶，做个有耐心的好孩子！”
“要我给你拿伦敦报纸来吗？”穆丽尔小姐说。“我出来时见它们搁在桌上，只是父亲说里面没什么东西，除了那桩骇人的凶杀案审理。”（当时上流社会正沉浸在其每日的刺激里，细细咀嚼伦敦东区贼窝里一桩格外耸人听闻的凶案的种种细节。）
“我对骇人听闻的事没有胃口，”亚瑟答道。“不过我希望，我们已领受了它们该教给我们的功课——尽管我们极容易把它倒着读！”
“你打起哑谜来了，”穆丽尔小姐说。“请讲明白些。看，”她边说边做，“我这就坐在你脚边，只当你是一位伽马列第二！多谢，不必。”（这句是对我说的，我起身想把她的椅子搬回原处。）“千万别费事。这棵树和这片草地就是顶好的安乐椅。那条人人都会读错的功课，*究竟*是什么呀？”
亚瑟沉默了一分钟。“我想先弄清楚自己*究竟*想说什么，”他缓缓地、若有所思地说，“然后再对*你*讲——因为你会*思*考它。”
亚瑟的话里，凡近于恭维的都如此罕见，她双颊顿时泛起喜悦的红晕，答道：“是*你*，给了我可供思考的念头。”
“读到同胞做出格外邪恶或野蛮的事，”亚瑟继续道，“人的第一个念头，往往是觉得自己看见了在*我们脚下*揭开的又一层罪的深渊：我们仿佛站在高地上，远远俯瞰那深渊。”
“我想我明白你的意思了。

===Original===

You mean that one ought to think﻿—not ‘God, I thank Thee that I am not as other men are’﻿—but ‘God, be merciful to me also, who might be, but for Thy grace, a sinner as vile as he!’ ”
“No,” said Arthur. “I meant a great deal more than that.”
She looked up quickly, but checked herself, and waited in silence.
“One must begin further back, I think. Think of some other man, the same age as this poor wretch. Look back to the time when they both began life﻿—before they had sense enough to know Right from Wrong. *Then*, at any rate, they were equal in God’s sight?”
She nodded assent.
“We have, then, two distinct epochs at which we may contemplate the two men whose lives we are comparing. At the first epoch they are, so far as moral responsibility is concerned, on precisely the same footing: they are alike incapable of doing right or wrong. At the second epoch the one man﻿—I am taking an extreme case, for contrast﻿—has won the esteem and love of all around him: his character is stainless, and his name will be held in honour hereafter: the other man’s history is one unvaried record of crime, and his life is at last forfeited to the outraged laws of his country. Now what have been the causes, in each case, of each man’s condition being what it is at the second epoch? They are of two kinds﻿—one acting from within, the other from without. These two kinds need to be discussed separately﻿—that is, if I have not already tired you with my prosing?”
“On the contrary,” said Lady Muriel, “it is a special delight to me to have a question discussed in this way﻿—analysed and arranged, so that one can understand it. Some books, that profess to argue out a question, are to me intolerably wearisome, simply because the ideas are all arranged haphazard﻿—a sort of ‘first come, first served.’ ”
“You are very encouraging,” Arthur replied, with a pleased look. “The causes, acting from *within*, which make a man’s character what it is at any given moment, are his successive acts of volition﻿—that is, his acts of choosing whether he will do this or that.”
“We are to assume the existence of Freewill?” I said, in order to have that point made quite clear.
“If not,” was the quiet reply, “*cadit quaestio*: and I have no more to say.”
“We *will* assume it!” the rest of the audience﻿—the majority, I may say, looking at it from Arthur’s point of view﻿—imperiously proclaimed. The orator proceeded.

===Chinese===

你是说，人应当想的——不是‘上帝啊，我感谢你，我不像别人’——而是‘上帝啊，也怜悯我吧，若非你的恩典，我本可能是个同他一样邪恶的罪人！’”
“不，”亚瑟说。“我的意思远不止于此。”
她幌地抬眼，又克制住自己，静默等待。
“依我看，得从更早说起。想一想另外一个人，同这可怜虫同龄。回望他们双双开始人生的时候——那时他们还懵懂得分不清是非。*那时*，至少在上帝眼里，他们是平等的？”
她点头称是。
“于是我们有了两个截然不同的时点，可以用来审视我们所比较的这两个人。在第一个时点，就道德责任而言，两人立足完全相同：都同样无所谓行善或作恶。到第二个时点，其中一人——我取个极端例子，好作对比——赢得了周围所有人的敬重与爱戴：品格无瑕，身后名声也将为人尊崇：另一人的历史则是一部始终如一的犯罪记录，性命最终抵偿给他所触犯的国法。那么，各是什么原因，使两人在第二个时点处境如此？原因有两类——一类自内而起，一类自外而来。这两类须分开讨论——就是说，如果我这些絮叨还没让你们厌烦的话？”
“恰恰相反，”穆丽尔小姐说，“一个问题这样讨论——条分缕析、安排妥帖，让人能明白——于我是种特别的乐趣。有些自称把问题辩个水落石出的书，我却读得烦闷欲死，只因为其中的想法全然乱排——一种‘先到先得’的架势。”
“你真会鼓舞人，”亚瑟面有喜色地答道。“自*内*而起的原因，即一个人在任一时刻品格之所以如此者，是他一连串的意志行为——也就是，他选择行此或行彼的行为。”
“我们得先假定自由意志的存在？”我说，为的是把这一点弄个明白。
“若不假定，”回答安安静静，“*问题即不成立*：我便无可再说了。”
“我们*就*假定它！”其余的听众——从亚瑟的视角看，我可以说是多数派——专断地宣布。演说家继续。

===Original===

“The causes, acting from *without*, are his surroundings﻿—what Mr. Herbert Spencer calls his ‘environment.’ Now the point I want to make clear is this, that a man is responsible for his acts of choosing, but *not* responsible for his environment. Hence, if these two men make, on some given occasion, when they are exposed to equal temptation, equal efforts to resist and to choose the right, their condition, in the sight of God, must be the same. If He is pleased in the one case, so will He be in the other; if displeased in the one case, so also in the other.”
“That is so, no doubt: I see it quite clearly,” Lady Muriel put in.
“And yet, owing to their different environments, the one may win a great victory over the temptation, while the other falls into some black abyss of crime.”
“But surely you would not say those men were equally guilty in the sight of God?”
“Either that,” said Arthur, “or else I must give up my belief in God’s perfect justice. But let me put one more case, which will show my meaning even more forcibly. Let the one man be in a high social position﻿—the other, say, a common thief. Let the one be tempted to some trivial act of unfair dealing﻿—something which he can do with the absolute certainty that it will never be discovered﻿—something which he can with perfect ease forbear from doing﻿—and which he distinctly knows to be a sin. Let the other be tempted to some terrible crime﻿—as men would consider it﻿—but under an almost overwhelming pressure of motives﻿—of course not *quite* overwhelming, as that would destroy all responsibility. Now, in this case, let the second man make a *greater* effort at resistance than the first. Also suppose *both* to fall under the temptation﻿—I say that the second man is, in God’s sight, *less* guilty than the other.”
Lady Muriel drew a long breath. “It upsets all one’s ideas of Right and Wrong﻿—just at first! Why, in that dreadful murder-trial, you would say, I suppose, that it was possible that the least guilty man in the Court was the murderer, and that possibly the judge who tried him, by yielding to the temptation of making one unfair remark, had committed a crime outweighing the criminal’s whole career!”
“Certainly I should,” Arthur firmly replied. “It sounds like a paradox, I admit.

===Chinese===

“自*外*而来的原因，是他的境遇——也就是赫伯特·斯宾塞先生所谓的‘环境’。现在我要说明白的要点是：人须对他的选择行为负责，却*不*对他的环境负责。因此，倘若这两人在某个场合、受到同等试探时，付出同等的努力去抵制、去择善而行，那么在上帝眼中，他们的光景必定相同。祂若在一人身上喜悦，也必在另一人身上喜悦；若在一人身上不悦，也必在另一人身上不悦。”
“这没错：我看得清清楚楚，”穆丽尔小姐插话。
“然而，由于环境不同，一人或许能大胜试探，另一人却坠入犯罪的黑暗深渊。”
“可你总不会说，这两人在上帝眼中罪责相等吧？”
“要么如此，”亚瑟说，“要么我就得放弃对上帝完美公义的信仰。再容我举一例，把我的意思摆得更透。设一人身居社会高位——另一人，就说是寻常窃贼。设前者受试探去做某件小小的不公交易——一件他做下后绝对无人察觉的事——一件他轻而易举即可不做的事——而且他明明知道那是罪。设后者受试探去犯一桩可怕的罪——以世人眼光看——却是在几乎不可抗拒的动机重压之下——当然*并非*全然不可抗拒，否则一切责任便无从谈起。这时，设第二人抵制的努力*大于*第一人。再设*两人*都屈从了试探——我说，在上帝眼中，第二人的罪责*更轻*。”
穆丽尔小姐长吸一口气。“这把人对是非的观念全搅翻了——至少起初如此！这么说，在那桩骇人的凶案审理里，你大概会说：法庭上罪责最轻的，可能恰是那个凶手；而审他的法官，或许只因忍不住说了一句不公道的话，所犯的罪便压过了罪犯的一生！”
“我正是这个意思，”亚瑟坚定地答道。“我承认，这听来像悖论。

===Original===

But just think what a grievous sin it must be, in God’s sight, to yield to some very slight temptation, which we could have resisted with perfect ease, and to do it deliberately, and in the full light of God’s Law. What penance can atone for a sin like *that*?”
“I can’t reject your theory,” I said. “But how it seems to widen the possible area of Sin in the world!”
“Is that so?” Lady Muriel anxiously enquired.
“Oh, not so, not so!” was the eager reply. “To me it seems to clear away much of the cloud that hangs over the world’s history. When this view first made itself clear to me, I remember walking out into the fields, repeating to myself that line of Tennyson ‘*There seemed no room for sense of wrong!*’ The thought, that perhaps the real guilt of the human race was infinitely less than I fancied it﻿—that the millions, whom I had thought of as sunk in hopeless depths of sin, were perhaps, in God’s sight, scarcely sinning at all﻿—was more sweet than words can tell! Life seemed more bright and beautiful, when once that thought had come! ‘*A livelier emerald twinkles in the grass, A purer sapphire melts into the sea!*’ ” His voice trembled as he concluded, and the tears stood in his eyes.
Lady Muriel shaded her face with her hand, and was silent for a minute. “It is a beautiful thought,” she said, looking up at last. “Thank you﻿—Arthur, for putting it into my head!”
The Earl returned in time to join us at tea, and to give us the very unwelcome tidings that a fever had broken out in the little harbour-town that lay below us﻿—a fever of so malignant a type that, though it had only appeared a day or two ago, there were already more than a dozen down in it, two or three of whom were reported to be in imminent danger.
In answer to the eager questions of Arthur﻿—who of course took a deep scientific interest in the matter﻿—he could give very few *technical* details, though he had met the local doctor. It appeared, however, that it was an almost *new* disease﻿—at least in *this* century, though it *might* prove to be identical with the “Plague” recorded in History﻿—*very* infectious, and frightfully rapid in its action. “It will not, however, prevent our party tomorrow,” he said in conclusion. “None of the guests belong to the infected district, which is, as you know, exclusively peopled by fishermen: so you may come without any fear.”

===Chinese===

可你只想想：在上帝眼中，向一桩本可毫不费力抵制的区区试探屈膝，蓄意行之，且行在上帝律法的朗朗光照之下——这该是多重的罪！这样的罪，什么补赎能抵偿呢？”
“我没法反驳你的理论，”我说。“可它似乎把世上可能的罪域大大拓宽了！”
“当真如此？”穆丽尔小姐急切地问。
“哦，不，不是这样！”回答急切起来。“在我看来，它倒像是拨开了笼罩世界历史的许多阴云。这见解初次在我心里澄明时，我记得自己走到田野上，反复吟着丁尼生那一句：‘*仿佛再无处可容错愕之感！*’人类真正的罪责，或许远比我臆想的轻微——那千千万万我原以为深陷罪渊、无可救药的人，在上帝眼中也许根本算不得犯罪——这个念头，其甘甜言语无法形容！这念头一来，生命顿觉更加明媚美好！‘*更鲜的翡翠在草间闪烁，更纯的蓝玉溶入大海！*’”他收尾时声音发颤，眼里禽着泪。
穆丽尔小姐以手遮面，静默了一分钟。“这是个美的念头，”她终于抬眼说。“谢谢你——亚瑟，把它放进了我心里！”
伯爵赶回来同我们用茶，也带来一个极不受欢迎的消息：山下那座小港城里爆发了一场热病——病型极为凶险，虽说露头才一两天，倒下的人已逾一打，据报其中两三人命悬一线。
亚瑟当然对这事怀着浓厚的科学兴味，急切追问，伯爵虽见过当地医生，却说不出几条*技术*细节。不过事情大致是：这几乎是一种*新*病——至少*本*世纪未见，尽管它*或许*与史册所载的“瘟疫”同源——传染性*极*强，病势骇人地迅疾。“不过，它搅不了咱们明天的宴会，”他末了说。“宾客中没有人来自疫区——那地方，你们知道，住的全是渔家：所以尽管放心赴会。”

===Original===

Arthur was very silent, all the way back, and, on reaching our lodgings, immediately plunged into medical studies, connected with the alarming malady of whose arrival we had just heard.

===Chinese===

一路归途，亚瑟缄默无言；一回到寓所，便立刻埋首医学典籍，钻研我们方才听说已至的那种骇人疾病。

## IX The Farewell-Party / IX 告别会
===Original===

On the following day, Arthur and I reached the Hall in good time, as only a few of the guests﻿—it was to be a party of eighteen﻿—had as yet arrived; and these were talking with the Earl, leaving us the opportunity of a few words apart with our hostess.
“Who is that *very* learned-looking man with the large spectacles?” Arthur enquired. “I haven’t met him here before, have I?”
“No, he’s a new friend of ours,” said Lady Muriel: “a German, I believe. He *is* such a dear old thing! And quite the most learned man I ever met﻿—with *one* exception, of course!” she added humbly, as Arthur drew himself up with an air of offended dignity.
“And the young lady in blue, just beyond him, talking to that foreign-looking man. Is *she* learned, too?”
“I don’t know,” said Lady Muriel. “But I’m told she’s a wonderful pianoforte-player. I hope you’ll hear her tonight. I asked that foreigner to take her in, because *he’s* very musical, too. He’s a French Count, I believe; and he sings *splendidly*!”
“Science﻿—music﻿—singing﻿—you have indeed got a complete party!” said Arthur. “I feel quite a privileged person, meeting all these stars. I *do* love music!”
“But the party isn’t *quite* complete!” said Lady Muriel. “You haven’t brought us those two beautiful children,” she went on, turning to me. “He brought them here to tea, you know, one day last summer,” again addressing Arthur; “and they *are* such darlings!”
“They are, *indeed*,” I assented.
“But why haven’t you brought them with you? You promised my father you *would*.”
“I’m very sorry,” I said; “but really it was impossible to bring them with me.” Here I most certainly *meant* to conclude the sentence: and it was with a feeling of utter amazement, which I cannot adequately describe, that I heard myself *going on speaking*. “﻿—but they are to join me here in the course of the evening” were the words, uttered in *my* voice, and seeming to come from *my* lips.
“I’m *so* glad!” Lady Muriel joyfully replied. “I *shall* enjoy introducing them to some of my friends here! When do you expect them?”
I took refuge in silence. The only *honest* reply would have been “That was not *my* remark. *I* didn’t say it, and *it isn’t true*!” But I had not the moral courage to make such a confession.

===Chinese===

第二天，亚瑟和我早早到了府邸，客人只到了寥寥几位——这将是十八人的聚会——都在同伯爵交谈，倒给了我们同女主人单独说几句的机会。
「那位戴大眼镜、一副*很有*学问模样的先生是谁？」亚瑟问。「我以前没在这儿见过他吧？」
「没有，他是我们新交的朋友，」穆丽尔小姐说：「我想是位德国人。他*可*真是个可爱的老头儿！而且是我见过的最有学问的人——当然，*有*一个例外！」她谦卑地补了一句，这时亚瑟挺起身子，摆出一副自尊受了冒犯的架势。
「还有他旁边那位穿蓝衣的年轻小姐，正同那个外国相貌的男人说话。*她*也有学问吗？」
「不知道，」穆丽尔小姐说。「不过听说她是位了不起的钢琴家。希望今晚您能听听她弹琴。我请那位外国人挕她入席，因为*他*也很懂音乐。他是位法国伯爵，我想；他唱歌唱得*好极了*！」
「科学——音乐——歌唱——您这场聚会真是样样齐全！」亚瑟说。「能见到这么多明星，我深感荣幸。我*最*爱音乐了！」
「可聚会还差*一点*才圆满！」穆丽尔小姐说着转向我。「您没把那两个漂亮的孩子带来。去年夏天有一天，他还带他们来喝过茶呢，」她又对亚瑟说；「他们*真是*太可爱了！」
「*确实*可爱，」我附和道。
「那您怎么不带他们来呢？您答应过我父亲*一定*带来的。」
「非常抱歉，」我说：「可实在没法带他们一起来。」说到这里，我分明*打算*就此打住：然而我竟听见自己*接着往下说*，那份惊愕我实在无法形容。「——不过今晚晚些时候他们会到这儿来找我」，这几个字用的是*我的*嗓音，似乎就出自我*自己的*嘴唇。
「那太好了！」穆丽尔小姐欢喜地答道。「*正好*把他们引见给这儿几位朋友！您预计他们什么时候到？」
我只好以沉默脱身。唯一*诚实*的回答本该是「那不是*我*说的话。*我*没说过，*那也不是真的*！」可我没有那份坦白的道德勇气。

===Original===

The character of a “lunatic” is not, I believe, very difficult to *acquire*: but it is amazingly difficult to *get rid of*: and it seemed quite certain that any such speech as *that* would *quite* justify the issue of a writ “*de lunatico inquirendo*.”
Lady Muriel evidently thought I had failed to hear her question, and turned to Arthur with a remark on some other subject; and I had time to recover from my shock of surprise﻿—or to awake out of my momentary “eerie” condition, whichever it was.
When things around me seemed once more to be real, Arthur was saying “I’m afraid there’s no help for it: they *must* be finite in number.”
“I should be sorry to have to believe it,” said Lady Muriel. “Yet, when one comes to think of it, there *are* no new melodies, nowadays. What people talk of as ‘the last new song’ always recalls to *me* some tune I’ve known as a child!”
“The day must come﻿—if the world lasts long enough﻿—” said Arthur, “when every possible tune will have been composed﻿—every possible pun perpetrated﻿—” (Lady Muriel wrung her hands, like a tragedy-queen) “and, worse than that, every possible *book* written! For the number of *words* is finite.”
“It’ll make very little difference to the *authors*,” I suggested. “Instead of saying ‘*what* book shall I write?’ an author will ask himself ‘*which* book shall I write?’ A mere verbal distinction!”
Lady Muriel gave me an approving smile. “But *lunatics* would always write new books, surely?” she went on. “They *couldn’t* write the sane books over again!”
“True,” said Arthur. “But *their* books would come to an end, also. The number of lunatic *books* is as finite as the number of lunatics.”
“And *that* number is becoming greater every year,” said a pompous man, whom I recognised as the self-appointed showman on the day of the picnic.
“So they say,” replied Arthur. “And, when ninety percent of us are lunatics,” (he seemed to be in a wildly nonsensical mood) “the asylums will be put to their proper use.”
“And that is﻿—?” the pompous man gravely enquired.
“*To shelter the sane!*” said Arthur. “*We* shall bar ourselves in. The lunatics will have it all their own way, *outside*. They’ll do it a little queerly, no doubt. Railway-collisions will be always happening: steamers always blowing up: most of the towns will be burnt down: most of the ships sunk﻿—”
“And most of the men *killed*!”

===Chinese===

我相信，「疯子」的名声*弄到手*并不太难：可要*甩掉*就难得惊人：而方才那样一句话，看来完全足以让法院签发一张「*审查疯癫令*」了。
穆丽尔小姐显然以为我没听清她的问题，便转向亚瑟谈起别的去了；我也得以从惊愕中缓过来——或者说，从我那阵转瞬即逝的「灵异」状态中醒过来，反正随它是哪种吧。
等我周围的事物重又显得真实，亚瑟正说着：「恐怕无可奈何：它们的数目*必定*有限。」
「要我相信这个，我可难受，」穆丽尔小姐说。「可是细想起来，如今*确实*没有新的曲调了。人们津津乐道的什么『最新歌曲』，在我听来总让我想起小时候就熟的某个调子！」
「那一天必会到来——只要世界延续得够久——」亚瑟说，「到那时，一切可能的曲子都已作尽——一切可能的谐音双关都说尽——」（穆丽尔小姐绞着双手，活像悲剧女王）「更糟的是，一切可能的*书*都写尽了！因为*词*的数目是有限的。」
「这对*作者们*倒没什么两样，」我插话道。「从前作家问自己：『我要写*什么*书？』将来则问：『我要写*哪一本*书？』一字之差罢了！」
穆丽尔小姐赞许地朝我一笑。「可*疯子们*总会写出新书来吧？」她接着说。「*总不能*把神志正常的书重写一遍呀！」
「不错，」亚瑟说。「可*他们*的书也会写到头。疯子*书*的数目，同疯子的数目一样有限。」
「而*那个*数目正一年大过一年，」一位神气活现的先生说——我认出他就是野餐那天自封的讲解员。
「据说如此，」亚瑟答道。「等到我们中间百分之九十都是疯子，」（他似乎正处在满口胡话的兴头上）「疯人院便可物尽其用了。」
「怎么个用法——？」那位自大的人一本正经地问。
「*收容神志正常的人*！」亚瑟说。「*我们*把自己关在里面。疯子们在外*头*，便可为所欲为。他们办起事来无疑会有些古怪。火车相撞将时时发生：轮船老在爆炸：大多数城镇会烧光：大多数船只会沉没——」
「大多数人也得*死掉*！」

===Original===

murmured the pompous man, who was evidently hopelessly bewildered.
“Certainly,” Arthur assented. “Till at last there will be *fewer* lunatics than sane men. Then *we* come out: *they* go in: and things return to their normal condition!”
The pompous man frowned darkly, and bit his lip, and folded his arms, vainly trying to think it out. “He is *jesting*!” he muttered to himself at last, in a tone of withering contempt, as he stalked away.
By this time the other guests had arrived; and dinner was announced. Arthur of course took down Lady Muriel: and *I* was pleased to find myself seated at her other side, with a severe-looking old lady (whom I had not met before, and whose name I had, as is usual in introductions, entirely failed to catch, merely gathering that it sounded like a compound-name) as my partner for the banquet.
She appeared, however, to be acquainted with Arthur, and confided to me in a low voice her opinion that he was “a very argumentative young man.” Arthur, for his part, seemed well inclined to show himself worthy of the character she had given him, and, hearing her say “I never take wine with my soup!” (this was *not* a confidence to me, but was launched upon Society, as a matter of general interest), he at once challenged a combat by asking her “*when* would you say that property *commence* in a plate of soup?”
“This is *my* soup,” she sternly replied: “and what is before you is *yours*.”
“No doubt,” said Arthur: “but *when* did I begin to own it? Up to the moment of its being put into the plate, it was the property of our host: while being offered round the table, it was, let us say, held in trust by the waiter: did it become mine when I accepted it? Or when it was placed before me? Or when I took the first spoonful?”
“He is a *very* argumentative young man!” was all the old lady would say: but she said it audibly, this time, feeling that Society had a right to know it.
Arthur smiled mischievously. “I shouldn’t mind betting you a shilling,” he said, “that the Eminent Barrister next you” (It certainly *is* possible to say words so as to make them begin with capitals!) “can’t answer me!”
“I *never* bet,” she sternly replied.
“Not even sixpenny points at *whist*?”
“*Never!*” she repeated. “*Whist* is innocent enough: but whist played for *money*!” She shuddered.
Arthur became serious again. “I’m afraid I can’t take that view,” he said.

===Chinese===

那位自大的人喃喃道，显然已被绕得晕头转向。
「那是自然，」亚瑟表示同意。「直到最后，疯子终于*少于*正常人。那时*我们*出来：*他们*进去：万事恢复正常！」
自大的人眉头紧锁，咬着嘴唇，抱着双臂，徒劳地想把这一切想通。「他是*说笑*！」末了他自言自语，语调轻蔑得能把人冻僵，说罢昂然而去。
这时其余客人也已到齐；晚宴开席了。亚瑟自然挕穆丽尔小姐入席：而*我*高兴地发现自己坐在她另一侧，同宴的搭档是一位面容严厉的老太太（我先前没见过她，而她的姓名，一如引见时的通例，我全然没听清，只听出像个复合姓）。
不过她似乎同亚瑟相识，还低声向我吐露她对亚瑟的评语：「是个顶爱抬杠的年轻人。」亚瑟呢，倒有心证明自己配得上她下的这个考语，听见她说「我从不就着汤喝酒！」（这句*不是*对我一个人的知心话，而是当作公众利益，向整个社交界发表的），他当即请战，问她：「依您看，一盘汤里的财产权*从何时*开始生效？」
「这是*我的*汤，」她严厉地回答：「您面前那盘是*您的*。」
「毫无疑问，」亚瑟说：「可我*从何时*起拥有它的？在被盛进盘子之前，它是我们主人的财产：在被端上桌巡回奉客时，不妨说由侍者代管：是我接下它的时候归我？还是摆到我面前时？还是我舀第一勺时？」
「他是个*顶爱抬杠*的年轻人！」老太太只肯说这一句：不过这回她是出声说的，觉得社交界有权知情。
亚瑟调皮地一笑。「我倒愿意同您赌一先令，」他说，「您身旁那位*名律师*」（有些词儿说出口时，确实*能*让人听出词首的大写字母！）「答不上我这个问题！」
「我*从不*打赌，」她严厉地答。
「惠斯特牌*六便士一局*也不来？」
「*从不*！」她重复道。「*惠斯特*本身无伤大雅：可要是为了*钱*打惠斯特！」她打了个寒噤。
亚瑟又正经起来。「这个看法，恕我不能苟同，」他说。

===Original===

“I consider that the introduction of small stakes for card-playing was one of the most *moral* acts Society ever did, *as* Society.”
“How was it so?” said Lady Muriel.
“Because it took Cards, once for all, out of the category of games at which *cheating* is possible. Look at the way Croquet is demoralising Society. Ladies are beginning to cheat at it, terribly: and, if they’re found out, they only laugh, and call it fun. But when there’s *money* at stake, that is out of the question. The swindler is *not* accepted as a wit. When a man sits down to cards, and cheats his friends out of their money, he doesn’t get much *fun* out of it﻿—unless he thinks it fun to be kicked downstairs!”
“If all gentlemen thought as badly of ladies as *you* do,” my neighbour remarked with some bitterness, “there would be very few﻿—very few﻿—.” She seemed doubtful how to end her sentence, but at last took “honeymoons” as a safe word.
“On the contrary,” said Arthur, the mischievous smile returning to his face, “if only people would adopt *my* theory, the number of honeymoons﻿—quite of a new kind﻿—would be greatly increased!”
“May we hear about this new kind of honeymoon?” said Lady Muriel.
“Let *X* be the gentleman,” Arthur began, in a slightly raised voice, as he now found himself with an audience of *six*, including “Mein Herr,” who was seated at the other side of my polynomial partner. “Let *X* be the gentleman, and *Y* the lady to whom he thinks of proposing. He applies for an Experimental Honeymoon. It is granted. Forthwith the young couple﻿—accompanied by the great-aunt of *Y*, to act as chaperone﻿—start for a month’s tour, during which they have many a moonlight-walk, and many a tête-à-tête conversation, and each can form a more correct estimate of the other’s character, in four *weeks*, than would have been possible in as many *years*, when meeting under the ordinary restrictions of Society. And it is only after their *return* that *X* finally decides whether he will, or will not, put the momentous question to *Y*!”
“In nine cases out of ten,” the pompous man proclaimed, “he would decide to break it off!”
“Then, in nine cases out of ten,” Arthur rejoined, “an unsuitable match would be prevented, and *both* parties saved from misery!”
“The only really *unsuitable* matches,” the old lady remarked, “are those made without sufficient *Money*. Love may come *afterwards*.

===Chinese===

「我认为，允许打牌时下小注，是社交界作为一个整体所做过的最有*道德*的举动之一。」
「怎么讲？」穆丽尔小姐说。
「因为它一劳永逸地把纸牌逐出了*作弊*可行的游戏之列。瞧瞧槌球正在怎样败坏社交风气。女士们开始作弊了，作弊得厉害：被抓住了，也只是一笑，说这是闹着玩。可一旦桌上有*钱*，这就无从谈起。骗子手*不会*被当作妙人。一个人坐下来打牌，从朋友手里把钱骗走，他从中得不到多少*乐趣*——除非他把被人踢下楼梯也当成乐趣！」
「要是所有的绅士都像*您*这样把女士想得这么坏，」我的邻座颇为尖刻地说，「那就很少有——很少有——」她似乎拿不准怎么收尾，最后选中「蜜月」作个保险的词。
「恰恰相反，」亚瑟说，脸上又浮起调皮的笑，「只要人们肯采纳*我的*理论，蜜月的数目——全新的一种——只会大大增加！」
「能听听这种新式蜜月吗？」穆丽尔小姐说。
「设*X*为绅士，」亚瑟提高了些嗓音开讲，他这才发现听众已有*六*位，其中包括坐在我那位复合姓氏搭档另一侧的「我先生」。「设*X*为绅士，*Y*为他有意求婚的女士。他申请一次实验性蜜月。申请获准。这对年轻人随即——由*Y*的姑婆随行，充当陪护——动身作一个月的旅行，其间有多少次月下漫步，多少次促膝长谈，四个*星期*里彼此对对方性情人品所能下的判断，比在社交界寻常礼法约束下相处同样多的*年头*还要准。而且只有*归来之后*，*X*才最终决定要不要向*Y*提出那个重大的问题！」
「十有八九，」那位自大的人宣称，「他会决定吹掉！」
「那么十有八九，」亚瑟应道，「一桩不般配的姻缘就得以免除，*双方*都免于痛苦！」
「唯一真正*不般配*的姻缘，」老太太发表意见道，「是*钱*不够的姻缘。爱情可以*后来*再有。开头却非有*钱*不可！」

===Original===

Money is needed *to begin with*!”
This remark was cast loose upon Society, as a sort of general challenge; and, as such, it was at once accepted by several of those within hearing: *Money* became the keynote of the conversation for some time; and a fitful echo of it was again heard, when the dessert had been placed upon the table, the servants had left the room, and the Earl had started the wine in its welcome progress round the table.
“I’m very glad to see you keep up the old customs,” I said to Lady Muriel as I filled her glass. “It’s really delightful to experience, once more, the peaceful feeling that comes over one when the waiters have left the room﻿—when one can converse without the feeling of being overheard, and without having dishes constantly thrust over one’s shoulder. How much more sociable it is to be able to pour out the wine for the ladies, and to hand the dishes to those who wish for them!”
“In that case, kindly send those peaches down here,” said a fat red-faced man, who was seated beyond our pompous friend. “I’ve been wishing for them﻿—diagonally﻿—for some time!”
“Yes, it *is* a ghastly innovation,” Lady Muriel replied, “letting the waiters carry round the wine at dessert. For one thing, they *always* take it the wrong way round﻿—which of course brings bad luck to *everybody* present!”
“Better go the *wrong* way than not go *at all*!” said our host. “Would you kindly help yourself?” (This was to the fat red-faced man.) “You are not a teetotaler, I think?”
“Indeed but I *am*!” he replied, as he pushed on the bottles. “Nearly twice as much money is spent in England on *Drink*, as on any other article of food. Read this card.” (What faddist ever goes about without a pocketful of the appropriate literature?) “The stripes of different colours represent the amounts spent on various articles of food. Look at the highest three. Money spent on butter and on cheese, thirty-five millions: on bread, seventy millions: on *intoxicating liquors*, one hundred and thirty-six millions! If I had my way, I would close every public-house in the land! Look at that card, and read the motto. *That’s where all the money goes to!*”
“Have you seen the *Anti-Teetotal Card*?” Arthur innocently enquired.
“No, Sir, I have not!” the orator savagely replied. “What is it like?”
“Almost exactly like this one. The coloured stripes are the same.

===Chinese===

这句话被抛向社交界，形同一份通牒；听见的几位当即应战：一时间，*钱*成了谈话的主调；待到甜点上桌、仆役退下、伯爵开动了那受人欢迎的巡桌传酒，这话头又断断续续地响起来。
「很高兴见到您家仍保留老规矩，」我给穆丽尔小姐斟酒时说。「侍者退出后那种安宁的感觉——说话不必担心被人听见，也不必有菜盘不断从肩头递过——再体验一回，实在惬意。能为女士们斟酒、能把菜递给想要的人，这多有社交的味道！」
「既然这样，劳驾把那盘桃子传到这边来，」一位红脸膛的胖子说，他坐在我们那位自大朋友的更远处。「我惦记它们——隔着斜对角——惦记好一阵子了！」
「是啊，让侍者上甜点时传酒，*实在*是个糟糕的新花样，」穆丽尔小姐答道，「一则，他们*总是*传错方向——这当然会给在座*每一位*带来厄运！」
「宁可传*错*方向，也强似*根本*不传！」我们的主人说。「您请自便好吗？」（这是对红脸胖子说的。）「您不戒酒，我想？」
「恰恰相反，我*正是*戒酒的！」他一面把酒瓶往前推，一面答道。「英国花在*酒*上的钱，差不多是任何其他一样食品的两倍。看看这张卡片。」（哪有满脑子怪念头的改革家出门不带一兜相应宣传品的？）「这些彩色条带表示各项食品的花费。看最高的三条。花在黄油和干酪上的钱，三千五百万：面包，七千万：*致醉的酒类*，一亿三千六百万！要依了我，我就把全国的酒馆统统关掉！看看这张卡，念念这句格言。*钱全都流到那儿去了！*」
「您见过*反禁酒卡*吗？」亚瑟无辜地问。
「没见过，先生！」那位演说家凶巴巴地答。「什么样子？」
「同这张几乎一模一样。彩色条带也相同。

===Original===

Only, instead of the words ‘Money spent on,’ it has ‘Incomes derived from sale of’; and, instead of ‘That’s where all the money goes to,’ its motto is ‘*That’s where all the money comes from!*’ ”
The red-faced man scowled, but evidently considered Arthur beneath his notice. So Lady Muriel took up the cudgels. “Do you hold the theory,” she enquired, “that people can preach teetotalism more effectually by being teetotalers themselves?”
“Certainly I do!” replied the red-faced man. “Now, here is a case in point,” unfolding a newspaper-cutting: “let me read you this letter from a teetotaler. To the Editor. Sir, I was once a moderate drinker, and knew a man who drank to excess. I went to him. ‘Give up this drink,’ I said. ‘It will ruin your health!’ ‘You drink,’ he said: ‘why shouldn’t I?’ ‘Yes,’ I said, ‘but I know when to leave off.’ He turned away from me. ‘You drink in your way,’ he said: ‘let me drink in mine. Be off!’ Then I saw that, to do any good with him, I must forswear drink. From that hour I haven’t touched a drop!”
“There! What do you say to *that*?” He looked round triumphantly, while the cutting was handed round for inspection.
“How very curious!” exclaimed Arthur, when it had reached him. “Did you happen to see a letter, last week, about early rising? It was strangely like this one.”
The red-faced man’s curiosity was roused. “Where did it appear?” he asked.
“Let me read it to you,” said Arthur. He took some papers from his pocket, opened one of them, and read as follows. “To the Editor. Sir, I was once a moderate sleeper, and knew a man who slept to excess. I pleaded with him. ‘Give up this lying in bed,’ I said, ‘It will ruin your health!’ ‘You go to bed,’ he said: ‘why shouldn’t I?’ ‘Yes,’ I said, ‘but I know when to get up in the morning.’ He turned away from me. ‘You sleep in your way,’ he said: ‘let me sleep in mine. Be off!’ Then I saw that to do any good with him, I must forswear sleep. From that hour I haven’t been to bed!”
Arthur folded and pocketed his paper, and passed on the newspaper-cutting. None of us dared to laugh, the red-faced man was evidently so angry. “Your parallel doesn’t run on all fours!” he snarled.
“*Moderate* drinkers never do so!” Arthur quietly replied. Even the stern old lady laughed at this.
“But it needs many other things to make a *perfect* dinner!” said Lady Muriel, evidently anxious to change the subject.

===Chinese===

只不过『花费于』几个字换成了『售出所得收入』；那句『钱全都流到那儿去了』的格言，也换成了『*钱全都是从那儿来的！*』」
红脸汉子沉下脸，可显然觉得犯不上理会亚瑟。于是穆丽尔小姐接过了武器。「您是否持这样的理论，」她问，「亲身戒酒的人，劝人戒酒更有效力？」
「我正是这么认为的！」红脸汉子答。「喏，这里恰有一个例证，」他展开一埚剪报：「让我给您念一封戒酒者的来信。致编辑。先生：我曾是个有节制的饮酒者，认识一个酗酒的人。我去找他。『把这酒戒了吧，』我说。『它会毁了你的健康！』『你还喝酒呢，』他说：『我为什么不喝？』『不错，』我说，『可我知道何时住手。』他扭头不理我。『你按你的方式喝，』他说：『让我按我的方式喝。走开！』于是我明白了，要对他有所助益，我必须誓戒饮酒。从那一刻起我一滴也未沾过！」
「怎么样！您对*这*有何话说？」他得意地环顾四周，剪报则传给众人过目。
「真太有意思了！」剪报到亚瑟手里时他喊道。「上星期您可碰巧见到一封谈早起的信？同这封像得出奇。」
红脸汉子的好奇心被勾了起来。「登在哪儿？」他问。
「念给您听，」亚瑟说。他从口袋里掣出几页纸，展开其一，念道：「致编辑。先生：我曾是个有节制的睡眠者，认识一个贪睡的人。我劝他。『别再赖床了，』我说，『它会毁了你的健康！』『你还上床睡觉呢，』他说：『我为什么不睡？』『不错，』我说，『可我知道早晨何时起身。』他扭头不理我。『你按你的方式睡，』他说：『让我按我的方式睡。走开！』于是我明白了，要对他有所助益，我必须誓戒睡眠。从那一刻起我再没上过床！」
亚瑟折好纸收回口袋，把剪报传了下去。谁也不敢笑，红脸汉子分明气得不轻。「你这个类比站不住脚！」他咆哮道。
「*有节制的*饮酒者从来只用两条腿站着！」亚瑟平静地答。连那位严厉的老太太也笑了。
「不过，要成一顿*尽善尽美*的晚宴，还缺许多别样的东西！」穆丽尔小姐说，显然急于岔开话头。

===Original===

“Mein Herr! What is *your* idea of a perfect dinner-party?”
The old man looked round smilingly, and his gigantic spectacles seemed more gigantic than ever. “A *perfect* dinner-party?” he repeated. “First, it must be presided over by our present hostess!”
“That, of *course*!” she gaily interposed. “But what *else*, Mein Herr?”
“I can but tell you what I have seen,” said Mein Herr, “in mine own﻿—in the country I have traveled in.”
He paused for a full minute, and gazed steadily at the ceiling﻿—with so dreamy an expression on his face, that I feared he was going off into a reverie, which seemed to be his normal state. However, after a minute, he suddenly began again.
“That which chiefly causes the failure of a dinner-party, is the running-short﻿—not of meat, nor yet of drink, but of *conversation*.”
“In an *English* dinner-party,” I remarked, “I have never known *small-talk* run short!”
“Pardon me,” Mein Herr respectfully replied, “I did not say ‘small-talk.’ I said ‘conversation.’ All such topics as the weather, or politics, or local gossip, are unknown among us. They are either vapid or controversial. What we need for *conversation* is a topic of *interest* and of *novelty*. To secure these things we have tried various plans﻿—Moving-Pictures, Wild-Creatures, Moving-Guests, and a Revolving-Humorist. But this last is only adapted to *small* parties.”
“Let us have it in four separate Chapters, please!” said Lady Muriel, who was evidently deeply interested﻿—as, indeed, most of the party were, by this time: and, all down the table, talk had ceased, and heads were leaning forwards, eager to catch fragments of Mein Herr’s oration.
“Chapter One! Moving-Pictures!” was proclaimed in the silvery voice of our hostess.
“The dining-table is shaped like a circular ring,” Mein Herr began, in low dreamy tones, which, however, were perfectly audible in the silence. “The guests are seated at the inner side as well as the outer, having ascended to their places by a winding-staircase, from the room below. Along the middle of the table runs a little railway; and there is an endless train of trucks, worked round by machinery; and on each truck there are two pictures, leaning back to back. The train makes two circuits during dinner; and, when it has been *once* round, the waiters turn the pictures round in each truck, making them face the other way.

===Chinese===

「我先生！在您看来，怎样才算一场完美的晚宴？」
老人含笑环顾四周，他那副巨大的眼镜显得比以往更巨大了。「*完美的*晚宴？」他重复道。「第一，须由我们眼下的女主人主持！」
「这*自然*！」她快活地插话。「此外*还有*什么呢，我先生？」
「我只能讲讲我亲眼见过的，」我先生说，「在我自个儿的——在我游历过的一个国家里。」
他足足停了一分钟，目不转睛地望着天花板——脸上那副如梦的神情，叫我担心他又要坠入沉思——那似乎是他的常态。不过一分钟后，他忽然又开了口。
「致使晚宴失败的主因，是短缺——不是短缺菜肴，也不是短缺酒水，而是短缺*谈资*。」
「在*英国*人的晚宴上，」我说，「我可从没见过*寒暄闲谈*短缺！」
「恕我冒昧，」我先生恭而敬之答，「我说的不是『寒暄闲谈』。我说的是『交谈』。天气啦、政治啦、地方上的流言啦，这类话题在我们那儿一概没有。它们要么乏味，要么惹起争执。我们要拿来*交谈*的，须是有*趣味*、有*新意*的话题。为了这个，我们试过种种法子——活动图画、野生动物、活动宾客，还有一位旋转幽默家。只是末一样只宜*小*型宴会。」
「请分四章讲给我们听！」穆丽尔小姐说，她显然深感兴趣——事实上此刻满座大都如此：长桌两侧谈话俱已止息，人人探着头，唯恐漏掉我先生宏论的碎片。
「第一章！活动图画！」女主人用银铃般的嗓音宣布。
「餐桌呈圆环形，」我先生用低沉入梦的声调开讲，不过在一片寂静里字字清晰。「客人内圈外圈环坐，从楼下房间循螺旋梯登阶入座。餐桌正中跑着一条小铁路；有机械驱动的一列无穷尽的货车循环不息；每辆货车上斜倚着两幅画，背对着背。一餐之间火车环行两周；跑完*一*圈后，侍者们把每辆车上的画转个向，让它们面朝另一边。

===Original===

Thus *every* guest sees *every* picture!”
He paused, and the silence seemed deader than ever. Lady Muriel looked aghast. “Really, if this goes on,” she exclaimed, “I shall have to drop a pin! Oh, it’s *my* fault, is it?” (In answer to an appealing look from Mein Herr.) “I was forgetting my duty. Chapter Two! Wild-Creatures!”
“We found the Moving-Pictures a *little* monotonous,” said Mein Herr. “People didn’t care to talk Art through a whole dinner; so we tried Wild-Creatures. Among the flowers, which we laid (just as *you* do) about the table, were to be seen, here a mouse, there a beetle; here a spider,” (Lady Muriel shuddered) “there a wasp; here a toad, there a snake;” (“Father!” said Lady Muriel, plaintively. “Did you hear *that*?”) “so we had plenty to talk about!”
“And when you got stung﻿—” the old lady began.
“They were all chained-up, dear Madam!”
And the old lady gave a satisfied nod.
There was no silence to follow, *this* time. “Third Chapter!” Lady Muriel proclaimed at once, “Moving-Guests!”
“Even the Wild-Creatures proved monotonous,” the orator proceeded. “So we left the guests to choose their own subjects; and, to avoid monotony, we changed *them*. We made the table of *two* rings; and the inner ring moved slowly round, all the time, along with the floor in the middle and the inner row of guests. Thus *every* inner guest was brought face-to-face with *every* outer guest. It was a little confusing, sometimes, to have to *begin* a story to one friend and *finish* it to another; but *every* plan has its faults, you know.”
“Fourth Chapter!” Lady Muriel hastened to announce. “The Revolving-Humorist!”
“For a *small* party we found it an excellent plan to have a round table, with a hole cut in the middle large enough to hold *one* guest. Here we placed our *best* talker. He revolved slowly, facing every other guest in turn: and he told lively anecdotes the whole time!”
“I shouldn’t like it!” murmured the pompous man. “It would make me giddy, revolving like that! I should decline to﻿—” here it appeared to dawn upon him that perhaps the assumption he was making was not warranted by the circumstances: he took a hasty gulp of wine, and choked himself.
But Mein Herr had relapsed into reverie, and made no further remark. Lady Muriel gave the signal, and the ladies left the room.

===Chinese===

如此，*每位*客人都能看到*每一幅*画！」
他停住了，寂静仿佛比先前更死寂。穆丽尔小姐面如土色。「说真的，再这样下去，」她喊道，「我可要掉一根针试试了！哦，是我的不是，对吧？」（这是回答我先生恳求的一瞥。）「我把自己的职责忘了。第二章！野生动物！」
「我们发现活动图画*有点*单调，」我先生说。「谁也不乐意一顷顿饭都谈艺术；于是我们试了野生动物。在（同*你们*一样）铺满餐桌的花卉之间，这儿可见一只老鼠，那儿一只甲虫；这儿一只蜘蛛，」（穆丽尔小姐打了个寒噤）「那儿一只黄蜂；这儿一只癞蛤蟆，那儿一条蛇；」（「父亲！」穆丽尔小姐哀声道。「您听见*这个*了吗？」）「这样我们就不愁没得谈了！」
「那要是被蜇了——」老太太开口道。
「它们都拴着链子呢，亲爱的夫人！」
老太太满意地点了点头。
这回没有寂静随之而来。「第三章！」穆丽尔小姐立刻宣布，「活动宾客！」
「连野生动物也终于显得单调了，」演说家继续道。「于是我们听任宾客自选话题；为免单调，我们更换*客人*。我们把餐桌做成*双*环；内环同中间的地板和内圈客人一道，始终缓缓旋转。这样*每位*内圈宾客都被轮番带到*每位*外圈宾客的对面。有时候，一个故事*开头*讲给这位朋友、*结尾*却讲给那位朋友，未免有点乱；可您知道，*任何*方案都有它的短处。」
「第四章！」穆丽尔小姐赶紧宣布。「旋转幽默家！」
「对*小*型宴会，我们发现一条妙计：用一张圆桌，正中挖一个洞，大小恰好容*一位*客人。我们把*最能*聊的这位安置于此。他缓缓旋转，依次面向其余每位宾客：一路讲着妙趣横生的轶事！」
「我可不喜欢！」那位自大的人喃喃道。「那样转来转去，非把我转晕了不可！我敬谢不——」说到这里他似乎才省悟，自己的这个假定未必合乎时宜：他慌忙灌了一大口酒，把自己呛住了。
而我先生已重新坠入沉思，再没有下文。穆丽尔小姐发了暗号，女士们离席而去。

## X Jabbering and Jam / 十 闲扯与果酱
===Original===

When the last lady had disappeared, and the Earl, taking his place at the head of the table, had issued the military order “Gentlemen! Close up the ranks, if you please!”, and when, in obedience to his command, we had gathered ourselves compactly round him, the pompous man gave a deep sigh of relief, filled his glass to the brim, pushed on the wine, and began one of his favorite orations. “They are charming, no doubt! Charming, but very frivolous. They drag us down, so to speak, to a lower level. They﻿—”
“Do not all pronouns require antecedent *nouns*?” the Earl gently enquired.
“Pardon me,” said the pompous man, with lofty condescension. “I had overlooked the noun. The ladies. We regret their absence. Yet we console ourselves. *Thought is free.* With them, we are limited to *trivial* topics﻿—Art, Literature, Politics, and so forth. One can bear to discuss *such* paltry matters with a lady. But no man, in his senses﻿—” (he looked sternly round the table, as if defying contradiction) “﻿—ever yet discussed **wine** with a lady!” He sipped his glass of port, leaned back in his chair, and slowly raised it up to his eye, so as to look through it at the lamp. “The vintage, my Lord?” he enquired, glancing at his host.
The Earl named the date.
“So I had supposed. But one likes to be certain. The *tint* is, perhaps, slightly pale. But the *body* is unquestionable. And as for the *bouquet*﻿—”
Ah, that magic Bouquet! How vividly that single word recalled the scene! The little beggar-boy turning his somersault in the road﻿—the sweet little crippled maiden in my arms﻿—the mysterious evanescent nursemaid﻿—all rushed tumultuously into my mind, like the creatures of a dream: and through this mental haze there still boomed on, like the tolling of a bell, the solemn voice of the great connoisseur of **wine**!
Even *his* utterances had taken on themselves a strange and dreamlike form. “No,” he resumed﻿—and *why* is it, I pause to ask, that, in taking up the broken thread of a dialogue, one *always* begins with this cheerless monosyllable? After much anxious thought, I have come to the conclusion that the object in view is the same as that of the schoolboy, when the sum he is working has got into a hopeless muddle, and when in despair he takes the sponge, washes it all out, and begins again.

===Chinese===

最后一位女士退场后，伯爵在上首落座，发出一道军令：“诸位先生！请向中看齐！”我们遵命，紧紧围拢到他身边；那位妄自尊大的绅士长舒一口气，把酒杯斟得满满的，推过酒瓶，开始了他最拿手的演说之一。“她们迷人，毫无疑问！迷人，但十分轻浮。可以说，她们把我们拖到一个较低的层次。她们——”
“所有代词不都需要先有*名词*吗？”伯爵温和地问。
“恕我失言，”妄自尊大的绅士居高临下地一摆手。“我漏掉了那个名词。女士们。我们惋惜她们不在场。然而我们也聊以自慰。*思想是自由的。*同她们在一起，我们只能局限在*琐碎*的话题上——艺术啦，文学啦，政治啦，等等。*这类*鸡毛蒜皮，同女士谈谈倒还受得了。但凡头脑清醒的男人——”（他严厉地环视餐桌，仿佛谁敢驳他就试试）“——还从来没有同女士讨论过**葡萄酒**呢！”他呷了一口杯中的波特酒，靠进椅背，又缓缓举杯齐眉，透过它对灯端详。“哪年的酒，勋爵？”他瞤一眼主人问。
伯爵报了年份。
“我也这么猜。不过人总想拿个准。*色*兴许稍浅了些。但*酒体*无可挑剔。至于那*香气*嘛——”
啊，那有魔力的“香气”！这一个词把旧景唤回得多么鲜活！路上翻跟头的小乞儿——我怀中甜美的小跛脚姑娘——那位神秘的、转瞬即逝的保姆——全都喧嚣着涌进我脑海，如梦里的人物：而隔着这片心头的迷雾，那位**葡萄酒**大鉴赏家庄严的嗓音仍嗡嗡响着，如钟声一般！
连*他*的言语也披上了一种奇异的、梦一般的形式。“不，”他重新开讲——且容我问一句，为什么人在接起中断的话头时，*总是*从这个丧气的单音节开始？苦苦思索良久，我得出结论：其用意同学童的一样——那道算术题已经算成一团乱麻，绝望之下，他抓起海绵，把整页擦个干净，从头再算。

===Original===

Just in the same way the bewildered orator, by the simple process of denying *everything* that has been hitherto asserted, makes a clean sweep of the whole discussion, and can “start fair” with a fresh theory. “No,” he resumed: “there’s nothing like cherry-jam, after all. That’s what *I* say!”
“Not for *all* qualities!” an eager little man shrilly interposed. “For *richness* of general tone I don’t say that it *has* a rival. But for *delicacy* of modulation﻿—for what one may call the ‘*harmonics*’ of flavour﻿—give *me* good old *raspberry*-jam!”
“Allow me one word!” The fat red-faced man, quite hoarse with excitement, broke into the dialogue. “It’s too important a question to be settled by Amateurs! I can give you the views of a *Professional*﻿—perhaps the most experienced jam-taster now living. Why, I’ve known him fix the age of strawberry-jam, to a *day*﻿—and we all know what a difficult jam it is to give a date to﻿—on a single tasting! Well, I put to him the *very* question you are discussing. His words were ‘*cherry*-jam is best, for mere *chiaroscuro* of flavour: *raspberry*-jam lends itself best to those resolved discords that linger so lovingly on the tongue: but, for rapturous *utterness* of saccharine perfection, it’s *apricot-jam first and the rest nowhere*!’ That was well put, *wasn’t* it?”
“Consummately put!” shrieked the eager little man.
“I know your friend well,” said the pompous man. “As a jam-taster, he has no rival! Yet I scarcely think﻿—”
But here the discussion became general: and his words were lost in a confused medley of names, every guest sounding the praises of his own favorite jam. At length, through the din, our host’s voice made itself heard. “Let us join the ladies!” These words seemed to recall me to waking life; and I felt sure that, for the last few minutes, I had relapsed into the “eerie” state.
“A strange dream!” I said to myself as we trooped upstairs. “Grown men discussing, as seriously as if they were matters of life and death, the hopelessly trivial details of mere *delicacies*, that appeal to no higher human function than the nerves of the tongue and palate! What a humiliating spectacle such a discussion would be in waking life!”

===Chinese===

那位被绕糊涂的演说家正是这样：把方才断言过的一切通通*否认*，一挥帚把整场讨论扫个干净，便能“重新起跑”，另起一套新鲜理论。“不，”他重新开讲：“说到底，什么都比不上樱桃酱。这是*我*的话！”
“并不是样样都好！”一个急猴猴的小个子尖声插进来。“论整体色调的*醇厚*，我不说它有敌手。可论变调的*细腻*——论那可以称作风味的‘*和声*’的东西——还是给*我*上好的老牌*覆盆子*酱！”
“容我说一句！”那个红脸膛的胖子闯进对话，激动得嗓音都哑了。“这个问题太要紧，不能交给外行来断！我能给你们一位*行家*的见解——兴许是当今在世经验最丰富的果酱品尝师。嘿，我见过他只尝一口，就把草莓酱的年头定到*天*——草莓酱多难断代，咱们都知道——一尝就准！我把你们正讨论的这个*原原本本*的问题摆给他。他的原话是：‘论风味单纯的*明暗烘托*，*樱桃*酱称最：*覆盆子*酱最宜表现那些在舌尖上恋恋不去的解决性和音：可若要甜之极致那种销魂的*彻头彻尾*，*杏子酱一马当先，其余望尘莫及*！’这话说得漂亮，*是不是*？”
“说得圆熟之至！”急猴猴的小个子尖声道。
“你这朋友我很熟，”妄自尊大的绅士说。“论尝果酱，他无人能敌！只是我几乎觉得——”
可这时讨论已成了大合奏：他的话音淹没在一片乱糟糟的果酱名目里，每位宾客都在夸自己心爱的那种。终于，主人的声音穿过喧嚷：“我们去陪女士们吧！”这句话仿佛把我唤回清醒的人生；我确信，方才那几分钟里，我又落回了那种“灵异”状态。
“好一场怪梦！”上楼时我自忖。“一屋子成年男子，一本正经地讨论区区*美食*里无可救药的琐节，郑重得仿佛人命关天——而这些东西，除了舌头和上颚的神经，打动不了人类任何更高官能！这样的讨论若在清醒时上演，该是多么丢人的一场戏！”

===Original===

When, on our way to the drawing-room, I received from the housekeeper my little friends, clad in the daintiest of evening costumes, and looking, in the flush of expectant delight, more radiantly beautiful than I had ever seen them before, I felt no shock of surprise, but accepted the fact with the same unreasoning apathy with which one meets the events of a dream, and was merely conscious of a vague anxiety as to how they would acquit themselves in so novel a scene﻿—forgetting that Court-life in Outland was as good training as they could need for Society in the more substantial world.
It would be best, I thought, to introduce them as soon as possible to some good-natured lady-guest, and I selected the young lady whose pianoforte-playing had been so much talked of. “I am sure you like children,” I said. “May I introduce two little friends of mine? This is Sylvie﻿—and this is Bruno.”
The young lady kissed Sylvie very graciously. She would have done the same for *Bruno*, but he hastily drew back out of reach. “Their faces are new to me,” she said. “Where do you come from, my dear?”
I had not anticipated so inconvenient a question; and, fearing that it might embarrass Sylvie, I answered for her. “They come from some distance. They are only here just for this one evening.”
“How far have you come, dear?” the young lady persisted.
Sylvie looked puzzled. “A mile or two, I *think*,” she said doubtfully.
“A mile or *three*,” said Bruno.
“You shouldn’t say ‘a mile or *three*,’ ” Sylvie corrected him.
The young lady nodded approval. “Sylvie’s quite right. It isn’t usual to say ‘a mile or *three*.’ ”
“It would be usual﻿—if we said it often enough,” said Bruno.
It was the young lady’s turn to look puzzled now. “He’s very quick, for his age!” she murmured. “You’re not more than seven, are you, dear?” she added aloud.
“I’m not so many as *that*,” said Bruno. “I’m *one*. Sylvie’s *one*. Sylvie and me is *two*. *Sylvie* taught me to count.”
“Oh, I wasn’t *counting* you, you know!” the young lady laughingly replied.
“Hasn’t oo *learnt* to count?” said Bruno.
The young lady bit her lip. “Dear! What embarrassing questions he *does* ask!” she said in a half-audible “aside.”
“Bruno, you shouldn’t!” Sylvie said reprovingly.
“Shouldn’t *what*?” said Bruno.
“You shouldn’t ask﻿—that sort of questions.”
“*What* sort of questions?” Bruno mischievously persisted.

===Chinese===

去客厅的路上，管家把我的两位小朋友交还给我：他们一身精美绝伦的晚装，脸上焕发着期待的欢喜，比我历来看过的更加明艳动人。我竟毫无惊诧之感，只以梦中人对梦事件的那种全无理由的淡然受了这事实，心里唯有一缕模糊的挂虑——在这样新奇的场面里，他们能否举止得体——倒忘了外乡的宫廷生活，正是他们应付这个更实在世界社交场合的最好训练。
我想，最好尽快把他们引见给一位好性子的女宾，便选中了那位琴艺被大家谈论了好多的年轻小姐。“您一定喜欢孩子，”我说。“我给您引见两位小朋友，可以吗？这是西尔维——这是布鲁诺。”
年轻小姐非常和蔼地亲了亲西尔维。她原想照样亲*布鲁诺*，他却慌忙退到够不着的地方。“这两张脸我面生，”她说。“你们从哪儿来呀，亲爱的？”
我没料到这么个不方便的问题，又怕它难住西尔维，便替她答了。“他们从远处来。只在这儿待今晚一晚。”
“走了多远呀，亲爱的？”年轻小姐追问。
西尔维面露困惑。“一两英里吧，*我想*，”她迟疑地说。
“一两*三*英里，”布鲁诺说。
“不该说‘一两*三*英里’，”西尔维纠正他。
年轻小姐点头称许。“西尔维说得对。‘一两*三*英里’不是通常的说法。”
“说惯了就通常了——要是我们常这么说的话，”布鲁诺说。
这回轮到年轻小姐困惑了。“这年纪，他脑子真快！”她低声说。“你总不过七岁吧，亲爱的？”她又扬声问。
“我没有那么多，”布鲁诺说。“我是*一*。西尔维是*一*。西尔维加我是*二*。是*西尔维*教我数数的。”
“哦，我没在*数*你呀，知道吗！”年轻小姐笑着答。
“那*咚*学过数数吗？”布鲁诺说。
年轻小姐咬了咬嘴唇。“天哪！他这问题问得*真*叫人下不来台！”她半大声地“打旁白”说。
“布鲁诺，不可以这样！”西尔维责备道。
“不可以*什么*？”布鲁诺说。
“不可以问——那类问题。”
“*哪*类问题？”布鲁诺顽皮地追问。

===Original===

“What *she* told you not,” Sylvie replied, with a shy glance at the young lady, and losing all sense of grammar in her confusion.
“Oo can’t pronounce it!” Bruno triumphantly cried. And he turned to the young lady, for sympathy in his victory. “I *knewed* she couldn’t pronounce ‘umbrella-sting’!”
The young lady thought it best to return to the arithmetical problem. “When I asked if you were *seven*, you know, I didn’t mean ‘how many *children*?’ I meant ‘how many *years*﻿—﻿—’ ”
“Only got *two* ears,” said Bruno. “Nobody’s got *seven* ears.”
“And you belong to this little girl?” the young lady continued, skilfully evading the anatomical problem.
“No, I doosn’t belong to *her*!” said Bruno. “Sylvie belongs to *me*!” And he clasped his arms round her as he added “She are my very mine!”
“And, do you know,” said the young lady, “I’ve a little sister at home, exactly like *your* sister? I’m sure they’d love each other.”
“They’d be very extremely useful to each other,” Bruno said, thoughtfully. “And they wouldn’t want no looking-glasses to brush their hair wiz.”
“Why not, my child?”
“Why, each one would do for the other one’s looking-glass, a-course!” cried Bruno.
But here Lady Muriel, who had been standing by, listening to this bewildering dialogue, interrupted it to ask if the young lady would favour us with some music; and the children followed their new friend to the piano.
Arthur came and sat down by me. “If rumour speaks truly,” he whispered, “we are to have a real treat!” And then, amid a breathless silence, the performance began.
She was one of those players whom Society talks of as “brilliant,” and she dashed into the loveliest of Haydn’s Symphonies in a style that was clearly the outcome of years of patient study under the best masters. At first it seemed to be the perfection of pianoforte-playing; but in a few minutes I began to ask myself, wearily, “*What* is it that is wanting? *Why* does one get no pleasure from it?”
Then I set myself to listen intently to every note; and the mystery explained itself. There *was* an almost-perfect mechanical *correctness*﻿—and there was nothing else!

“咚说不上来！”布鲁诺得意洋洋地喊。又转向年轻小姐，讨一份分享胜利的同情。“我*就知道*她说不上来‘雨伞蜇’！”
年轻小姐觉得最好还是回到那道算术题上。“我问你七不七岁的时候，你知道，我不是问‘几个*孩子*’，是问‘几*岁*——’”

“那你是属于这位小姐姐的啦？”年轻小姐继续问，巧妙地绕开了解剖学难题。
“不，俺不归*她*！”布鲁诺说。“西尔维归*俺*！”说着他张开胳膊搂住她，又补一句：“她就是俺的俺！”
“还有，你知道吗，”年轻小姐说，“我家里有个小妹妹，同*你*妹妹一模一样？我保准她们会亲亲相爱。”
“她们俩对彼此会非常、非常有用，”布鲁诺若有所思地说。“梳头发也不用要镜子了。”
“为什么不用，孩子？”
“嘿，一个正好当另一个的镜子呗，当然！”布鲁诺嚷道。
这时穆丽尔小姐——她一直站在一旁听这段叫人发懵的对话——插进来，问年轻小姐可否赏光奏上一曲；孩子们便跟着他们的新朋友走向钢琴。
亚瑟过来坐到我身边。“要是传闻不虚，”他悄声道，“我们有耳福了！”随后，屏息的寂静中，演奏开始了。
她属于社交界口中“光彩夺目”的那类琴师，一出手便闯入海顿最可爱的一部交响曲，那派头显然出自名师门下多年苦学的功底。起初，这似乎是钢琴演奏的完美境界；可几分钟不到，我便倦倦地自问：“*缺*的是什么？*为什么*人从中得不着快乐？”
于是我凝神去听每一个音；谜底自行揭开。那里面确有一种近乎完美的机械性*精确*——此外空无一物！

===Original===

False notes, of course, did not occur: she knew the piece too well for *that*; but there was just enough irregularity of *time* to betray that the player had no real “ear” for music﻿—just enough inarticulateness in the more elaborate passages to show that she did not think her audience worth taking real pains for﻿—just enough mechanical monotony of accent to take all *soul* out of the heavenly modulations she was profaning﻿—in short, it was simply irritating; and, when she had rattled off the finale and had struck the final chord as if, the instrument being now done with, it didn’t matter how many wires she broke, I could not even *affect* to join in the stereotyped “Oh, *thank* you!” which was chorused around me.
Lady Muriel joined us for a moment. “Isn’t it *beautiful*?” she whispered, to Arthur, with a mischievous smile.
“No, it isn’t!” said Arthur. But the gentle sweetness of his face quite neutralised the apparent rudeness of the reply.
“Such execution, you know!” she persisted.
“That’s what she *deserves*,” Arthur doggedly replied: “but people are so prejudiced against capital﻿—”
“Now you’re beginning to talk nonsense!” Lady Muriel cried. “But you *do* like Music, don’t you? You said so just now.”
“Do I like *Music*?” the Doctor repeated softly to himself. “My dear Lady Muriel, there is Music and Music. Your question is painfully vague. You might as well ask ‘Do you like *People*?’ ”
Lady Muriel bit her lip, frowned, and stamped with one tiny foot. As a dramatic representation of ill-temper, it was distinctly *not* a success. However, it took in *one* of her audience, and Bruno hastened to interpose, as peacemaker in a rising quarrel, with the remark “*I* likes Peoples!”
Arthur laid a loving hand on the little curly head. “What? *All* Peoples?” he enquired.
“Not *all* Peoples,” Bruno explained. “Only but Sylvie﻿—and Lady Muriel﻿—and him﻿—” (pointing to the Earl) “and oo﻿—and oo!”
“You shouldn’t point at people,” said Sylvie. “It’s very rude.”
“In Bruno’s World,” I said, “there are only *four* People﻿—worth mentioning!”
“In Bruno’s World!” Lady Muriel repeated thoughtfully. “A bright and flowery world. Where the grass is always green, where the breezes always blow softly, and the rain-clouds never gather; where there are no wild beasts, and no deserts﻿—”
“There *must* be deserts,” Arthur decisively remarked. “At least if it was *my* ideal world.”
