他微微喘起来了，他知道紧要关头就要到。）
“快到家门口，他又往提篮里看了一眼，他看见——”
“只剩——”布鲁诺起了个头，忽然心生慷慨，望向我。“*这回*让咚来说吧，先生先生！”他悄声说。这提议宽宏大量，可我不肯夺他这份乐子。“接着说，布鲁诺，”我说，“你说得最最精彩。”“只剩——可是——*一*只——狐狸！”布鲁诺无比庄严地说。
“‘大狐狸，’”西尔维说，讲得性起，连讲述的腔调都丢了，“‘你一直*这么*乖，我简直不能相信*你会*不听话：可是我*怕*你把自己的小妹妹吃了？’大狐狸说：‘呃哇呜！呃哇呜！’接着就噎住了。布鲁诺往它嘴里瞧，嘴里*当真*是满的！”（西尔维停下来换气，布鲁诺仰面躺进雏菊丛中，得意洋洋地望着我。“是不是*棒极了*，先生先生？”他说。我努力摆出批评家的腔调。“是棒极了，”我说：“就是吓得人慌！”“咚要是愿意，可以坐得离*俺*近一点儿，”布鲁诺说。）
“于是布鲁诺回了家：把提篮拎进厨房，打开。他看见——”这一回西尔维望着*我*，仿佛觉得我受了冷落，总该容我猜上一回。
“他猜不着！”布鲁诺急切地喊。“俺怕是*只好*告诉他啦！提篮里什么——*啥也没*有！”我吓得一哆嗦，布鲁诺高兴得直拍手。“他*真的*怕了，西尔维！快讲后面的！”
“于是布鲁诺说：‘大狐狸，你把*你自己*吃了没有，你这坏狐狸？’大狐狸说：‘呃哇呜！’这时布鲁诺才看见，提篮里只剩下一张*嘴*！他便拿起那张嘴，掰开，摇啊，摇啊！终于，把小狐狸从它自己的嘴里摇了出来！接着他说：‘再张开嘴，你个小坏蛋！’他又摇啊，摇啊！摇出了二狐狸！又说：‘现在张开*你的*嘴！’他又摇啊，摇啊！摇出了小狐狸，还有全部的苹果、全部的面包！
“然后布鲁诺让小狐狸们背墙站好：对他们发表了一小段讲话。‘好了，小狐狸们，你们开头开得坏透了——得受罚。先上育婴房去，洗脸，换上干净围嘴。

===Original===

Then you’ll hear the bell ring for supper. Then you’ll come down: and *you won’t have any supper*: but you’ll have a good *whipping*! Then you’ll go to bed. Then in the morning you’ll hear the bell ring for breakfast. *But you won’t have any breakfast!* You’ll have a good *whipping*! Then you’ll have your lessons. And, perhaps, if you’re *very* good, when dinnertime comes, you’ll have a little dinner, and no more whipping!’ ” (“How *very* kind he was!” I whispered to Bruno. “*Middling* kind,” Bruno corrected me gravely.)
“So the little Foxes ran up to the nursery. And soon Bruno went into the hall, and rang the big bell. ‘Tingle, tingle, tingle! Supper, supper, supper!’ Down came the little Foxes, in such a hurry for their supper! Clean pinafores! Spoons in their hands! And, when they got into the dining-room, there was ever such a white tablecloth on the table! But there was nothing on it but a big whip. And they had *such* a whipping!” (I put my handkerchief to my eyes, and Bruno hastily climbed upon my knee and stroked my face. “Only *one* more whipping, Mister Sir!” he whispered. “Don’t cry more than oo can’t help!”)
“And the next morning early, Bruno rang the big bell again. ‘Tingle, tingle, tingle! Breakfast, breakfast, breakfast!’ Down came the little Foxes! Clean pinafores! Spoons in their hands! No breakfast! Only the big whip! Then came lessons,” Sylvie hurried on, for I still had my handkerchief to my eyes. “And the little Foxes were ever so good! And they learned their lessons backwards, and forwards, and upside-down. And at last Bruno rang the big bell again. ‘Tingle, tingle, tingle! Dinner, dinner, dinner!’ And when the little Foxes came down﻿—” (“Had they clean pinafores on?” Bruno enquired. “Of course!” said Sylvie. “And spoons?” “Why, you *know* they had!” “Couldn’t be *certain*,” said Bruno.) “﻿—they came as slow as slow! And they said ‘Oh! There’ll be no dinner! There’ll only be the big whip!’ But, when they got into the room, they saw the most *lovely* dinner!” (“Buns?” cried Bruno, clapping his hands.) “Buns, and cake, and﻿—” (“﻿—and jam?” said Bruno.) “Yes, jam﻿—and soup﻿—and﻿—” (“﻿—and *sugar plums*!” Bruno put in once more; and Sylvie seemed satisfied.)
“And ever after that, they *were* such good little Foxes!

===Chinese===

然后你们会听见晚饭铃响。然后你们下楼来：*晚饭是没有的*：倒有一顿好*鞭子*！然后你们上床睡觉。然后早上你们会听见早饭铃响。*早饭也没有！*有好*鞭子*一顿！然后你们上课。也许，要是你们*乖得不得了*，到了午饭时辰，能有一点点午饭，鞭子也就免了！’”（“他心肠*可真好*！”我悄悄对布鲁诺说。“*马马虎虎*的好，”布鲁诺一本正经地纠正我。）
“于是小狐狸们跑上育婴房。不多时，布鲁诺走进大厅，摇响大铃。‘叮铃，叮铃，叮铃！晚饭，晚饭，晚饭！’小狐狸们冲下楼来，赶晚饭赶得急慌慌！围嘴儿干干净净！手里小勺握着！进了饭厅，桌上铺的白桌布那个白呀！可桌上只有一样东西——一根大鞭子。于是他们挨了*好一顿*鞭子！”（我拿手绢掩住眼，布鲁诺赶忙爬上我的膝头，抚摸我的脸。“再挨*一*顿鞭子就没有了，先生先生！”他悄声说。“能忍住就别哭啦！”）
“第二天一大早，布鲁诺又摇响大铃。‘叮铃，叮铃，叮铃！早饭，早饭，早饭！’小狐狸们下楼来！围嘴儿干干净净！手里小勺握着！早饭没有！只有大鞭子！接着上课，”西尔维赶紧往下讲，因为我手绢还掩着眼。“小狐狸们乖得不得了！功课倒着背、顺着背、翻个儿背，样样滚瓜烂熟。终于布鲁诺又摇响大铃。‘叮铃，叮铃，叮铃！午饭，午饭，午饭！’小狐狸们下楼来时——”（“围嘴儿干净吗？”布鲁诺问。“当然！”西尔维说。“小勺呢？”“哎呀，你*明明知道*他们拿着！”“不敢*说死*，”布鲁诺说。）“——他们走得慢啊慢！嘴里念叨：‘哦！又没有午饭！只有大鞭子！’可一进屋——他们看见了*顶顶可爱*的午饭！”（“有小圆面包？”布鲁诺拍着手喊。）“小圆面包，还有蛋糕，还有——”（“——还有果酱？”布鲁诺说。）“对，果酱——还有汤——还有——”（“——还有*糖李子*！”布鲁诺又插一句；西尔维似乎满意了。）
“打那以后，他们*当真*成了乖乖的小狐狸！

===Original===

They did their lessons as good as gold﻿—and they never did what Bruno told them not to﻿—and they never ate each other any more﻿—and *they never ate themselves*!”
The story came to an end so suddenly, it almost took my breath away; however I did my best to make a pretty speech of thanks. “I’m sure it’s very﻿—very﻿—very much so, I’m sure!” I seemed to hear myself say.

===Chinese===

功课做得金子般乖乖——布鲁诺不许的事一件也不做——他们再也不互相吃——而且*再也不吃自己*！”
故事收得那么突然，几乎叫我背过气去；我还是竭力致了一段漂亮的谢辞。“我确信这实在——实在——实在如此之极，我确信！”我仿佛听见自己这样说。

## XVI Beyond These Voices / XVI 在这些声音之外
===Original===

“I didn’t quite catch what you said!” were the next words that reached my ear, but certainly *not* in the voice either of Sylvie or of Bruno, whom I could just see, through the crowd of guests, standing by the piano, and listening to the Count’s song. Mein Herr was the speaker. “I didn’t quite catch what you said!” he repeated. “But I’ve no doubt you take *my* view of it. Thank you *very* much for your kind attention. There is only but *one* verse left to be sung!” These last words were not in the gentle voice of Mein Herr, but in the deep bass of the French Count. And, in the silence that followed, the final stanza of “Tottles” rang through the room.

> See now this couple settled down
> In quiet lodgings, out of town:
> Submissively the tearful wife
> Accepts a plain and humble life:
> Yet begs one boon on bended knee:
> ‘My ducky-darling, don’t resent it!
> Mamma might come for two or three﻿—’
> “**never**!” yelled Tottles. And he meant it.

===Chinese===

「您方才的话我没大听清！」——这是我耳中接着响起的下一句话，可那嗓音分明*不是*西尔维的，也不是布鲁诺的；隔着满屋宾客，我恰好望见他们俩站在钢琴旁，听伯爵唱歌。说话的是我先生。「您方才的话我没大听清！」他重复道。「不过我毫不怀疑，您同*我*的看法一致。多谢您善意的聆听。只剩*一*段词儿要唱了！」末了这几句，不是我先生温和的嗓音，而是法国伯爵深沉的男低音。随后的寂静里，《托托尔斯》的最后一段歌词响彻了大厅。

> 瞧这一对儿落了户，
> 城外幽居远尘俗：
> 含泪的妻子恭顺本分，
> 甘守平淡卑微的浮生：
> 只屈膝恳求一件事：
> 「我的鸭鸭宝贝，可别见怪！
> 说不定妈妈要来住两三天——」
> 「决不！」托托尔斯吼道。他可是当真的。

===Original===

The conclusion of the song was followed by quite a chorus of thanks and compliments from all parts of the room, which the gratified singer responded to by bowing low in all directions. “It is to me a great privilege,” he said to Lady Muriel, “to have met with this so marvellous a song. The accompaniment to him is so strange, so mysterious: it is as if a new music were to be invented! I will play him once again so as that to show you what I mean.” He returned to the piano, but the song had vanished.
The bewildered singer searched through the heap of music lying on an adjoining table, but it was not there, either. Lady Muriel helped in the search: others soon joined: the excitement grew. “What *can* have become of it?” exclaimed Lady Muriel. Nobody knew: one thing only was certain, that no one had been near the piano since the Count had sung the last verse of the song.
“Nevare mind him!” he said, most good-naturedly. “I shall give it you with memory alone!” He sat down, and began vaguely fingering the notes; but nothing resembling the tune came out. Then he, too, grew excited. “But what oddness! How much of singularity! That I might lose, not the words alone, but the tune also﻿—that is quite curious, I suppose?”
We all supposed it, heartily.
“It was that sweet little boy, who found it for me,” the Count suggested. “Quite perhaps *he* is the thief?”
“Of course he is!” cried Lady Muriel. “Bruno! Where are you, my darling?”
But no Bruno replied: it seemed that the two children had vanished as suddenly, and as mysteriously, as the song.
“They are playing us a trick!” Lady Muriel gaily exclaimed. “This is only an extempore game of Hide-and-Seek! That little Bruno is an embodied Mischief!”
The suggestion was a welcome one to most of us, for some of the guests were beginning to look decidedly uneasy. A general search was set on foot with much enthusiasm: curtains were thrown back and shaken, cupboards opened, and ottomans turned over; but the number of possible hiding-places proved to be strictly limited; and the search came to an end almost as soon as it had begun.
“They must have run out, while we were wrapped up in the song,” Lady Muriel said, addressing herself to the Count, who seemed more agitated than the others; “and no doubt they’ve found their way back to the housekeeper’s room.”
“Not by *this* door!”

===Chinese===

歌声一收尾，满屋各处随即响起一片道谢与赞美的大合唱，心满意足的歌者向四面八方频频躬身还礼。「能遇上这样一支神妙的歌，」他对穆丽尔小姐说，「于我是莫大的荣幸。那伴奏如此奇特，如此神秘：简直像是要发明出一种新的音乐！我要再弹一遍，好让您明白我的意思。」他回到钢琴前，可那首歌不见了。
茫然的歌者翻检着旁边桌上一堆乐谱，可那儿也没有。穆丽尔小姐帮着找：旁人很快也加入：群情愈发激动。「它到底*能*去哪儿了呢？」穆丽尔小姐喊道。谁也不知道：只有一件事确凿无疑——自伯爵唱完末段歌词起，谁也不曾走近过钢琴。
「别在意它！」他好脾气地说。「我凭记忆再给您唱一遍！」他坐下，开始恍惚地抚弄琴键；可出来的东西没有一句像那调子。这下连他也激动起来。「多古怪啊！多么离奇！我竟丢了词儿不算，连调子也丢了——这可太蹊跷了，是不是？」
我们人人深以为然，蹊跷极了。
「是那个可爱的小男孩替我找到的，」伯爵提示道。「说不定*他*就是那个贼？」
「当然是他！」穆丽尔小姐喊道。「布鲁诺！你在哪儿呀，亲爱的？」
可布鲁诺没有应声：看来这两个孩子同那首歌一样，来得突兀，去得神秘。
「他们是在同咱们玩把戏呢！」穆丽尔小姐快活地喊。「这不过是一场即兴的捉迷藏！小布鲁诺就是个捣蛋精托生的！」
这个说法大家多半乐于接受，因为有的客人已经露出明显的不安。一场大搜查在极高的热情中展开：窗帘被捾开抖了又抖，橱柜纷纷打开，软墩一一翻倒；可是可行的藏身之处实在有限；搜查几乎刚开始便告结束。
「准是趁咱们听得入神，他们跑出去了，」穆丽尔小姐对伯爵说——他看上去比别人更惶惶不安；「不用说，他们已经摸回管家屋里去了。」
「绝不是*这*扇门！」

===Original===

was the earnest protest of a knot of two or three gentlemen, who had been grouped round the door (one of them actually leaning against it) for the last half-hour, as they declared. “*This* door has not been opened since the song began!”
An uncomfortable silence followed this announcement. Lady Muriel ventured no further conjectures, but quietly examined the fastenings of the windows, which opened as doors. They all proved to be well fastened, *inside*.
Not yet at the end of her resources, Lady Muriel rang the bell. “Ask the housekeeper to step here,” she said, “and to bring the children’s walking-things with her.”
“I’ve brought them, my Lady,” said the obsequious housekeeper, entering after another minute of silence. “I thought the young lady would have come to my room to put on her boots. Here’s your boots, my love!” she added cheerfully, looking in all directions for the children. There was no answer, and she turned to Lady Muriel with a puzzled smile. “Have the little darlings hid themselves?”
“I don’t see them, just now,” Lady Muriel replied, rather evasively. “You can leave their things here, Wilson. *I’ll* dress them, when they’re ready to go.”
The two little hats, and Sylvie’s walking-jacket, were handed round among the ladies, with many exclamations of delight. There certainly was a sort of witchery of beauty about them. Even the little boots did not miss their share of favorable criticism. “Such natty little things!” the musical young lady exclaimed, almost fondling them as she spoke. “And what tiny tiny feet they must have!”
Finally, the things were piled together on the centre-ottoman, and the guests, despairing of seeing the children again, began to wish good night and leave the house.
There were only some eight or nine left﻿—to whom the Count was explaining, for the twentieth time, how he had had his eye on the children during the last verse of the song; how he had then glanced round the room, to see what effect “de great chest-note” had had upon his audience; and how, when he looked back again, they had both disappeared﻿—when exclamations of dismay began to be heard on all sides, the Count hastily bringing his story to an end to join in the outcry.
The walking-things had all disappeared!
After the utter failure of the search for the *children*, there was a very halfhearted search made for their *apparel*.

===Chinese===

两三位聚作一小团的先生们恳切地抗议——据他们自己声明，这半个钟头里他们一直围在门边（其中一位还真倚在门上）。「打唱歌起，*这*扇门就没开过！」
这番宣告之后是一阵尴尬的沉默。穆丽尔小姐不再作进一步的猜测，只默默去检查那几扇落地窗的插销。扇扇都从*里*面闩得严严实实。
穆丽尔小姐还没到江郎才尽的地步，她摇了铃。「请管家太太过来一趟，」她说，「让她把孩子们出门的衣帽鞋一并带来。」
「都带来了，小姐，」殷勤的管家太太在又一阵沉默之后进屋说。「我原以为小姐会到我屋里穿靴子呢。你的靴子在这儿，小亲亲！」她快活地补了一句，四下张望着找孩子们。没有应声，她转向穆丽尔小姐，一脸困惑的笑。「小宝贝们藏起来了？」
「这会儿我没瞧见他们，」穆丽尔小姐答得颇为含糊。「东西放这儿就行，威尔逊。等他们要走时，*我*来给他们穿戴。」
两顶小帽子，还有西尔维的出门短外套，在女士们手里传看，引来一阵阵赞叹。它们周身确有一种魔咒般的美。连那双小靴子也分毫不缺地赢得了好评。「多俊巧的小玩意儿！」那位擅长音乐的年轻小姐喊道，说话间几乎爱抚起它们来。「他们的脚该多小多小呀！」
末了，衣帽什物被归拢堆在中央的软墩上，客人们再见孩子无望，纷纷道晚安离去。
屋里只剩八九个人——伯爵正对他们第二十遍解释：唱歌末段时，他的眼睛如何一直盯着那两个孩子；随后他如何环视大厅，看看「那伟大的胸声」在听众身上产生了何等效果；等他再回头，两个孩子又如何都不见了——正说着，惊呼从四面八方响起，伯爵匆匆收住故事，加入了大呼小叫。
那些出门衣帽全都不见了！
搜寻*孩子*既已彻底失败，搜寻他们的*衣装*便进行得有气无力。

===Original===

The remaining guests seemed only too glad to get away, leaving only the Count and our four selves.
The Count sank into an easy-chair, and panted a little.
“Who then *are* these dear children, I pray you?” he said. “Why come they, why go they, in this so little ordinary a fashion? That the music should make itself to vanish﻿—that the hats, the boots, should make themselves to vanish﻿—how is it, I pray you?”
“I’ve no idea where they are!” was all I could say, on finding myself appealed to, by general consent, for an explanation.
The Count seemed about to ask further questions, but checked himself.
“The hour makes himself to become late,” he said. “I wish to you a very good night, my Lady. I betake myself to my bed﻿—to dream﻿—if that indeed I be not dreaming now!” And he hastily left the room.
“Stay awhile, stay awhile!” said the Earl, as I was about to follow the Count. “*You* are not a guest, you know! Arthur’s friend is at *home* here!”
“Thanks!” I said, as, with true English instincts, we drew our chairs together round the fireplace, though no fire was burning﻿—Lady Muriel having taken the heap of music on her knee, to have one more search for the strangely-vanished song.
“Don’t you sometimes feel a wild longing,” she said, addressing herself to me, “to have something more to do with your hands, while you talk, than just holding a cigar, and now and then knocking off the ash? Oh, I know all that you’re going to say!” (This was to Arthur, who appeared about to interrupt her.) “The Majesty of Thought supersedes the work of the fingers. A Man’s severe thinking, *plus* the shaking-off a cigar-ash, comes to the same total as a Woman’s trivial fancies, *plus* the most elaborate embroidery. *That’s* your sentiment, isn’t it, only better expressed?”
Arthur looked into the radiant, mischievous face, with a grave and very tender smile. “Yes,” he said resignedly: “that is my sentiment, exactly.”
“Rest of body, and activity of mind,” I put in. “Some writer tells us *that* is the acme of human happiness.”
“Plenty of *bodily* rest, at any rate!” Lady Muriel replied, glancing at the three recumbent figures around her. “But what you call activity of *mind*﻿—”
“﻿—is the privilege of young Physicians *only*,” said the Earl. “We old men have no claim to be active! *What can an old man do but die?*”
“A good many other things, I should *hope*,” Arthur said earnestly.

===Chinese===

余下的客人巴不得赶紧脱身，屋里只留下伯爵和我们四个。
伯爵跌坐进一把安乐椅，微微喘着气。
「这两位可爱的孩子，究竟*是*什么人呢，我请问？」他说。「他们为何这样来，为何这样去，这般毫不寻常？那乐谱竟自己不见了——帽子、靴子，也自己不见了——这是怎么回事，我请问？」
「他们在哪儿，我一无所知！」众人一致把目光投向我讨解释，我能说的只有这一句。
伯爵似乎还想再问，却又忍住了。
「时辰自己变晚了，」他说。「祝您晚安，小姐。我这就上床——去做梦——倘若我现在不是正在做梦的话！」说完他匆匆出了屋。
「再坐会儿，再坐会儿！」我正要随伯爵告辞，伯爵大人说话了。「*您*可不算客人，您知道的！亚瑟的朋友在这里就*是*在家里！」
「多谢！」我说。凭着纯正的英国人天性，我们把椅子拖拢到壁炉旁围坐——炉里并没有火——穆丽尔小姐把那堆乐谱抱到膝上，想把那支离奇消失的歌再搜寻一遍。
「您有时候会不会有种狂热的渴望，」她对我说，「希望谈话时手里有点别的事做，而不只是攥着支雪茄、时不时弹掉烟灰？哦，您要说什么我全知道！」（这是对亚瑟说的，他正要插嘴。）「思想之尊贵取代了手指的劳作。男人的严肃思考，*加上*弹一弹雪茄灰，同女人的琐碎遐想，*加上*最精巧的刺绣，加出来是同一个总数。*这*就是您的高见吧，只不过我说得更漂亮些？」
亚瑟望着那张容光焕发、调皮捣蛋的脸，露出庄重而十分温柔的笑。「是的，」他认命地说：「正是我的高见，一字不差。」
「身体歇着，头脑动着，」我插言。「有位作家告诉我们，*那*是人类幸福的极致。」
「*身体*歇着，倒是歇足了！」穆丽尔小姐答，瞟了一眼她周围三个瘫坐的人影。「可你们所说的头脑*动*着——」
「——是*年轻*大夫独有的特权，」伯爵说。「我们老头子可没资格动！*老人家除了死，还能做什么呢？*」
「还有好些别的事可做呢，我*希望*，」亚瑟诚恳地说。

===Original===

“Well, maybe. Still you have the advantage of me in many ways, dear boy! Not only that *your* day is dawning while *mine* is setting, but your *interest* in Life﻿—somehow I can’t help envying you *that*. It will be many a year before you lose your hold of *that*.”
“Yet surely many human interests *survive* human Life?” I said.
“Many do, no doubt. And *some* forms of Science; but only *some*, I think. Mathematics, for instance: *that* seems to possess an endless interest: one can’t imagine *any* form of Life, or *any* race of intelligent beings, where Mathematical truth would lose its meaning. But I fear *Medicine* stands on a different footing. Suppose you discover a remedy for some disease hitherto supposed to be incurable. Well, it is delightful for the moment, no doubt﻿—full of interest﻿—perhaps it brings you fame and fortune. But what then? Look on, a few years, into a life where disease has no existence. What is your discovery worth, *then*? Milton makes Jove promise too much. ‘*Of so much fame in heaven expect thy meed.*’ Poor comfort, when one’s ‘fame’ concerns matters that will have ceased to have a meaning!”
“At any rate, one wouldn’t care to make any *fresh* medical discoveries,” said Arthur. “I see no help for *that*﻿—though I shall be sorry to give up my favorite studies. Still, medicine, disease, pain, sorrow, sin﻿—I fear they’re all linked together. Banish sin, and you banish them all!”
“*Military* science is a yet stronger instance,” said the Earl. “Without sin, *war* would surely be impossible. Still any mind, that has had in this life any keen interest, not in *itself* sinful, will surely find itself *some* congenial line of work hereafter. Wellington may have no more *battles* to fight﻿—and yet﻿—

> ‘We doubt not that, for one so true,
> There must be other, nobler work to do,
> Than when he fought at Waterloo,
> And Victor he must ever be!’ ”

===Chinese===

「嗯，也许吧。不过在许多方面你仍胜过我，好孩子！不只是*你的*日子正在破晓，而*我的*日头正在西沉；还有你对人生的*兴味*——不知怎么，我不由得不羡慕。要过许多许多年，你才会松开它呢。」
「可人的许多兴味*必定*比人本身活得更久吧？」我说。
「许多会的，无疑。还有*某几门*科学；但我看只是某几门。譬如数学：*它*似乎拥有无穷的兴味：你想不出*任何*形态的生命、*任何*种族的智慧生灵，在那里数学真理会失去意义。可我怕*医学*的立足点不同。假定你发现了一种疗法，医治某种至今公认不治的病。嗯，眼前自然令人欣喜——趣味盎然——或许还带来名利。可再往后呢？往后再看几年，看到那个疾病不复存在的生命里。到*那时*，你的发现还值什么？弥尔顿让朱庇特许的愿太大啦。『*在天庭享有如此声名，静候你的酬报。*』当一个人的『声名』系于那些终将失去意义的事情时，这算什么安慰！」
「不管怎样，到那时谁也不会想去作什么*新的*医学发现了，」亚瑟说。「这*没法子*——虽然放弃我心爱的研究我会难过。再说，医药、疾病、痛苦、忧患、罪孽——我怕它们是拴在一根绳上的。逐去罪孽，便把它们统统逐去了！」
「*军事*学是更明显的例子，」伯爵说。「没有罪，*战争*必无从发生。不过，凡在此生有过任何炽热兴味的心智——只要那兴味本身无罪——来世必能找到*某*种合宜的事做。威灵顿也许再无*战役*可打——然而——

> 『我们深信，如此赤诚之士，
> 必另有更高贵的功业等着他，
> 远胜当年滑铁卢一战，
> 而他必是永远的胜利者！』」

===Original===

He lingered over the beautiful words, as if he loved them: and his voice, like distant music, died away into silence.
After a minute or two he began again. “If I’m not wearying you, I would like to tell you an idea of the future Life which has haunted me for years, like a sort of waking nightmare﻿—I can’t reason myself out of it.”
“Pray do,” Arthur and I replied, almost in a breath. Lady Muriel put aside the heap of music, and folded her hands together.
“The one idea,” the Earl resumed, “that has seemed to me to overshadow all the rest, is that of *Eternity*﻿—involving, as it seems to do, the necessary *exhaustion* of all subjects of human interest. Take Pure Mathematics, for instance﻿—a Science independent of our present surroundings. I have studied it, myself, a little. Take the subject of circles and ellipses﻿—what we call ‘curves of the second degree.’ In a future Life, it would only be a question of so many years (or *hundreds* of years, if you like), for a man to work out *all* their properties. Then he *might* go to curves of the third degree. Say *that* took ten times as long (you see we have *unlimited* time to deal with). I can hardly imagine his *interest* in the subject holding out even for those; and, though there is no limit to the *degree* of the curves he might study, yet surely the time, needed to exhaust *all* the novelty and interest of the subject, would be absolutely *finite*? And so of all other branches of Science. And, when I transport myself, in thought, through some thousands or millions of years, and fancy myself possessed of as much Science as one created reason can carry, I ask myself ‘What then? With nothing more to learn, can one rest content on *knowledge*, for the eternity yet to be lived through?’ It has been a very wearying thought to me. I have sometimes fancied one *might*, in that event, say ‘It is better *not* to be,’ and pray for personal *annihilation*﻿—the Nirvana of the Buddhists.”
“But that is only half the picture,” I said. “Besides working for *oneself*, may there not be the helping of *others*?”
“Surely, surely!” Lady Muriel exclaimed in a tone of relief, looking at her father with sparkling eyes.
“Yes,” said the Earl, “so long as there *were* any others needing help. But, given ages and ages more, surely all created reasons would at length reach the same dead level of *satiety*.

===Chinese===

他在优美的诗句上流连，仿佛爱着它们：他的声音，像远处的音乐，渐渐消逝于寂静。
一两分钟后他又开口。「你们若不嫌烦，我想讲讲一个关于来生的念头，它纠缠我多年了，像一场醒着的梦魇——我讲道理也讲不脱它。」
「请讲请讲，」亚瑟和我几乎异口同声。穆丽尔小姐把那堆乐谱放到一边，双手交握。
「在我心里盖过其余一切的念头，」伯爵接着说，「是*永恒*——因为它似乎必然带来一切人间趣味的*枯竭*。就拿纯粹数学来说——一门不依赖我们当下环境的科学。我自己就稍微涉猎过。拿圆和椭圆这个题目来说——就是我们所谓的『二次曲线』。在来生，一个人只须若干年（或者说*几百*年，随你），便可推演完它们的*全部*性质。然后他*也许*转向三次曲线。假定*那*要花十倍的时间（你瞧，我们有*无限*的时间可支配）。我很难想象他对这题目的*兴味*连那些也撑得住；而且，尽管他可研究的曲线*次数*没有上限，可穷尽这题目的全部新意与趣味所需的时间，必定是*有限*的，不是吗？其他一切科学门类莫不如此。而当我神游几千年、几百万年之后，设想自己怀有一个受造心智所能承载的全部科学，我问自己：『那时又如何？再无可学之时，人能安于*知识*，去度过尚待度过的永恒吗？』这于我一直是个令人疲惫的念头。有时我想，处此境地，人*也许*会说：『还是*不*存在为好』，祈求个人的*寂灭*——佛家的涅槃。」
「可那只是半幅图画，」我说。「除了为*自己*用功，难道没有帮助*他人*这回事吗？」
「当然，当然有！」穆丽尔小姐如释重负地喊道，目光灼灼地望着父亲。
「是的，」伯爵说，「只要*还有*别人需要帮助。可是再过一纪又一纪，一切受造心智终将抵达同一个餍足的死水平。

===Original===

And *then* what is there to look forward to?”
“I know that weary feeling,” said the young Doctor. “I have gone through it all, more than once. Now let me tell you how I have put it to myself. I have imagined a little child, playing with toys on his nursery-floor, and yet able to *reason*, and to look on, thirty years ahead. Might he not say to himself ‘By that time I shall have had enough of bricks and ninepins. How weary Life will be!’ Yet, if we look forward through those thirty years, we find him a great statesman, full of interests and joys far more intense than his baby-life could give﻿—joys wholly inconceivable to his baby-mind﻿—joys such as no baby-language could in the faintest degree describe. Now, may not our life, a million years hence, have the same relation, to our life now, that the man’s life has to the child’s? And, just as one might try, all in vain, to express to that child, in the language of bricks and ninepins, the meaning of ‘politics,’ so perhaps all those descriptions of Heaven, with its music, and its feasts, and its streets of gold, may be only attempts to describe, in *our* words, things for which we *really* have no words at all. Don’t you think that, in *your* picture of another life, you are in fact transplanting that child into political life, without making any allowance for his growing up?”
“I think I understand you,” said the Earl. “The music of Heaven *may* be something beyond our powers of thought. Yet the music of Earth is sweet! Muriel, my child, sing us something before we go to bed!”
“Do,” said Arthur, as he rose and lit the candles on the cottage-piano, lately banished from the drawing-room to make room for a “semi-grand.” “There is a song here, that I have never heard you sing.

> ‘Hail to thee, blithe spirit!
> Bird thou never wert,
> That from Heaven, or near it,
> Pourest thy full heart!’ ”

he read from the page he had spread open before her.
“And our little life here,” the Earl went on, “is, to that grand time, like a child’s summer-day! One gets tired as night draws on,” he added, with a touch of sadness in his voice, “and one gets to long for bed! For those welcome words ‘Come, child, ’tis bedtime!’ ”

===Chinese===

到*那时*，还有什么可盼的呢？」
「这疲惫的感觉我知道，」年轻的医生说。「它我全经历过，不止一回。让我告诉你们，我是怎么给自己解的。我想象一个小孩子，在育儿室的地板上玩着玩具，却又能*推理*，能往前看三十年。他会不会对自己说：『到那时候，积木和九柱戏我早玩腻了。人生将多么乏味啊！』然而，我们顺着这三十年望过去，看到的是一位伟大的政治家，满怀远比婴孩时代所能给的更炽烈的兴味与欢乐——那些欢乐，他的婴孩心智全然无法想象——没有任何婴孩语言能描摹其万一。那么，我们百万年后的生命，之于我们现在的生命，会不会正是那成年人的生命之于孩童的生命？正如人们用积木和九柱戏的语言，向那孩子解释『政治』，必是徒劳；同样，人们对于天堂的一切描画——它的音乐、它的盛宴、它的黄金街——也许不过是想用*我们的*语言，去描述我们*实在*没有语言可描述的事物。您不觉得，在您那幅来生图景里，您其实是把那孩子移栽进了政治生活，却全没算上他会长大么？」
「我想我懂你的意思了，」伯爵说。「天堂的音乐*也许*远超我们思想的能力。可尘世的音乐毕竟是甜美的！穆丽尔，我的孩子，睡觉前给我们唱点什么吧！」
「唱吧，」亚瑟说着起身，点亮那架小立式钢琴上的蜡烛——那琴新近被从客厅里撵了出来，好给一架「半大」三角琴腾地方。「这儿有一支歌，我从没听你唱过。

> 「万福，欢畅的精灵！
> 你何曾是只禽鸟，
> 你自天庭，或天庭的近旁，
> 倾洒出你整个的心魂！」

他把她面前摊开的那页念了出来。
「而我们的这点小生命，」伯爵接着说，「同那伟大的时辰相比，就像孩子的夏日！天一黑，人就倦了，」他添了一句，嗓音里带一丝凄然，「人就盼着上床了！盼着那句受欢迎的话：『来，孩子，该睡觉了！』」

## XVII To the Rescue! / 十七 赴救援！
===Original===

“It *isn’t* bedtime!” said a sleepy little voice. “The owls hasn’t gone to bed, and I s’a’n’t go to seep wizout oo sings to me!”
“Oh, Bruno!” cried Sylvie. “Don’t you know the owls have only just got up? But the *frogs* have gone to bed, ages ago.”
“Well, *I* aren’t a frog,” said Bruno.
“What shall I sing?” said Sylvie, skilfully avoiding the argument.
“Ask Mister Sir,” Bruno lazily replied, clasping his hands behind his curly head, and lying back on his fern-leaf, till it almost bent over with his weight. “This aren’t a comfable leaf, Sylvie. Find me a comfabler﻿—please!” he added, as an afterthought, in obedience to a warning finger held up by Sylvie. “I doosn’t like being feet-upwards!”
It was a pretty sight to see﻿—the motherly way in which the fairy-child gathered up her little brother in her arms, and laid him on a stronger leaf. She gave it just a touch to set it rocking, and it went on vigorously by itself, as if it contained some hidden machinery. It certainly wasn’t the wind, for the evening-breeze had quite died away again, and not a leaf was stirring over our heads.
“Why does that one leaf rock so, without the others?” I asked Sylvie. She only smiled sweetly and shook her head. “I don’t know *why*,” she said. “It always does, if it’s got a fairy-child on it. It *has* to, you know.”
“And can people see the leaf rock, who can’t see the Fairy on it?”
“Why, of course!” cried Sylvie. “A leaf’s a leaf, and everybody can see it; but Bruno’s Bruno, and they can’t see *him*, unless they’re eerie, like you.”
Then I understood how it was that one sometimes sees﻿—going through the woods in a still evening﻿—one fern-leaf rocking steadily on, all by itself. Haven’t you ever seen that? Try if you can see the fairy-sleeper on it, next time; but don’t *pick* the leaf, whatever you do; let the little one sleep on!
But all this time Bruno was getting sleepier and sleepier. “Sing, sing!” he murmured fretfully. Sylvie looked to me for instructions. “What shall it be?” she said.
“Could you sing him the nursery-song you once told me of?” I suggested. “The one that had been put through the mind-mangle, you know. ‘*The little man that had a little gun*,’ I think it was.”
“Why, that are one of the *Professor’s* songs!” cried Bruno. “I likes the little man; and I likes the way they spinned him﻿—like a teetle-totle-tum.”

===Chinese===

“*还不到*睡觉时间！”一个睡意惺忪的小嗓音说。“猫头鹰还没上床呢，咚不给俺唱歌，俺就不碎觉！”
“哦，布鲁诺！”西尔维喊。“你不知道猫头鹰才刚起床吗？倒是*青蛙*早就睡了，睡了好久好久了。”
“可*俺*又不是青蛙，”布鲁诺说。
“我唱什么呢？”西尔维说，巧妙地绕开这场争论。
“问先生先生，”布鲁诺懒洋洋地答，双手枕在卷发脑袋后面，仰面躺上他的蕨叶，压得叶子几乎要折过去。“这片叶子不舒服，西尔维。给俺换个舒服的——麻烦你了！”他又补了一句，算是亡羊补牢，因为西尔维竖起了一根警告的手指。“俺不喜欢头朝下脚朝上！”
那景象真好看——仙童小姑娘那副小妈妈的样子，把小弟弟抱进怀里，安放到一片结实的叶子上。她轻轻一碰，叶子便摇起来，随后自顾自摇得欢畅，仿佛里头藏着什么隐秘的机关。那当然不是风，因为晚风又完全止息了，头顶没有一片叶子在动。
“怎么单单那片叶子摇，别的叶子不摇？”我问西尔维。她只是甜甜一笑，摇摇头。“我*不*知道为什么，”她说。“只要有仙童睡在上面，它就总摇。它*不得不*摇，你知道的。”
“那看不见仙童的人，也看得见叶子在摇吗？”
“哎呀，当然！”西尔维叫道。“叶子就是叶子，谁都看得见；可布鲁诺是布鲁诺，他们就看不见*他*——除非他们有灵异之眼，像你一样。”
这一来我才明白，为什么人在寂静的傍晚穿过树林，有时会看见一片蕨叶独自悠悠地摇个不停。你可曾见过？下回试试，看能不能看见叶上酣睡的小仙童；只是千万别把叶子*摘*了；让小家伙睡下去吧！
可这一路布鲁诺越困越深。“唱歌，唱歌！”他烦躁地喃咕。西尔维向我请示。“唱哪个呢？”她说。
“能不能给他唱你跟我说过的那支童谣？”我提议。“就是被‘绞脑机’绞过的那支，你知道。‘《小人儿扛着小枪》’，我记得是。”
“咦，那是*教授*的歌！”布鲁诺喊。“俺喜欢那个小人儿；俺喜欢他们把他转起来的样子——像个滴滴嘟噜陀螺。”

“He’s sound asleep now,” said Sylvie, carefully tucking in the edge of a violet-leaf, which she had been spreading over him as a sort of blanket: “good night!”
“Good night!” I echoed.
“You may well say ‘good night’!” laughed Lady Muriel, rising and shutting up the piano as she spoke. “When you’ve been nid﻿—nid﻿—nodding all the time I’ve been singing for your benefit! What was it all about, now?” she demanded imperiously.
“Something about a duck?” I hazarded. “Well, a bird of some kind?” I corrected myself, perceiving at once that *that* guess was wrong, at any rate.
“*Something about a bird of some kind!*” Lady Muriel repeated, with as much withering scorn as her sweet face was capable of conveying. “And that’s the way he speaks of Shelley’s Skylark, is it? When the Poet particularly says ‘*Hail to thee, blithe spirit!* Bird *thou never wert*!’ ”
She led the way to the smoking-room, where, ignoring all the usages of Society and all the instincts of Chivalry, the three Lords of the Creation reposed at their ease in low rocking-chairs, and permitted the one lady who was present to glide gracefully about among us, supplying our wants in the form of cooling drinks, cigarettes, and lights. 她领头进了吸烟室。在那里，我们三位“万物之灵”全然不顾社交的规矩和骑士的风度，各自舒舒服服歪在低摇椅里，任在场唯一的女士仪态万方地在我们之间穿梭，以冷饮、香烟和火种满足我们的需要。不，三位里头也只有*一位*有点骑士气，不满足于一句平平常常的“谢谢”，还引了诗人那段精妙的描写——杰莱恩特受伊妮德侍奉时，如何被触动

As no topic of conversation seemed to occur to anyone, and as we were, all four, on those delightful terms with one another (the only terms, I think, on which any friendship, that deserves the name of *intimacy*, can be maintained) which involve no sort of necessity for *speaking* for mere speaking’s sake, we sat in silence for some minutes.
At length I broke the silence by asking “Is there any fresh news from the harbour about the Fever?”
“None since this morning,” the Earl said, looking very grave. “But that was alarming enough. The Fever is spreading fast: the London doctor has taken fright and left the place, and the only one now available isn’t a regular doctor at all: he is apothecary, and doctor, and dentist, and I don’t know what other trades, all in one. It’s a bad outlook for those poor fishermen﻿—and a worse one for all the women and children.”
“How many are there of them altogether?” Arthur asked.
“There were nearly one hundred, a week ago,” said the Earl: “but there have been twenty or thirty deaths since then.”
“And what religious ministrations are there to be had?”
“There are three brave men down there,” the Earl replied, his voice trembling with emotion, “gallant heroes as ever won the Victoria Cross! I am certain that no one of the three will ever leave the place merely to save his own life. There’s the Curate: his wife is with him: they have no children. Then there’s the Roman Catholic Priest. And there’s the Wesleyan Minister. They go amongst their own flocks, mostly; but I’m told that those who are dying like to have *any* of the three with them. How slight the barriers seem to be that part Christian from Christian, when one has to deal with the great facts of Life and the reality of Death!”
“So it must be, and so it should be﻿—” Arthur was beginning, when the front-door bell rang, suddenly and violently.
We heard the front-door hastily opened, and voices outside: then a knock at the door of the smoking-room, and the old housekeeper appeared, looking a little scared.
“Two persons, my Lord, to speak with Dr. Forester.”
Arthur stepped outside at once, and we heard his cheery “Well, my men?”

===Chinese===

而且言行合一——这份大胆的放肆，我必须报告，他并*未*受到应有的申斥。
谁也想不起什么话题，而我们四个又正处于那种彼此相得的妙境（我想，凡当得起*知交*二字的友谊，唯有在这种境界里才维持得长久）——不必为了说话而*说话*——于是我们默默坐了几分钟。
终于我打破沉默，问道：“港口那边，热病有什么新消息吗？”
“早上之后没有了，”伯爵说，面色极为凝重。“可早上的消息已经够吓人。热病蔓延得很快：伦敦来的医生吓破了胆，走了；如今唯一顶事的压根不是正经医生：他一人兼着药师、大夫、牙医，天知道还有多少行当。那些可怜的渔夫，前景不妙——妇女孩子们，更糟。”
“他们一共还有多少人？”亚瑟问。
“一星期前将近一百人，”伯爵说：“此后已经死了二三十个。”
“那边的宗教照应呢，可有？”
“那边有三位勇士，”伯爵答道，嗓音激动得发颤，“个个都是配得维多利亚十字勋章的英杰！我确信，三人中没有一个会为保自己性命而离开那里。一位是副牧师：妻子陪着：没有孩子。还有一位是天主教神父。还有一位是卫斯理会牧师。他们大体各顾各的羊群；但我听说，垂死的人愿意*他们中任何一位*守在身旁。一旦面对生的要事、死的实情，那把基督徒与基督徒隔开的樊篱，显得多么微薄！”
“理当如此，也必当如此——”亚瑟刚起话头，前门铃骤然狂响。
我们听见前门仓皇打开，门外人声嘈嘈：接着吸烟室门上一敲，老管家现身，神色微带惊惶。
“两个人，勋爵，要同福雷斯特医生说话。”
亚瑟立刻跨出门去，我们听见他那快活的“怎么了，伙计们？”

===Original===

but the answer was less audible, the only words I could distinctly catch being “ten since morning, and two more just﻿—”
“But there *is* a doctor there?” we heard Arthur say: and a deep voice, that we had not heard before, replied “Dead, Sir. Died three hours ago.”
Lady Muriel shuddered, and hid her face in her hands: but at this moment the front-door was quietly closed, and we heard no more.
For a few minutes we sat quite silent: then the Earl left the room, and soon returned to tell us that Arthur had gone away with the two fishermen, leaving word that he would be back in about an hour. And, true enough, at the end of that interval﻿—during which very little was said, none of us seeming to have the heart to talk﻿—the front-door once more creaked on its rusty hinges, and a step was heard in the passage, hardly to be recognised as Arthur’s, so slow and uncertain was it, like a blind man feeling his way.
He came in, and stood before Lady Muriel, resting one hand heavily on the table, and with a strange look in his eyes, as if he were walking in his sleep.
“Muriel﻿—my love﻿—” he paused, and his lips quivered: but after a minute he went on more steadily. “Muriel﻿—my darling﻿—they﻿—*want* me﻿—down in the harbour.”
“*Must* you go?” she pleaded, rising and laying her hands on his shoulders, and looking up into his face with her great eyes brimming over with tears. “Must *you* go, Arthur? It may mean﻿—death!”
He met her gaze without flinching. “It *does* mean death,” he said, in a husky whisper: “but﻿—darling﻿—I am *called*. And even my life itself﻿—” His voice failed him, and he said no more.
For a minute she stood quite silent, looking upwards with a helpless gaze, as if even prayer were now useless, while her features worked and quivered with the great agony she was enduring. Then a sudden inspiration seemed to come upon her and light up her face with a strange sweet smile. “*Your* life?” she repeated. “It is not *yours* to give!”
Arthur had recovered himself by this time, and could reply quite firmly, “That is true,” he said. “It is not *mine* to give. It is *yours*, now, my﻿—wife that is to be! And you﻿—do *you* forbid me to go? Will you not spare me, my own beloved one?”
Still clinging to him, she laid her head softly on his breast. She had never done such a thing in my presence before, and I knew how deeply she must be moved.

===Chinese===

答话却听不真了，我只清楚听见“早上到现在十个，刚又添了两个——”
“可那儿总*有*位医生吧？”我们听见亚瑟说；一个我们从没听过深沉嗓音答道：“死了，先生。三个钟头前断的气。”
穆丽尔小姐一颤，双手掩面：这时前门轻轻合上，我们便什么也听不见了。
有几分钟我们枯坐无言：随后伯爵离室，很快回来说，亚瑟已随那两个渔夫去了，留下话说约一小时后回来。果然不出所料，一小时期满——这一小时里大家几乎没说话，谁都像没了谈兴——前门又在锈涩的合页上吱呀一响，过道里传来一阵脚步声，那脚步又慢又虚，像个盲人摸索着走路，几乎认不出是亚瑟的。
他走进来，立在穆丽尔小姐面前，一只手沉沉地攆着桌子，眼里一种古怪的神情，仿佛在梦游。
“穆丽尔——我的爱人——”他顿住，嘴唇发颤：过了一分钟，才又稳稳说下去。“穆丽尔——我的宝贝——他们——*需要*我——在下面的港城。”
“*一定*要去吗？”她夬求道，起身把双手搭上他的肩，泪汪汪的大眼睛仰望着他的脸。“非*你*去不可吗，亚瑟？那也许——就是死！”
他毫不畏缩地迎住她的目光。“就是死，”他声音沙哑地低语：“可是——宝贝——我是*蒙召*而去。就连我这条命本身——”他的嗓音撐不住了，再没说下去。
有一分钟她静静立着，无助地仰望，仿佛连祷告都已无用，五官在那巨大的苦楚中抽动、颤抖。随后一线天启仿佛降在她身上，她脸上亮起一抹奇异而甜美的微笑。“*你的*命？”她重复道。“它*不归你*给！”
这时亚瑟已定住神，能斩钉截铁地作答了。“这话不假，”他说。“它*不归我*给。如今它归*你*，我的——未婚的妻子！那么你——*你*不许我去吗？你不肯饶过我吗，我最亲爱的人？”
她依然攀着他，把头轻轻靠上他的胸膛。在我面前她从不曾有过这样的举动，我知道她必定动了极深的情。

===Original===

“I *will* spare you,” she said, calmly and quietly, “to God.”
“And to God’s poor,” he whispered.
“And to God’s poor,” she added. “When must it be, sweet love?”
“Tomorrow morning,” he replied. “And I have much to do before then.”
And then he told us how he had spent his hour of absence. He had been to the Vicarage, and had arranged for the wedding to take place at eight the next morning (there was no legal obstacle, as he had, some time before this, obtained a Special License) in the little church we knew so well. “My old friend here,” indicating me, “will act as ‘Best Man,’ I know: your father will be there to give you away: and﻿—and﻿—you will dispense with bride’s-maids, my darling?”
She nodded: no words came.
“And then I can go with a willing heart﻿—to do God’s work﻿—knowing that we are *one*﻿—and that we are together in *spirit*, though not in bodily presence﻿—and are most of all together when we pray! Our *prayers* will go up together﻿—”
“Yes, yes!” sobbed Lady Muriel. “But you must not stay longer now, my darling! Go home and take some rest. You will need all your strength tomorrow﻿—”
“Well, I will go,” said Arthur. “We will be here in good time tomorrow. Good night, my own own darling!”
I followed his example, and we two left the house together. As we walked back to our lodgings, Arthur sighed deeply once or twice, and seemed about to speak﻿—but no words came, till we had entered the house, and had lit our candles, and were at our bedroom-doors. Then Arthur said “Good night, old fellow! God bless you!”
“God bless you!” I echoed, from the very depths of my heart.
We were back again at the Hall by eight in the morning, and found Lady Muriel and the Earl, and the old Vicar, waiting for us. It was a strangely sad and silent party that walked up to the little church and back; and I could not help feeling that it was much more like a funeral than a wedding: to Lady Muriel it *was* in fact, a funeral rather than a wedding, so heavily did the presentiment weigh upon her (as she told us afterwards) that her newly-won husband was going forth to his death.
Then we had breakfast; and, all too soon, the vehicle was at the door, which was to convey Arthur, first to his lodgings, to pick up the things he was taking with him, and then as far towards the death-stricken hamlet as it was considered safe to go.

===Chinese===

“我肯放你走，”她说，平静而安详，“放你给上帝。”
“也给上帝的穷苦人，”他悄声道。
“也给上帝的穷苦人，”她补上。“什么时候走，亲爱的？”
“明天一早，”他答。“之前还有许多事要办。”
接着他讲了这一小时外出做了什么。他去了牧师宅，安排婚礼于明晨八点举行（法律上并无障碍，他早些时候已领了特许结婚证书）就在我们熟得不能再熟的小教堂里。“我这位老朋友，”他指指我，“会做男傧相，我知道：令尊会来牵你入场：还有——还有——女傧相就免了吧，我的宝贝？”
她点头：说不出话。
“然后我就能怀着甘愿的心去——去做上帝的工——知道我们已是*一体*——知道我们灵里相守，纵使身不在一处——而祷告时更是至亲至近地同在！我们的*祷告*会一同升上去——”
“是，是！”穆丽尔小姐哽咽道。“可你现在不能再待了，亲爱的！回家歇歇吧。明天你用得着全部气力——”
“好，我这就走，”亚瑟说。“明天我们准时来。晚安，我最最亲爱的宝贝！”
我学着样子告辞，我们两人一同离宅。回寓所的路上，亚瑟深深叹了一两口气，几次像要开口——却终究无言，直到进了家门、点上蜡烛、各自到了卧室门口，亚瑟才说：“晚安，老伙计！上帝保佑你！”
“上帝保佑你！”我从心底深处应道。
早晨八点我们回到府邸，穆丽尔小姐、伯爵和老牧师已在等候。一行人走向小教堂又走回，沉默得出奇、悲伤得出奇；我不禁觉得，这更像一场葬礼而非婚礼：对穆丽尔小姐来说，它*事实上*就是葬礼多过婚礼——预感沉沉压着她（她后来这样告诉我们）：她新婚的丈夫，正走向他的死亡。
随后我们用了早餐；很快，太快了——车已到门口，它将载亚瑟先回寓所取他要带的物件，再往那瘟疫肆虐的村落走——走到被认为安全的尽头为止。

===Original===

One or two of the fishermen were to meet him on the road, to carry his things the rest of the way.
“And are you quite sure you are taking all that you will need?” Lady Muriel asked.
“All that I shall need as a *doctor*, certainly. And my own personal needs are few: I shall not even take any of my own wardrobe﻿—there is a fisherman’s suit, ready-made, that is waiting for me at my lodgings. I shall only take my watch, and a few books, and﻿—stay﻿—there *is* one book I should like to add, a pocket-Testament﻿—to use at the bedsides of the sick and dying﻿—”
“Take mine!” said Lady Muriel: and she ran upstairs to fetch it. “It has nothing written in it but ‘Muriel,’ ” she said as she returned with it: “shall I inscribe﻿—”
“No, my own one,” said Arthur, taking it from her. “What *could* you inscribe better than that? Could any human name mark it more clearly as my own individual property? Are *you* not mine? Are you not,” (with all the old playfulness of manner) “as Bruno would say, ‘my *very mine*’?”
He bade a long and loving adieu to the Earl and to me, and left the room, accompanied only by his wife, who was bearing up bravely, and was﻿—*outwardly*, at least﻿—less overcome than her old father. We waited in the room a minute or two, till the sound of wheels had told us that Arthur had driven away; and even then we waited still, for the step of Lady Muriel, going upstairs to her room, to die away in the distance. Her step, usually so light and joyous, now sounded slow and weary, like one who plods on under a load of hopeless misery; and I felt almost as hopeless, and almost as wretched, as she. “Are we four destined *ever* to meet again, on this side the grave?” I asked myself, as I walked to my home. And the tolling of a distant bell seemed to answer me, “No! No! No!”

===Chinese===

一两个渔夫会到路上接他，替他把东西扛完余下的路。
“你确定要带的东西都齐了？”穆丽尔小姐问。
“当*医生*要用的，准都齐了。我自己的需用很少：连自家的衣裳也不带——寓所里有一套现成的渔夫装等着我。我只带表，几本书，还有——慢着——倒*有*一本书想添上：一本袖珍《新约》——好在病人和垂死者的床边用——”
“带我的！”穆丽尔小姐说：跑上楼去取。“里面只写了一个名字：‘穆丽尔’，”她拿着书回来说：“要不要我再题——”
“不必，我的宝贝，”亚瑟说着从她手里接过书。“*还有什么*题得比这更好？人世间哪个名字，能比它更清楚地标明这是我的私产？*你*不就是我的吗？你不就是——”（神情又是旧日那副顽皮）“照布鲁诺的说法，‘俺的俺’吗？”
他向伯爵和我道了长长的、深情的一别，离了房间，只有妻子相送——她强撐着，*至少表面上*比老父亲撐得住。我们在屋里又等了一两分钟，直到车轮声告诉我们亚瑟已经驶远；即便那时我们还在等，等穆丽尔小姐上楼回房的脚步声在远处消逝。她的脚步平日那样轻快，此刻听来迟缓而疲惫，像一个顶着无望苦楚的重担、一步步捱着的人；我感到几乎同她一样无望、一样凄惶。“坟墓这一边，我们四个还注定有重逢之日吗？”走回家去的路上，我自问。远处一口钟的鸣响仿佛回答我：“没有！没有！没有！”

## XVIII A Newspaper-Cutting / XVIII 一帧剪报
===Original===

> Our readers will have followed with painful interest, the accounts we have from time to time published of the terrible epidemic which has, during the last two months, carried off most of the inhabitants of the little fishing-harbour adjoining the village of Elveston. The last survivors, numbering twenty-three only, out of a population which, three short months ago, exceeded one hundred and twenty, were removed on Wednesday last, under the authority of the Local Board, and safely lodged in the County Hospital: and the place is now veritably “a city of the dead,” without a single human voice to break its silence.
> The rescuing party consisted of six sturdy fellows﻿—fishermen from the neighbourhood﻿—directed by the resident Physician of the Hospital, who came over for that purpose, heading a train of hospital-ambulances. The six men had been selected﻿—from a much larger number who had volunteered for this peaceful “forlorn hope”﻿—for their strength and robust health, as the expedition was considered to be, even now, when the malady has expended its chief force, not unattended with danger.
> Every precaution that science could suggest, against the risk of infection, was adopted: and the sufferers were tenderly carried on litters, one by one, up the steep hill, and placed in the ambulances which, each provided with a hospital nurse, were waiting on the level road. The fifteen miles, to the Hospital, were done at a walking-pace, as some of the patients were in too prostrate a condition to bear jolting, and the journey occupied the whole afternoon.
> The twenty-three patients consist of nine men, six women, and eight children. It has not been found possible to identify them all, as some of the children﻿—left with no surviving relatives﻿—are infants; and two men and one woman are not yet able to make rational replies, the brainpowers being entirely in abeyance. Among a more well-to-do-race, there would no doubt have been names marked on the clothes; but here no such evidence is forthcoming.
> Besides the poor fishermen and their families, there were but five persons to be accounted for: and it was ascertained, beyond a doubt, that all five are numbered with the dead. It is a melancholy pleasure to place on record the names of these genuine martyrs﻿—than whom none, surely, are more worthy to be entered on the glory-roll of England’s heroes!

===Chinese===

> 本报读者想必一直怀着揪心之忱，关注本报不时刊出的关于那场可怕瘟疫的报道。过去两个月间，这场瘟疫夺去了埃尔夫斯顿（Elveston）村畔小渔港中大多数居民的生命。仅存的幸存者——总共二十三人，而在短短三个月前，此地人口还逾一百二十——已于上星期三由地方委员会下令迁出，妥善安置于郡医院：那地方如今真成了「死人之城」，再无一声人语来打破它的寂静。
> 救援队由六名健壮的汉子组成——都是邻近的渔民——由医院的驻院医生率领，他为此专程赶来，身后跟着一列医院救护车。这六人是从多得多的自愿报名者中挑选出来的——为的是这场和平的「敢死之役」——挑的是体魄强健者，因为即便在瘟疫势焰已衰的今日，此行仍非全无危险。
> 凡科学所能想出的防疫措施，无不一一采用：病者们被温柔地一一抬上担架，抬上陡坡，安放进救护车里——每辆车都配有医院的护士，候在平路上。去医院的十五英里路走的是步行的速度，因有的病人衰弱得禁不起颠簸，整个路程占去了一下午。
> 二十三名病人中，有男子九人、女子六人、儿童八人。尚无法一一辨明他们的身份，因有的孩子——再无亲人存活——尚在襁褓；另有两名男子和一名女子还无法作理智的回答，脑力完全失灵。若是家道稍殷实的人家，衣裳上想必会写有姓名；但此处却寻不出这样的凭据。
> 除可怜的渔民及其家属之外，尚有五人须查个下落：现已确凿查明，五人皆已罹难。将这些真正的殉道者的名字载入记录，是一件令人悲恸的快事——可以断言，再没有谁比他们更配刻入英格兰英雄的光荣名册！

===Original===

They are as follows:﻿—
> The Rev. James Burgess, M.A., and Emma his wife. He was the Curate at the Harbour, not thirty years old, and had been married only two years. A written record was found in their house, of the dates of their deaths.
> Next to theirs we will place the honoured name of Dr. Arthur Forester, who, on the death of the local physician, nobly faced the imminent peril of death, rather than leave these poor folk uncared for in their last extremity. No record of his name, or of the date of his death, was found: but the corpse was easily identified, although dressed in the ordinary fisherman’s suit (which he was known to have adopted when he went down there), by a copy of the New Testament, the gift of his wife, which was found, placed next his heart, with his hands crossed over it. It was not thought prudent to remove the body, for burial elsewhere: and accordingly it was at once committed to the ground, along with four others found in different houses, with all due reverence. His wife, whose maiden name was Lady Muriel Orme, had been married to him on the very morning on which he undertook his self-sacrificing mission.
> Next we record the Rev. Walter Saunders, Wesleyan Minister. His death is believed to have taken place two or three weeks ago, as the words “Died October 5” were found written on the wall of the room which he is known to have occupied﻿—the house being shut up, and apparently not having been entered for some time.
> Last﻿—though not a whit behind the other four in glorious self-denial and devotion to duty﻿—let us record the name of Father Francis, a young Jesuit Priest who had been only a few months in the place. He had not been dead many hours when the exploring party came upon the body, which was identified, beyond the possibility of doubt, by the dress, and by the crucifix which was, like the young Doctor’s Testament, clasped closely to his heart.
> Since reaching the hospital, two of the men and one of the children have died. Hope is entertained for all the others: though there are two or three cases where the vital powers seem to be so entirely exhausted that it is but “hoping against hope” to regard ultimate recovery as even possible.

===Chinese===

他们的名字如下：——
> 詹姆斯·伯吉斯牧师（Rev. James Burgess），文学硕士，及其妻埃玛。他是渔港的副牧师，年纪不满三十，成婚方两年。他们的屋里寻得一份亲笔记录，记着二人身故的日期。
> 其次，我们谨录下阿瑟·福雷斯特（Arthur Forester）医生的光荣名字。本地医生亡故后，他毅然直面迫近的死险，而不肯任这些可怜人在最后的绝境中无人照看。他的名字与身故日期均未寻得记录：但尸体一望便知，虽然他穿着寻常渔夫的装束（众所周知他下去时换上的就是这身），因为人们发现他怀中贴身处有一部《新约》——妻子的赠礼——双手交叠，捧在它的上面。人们认为不宜迁走遗体另葬他处：于是当即连同在不同屋中寻获的另外四具，一并恭敬入土。他的妻子——闺名穆丽尔·奥姆小姐（Lady Muriel Orme）——与他成婚，正是在他领受这桩自我牺牲之使命的那天早晨。
> 再次，我们记录卫斯理宗牧师沃尔特·桑德斯牧师（Rev. Walter Saunders）。据信他故去已两三周，因在他确知住过的那间屋子的墙上，见有「十月五日卒」的字样——那屋子门窗紧闭，显见多时无人入内。
> 最后——在光荣的自我牺牲与恪尽职守上丝毫不逊于前四位——让我们记下弗朗西斯神父（Father Francis）的名字，一位来此才数月的年轻耶稣会神父。探险队寻获他的遗体时，他去世不过数小时。尸身的辨认确凿无疑：凭他的法衣，也凭那枚十字架——同年轻医生的《新约》一样——紧紧捧在他的心口。
> 抵达医院后，又有一名男子和一名孩童故去。其余诸人皆存生望：只是有两三例，生命之力似已耗竭殆尽，若还把最终痊愈当作哪怕可能之事，那不过是「明知无望而望」罢了。

## XIX A Fairy-Duet / 十九 仙子二重唱
===Original===

The year﻿—what an eventful year it had been for me!﻿—was drawing to a close, and the brief wintry day hardly gave light enough to recognise the old familiar objects, bound up with so many happy memories, as the train glided round the last bend into the station, and the hoarse cry of “Elveston! Elveston!” resounded along the platform.
It was sad to return to the place, and to feel that I should never again see the glad smile of welcome, that had awaited me here so few months ago. “And yet, if I were to find him here,” I muttered, as in solitary state I followed the porter, who was wheeling my luggage on a barrow, “and if he *were* to ‘*strike a sudden hand in mine, And ask a thousand things of home*,’ I should not﻿—no, ‘*I should not feel it to be strange*’!”
Having given directions to have my luggage taken to my old lodgings, I strolled off alone, to pay a visit, before settling down in my own quarters, to my dear old friends﻿—for such I indeed felt them to be, though it was barely half a year since first we met﻿—the Earl and his widowed daughter.
The shortest way, as I well remembered, was to cross through the churchyard. I pushed open the little wicket-gate and slowly took my way among the solemn memorials of the quiet dead, thinking of the many who had, during the past year, disappeared from the place, and had gone to “join the majority.” A very few steps brought me in sight of the object of my search. Lady Muriel, dressed in the deepest mourning, her face hidden by a long crape veil, was kneeling before a little marble cross, round which she was fastening a wreath of flowers.
The cross stood on a piece of level turf, unbroken by any mound, and I knew that it was simply a memorial-cross, for one whose dust reposed elsewhere, even before reading the simple inscription:﻿—

> *In loving Memory of*
> **Arthur Forester**, M.D.
> *whose mortal remains lie buried by the sea:
> whose spirit has returned to God who gave it*.
> “Greater love hath no man than this, that
> a man lay down his life for his friends.”

===Chinese===

这一年——于我它是何等多事的一年！——已近尾声。短促的冬日几乎给不出足够的光，让人辨认那些旧日熟稔、系着多少快活回忆的景物；列车已滑过最后一道弯驶进车站，“埃尔夫斯顿！埃尔夫斯顿！”沙哑的呼喊响彻月台。
重回此地是伤心的——想到再也见不到那迎候我的欢喜笑容，不过几个月前，它还在这里等过我。“然而，倘若我在此地遇见他，”我喃喃自语，孤零零地跟着推行李车的搬运工，“倘若他真会‘*突然一握住我的手，问起家乡的千般事*’，我也不会——不，‘*我不会觉得异样*’！”
吩咐把行李送往旧寓所后，我便独自漫步出门，在安顿之前先去拜访两位亲爱的老友——虽然相识不过半年，我心里确实把他们当老友——伯爵和他新寡的女儿。
最短的路，我记得清楚，是横穿教堂墓园。我推开小小的栅栏门，在静穆的亡者碑丛间缓缓前行，想着这一年里从此地消逝、去“归入多数人行列”的那么多故人。没走几步，我便望见了要寻的人。穆丽尔小姐一身重孝，长长的黑纱罩着面庞，正跪在一座小小的大理石十字架前，往架上系一只花环。
十字架立在一片平整草地上，没有隆起的坟专：不必读那朴素的铭文我已知道，这只是一座纪念十字架，为一位遗骨另葬他方的人而立：——

> *谨以此深情纪念*
> **阿瑟·福雷斯特**医学博士
> *其尘躯长眠于海滨：
> 其灵魂归于赐它的上帝*。
> “人为朋友舍命，人的爱心没有比这个大的。”

===Original===

She threw back her veil on seeing me approach, and came forwards to meet me, with a quiet smile, and far more self-possessed than I could have expected.
“It is quite like old times, seeing *you* here again!” she said, in tones of genuine pleasure. “Have you been to see my father?”
“No,” I said: “I was on my way there, and came through here as the shortest way. I hope he is well, and you also?”
“Thanks, we are both quite well. And you? Are you any better yet?”
“Not much better, I fear: but no worse, I am thankful to say.”
“Let us sit here awhile, and have a quiet chat,” she said. The calmness﻿—almost indifference﻿—of her manner quite took me by surprise. I little guessed what a fierce restraint she was putting upon herself.
“One can be so quiet here,” she resumed. “I come here every﻿—every day.”
“It is very peaceful,” I said.
“You got my letter?”
“Yes, but I delayed writing. It is so hard to say﻿—on *paper*﻿—”
“I know. It was kind of you. You were with us when we saw the last of﻿—” She paused a moment, and went on more hurriedly. “I went down to the harbour several times, but no one knows which of those vast graves it is. However, they showed me the house he died in: that was some comfort. I stood in the very room where﻿—where﻿—.” She struggled in vain to go on. The floodgates had given way at last, and the outburst of grief was the most terrible I had ever witnessed. Totally regardless of my presence, she flung herself down on the turf, burying her face in the grass, and with her hands clasped round the little marble cross, “Oh, my darling, my darling!” she sobbed. “And God meant your life to be so beautiful!”
I was startled to hear, thus repeated by Lady Muriel, the very words of the darling child whom I had seen weeping so bitterly over the dead hare. Had some mysterious influence passed, from that sweet fairy-spirit, ere she went back to Fairyland, into the human spirit that loved her so dearly? The idea seemed too wild for belief. And yet, are there not “*more things in heaven and earth than are dreamt of in our philosophy*?”
“God *meant* it to be beautiful,” I whispered, “and surely it *was* beautiful? God’s purpose never fails!” I dared say no more, but rose and left her. At the entrance-gate to the Earl’s house I waited, leaning on the gate and watching the sun set, revolving many memories﻿—some happy, some sorrowful﻿—until Lady Muriel joined me.

===Chinese===

她见我走近，撩开面纱，迎上前来，静静微笑着，镇定得出乎我的意料。
“又在这儿见到*你*，真像回到了从前！”她说，语调里是真实的欢喜。“去看过我父亲了吗？”
“还没有，”我说：“正要去呢，抄这儿近。希望他安好，你也安好？”
“多谢，我们父女都好。你呢？身子好些了吗？”
“怕是好不了多少：不过也没坏下去，这我感恩。”
“咱们在这儿坐一会儿，静静聊聊，”她说。她那安详——近乎淡漠——的仪态令我暗暗吃惊。我哪里猜得到，她正给自己上着多么酷烈的缰绳。
“在这里人能这样静，”她续道。“我每天都——每天都来。”
“这里很安宁，”我说。
“收到我的信了？”
“收到了，只是我迟迟没回。有些话，在*纸*上——实在难说。”
“我知道。你真厚道。我们与他最后诀别时，你在场——”她顿了顿，又急急说下去。“我下港城去了好几趟，可谁也说不清那片巨大的墓穴里哪一处是他。不过，他们领我看了他去世的那所房子：那也算一点安慰。我就站在那个房间里，那个——那个——”她挣扎着，却再说不出。泪闸终于溃决，那悲恸的迸发是我此生仅见的最骇人的一幕。她全然顾不得我在场，扑倒在草地上，脸埋进草里，双手紧抱着那小小的大理石十字架：“哦，我的爱人，我的爱人！”她呜咽道。“上帝原是要你的生命那样美的呀！”
听到穆丽尔小姐口中道出的，竟是我听见那可爱的小姑娘伏在死野兔上痛哭时说过的原话，我不禁悚然。莫非在她返回仙境之前，有什么神秘的影响，从那甜美仙灵身上，传进了这般深爱她的人类魂灵？这念头狂诞得难以置信。然而，“*天地间的事，岂不比我们哲学所梦想的更多*？”
“上帝*原是要*它美的，”我低声道，“而它*确实*美了，不是吗？上帝的旨意从不落空！”我不敢再多说，起身离了她。我在伯爵宅邸的门前候着，倚门望着落日，许多往事在心里翻涌——有欢喜的，也有悲怆的——直到穆丽尔小姐出来与我相会。

===Original===

She was quite calm again now. “Do come in,” she said. “My father will be so pleased to see you!”
The old man rose from his chair, with a smile, to welcome me; but his self-command was far less than his daughter’s, and the tears coursed down his face as he grasped both my hands in his, and pressed them warmly.
My heart was too full to speak; and we all sat silent for a minute or two. Then Lady Muriel rang the bell for tea. “You *do* take five o’clock tea, I know!” she said to me, with the sweet playfulness of manner I remembered so well, “even though you *can’t* work your wicked will on the Law of Gravity, and make the teacups descend into Infinite Space, a little faster than the tea!”
This remark gave the tone to our conversation. By a tacit mutual consent, we avoided, during this our first meeting after her great sorrow, the painful topics that filled our thoughts, and talked like lighthearted children who had never known a care.
“Did you ever ask yourself the question,” Lady Muriel began, apropos of nothing, “what is the *chief* advantage of being a Man instead of a Dog?”
“No, indeed,” I said: “but I think there are advantages on the *Dog’s* side of the question, as well.”
“No doubt,” she replied, with that pretty mock-gravity that became her so well: “but, on *Man’s* side, the chief advantage seems to me to consist in *having pockets*! It was borne in upon me﻿—upon *us*, I should say; for my father and I were returning from a walk﻿—only yesterday. We met a dog carrying home a bone. What it wanted it for, I’ve no idea: certainly there was no *meat* on it﻿—”
A strange sensation came over me, that I had heard all this, or something exactly like it, before: and I almost expected her next words to be “perhaps he meant to make a cloak for the winter?” However what she really said was “and my father tried to account for it by some wretched joke about *pro bono publico*. Well, the dog laid down the bone﻿—*not* in disgust with the pun, which would have shown it to be a dog of taste﻿—but simply to rest its jaws, poor thing! I *did* pity it so! Won’t you join my *Charitable Association for supplying dogs with pockets*? How would *you* like to have to carry your walking-stick in your mouth?”

===Chinese===

这时她已完全平静。“快请进，”她说。“父亲见到你不知多高兴呢！”
老人含笑从椅中起身迎我；可他的自持远不及女儿，他双手攋住我的双手热切紧握时，泪水已顺着面颊淌下。
我心头发满，说不出话；大家静坐了一两分钟。随后穆丽尔小姐摇铃备茶。“五点钟的茶你*一定*是要用的，我知道！”她对我说，那份甜美的顽皮神态我记得多么清楚，“哪怕你*没能*对万有引力律为所欲为，没法让茶杯坠入无限太空时，坠得比茶快那么一点儿！”
这句话给谈话定了调。凭着心照不宣的默契，在她大悲之后的这第一面里，我们避开了充塞心头的沉痛话题，谈得像两个从不知忧愁为何物的轻快孩子。
“你可曾问过自己，”穆丽尔小姐没头没脑地开了腔，“做人而不做狗，*头一样*的好处是什么？”
“还真没有，”我说：“不过我想这问题上，*狗*那头也自有好处。”
“这毫无疑问，”她答道，带着那副与她极相称的俏皮庄严：“不过，*人*这一头，依我看，头一样好处全在*有口袋*！这一点是我不由得悟到的——该说*我们*悟到的；因为我和父亲昨天散步回来——遇见一条狗往家叼骨头。叼去做什么用，我毫无头绪：那上头肯定一点*肉*也没有——”
一阵奇异之感攋住了我：这些话，或与此一模一样的话，我从前听过；我几乎要等她下一句是“也许它想给冬天做件斗篷？”然而她真正说的是：“我父亲想用一个关于*为了公众*的蹩脚笑话来解释。后来那狗放下了骨头——*不是*嫌那个双关语恶心，若嫌恶心倒显得它是条有品位的狗——只是想让腮帮子歇歇，可怜的家伙！我*真是*太心疼它了！你愿不愿意加入我的‘*为狗供应口袋慈善会*’？要是*你*得用嘴叼着手杖走路，你乐意吗？”

===Original===

Ignoring the difficult question as to the *raison d’être* of a walking-stick, supposing one had no *hands*, I mentioned a curious instance, I had once witnessed, of reasoning by a dog. A gentleman, with a lady, and child, and a large dog, were down at the end of a pier on which I was walking. To amuse his child, I suppose, the gentleman put down on the ground his umbrella and the lady’s parasol, and then led the way to the other end of the pier, from which he sent the dog back for the deserted articles. I was watching with some curiosity. The dog came racing back to where I stood, but found an unexpected difficulty in picking up the things it had come for. With the umbrella in its mouth, its jaws were so far apart that it could get no firm grip on the parasol. After two or three failures, it paused and considered the matter.
Then it put down the umbrella and began with the parasol. Of course that didn’t open its jaws nearly so wide, and it was able to get a good hold of the umbrella, and galloped off in triumph. One couldn’t doubt that it had gone through a real train of logical thought.
“I entirely agree with you,” said Lady Muriel: “but don’t orthodox writers condemn that view, as putting Man on the level of the lower animals? Don’t they draw a sharp boundary-line between Reason and Instinct?”
“That certainly *was* the orthodox view, a generation ago,” said the Earl. “The truth of Religion seemed ready to stand or fall with the assertion that Man was the only reasoning animal. But that is at an end now. Man can still claim *certain* monopolies﻿—for instance, such a use of *language* as enables us to utilise the work of many, by ‘division of labour.’ But the belief, that we have a monopoly of *Reason*, has long been swept away. Yet no catastrophe has followed. As some old poet says, ‘*God is where he was*.’ ”
“Most religious believers would *now* agree with Bishop Butler,” said I, “and not reject a line of argument, even if it led straight to the conclusion that animals have some kind of *soul*, which survives their bodily death.”
“I *would* like to know *that* to be true!” Lady Muriel exclaimed. “If only for the sake of the poor horses. Sometimes I’ve thought that, if anything *could* make me cease to believe in a God of perfect justice, it would be the sufferings of horses﻿—without guilt to deserve it, and without any compensation!”

===Chinese===

我避开“倘若人没有*手*，手杖何以存在”这一难题，讲了一桩我亲眼所见的狗儿推理的奇事。一位绅士，带着女士、孩子和一条大狗，正在我漫步的栈桥那一头。大约是为了逗孩子，绅士把他的雨伞和女士的阳伞放在地上，然后领路走向栈桥另一头，从那里差狗回去取那两件被撞下的物件。我颇好奇地观看着。狗一路奔回我站的地方，却在叼取来取的东西时遇到意想不到的难处。嘴里横着雨伞，两颚张得太开，叼不牢那柄阳伞。失败了两三回，它停下来，思考这件事。
然后它放下雨伞，先叼阳伞。这一来两颚自然不必张那么开，它稳稳咬住了雨伞，得胜疾驰而去。谁也不能否认，它是真真切切过了一遍逻辑推理的链条。
“我完全同意你，”穆丽尔小姐说：“可正统的作者们不是谴责这种观点，说它把人拉到与低等动物齐平吗？他们不是在理性与本能之间划了一条森严的界线？”
“一代人以前，这*确实*是正统观点，”伯爵说。“宗教的真理，仿佛就要随着‘人是唯一有理性的动物’这一断言同生共死。如今这都完了。人仍可声称*某些*专利——比如对*语言*那样的运用，使我们能借‘分工’利用众人之力。但‘理性为我所独占’的信念，早已被扫荡一空。然而天并没塌下来。正如一位老诗人所说：‘*上帝仍在祂的旧处*。’”
“如今多数信教的人都会*同意*巴特勒主教的意见了，”我说，“不会拒绝一条论证，哪怕它径直导出结论：动物也有某种*灵魂*，肉体死后依然存续。”
“*那*若是真的，我*多想*知道！”穆丽尔小姐感叹。“哪怕只为那些可怜的马。有时我想，倘若有什么*能*让我不再信一位全然公义的上帝，那便是马所受的苦——无罪而受，又无半点补偿！”

===Original===

“It is only part of the great Riddle,” said the Earl, “why innocent beings *ever* suffer. It *is* a great strain on Faith﻿—but not a *breaking* strain, I think.”
“The sufferings of *horses*,” I said, “are chiefly caused by *Man’s* cruelty. So *that* is merely one of the many instances of Sin causing suffering to others than the Sinner himself. But don’t you find a *greater* difficulty in sufferings inflicted by animals upon each other? For instance, a cat playing with a mouse. Assuming it to have no *moral* responsibility, isn’t that a greater mystery than a man over-driving a horse?”
“I think it *is*,” said Lady Muriel, looking a mute appeal to her father.
“What right have we to make that assumption?” said the Earl. “*Many* of our religious difficulties are merely deductions from unwarranted assumptions. The wisest answer to most of them, is, I think, ‘*behold, we know not anything*.’ ”
“You mentioned ‘division of labour,’ just now,” I said. “Surely it is carried to a wonderful perfection in a hive of bees?”
“So wonderful﻿—so entirely superhuman﻿—” said the Earl, “and so entirely inconsistent with the intelligence they show in other ways﻿—that I feel no doubt at all that it is *pure* Instinct, and *not*, as some hold, a very high order of Reason. Look at the utter stupidity of a bee, trying to find its way out of an open window! It *doesn’t* try, in any reasonable sense of the word: it simply bangs itself about! We should call a puppy *imbecile*, that behaved so. And yet we are asked to believe that its intellectual level is above Sir Isaac Newton!”
“Then you hold that *pure* Instinct contains no *Reason* at all?”
“On the contrary,” said the Earl, “I hold that the work of a beehive involves Reason of the *highest* order. But none of it is done by the *Bee*. *God* has reasoned it all out, and has put into the mind of the Bee the *conclusions*, only, of the reasoning process.”
“But how do their minds come to work *together*?” I asked.
“What right have we to assume that they *have* minds?”
“Special pleading, special pleading!” Lady Muriel cried, in a most unfilial tone of triumph. “Why, you yourself said, just now, ‘the mind of the Bee’!”
“But I did *not* say ‘*minds*,’ my child,” the Earl gently replied. “It has occurred to me, as the most probable solution of the ‘Bee’-mystery, that a swarm of Bees *have only one mind among them*.

===Chinese===

“那不过是天字第一号谜题的一部分，”伯爵说，“无辜者*为何*受苦。这确实给信心极大的拉力——但我看，还不是*拉断*的力。”
“*马*的苦，”我说，“主要是*人*的残忍造成的。所以*那*不过是‘罪殃及罪人之外的旁人’的又一例。可动物加于彼此的苦难，你不觉得是*更大*的难题吗？比如猫戏老鼠。姑且假定它不负*道德*责任，这岂不比人狠赶马匹更神秘？”
“我也觉得*是*，”穆丽尔小姐说着，无声地求援于父亲。
“我们有什么权利作那个假定？”伯爵说。“我们的宗教难题，*许多*不过是从无凭的假定里推出来的。对其中大半，最明智的回答，我想是：‘*看哪，我们一无所知*。’”
“方才你提到‘分工’，”我说。“在一窝蜜蜂里，它不是臻于神妙的完美吗？”
“神妙之至——全然超乎人类——”伯爵说，“又同它们在其他方面显露的智力全不相称——所以我毫不怀疑那是*纯粹*本能，而*不是*某些人主张的极高理性。瞧瞧蜜蜂想飞出一扇敞开的窗时那份十足的愚蠢！以‘尝试’一词的任何像样含义讲，它*根本*不尝试：它只管乱撞！小狗若这般行事，我们得叫它*痴儿*。可人家偏要我们相信，它的智力在艾萨克·牛顿爵士之上！”
“那么您认为*纯粹*本能里*毫无*理性可言？”
“恰恰相反，”伯爵说，“我认为一座蜂巢的工程牵涉*最高*等的理性。只是没有一项出自*蜜蜂*自己。*上帝*把一切推演完毕，放进蜜蜂心里的，只是推理过程的*结论*。”
“可它们的心智怎么会同*心协力*呢？”我问。
“我们有什么权利假定它们*各有*心智？”
“诡辩，诡辩！”穆丽尔小姐喊道，那得胜的口吻最无孝道。“咦，您自己方才还说‘蜜蜂的心智’呢！”
“可我没说过‘*心智们*’，孩子，”伯爵温言答道。“我想到过一个也许是‘蜂’之谜最可能的解：一窝蜜蜂*总共只有一颗心智*。

===Original===

We often see one mind animating a most complex collection of limbs and organs, *when joined together*. How do we know that any material connection is necessary? May not mere neighbourhood be enough? If so, a swarm of bees is simply a single animal whose many limbs are not quite close together!”
“It is a bewildering thought,” I said, “and needs a night’s rest to grasp it properly. Reason and Instinct *both* tell me I ought to go home. So, good night!”
“I’ll ‘set’ you part of the way,” said Lady Muriel. “I’ve had no walk today. It will do me good, and I have more to say to you. Shall we go through the wood? It will be pleasanter than over the common, even though it *is* getting a little dark.”
We turned aside into the shade of interlacing boughs, which formed an architecture of almost perfect symmetry, grouped into lovely groined arches, or running out, far as the eye could follow, into endless aisles, and chancels, and naves, like some ghostly cathedral, fashioned out of the dream of a moonstruck poet.
“Always, in this wood,” she began after a pause (silence seemed natural in this dim solitude), “I begin thinking of Fairies! May I ask you a question?” she added hesitatingly. “Do you believe in Fairies?”
The momentary impulse was so strong to tell her of my experiences in this very wood, that I had to make a real effort to keep back the words that rushed to my lips. “If you mean, by ‘believe,’ ‘believe in their *possible* existence,’ I say ‘Yes.’ For their *actual* existence, of course, one would need *evidence*.”
“You were saying, the other day,” she went on, “that you would accept *anything*, on good evidence, that was not *à priori* impossible. And I think you named *Ghosts* as an instance of a *provable* phenomenon. Would *Fairies* be another instance?”
“Yes, I think so.” And again it was hard to check the wish to say more: but I was not yet sure of a sympathetic listener.
“And have you any theory as to what sort of place they would occupy in Creation? Do tell me what you think about them! Would they, for instance (supposing such beings to exist), would they have any moral responsibility? I mean” (and the light bantering tone suddenly changed to one of deep seriousness) “would they be capable of *sin*?”

===Chinese===

一个心智驱动一大套复杂的肢体器官，*当它们连在一起时*——这我们见得多了。我们凭什么知道非得有物质的联结不可？也许，仅仅比邻而居就足够了！若当真如此，一窝蜜蜂不过是一头动物，只是它的许多条胳膊腿隔得不那么近罢了！”
“这想法真叫人晕眩，”我说，“得睡上一夜才消化得了。理性和本能*双双*告诉我该回家了。那么，晚安！”
“我送你一程，”穆丽尔小姐说。“今天我还没散过步。走走对我有好处，我还有话对你说。咱们穿林子去好不好？比走公地舒坦——虽说天*是*有点黑了。”
我们折进交柯错叶的浓荫里。枝干织成近乎完美对称的建筑，聚成一道道可爱的肋拱，又向目力尽头延伸开去，化作无尽的长廊、圣坛、中殿，像一座幽灵般的大教堂，出自中了月光之魔的诗人的梦境。
“一走进这片林子，”静了片刻她开口（在这昏暝的幽境里，沉默显得再自然不过），“我就开始想仙女！能问你一个问题吗？”她迟疑地补了一句。“你信有仙女吗？”
把我在这片林子里的经历讲给她听的冲动一时如此强烈，我费了好大的劲，才拦住涌到唇边的话。“如果你说的‘信’，是信它们*可能*存在，我说‘信’。至于它们*实际*存在，那当然需要*证据*。”
“那天你还说过，”她续道，“只要证据确凿，凡非*先验*不可能之事，你*一概*接受。我记得你还举了*鬼魂*作*可证明*现象的例子。*仙女*算不算又一个？”
“算，我想算。”想说下去的欲望又一次难以邏制：可我还没把握，听的人是否同调。
“那在造物之中，你会把他们安在什么位置，可有理论？快告诉我你是怎么想的！比如（假设这类生灵存在），他们会负道德责任吗？我是说”（轻俏的调侃口吻陡然转为深深的郑重）“他们会*犯罪*吗？”

===Original===

“They can reason﻿—on a lower level, perhaps, than men and women﻿—never rising, I think, above the faculties of a child; and they have a moral sense, most surely. Such a being, without *free will*, would be an absurdity. So I am driven to the conclusion that they *are* capable of sin.”
“You believe in them?” she cried delightedly, with a sudden motion as if about to clap her hands. “Now tell me, have you any reason for it?”
And still I strove to keep back the revelation I felt sure was coming. “I believe that there is *life* everywhere﻿—not *material* only, not merely what is palpable to our senses﻿—but immaterial and invisible as well. We believe in our own immaterial essence﻿—call it ‘soul,’ or ‘spirit,’ or what you will. Why should not other similar essences exist around us, *not* linked on to a visible and *material* body? Did not God make this swarm of happy insects, to dance in this sunbeam for one hour of bliss, for no other object, that we can imagine, than to swell the sum of conscious happiness? And where shall we dare to draw the line, and say ‘He has made all these and no more’?”
“Yes, yes!” she assented, watching me with sparkling eyes. “But these are only reasons for not *denying*. You have more reasons than this, have you not?”
“Well, yes,” I said, feeling I might safely tell all now. “And I could not find a fitter time or place to say it. I have *seen* them﻿—and in this very wood!”
Lady Muriel asked no more questions. Silently she paced at my side, with head bowed down and hands clasped tightly together. Only, as my tale went on, she drew a little short quick breath now and then, like a child panting with delight. And I told her what I had never yet breathed to any other listener, of my double life, and, more than that (for *mine* might have been but a noonday-dream), of the double life of those two dear children.
And when I told her of Bruno’s wild gambols, she laughed merrily; and when I spoke of Sylvie’s sweetness and her utter unselfishness and trustful love, she drew a deep breath, like one who hears at last some precious tidings for which the heart has ached for a long while; and the happy tears chased one another down her cheeks.
“I have often longed to meet an angel,” she whispered, so low that I could hardly catch the words. “I’m *so* glad I’ve seen Sylvie! My heart went out to the child the first moment that I saw her﻿—Listen!”

===Chinese===

“他们能推理——许是比男女世人低一层——我想，从不高过孩童的官能；而他们有道德感，这是千真万确的。这样的生灵，若没有*自由意志*，便是荒谬。于是我不得不归于这个结论：他们*能*犯罪。”
“你信他们？”她欢喜地喊，身子一动，像要拍起手来。“那快告诉我，你可有什么缘由？”
而我仍在克制，不愿道破我感到即将来临的启示。“我相信*生命*无处不在——不止*物质*的，不止我们感官摸得着的——还有非物质、不可见的。我们信自家那非物质的本质——你叫它‘灵魂’也好，‘心灵’也好，随你。那为何不能有别的同类本质环在我们四周，*不*拴在可见的、*物质*的躯体上？上帝造出这一群快活的虫豸，让它们在这道阳光里跳一小时极乐之舞，就我们所能想见，除了增添有知觉的幸福之总量，别无目的——那这条线，我们敢画在哪里，说‘祂造了这些，不再多了’？”
“对，对！”她应道，目光灼灼地望着我。“可这些不过是叫人*无从否认*的理由。你另有缘由，对不对？”
“嗯，有，”我说，觉得此刻和盘托出已无妨。“而且再也找不到更合适的时地来说了。我*见过*他们——就在这片林子里！”
穆丽尔小姐不再发问。她默默走在我身旁，低着头，双手紧紧交握。只是随着我的讲述，她时不时短促地急吸一口气，像孩子欢喜得直喘。我把从未对任何旁人吐露的事讲给她听——讲我的双重生命；更进一步（因为*我的*那份或许只是一场白日梦），讲那两个可爱的孩子的双重生命。
讲到布鲁诺的疯玩疯闹，她笑出了声；讲到西尔维的甜美、她全然的无私与信靠的爱，她深深吸一口气，像终于听见了心念已久的好消息，幸福的泪珠一颗追着一颗滚下脸颊。
“我常常渴望遇见一位天使，”她低语，低得我几乎听不清。“见过西尔维，我*真*高兴！第一眼见到那孩子，我的心就奔向她了——听！”

===Original===

she broke off suddenly. “That’s Sylvie singing! I’m sure of it! Don’t you know her voice?”
“I have heard *Bruno* sing, more than once,” I said: “but I never heard Sylvie.”
“I have only heard her *once*,” said Lady Muriel. “It was that day when you brought us those mysterious flowers. The children had run out into the garden; and I saw Eric coming in that way, and went to the window to meet him: and Sylvie was singing, under the trees, a song I had never heard before. The words were something like ‘I think it is Love, I feel it is Love.’ Her voice sounded far away, like a dream, but it was beautiful beyond all words﻿—as sweet as an infant’s first smile, or the first gleam of the white cliffs when one is coming *home* after weary years﻿—a voice that seemed to fill one’s whole being with peace and heavenly thoughts﻿—Listen!” she cried, breaking off again in her excitement. “That *is* her voice, and that’s the very song!”
I could distinguish no words, but there was a dreamy sense of music in the air that seemed to grow ever louder and louder, as if coming nearer to us. We stood quite silent, and in another minute the two children appeared, coming straight towards us through an arched opening among the trees. Each had an arm round the other, and the setting sun shed a golden halo round their heads, like what one sees in pictures of saints. They were looking in our direction, but evidently did not see us, and I soon made out that Lady Muriel had for once passed into a condition familiar to *me*, that we were both of us “eerie,” and that, though we could see the children so plainly, we were quite invisible to *them*.
The song ceased just as they came into sight: but, to my delight, Bruno instantly said “Let’s sing it all again, Sylvie! It *did* sound so pretty!” And Sylvie replied “Very well. It’s *you* to begin, you know.”
So Bruno began, in the sweet childish treble I knew so well:﻿—

> “Say, what is the spell, when her fledgelings are cheeping,
> That lures the bird home to her nest?
> Or wakes the tired mother, whose infant is weeping,
> To cuddle and croon it to rest?
> What’s the magic that charms the glad babe in her arms,
> Till it cooes with the voice of the dove?”

===Chinese===

她突然刹住。“是西尔维在唱歌！我敢肯定！你听不出她的嗓音？”
“*布鲁诺*的歌我听过不止一回，”我说：“西尔维的，还从没听过。”
“我也只听过*一回*，”穆丽尔小姐说。“就是你给我们送神秘鲜花的那天。孩子们跑进花园去了；我望见埃里克从那边来，便到窗口迎他：西尔维正在树下唱着一支我从未听过的歌。词儿好像是‘我想那是爱，我觉得那是爱’。她的嗓音听着很远，像一场梦，可那份美，言语根本形容不出——甜得像婴儿的第一个笑，像倦游多年归*家*的人望见白垩海崖的第一闪——一副嗓音，仿佛把人的整个生命都注满安宁与天上的念头——听！”她兴奋得又刹住。“*正*是她的嗓音，正是那支歌！”
词句我分辨不出，可空气里有种梦一样的乐声，仿佛越响越亮，正向我们逼近。我们静静立着，一分钟后，两个孩子的身影出现了，正穿过树间一道拱门径直朝我们走来。两人互相搜着，落日给他们头上洒下一圈金色的光轮，恰似圣像画里所见。他们望着我们这个方向，却显然看不见我们；我很快明白，穆丽尔小姐这一回也进入了*我*所熟稔的那种状态——我们两个都“灵异”了——尽管我们把孩子看得清清楚楚，他们却全然看不见*我们*。
他们刚一露面，歌声便停了：可喜的是，布鲁诺立刻说：“咱们从头再唱一遍吧，西尔维！刚才*真是*好听极了！”西尔维答：“好呀。该*你*起头，你知道的。”
于是布鲁诺用我熟得不能再熟的甜甜童声起了头：——

> “请问，是什么咒语，当雏儿啾啾啼叫，
> 引母鸟归了巢？
> 又是什么唤醒倦乏的母亲，当婴孩哭闹，
> 把他搜紧、哼着他睡倒？
> 是什么魔力迷住她怀中欢喜的宝宝，
> 直到他咕咕学鸽叫？”

===Original===

And now ensued quite the strangest of all the strange experiences that marked the wonderful year whose history I am writing﻿—the experience of *first* hearing Sylvie’s voice in song. Her part was a very short one﻿—only a few words﻿—and she sang it timidly, and very low indeed, scarcely audibly, but the *sweetness* of her voice was simply indescribable; I have never heard any earthly music like it.

> “ ’Tis a secret, and so let us whisper it low﻿—
> And the name of the secret is Love!”

On me the first effect of her voice was a sudden sharp pang that seemed to pierce through one’s very heart. (I had felt such a pang only once before in my life, and it had been from *seeing* what, at the moment, realised one’s idea of perfect beauty﻿—it was in a London exhibition, where, in making my way through a crowd, I suddenly met, face to face, a child of quite unearthly beauty.) Then came a rush of burning tears to the eyes, as though one could weep one’s soul away for pure delight. And lastly there fell on me a sense of awe that was almost terror﻿—some such feeling as Moses must have had when he heard the words “*Put off thy shoes from off thy feet, for the place whereon thou standest is holy ground*.” The figures of the children became vague and shadowy, like glimmering meteors: while their voices rang together in exquisite harmony as they sang:﻿—

> “For I think it is Love,
> For I feel it is Love,
> For I’m sure it is nothing but Love!”

By this time I could see them clearly once more. Bruno again sang by himself:﻿—

> “Say, whence is the voice that, when anger is burning,
> Bids the whirl of the tempest to cease?
> That stirs the vexed soul with an aching﻿—a yearning
> For the brotherly handgrip of peace?
> Whence the music that fills all our being﻿—that thrills
> Around us, beneath, and above?”

Sylvie sang more courageously, this time: the words seemed to carry her away, out of herself:﻿—

> “ ’Tis a secret: none knows how it comes, how it goes:
> But the name of the secret is Love!”

And clear and strong the chorus rang out:﻿—

> “For I think it is Love,
> For I feel it is Love,
> For I’m sure it is nothing but Love!”

Once more we heard Bruno’s delicate little voice alone:﻿—

===Chinese===

接下来的一幕，在我笔下这部奇年的信史里，是所有奇遇中最最奇妙的一桩——*第一次*听见西尔维的歌声。她的部分极短——只有寥寥数词——她唱得怯怯的，声音低得几乎听不见，可她嗓音的那份*甜*，简直无从形容；人间的音乐，我从没听过可与之相比的。

> “那是个秘密，所以让我们低声耳语——
> 这秘密的名字，是爱！”

她的嗓音给我的头一击，是一阵骤然而锋利的痛，仿佛直直刺透心房。（这样的痛，我平生只经历过一回，而且来自*看见*了在那一刻把人心目中完美之美实现出来的东西——那是在伦敦一场展览会上，我从人群中挤过，突然与一个美得全然出尘的孩子打了个照面。）随后是灼热的眼泪涌上眼眶，仿佛人能为了纯粹的欢喜把灵魂哭散。最后，一种近乎惊怖的敬畏落在我身上——摩西听见“*把你脚上的鞋脱下来，因为你所站之地是圣地*”时，想必就是这样的心情。孩子们的身影变得朦胧虚淡，像闪烁的流星：而他们的嗓音正以精致的和谐齐声唱着：——

> “因为我想那是爱，
> 因为我觉得那是爱，
> 因为我确信那除了爱别无其他！”

这时我又能看清他们了。布鲁诺又独唱起来：——

> “请问，那声音来自何处，当怒火正炽，
> 它喝令风暴的旋涡止息？
> 它以一阵酸楚、一阵渴慕
> 搅动烦乱的心，渴盼兄弟般相握的和平之手？
> 那乐声来自何处，充满我们的生命——震颤于
> 我们的四周、脚下、头顶？”

这一次西尔维唱得胆大了些：歌词仿佛带她出了神，出了自己：——

> “那是个秘密：无人知它如何来、如何去：
> 可这秘密的名字，是爱！”

合唱清亮而昂扬地响起：——

> “因为我想那是爱，
> 因为我觉得那是爱，
> 因为我确信那除了爱别无其他！”

我们又一次只听见布鲁诺细细的小嗓音：——

===Original===

> “Say whose is the skill that paints valley and hill,
> Like a picture so fair to the sight?
> That flecks the green meadow with sunshine and shadow,
> Till the little lambs leap with delight?”

And again uprose that silvery voice, whose angelic sweetness I could hardly bear:﻿—

> “ ’Tis a secret untold to hearts cruel and cold,
> Though ’tis sung, by the angels above,
> In notes that ring clear for the ears that can hear﻿—
> And the name of the secret is Love!”

And then Bruno joined in again with

> “For I think it is Love,
> For I feel it is Love,
> For I’m sure it is nothing but Love!”

“That *are* pretty!” the little fellow exclaimed, as the children passed us﻿—so closely that we drew back a little to make room for them, and it seemed we had only to reach out a hand to touch them: but this we did not attempt.
“No use to try and stop them!” I said, as they passed away into the shadows. “Why, they could not even *see* us!”
“No use at all,” Lady Muriel echoed with a sigh. “One would *like* to meet them again, in living form! But I feel, somehow, *that* can never be. They have passed out of *our* lives!” She sighed again; and no more was said, till we came out into the main road, at a point near my lodgings.
“Well, I will leave you here,” she said. “I want to get back before dark: and I have a cottage-friend to visit, first. Good night, dear friend! Let us see you soon﻿—and often!” she added, with an affectionate warmth that went to my very heart. “*For those are few we hold as dear!*”
“Good night!” I answered. “Tennyson said that of a worthier friend than me.”
“Tennyson didn’t know what he was talking about!” she saucily rejoined, with a touch of her old childish gaiety; and we parted.

===Chinese===

> “请问那是谁的妙笔，把山谷与丘峦，
> 涂成这样赏心悦目的画卷？
> 把阳光和阴影洒满青青草甸，
> 直教小羊羔欢喜得蹦跳蹁跹？”

那银铃般的嗓音又扬起，那份天使般的甜，我几乎承受不住：——

> “那是个秘密，从不说给冷酷的心听，
> 虽然天上的天使把它歌唱，
> 唱得清越，只给听得见的耳朵听——
> 这秘密的名字，是爱！”

随后布鲁诺又加入了进来，一起唱

> “因为我想那是爱，
> 因为我觉得那是爱，
> 因为我确信那除了爱别无其他！”

“*真*好听！”小家伙感叹道，这时孩子们正打我们身边经过——近得我们微微退后给他们让路，仿佛只消伸只手就能碰到他们：但我们没有这样做。
“想拦也拦不住！”孩子们没入暗影时我说。“咦，他们根本*看不见*咱们呀！”
“一点用也没有，”穆丽尔小姐应声叹道。“谁不想*再*见他们，活生生地相见！可我不知怎么就觉得，*那*永远不会有了。他们已走出*我们的*生命！”她又是一叹；再没人说话，直到我们走上大路，到了我寓所近旁。
“好，我就送你到这儿，”她说。“我想赶在天黑前回去：还要先探望一位村舍里的朋友。晚安，亲爱的朋友！早些再来看我们——常来！”她又添了一句，那份深情的暖意直沁我心。“*因为我们视为至亲的人，寥寥无几！*”
“晚安！”我答。“丁尼生这话，说的是一位比我更配的人。”
“丁尼生又不知道自己在说什么！”她俏皮地回敬，旧日那份孩子气的欢快闪了一闪；我们便道别了。

## XX Gammon and Spinach / XX 火腿与菠菜
===Original===

My landlady’s welcome had an extra heartiness about it: and though, with a rare delicacy of feeling, she made no direct allusion to the friend whose companionship had done so much to brighten life for me, I felt sure that it was a kindly sympathy with my solitary state that made her so specially anxious to do all she could think of to ensure my comfort, and make me feel at home.
The lonely evening seemed long and tedious: yet I lingered on, watching the dying fire, and letting Fancy mould the red embers into the forms and faces belonging to bygone scenes. Now it seemed to be Bruno’s roguish smile that sparkled for a moment, and died away: now it was Sylvie’s rosy cheek: and now the Professor’s jolly round face, beaming with delight. “You’re welcome, my little ones!” he seemed to say. And then the red coal, which for the moment embodied the dear old Professor, began to wax dim, and with its dying lustre the words seemed to die away into silence. I seized the poker, and with an artful touch or two revived the waning glow, while Fancy﻿—no coy minstrel she﻿—sang me once again the magic strain I loved to hear.
“You’re welcome, little ones!” the cheery voice repeated. “I told them you were coming. Your rooms are all ready for you. And the Emperor and the Empress﻿—well, I think they’re rather pleased than otherwise! In fact, Her Highness said ‘I hope they’ll be in time for the Banquet!’ Those were her very words, I assure you!”
“Will Uggug be at the Banquet?” Bruno asked. And both children looked uneasy at the dismal suggestion.
“Why, of course he will!” chuckled the Professor. “Why, it’s his *birthday*, don’t you know? And his health will be drunk, and all that sort of thing. What would the Banquet be without *him*?”
“Ever so much nicer,” said Bruno. But he said it in a *very* low voice, and nobody but Sylvie heard him.
The Professor chuckled again. “It’ll be a jolly Banquet, now *you’ve* come, my little man! I *am* so glad to see you again!”
“I ’fraid we’ve been very long in coming,” Bruno politely remarked.
“Well, yes,” the Professor assented. “However, you’re very short now you’re come: that’s *some* comfort.” And he went on to enumerate the plans for the day. “The Lecture comes first,” he said. “*That* the Empress *insists* on. She says people will eat so much at the Banquet, they’ll be too sleepy to attend to the Lecture afterwards﻿—and perhaps she’s right.

===Chinese===

女房东的欢迎里透着一格外的热诚：尽管她怀着难得的细腻，绝口不提那位曾为我的生活增添了许多亮色的朋友，我却确信，正是对我孤身处境的善意体恤，才使她这般煞费苦心，想尽法子让我舒适自在，宾至如归。
孤单的夜晚显得漫长而乏味：我却仍守着炉边，望那渐渐熄灭的炉火，任凭幻想把红炭捏成往昔场景里的身影与面容。这一刻像是布鲁诺顽皮的笑靥闪了一闪，随即隐去：这一刻是西尔维玫瑰色的脸颊：这一刻又是教授那乐呵呵的圆脸，喜气洋洋。「欢迎啊，我的小家伙们！」他仿佛在说。随后，那块暂时化身为亲爱的老教授的红炭黯淡下去，话音也仿佛随着它将熄的光华，渐渐消逝于寂静。我抓起火钳，巧妙地拨上一两下，让衰微的红光重振，而幻想——她可不是羞答答的歌手——便又为我唱起我爱听的那支魔曲。
「欢迎，小家伙们！」那快活的嗓音重复道。「我早告诉他们你们要来。你们的房间全收拾好了。至于皇帝和皇后嘛——嗯，我看他们多半是高兴的！实际上，皇后陛下说：『但愿他们赶得上宴会！』她的原话就是这样，我保证！」
「乌格格也出席宴会吗？」布鲁诺问。这个不祥的提议让两个孩子都面有忧色。
「咦，他当然出席！」教授呵呵笑道。「要知道，那天是他的*生日*呀！大家要为他的健康干杯，还要有诸如此类的名堂。没有*他*还算什么宴会呢？」
「要好受得多呢，」布鲁诺说。可他说得*极*轻，除了西尔维谁也没听见。
教授又呵呵一笑。「*你们*来了，这才是一场痛快的宴会，小家伙！又见到你们，我*真*高兴！」
「我怕我们花的工夫老*长*了，」布鲁诺客气地说。
「嗯，是长了点，」教授表示同意。「不过你们如今一到，就老*短*了：这总算*一点*安慰。」接着他历数起这一天的安排。「先讲演，」他说。「*这个*是皇后*坚持*的。她说宴会上人人会吃得太多，饭后该瞌睡得听不进讲演——也许她说得对。

===Original===

There’ll just be a little *refreshment*, when the people first arrive﻿—as a kind of surprise for the Empress, you know. Ever since she’s been﻿—well, not *quite* so clever as she once was﻿—we’ve found it desirable to concoct little surprises for her. *Then* comes the Lecture﻿—”
“What? The Lecture you were getting ready﻿—ever so long ago?” Sylvie enquired.
“Yes﻿—that’s the one,” the Professor rather reluctantly admitted. “It *has* taken a goodish time to prepare. I’ve got so many other things to attend to. For instance, I’m Court-Physician. I have to keep all the Royal Servants in good health﻿—and that reminds me!” he cried, ringing the bell in a great hurry. “This is Medicine-Day! We only give Medicine once a week. If we were to begin giving it every day, the bottles would *soon* be empty!”
“But if they were ill on the *other* days?” Sylvie suggested.
“What, ill on the wrong *day*!” exclaimed the Professor. “Oh, that would never do! A Servant would be dismissed *at once*, who was ill on the wrong day! This is the Medicine for *today*,” he went on, taking down a large jug from a shelf. “I mixed it, myself, first thing this morning. Taste it!” he said, holding out the jug to Bruno. “Dip in your finger, and taste it!”
Bruno did so, and made such an excruciatingly wry face that Sylvie exclaimed, in alarm, “Oh, Bruno, you mustn’t!”
“It’s welly extremely nasty!” Bruno said, as his face resumed its natural shape.
“Nasty?” said the Professor. “Why, of *course* it is! What would Medicine be, if it wasn’t *nasty*?”
“Nice,” said Bruno.
