I hope you’ll enjoy the dinner﻿—such as it is; and that you won’t mind the heat﻿—such as it isn’t.”
The sentence *sounded* well, but somehow I couldn’t quite understand it; and the Other Professor seemed to be no better off. “Such as it isn’t *what*?” he peevishly enquired.
“It isn’t as hot as it might be,” the Professor replied, catching at the first idea that came to hand.
“Ah, I see what you mean *now*!” the Other Professor graciously remarked. “It’s very badly expressed, but I quite see it *now*! Thirteen minutes and a half ago,” he went on, looking first at Bruno and then at his watch as he spoke, “you said ‘this Cat’s very kind to the Mouses.’ It must be a singular animal!”
“So it *are*,” said Bruno, after carefully examining the Cat, to make sure how many there were of it.
“But how do you know it’s kind to the Mouses﻿—or, more correctly speaking, the *Mice*?”
“ ’Cause it *plays* with the Mouses,” said Bruno; “for to amuse them, oo know.”
“But that is just what I *don’t* know,” the Other Professor rejoined. “My belief is, it plays with them to *kill* them!”
“Oh, that’s quite a *accident*!” Bruno began, so eagerly, that it was evident he had already propounded this very difficulty to the Cat. “It ’splained all that to me, while it were drinking the milk. It said ‘I teaches the Mouses new games: the Mouses likes it ever so much.’ It said ‘Sometimes little accidents happens: sometimes the Mouses kills theirselves.’ It said ‘I’s always *welly* sorry, when the Mouses kills theirselves.’ It said﻿—”
“If it was so *very* sorry,” Sylvie said, rather disdainfully, “it wouldn’t *eat* the Mouses after they’d killed themselves!”
But this difficulty, also, had evidently not been lost sight of in the exhaustive ethical discussion just concluded. “It said﻿—” (the orator constantly omitted, as superfluous, his own share in the dialogue, and merely gave us the replies of the Cat) “It said ‘Dead Mouses *never* objecks to be eaten.’ It said ‘There’s no use wasting good Mouses.’ It said ‘Wifful﻿—’ sumfinoruvver. It said ‘And oo may live to say “How much I wiss I had the Mouse that then I frew away!” ’ It said﻿—.”
“It hadn’t *time* to say such a lot of things!” Sylvie interrupted indignantly.
“Oo doosn’t know how Cats speaks!” Bruno rejoined contemptuously. “Cats speaks *welly* quick!”

===Chinese===

但愿诸位用得开心——饭嘛，就这么个样；也望诸位别嫌热——热嘛，可不这么个样。」
这话*听*着挺像样，可我不知怎的没能全懂；另一位教授的处境也没好到哪儿去。「『不这么个样』是*什么*样？」他焦躁地问。
「就是没热到可能的那种热法，」教授答，一把抓住手边想到的头一个主意。
「啊，这我*现在*懂你的意思了！」另一位教授宽宏地评道。「表达得很糟，可我*现在*全懂了！十三分半钟前，」他接着说，说话时先看看布鲁诺、再看看表，「你说过『这只猫对老鼠们可好了』。那它准是只奇特的动物！」
「它*确实*是，」布鲁诺说，说完先仔细查验了一遍那只猫，确证它到底有几只。
「可你怎么知道它对老鼠们好——或者，说得更正确些，对*鼠们*好？」
「因为它跟老鼠们*玩*呀，」布鲁诺说；「为了逗它们开心，你知道。」
「可这恰恰是我*不*知道的，」另一位教授反驳。「依我看，它跟它们玩是为了*弄死*它们！」
「哦，那纯属*意外*！」布鲁诺急急开腔，那份急切表明，他早把这个难题向猫本人提出过了。「它喝奶的时候，全解释给我听了。它说：『俺教老鼠们新游戏：老鼠们可喜欢啦。』它说：『有时候出点小意外：有时候老鼠们自杀了。』它说：『老鼠们自杀的时候，俺总是*好——好*难过。』它说——」
「它要是*那么*难过，」西尔维相当轻蔑地说，「就不至于在老鼠们自杀之后还*吃*掉它们！」
可这个难题，在那场刚刚结束的穷根究底的伦理讨论里，显然也早有防备。「它说——」（这位演说家总把自己的那半截对话当作多余省去，只把猫的答话转给我们）「它说：『死了的老鼠*从来*不反对被吃。』它说：『糟蹋好老鼠没有用处。』它说：『故意——』什么的来着。它说：『你兴许有朝一日要说：唉，俺扔掉的那只老鼠，如今多想它还在！』它说——。」
「它哪有*工夫*说这么多话！」西尔维气愤地打断。
「你不知道猫说话有多利索！」布鲁诺轻蔑地回敬。「猫说话*可——可*快啦！」

## XXIII The Pig-Tale / 二十三 猪尾事
===Original===

By this time the appetites of the guests seemed to be nearly satisfied, and even *Bruno* had the resolution to say, when the Professor offered him a fourth slice of plum-pudding, “I thinks three helpings is enough!”
Suddenly the Professor started as if he had been electrified. “Why, I had nearly forgotten the most important part of the entertainment! The Other Professor is to recite a Tale of a Pig﻿—I mean a Pig-Tale,” he corrected himself. “It has Introductory Verses at the beginning, and at the end.”
“It can’t have Introductory Verses at the *end*, can it?” said Sylvie.
“Wait till you hear it,” said the Professor: “then you’ll see. I’m not sure it hasn’t some in the *middle*, as well.” Here he rose to his feet, and there was an instant silence through the Banqueting-Hall: they evidently expected a speech.
“Ladies, and gentlemen,” the Professor began, “the Other Professor is so kind as to recite a Poem. The title of it is ‘The Pig-Tale.’ He never recited it before!” (General cheering among the guests.) “He will never recite it again!” (Frantic excitement, and wild cheering all down the hall, the Professor himself mounting the table in hot haste, to lead the cheering, and waving his spectacles in one hand and a spoon in the other.)
Then the Other Professor got up, and began:﻿—

===Chinese===

到这时，宾客们的胃口看来已近满足，连*布鲁诺*都有了决断——教授给他递上第四块葡萄干布丁时，他说：“俺想三块就够啦！”
教授突然一激灵，像被电着了。“哎呀，险些忘了余兴最要紧的一桩！另一位教授要朗诵一篇猪的故事——俺是说，一篇《猪尾事》，”他纠正自己。“它开头有序诗，结尾也有。”
“结尾哪能有序诗呢，是不是？”西尔维说。
“等听了你就明白，”教授说：“到时候你就懂了。*中间*是不是也夹着几段，俺不敢说没有。”说着他站起身，宴会厅里霎时鸦雀无声：众人显然在等一篇致辞。
“女士们，先生们，”教授开腔，“另一位教授慨然应允，将为我们朗诵一首诗。诗题《猪尾事》。他从没朗诵过它！”（宾客齐声喝彩。）“他也永不再朗诵它！”（全场狂热沸腾，欢呼沿大厅一路滚下去，教授本人火速登上餐桌领呼，一手挥眼镜，一手挥汤匙。）
于是另一位教授起身，开口道：——

===Original===

> Little Birds are dining
> Warily and well,
> Hid in mossy cell:
> Hid, I say, by waiters
> Gorgeous in their gaiters﻿—
> I’ve a Tale to tell.
> Little Birds are feeding
> Justices with jam,
> Rich in frizzled ham:
> Rich, I say, in oysters
> Haunting shady cloisters﻿—
> That is what I am.
> Little Birds are teaching
> Tigresses to smile,
> Innocent of guile:
> Smile, I say, not smirkle﻿—
> Mouth a semicircle,
> That’s the proper style.
> Little Birds are sleeping
> All among the pins,
> Where the loser wins:
> Where, I say, he sneezes
> When and how he pleases﻿—
> So the Tale begins.
> There was a Pig that sat alone
> Beside a ruined Pump:
> By day and night he made his moan﻿—
> It would have stirred a heart of stone
> To see him wring his hoofs and groan,
> Because he could not jump.
> A certain Camel heard him shout﻿—
> A Camel with a hump.
> “Oh, is it Grief, or is it Gout?
> What is this bellowing about?”
> That Pig replied, with quivering snout,
> “Because I cannot jump!”
> That Camel scanned him, dreamy-eyed.
> “Methinks you are too plump.
> I never knew a Pig so wide﻿—
> That wobbled so from side to side﻿—
> Who could, however much he tried,
> Do such a thing as *jump*!
> “Yet mark those trees, two miles away,
> All clustered in a clump:
> If you could trot there twice a day,
> Nor ever pause for rest or play,
> In the far future﻿—Who can say?﻿—
> You may be fit to jump.”
> That Camel passed, and left him there,
> Beside the ruined Pump.
> Oh, horrid was that Pig’s despair!
> His shrieks of anguish filled the air.
> He wrung his hoofs, he rent his hair,
> Because he could not jump.
> There was a Frog that wandered by﻿—
> A sleek and shining lump:
> Inspected him with fishy eye,
> And said “O Pig, what makes you cry?”
> And bitter was that Pig’s reply,
> “Because I cannot jump!”
> That Frog he grinned a grin of glee,
> And hit his chest a thump
> “O Pig,” said, “be ruled by me,
> And you shall see what you shall see.
> This minute, for a trifling fee,
> I’ll teach you how to jump!
> “You may be faint from many a fall,
> And bruised by many a bump:
> But, if you persevere through all,
> And practise first on something small,
> Concluding with a ten-foot wall,
> You’ll find that you can jump!”
> That Pig looked up with joyful start:
> “Oh Frog, you *are* a trump!

===Chinese===

> 小鸟儿们正用餐，
> 又谨慎又美满，
> 藏进青苔的地窝：
> 我说，藏——是让侍者藏的，
> 侍者们绑腿辉煌——
> 我有个故事要讲。
> 小鸟儿们正喂养，
> 喂法官吃果酱，
> 卷炸火腿满堂香：
> 我说，香——还盛产牡蛎，
> 出没在阴凉的修道院——
> 我就是这么一号人。
> 小鸟儿们正教
> 母老虎学微笑，
> 心地纯真不狡：
> 我说，笑——不许撇嘴皮笑，
> 嘴弯成半圆才好，
> 那才是正经腔调。
> 小鸟儿们正睡觉，
> 睡在木柱的瓶阵间，
> 那里输家反赢钱：
> 我说，那里他打喷嚏，
> 想何时打就何时打——
> 故事这就开场啦。
> 有一头猪独自坐，
> 坐在破水泵旁：
> 日日夜夜它哭诉——
> 石头人见了也要动容：
> 看它绞着蹄子唉声叹气，
> 只因为它不会跳。
> 有头骆驼听它吼——
> 一头带峰的骆驼。
> “哦，是伤心，还是痛风？
> 这般嚎叫为了哪桩？”
> 猪答道，鼻子直哆嗦：
> “只因俺不会跳！”
> 骆驼眯眼把它打量。
> “我看你太肥硕。
> 从没见过这么宽的猪——
> 摇摇摆摆左右晃——
> 俺不信它再怎么努力，
> 能做成‘跳’这桩事！
> 不过瞧瞧两英里外那些树，
> 一丛丛挤成团：
> 你若能每天小跑两趟，
> 从不停下来歇息或玩耍，
> 遥远的将来——谁说得准？——
> 或许你就配跳了。”
> 骆驼走远，撇下它，
> 撇在破水泵旁。
> 哦，那猪的绝望多可怕！
> 惨嚎声声震四方。
> 它绞着蹄，扯着毛，
> 只因为它不会跳。
> 有只青蛙游荡过——
> 一团油光水滑的疙瘩：
> 鼓着死鱼眼把它瞧，
> 说道：“猪啊，你哭个啥？”
> 猪的回答好不辛酸：
> “只因俺不会跳！”
> 那青蛙咧嘴嘻嘻笑，
> 胸口撃地捶一拳：
> “猪啊，”它说，“听俺的，
> 保你看见好戏。
> 这一会儿，只收一点点学费，
> 俺教你怎样跳！
> 你兴许摔得头晕眼花，
> 撞得青一块紫一块：
> 可只要事事熬得住，
> 先拿小东西练起，
> 末了拿十尺高墙收官，
> 你就会发现自己能跳！”
> 那猪一喜，抬头望：
> “青蛙啊，你*真是*个大好人！

===Original===

> Your words have healed my inward smart﻿—
> Come, name your fee and do your part:
> Bring comfort to a broken heart,
> By teaching me to jump!”
> “My fee shall be a mutton-chop,
> My goal this ruined Pump.
> Observe with what an airy flop
> I plant myself upon the top!
> Now bend your knees and take a hop,
> For that’s the way to jump!”
> Uprose that Pig, and rushed, full whack,
> Against the ruined Pump:
> Rolled over like an empty sack,
> And settled down upon his back,
> While all his bones at once went ‘Crack!’
> It was a fatal jump.

When the Other Professor had recited this Verse, he went across to the fireplace, and put his head up the chimney. In doing this, he lost his balance, and fell headfirst into the empty grate, and got so firmly fixed there that it was some time before he could be dragged out again.
Bruno had had time to say “I thought he wanted to see how many peoples was up the chimbley.”
And Sylvie had said “*Chimney*﻿—not chimbley.”
And Bruno had said “Don’t talk ’ubbish!”
All this, while the Other Professor was being extracted.
“You must have blacked your face!” the Empress said anxiously. “Let me send for some soap?”
“Thanks, no,” said the Other Professor, keeping his face turned away. “Black’s quite a respectable colour. Besides, soap would be no use without water.”
Keeping his back well turned away from the audience, he went on with the Introductory Verses:﻿—

===Chinese===

> 你一言医好俺心头之痛——
> 来，开个价，出把力：
> 救一救这颗破碎的心，
> 教会俺怎样跳！”
> “学费要一块羊排，
> 目标是这破水泵。
> 瞧俺多么轻盈一扑，
> 稳稳落上泵顶住！
> 这就弯下膝盖蹦一下，
> 跳就是这么个跳法！”
> 那猪爬起，一头猛冲，
> 冲向那破水泵：
> 像只空口袋翻滚在地，
> 仰面朝天躺定了，
> 浑身骨头顿时“咔嚓！”响——
> 那是一跳送了命。

另一位教授诵完这一节，走到壁炉边，把头探进了烟囱。这一探失了平衡，头朝下载进空炉膛，卡得死紧，好一阵子才被人拽出来。
这中间布鲁诺来得及说：“俺当他想数数烟通里有几个人。”
西尔维则说过：“是*烟囱*——不是烟通。”
布鲁诺又说过：“少说‘费话’！”
这些都是趁另一位教授往外拔的时候说的。
“您的脸准是熏黑了！”皇后焦急地说。“要俺叫人拿肥皂来吗？”
“多谢，不必，”另一位教授说，脸始终背着人。“黑色是相当体面的颜色。再说，没有水，肥皂也没用。”
他把后背严严实实留给观众，继续念序诗：——

===Original===

> Little Birds are writing
> Interesting books,
> To be read by cooks:
> Read, I say, not roasted﻿—
> Letterpress, when toasted,
> Loses its good looks.
> Little Birds are playing
> Bagpipes on the shore,
> Where the tourists snore:
> “Thanks!” they cry. “ ’Tis thrilling!
> Take, oh take this shilling!
> Let us have no more!”
> Little Birds are bathing
> Crocodiles in cream,
> Like a happy dream:
> Like, but not so lasting﻿—
> Crocodiles, when fasting,
> Are not all they seem!
> That Camel passed, as Day grew dim
> Around the ruined Pump.
> “O broken heart! O broken limb!
> It needs,” that Camel said to him,
> “Something more fairy-like and slim,
> To execute a jump!”
> That Pig lay still as any stone,
> And could not stir a stump:
> Nor ever, if the truth were known,
> Was he again observed to moan,
> Nor ever wring his hoofs and groan,
> Because he could not jump.
> That Frog made no remark, for he
> Was dismal as a dump:
> He knew the consequence must be
> That he would never get his fee﻿—
> And still he sits, in miserie,
> Upon that ruined Pump!

===Chinese===

> 小鸟儿们在著书，
> 著些有趣的书，
> 供厨娘们阅读：
> 我说，读——不是烤读——
> 铅字一旦烤糊，
> 就没了好看处。
> 小鸟儿们在吹奏，
> 海岸边把风笛奏，
> 游客们鼾声如牛：
> “多谢！”他们喊。“真带劲！
> 收下，收下这一先令！
> 千万别再往下奏！”
> 小鸟儿们在洗浴，
> 拿奶油洗鳄鱼，
> 好似一场欢喜梦：
> 像梦，却没那么久长——
> 鳄鱼饿着肚皮的时光，
> 可不是看着那模样！
> 骆驼又过，天色昏，
> 昏在那破水泵旁。
> “哦破碎的心！哦断掉的肢！”骆驼对它说，
> “要完成‘跳’这样的事，
> 得换个更仙灵、更苗条的身子！”
> 那猪僵卧如顽石，
> 一根蹄茬也动不得：
> 老实说，从此再没人
> 见它哭过半声，
> 也没见它绞蹄叹气，
> 只因为它再不用跳。
> 那青蛙一言不发，因为
> 它蔫得像个闷葫芦：
> 它知道后果必定是
> 学费再也要不回——
> 它至今还坐那儿，惨兮兮，
> 坐在那破水泵上！

===Original===

“It’s a miserable story!” said Bruno. “It begins miserably, and it ends miserablier. I think I shall cry. Sylvie, please lend me your handkerchief.”
“I haven’t got it with me,” Sylvie whispered.
“Then I won’t cry,” said Bruno manfully.
“There are more Introductory Verses to come,” said the Other Professor, “but I’m hungry.” He sat down, cut a large slice of cake, put it on Bruno’s plate, and gazed at his own empty plate in astonishment.
“Where did you get that cake?” Sylvie whispered to Bruno.
“He gived it me,” said Bruno.
“But you shouldn’t ask for things! You *know* you shouldn’t!”
“I *didn’t* ask,” said Bruno, taking a fresh mouthful: “he *gived* it me.”
Sylvie considered this for a moment: then she saw her way out of it. “Well, then, ask him to give *me* some!”
“You seem to enjoy that cake?” the Professor remarked.
“Doos that mean ‘munch’?” Bruno whispered to Sylvie.
Sylvie nodded. “It means ‘to munch’ and ‘to *like* to munch.’ ”
Bruno smiled at the Professor. “I *doos* enjoy it,” he said.
The Other Professor caught the word. “And I hope you’re enjoying *yourself*, little Man?” he enquired.
Bruno’s look of horror quite startled him. “No, *indeed* I aren’t!” he said.
The Other Professor looked thoroughly puzzled. “Well, well!” he said. “Try some cowslip wine!” And he filled a glass and handed it to Bruno. “Drink this, my dear, and you’ll be quite another man!”
“Who shall I be?” said Bruno, pausing in the act of putting it to his lips.
“Don’t ask so many questions!” Sylvie interposed, anxious to save the poor old man from further bewilderment. “Suppose we get the Professor to tell us a story.”
Bruno adopted the idea with enthusiasm. “*Please* do!” he cried eagerly. “Sumfin about tigers﻿—and bumblebees﻿—and robin-redbreasts, oo knows!”
“Why should you always have *live* things in stories?” said the Professor. “Why don’t you have events, or circumstances?”
“Oh, *please* invent a story like that!” cried Bruno.
The Professor began fluently enough. “Once a coincidence was taking a walk with a little accident, and they met an explanation﻿—a *very* old explanation﻿—so old that it was quite doubled up, and looked more like a conundrum﻿—” he broke off suddenly.
“*Please* go on!” both children exclaimed.
The Professor made a candid confession. “It’s a very difficult sort to invent, I find. Suppose Bruno tells one, first.”

===Chinese===

“这故事惨兮兮！”布鲁诺说。“开头惨，结尾更惨。俺看俺要哭了。西尔维，把手绢借俺用用。”
“俺没带在身上，”西尔维悄声说。
“那俺就不哭了，”布鲁诺好汉似的说。
“序诗还有几段没念，”另一位教授说，“可俺饿了。”他坐下，切了一大块蛋糕，撞在布鲁诺盘里，然后惊愕地盯着自己那只空盘发怔。
“那蛋糕哪儿来的？”西尔维悄声问布鲁诺。
“他给俺的，”布鲁诺说。
“可不许开口讨东西！你*明知道*不许的！”
“俺*没*讨，”布鲁诺说着又咬了一大口：“是他*给*俺的。”
西尔维琢磨了一小会儿：随即想出了路子。“那好，你去叫他给*俺*也来点！”
“你好像很享受那块蛋糕？”教授搭话。
“这词是‘嚼’的意思吗？”布鲁诺悄声问西尔维。
西尔维点头。“意思是‘嚼’，还‘爱*嚼*’。”
布鲁诺朝教授一笑。“俺*确实*享受，”他说。
另一位教授抓住了这个词。“那俺也希望你享受*你自己*呢，小人儿？”他问。
布鲁诺一脸惊怖，把他吓了一跳。“不，俺才*不*呢！”他说。
另一位教授彻底糊涂了。“罢了，罢了！”他说。“尝尝报春酒！”他斟满一杯递给布鲁诺。“喝了它，亲爱的，你就会变成截然另一个人！”
“那俺会变成谁？”布鲁诺举杯将饮，停住问。
“别问这么多问题！”西尔维插进来，急着救可怜的老人家免于更晕。“不如请教授给咱们讲个故事。”
布鲁诺热烈采纳。“*求*您讲！”他急切地喊。“讲点老虎——还有大黄蜂——还有知更雀什么的，咚懂的！”
“为什么故事里总要*活*物？”教授说。“为什么不要事件，或者境况？”
“哦，*求*您就编一个那样的故事！”布鲁诺喊。
教授起头起得颇为流利。“从前，一个巧合带着一个小意外出门散步，迎面遇上一个解释——一个*非常*年迈的解释——老得完全佝偻了腰，看上去更像一条谜语——”他突然刹住。
“*求*您讲下去！”两个孩子齐喊。
教授坦白招认。“我发现这一种实在难编。不如布鲁诺先讲一个。”

===Original===

Bruno was only too happy to adopt the suggestion.
“Once there were a Pig, and a Accordion, and two Jars of Orange-marmalade﻿—”
“The dramatis personae,” murmured the Professor. “Well, what then?”
“So, when the Pig played on the Accordion,” Bruno went on, “one of the Jars of Orange-marmalade didn’t like the tune, and the other Jar of Orange-marmalade did like the tune﻿—I *know* I shall get confused among those Jars of Orange-marmalade, Sylvie!” he whispered anxiously.
“I will now recite the other Introductory Verses,” said the Other Professor.

> Little Birds are choking
> Baronets with bun,
> Taught to fire a gun:
> Taught, I say, to splinter
> Salmon in the winter﻿—
> Merely for the fun.
> Little Birds are hiding
> Crimes in carpetbags,
> Blessed by happy stags:
> Blessed, I say, though beaten﻿—
> Since our friends are eaten
> When the memory flags.
> Little Birds are tasting
> Gratitude and gold,
> Pale with sudden cold
> Pale, I say, and wrinkled﻿—
> When the bells have tinkled
> And the Tale is told.

===Chinese===

布鲁诺求之不得。
“从前有一头猪，还有一架手风琴，还有两罐橘子酱——”
“剧中人物表齐了，”教授低声说。“然后呢？”
“于是，猪拉起手风琴，”布鲁诺续道，“两罐橘子酱里有一罐不喜欢这个调子，另一罐橘子酱喜欢——俺*知道*俺准会在那几罐橘子酱里搅糊涂的，西尔维！”他着急地悄声说。
“现在俺来念剩下的序诗。”另一位教授说。

> 小鸟儿们在塞饱，
> 拿圆面包噎从男爵，
> 还教他们放枪：
> 我说，教——教他们崩碎
> 冬天里的鲑鱼——
> 纯为好玩一场。
> 小鸟儿们在窝藏，
> 把罪案藏进毡提包，
> 神鹿降福把它们保：
> 我说，保——虽则挨了揍——
> 只因朋友被吃掉的时候，
> 记性也就不牢靠。
> 小鸟儿们在品尝，
> 品尝感恩与黄金，
> 骤寒冻得脸发青：
> 我说，青——还起了皱纹——
> 那时钟声叮咛，
> 故事也就念到终。

===Original===

“The next thing to be done,” the Professor cheerfully remarked to the Lord Chancellor, as soon as the applause, caused by the recital of the Pig-Tale, had come to an end, “is to drink the Emperor’s health, is it not?”
“Undoubtedly!” the Lord Chancellor replied with much solemnity, as he rose to his feet to give the necessary directions for the ceremony. “Fill your glasses!” he thundered. All did so, instantly. “Drink the Emperor’s health!” A general gurgling resounded all through the Hall. “Three cheers for the Emperor!” The faintest possible sound followed *this* announcement: and the Chancellor, with admirable presence of mind, instantly proclaimed “A speech from the Emperor!”
The Emperor had begun his speech almost before the words were uttered. “However unwilling to be Emperor﻿—since you all wish me to be Emperor﻿—you know how badly the late Warden managed things﻿—with such enthusiasm as you have shown﻿—he persecuted you﻿—he taxed you too heavily﻿—you know who is fittest man to be Emperor﻿—my brother had no sense﻿—.”
How long this curious speech might have lasted it is impossible to say, for just at this moment a hurricane shook the palace to its foundations, bursting open the windows, extinguishing some of the lamps, and filling the air with clouds of dust, which took strange shapes in the air, and seemed to form words.
But the storm subsided as suddenly as it had risen﻿—the casements swung into their places again: the dust vanished: all was as it had been a minute ago﻿—with the exception of the Emperor and Empress, over whom had come a wondrous change. The vacant stare, the meaningless smile, had passed away: all could see that these two strange beings had returned to their senses.
The Emperor continued his speech as if there had been no interruption. “And we have behaved﻿—my wife and I﻿—like two arrant Knaves. We deserve no better name. When my brother went away, you lost the best Warden you ever had. And I’ve been doing my best, wretched hypocrite that I am, to cheat you into making me an Emperor. Me! One that has hardly got the wits to be a shoeblack!”
The Lord Chancellor wrung his hands in despair. “He is mad, good people!” he was beginning. But both speeches stopped suddenly﻿—and, in the dead silence that followed, a knocking was heard at the outer door.
“What is it?” was the general cry. People began running in and out.

===Chinese===

猪尾事的朗诵赢得的掌声一停，教授便喜洋洋地对大法官说：“接下来该行的节目，是敬祝皇帝健康，对吧？”
“毫无疑问！”大法官极庄重地答，起身指挥大典的各项必要号令。“斟满酒杯！”他炸雷般吼。人人即刻照办。“敬祝皇帝健康！”咕咚咕咚的声响传遍大厅。“为皇帝三呼！”这道号令之后，响起的是可能范围内最微弱的声音：大法官临危不乱，令人叹服，当即宣布：“皇帝陛下致辞！”
话音未落，皇帝的致辞几乎已经开始。“尽管朕并不情愿为帝——既然你们都愿朕为帝——你们知道先典狱长把事情管得多糟——以你们方才表露的热忱——他迫害过你们——他征你们的税太重——你们知道谁最适合为帝——朕兄毫无见识——。”
这篇古怪的演讲会持续多久，无从知晓，因为正在此刻，一场飓风把宫殿摇撞到地基，轰开窗户，吹熄了几盏灯，尘雾漫天，在空中凝成奇形，仿佛拼出字来。
可风暴骤起骤息——窗扇荡回原位：尘雾消散：一切又同刚才一样——除了皇帝和皇后，他们身上起了神妙的变化。那空洞的凝视、无意义的傻笑，都不见了：谁都看得出，这两个怪人已恢复神智。
皇帝的致辞续了下去，仿佛从未中断。“而我们一直行事——朕与朕妻——像两个不折不扣的无赖。我们不配更好的称呼。朕兄一去，你们失去了此生最好的典狱长。而朕呢，这个可悲的伪君子，一直千方百计哄骗你们立朕为帝。朕！一个连擦鞋匠的头脑都凑不齐的人！”
大法官绝望地绞手。“他疯了，善良的人们！”他正要说。可两篇演讲都戛然而止——随之而来的一片死寂里，外门传来叩门声。
“出了什么事？”四下齐声喊。人们跑进跑出。

===Original===

The excitement increased every moment. The Lord Chancellor, forgetting all the rules of Court-ceremony, ran full speed down the hall, and in a minute returned, pale and gasping for breath.

===Chinese===

骚动一阵紧过一阵。大法官全然忘了宫廷礼仪的规矩，全速奔下大厅，转瞬便回来了，面无人色，喘作一团。

## XXIV The Beggar’s Return / 乞儿的归来
===Original===

“Your Imperial Highnesses!” he began. “It’s the old Beggar again! Shall we set the dogs at him?”
“Bring him here!” said the Emperor.
The Chancellor could scarcely believe his ears. “*Here*, your Imperial Highness? Did I rightly understand﻿—.”
“Bring him here!” the Emperor thundered once more. The Chancellor tottered down the hall﻿—and in another minute the crowd divided, and the poor old Beggar was seen entering the Banqueting-Hall.
He was indeed a pitiable object: the rags, that hung about him, were all splashed with mud: his white hair and his long beard were tossed about in wild disorder. Yet he walked upright, with a stately tread, as if used to command: and﻿—strangest sight of all﻿—Sylvie and Bruno came with him, clinging to his hands, and gazing at him with looks of silent love.
Men looked eagerly to see how the Emperor would receive the bold intruder. Would he hurl him from the steps of the dais? But no. To their utter astonishment, the Emperor knelt as the beggar approached, and with bowed head murmured “Forgive us!”
“Forgive us!” the Empress, kneeling at her husband’s side, meekly repeated.
The Outcast smiled. “Rise up!” he said. “I forgive you!” And men saw with wonder that a change had passed over the old beggar, even as he spoke. What had seemed, but now, to be vile rags and splashes of mud, were seen to be in truth kingly trappings, broidered with gold, and sparkling with gems. All knew him now, and bent low before the Elder Brother, the true Warden.
“Brother mine, and Sister mine!” the Warden began, in a clear voice that was heard all through that vast hall. “I come not to disturb you. Rule on, as Emperor, and rule wisely. For I am chosen King of Elfland. Tomorrow I return there, taking nought from hence, save only﻿—save only﻿—” his voice trembled, and with a look of ineffable tenderness, he laid his hands in silence on the heads of the two little ones who clung around him.
But he recovered himself in a moment, and beckoned to the Emperor to resume his place at the table. The company seated themselves again﻿—room being found for the Elfin-King between his two children﻿—and the Lord Chancellor rose once more, to propose the next toast.
“The next toast﻿—the hero of the day﻿—why, he isn’t here!” he broke off in wild confusion.
Good gracious! Everybody had forgotten Prince Uggug!
“He was told of the Banquet, of course?” said the Emperor.

===Chinese===

「皇帝陛下！皇后陛下！」他开口道。「又是那个老乞儿！要不要放狗咬他？」
「带他上来！」皇帝说。
大法官简直不敢相信自己的耳朵。「带到*这里*来，陛下？臣没有听错吧——」
「带他上来！」皇帝再次雷鸣般喝道。大法官踉踉跄跄奔下大厅——不到一分钟，人群分作两边，只见那可怜的老乞儿正走进宴会厅。
他确实一副可怜相：身上挂着的破衣烂衫溅满泥浆，白发和长须乱蓬蓬地披散着。然而他步履挺拔，气度威严，仿佛惯于发号施令：而且——最奇异的景象是——西尔维和布鲁诺也随他同来，紧攥着他的双手，默默含情地仰望着他。
众人急切地想看皇帝会如何接待这个胆大包天的闯入者。会把他从御阶上掷下去吗？却不。令他们万分错愕的是，乞儿走近时，皇帝竟跪了下来，垂首低语：「宽恕我们！」
「宽恕我们！」皇后跪在丈夫身旁，温顺地跟着说。
那被放逐者微微一笑。「起来吧！」他说，「我宽恕你们！」众人惊奇地看到，老乞儿的话音未落，身上已起了变化。方才看来还是污秽的破布和泥点，此刻显出真相，竟是绣金镶宝、光彩夺目的王者华服。众人这才认出他来，纷纷在那位兄长、真正的守护人面前深深躬身。
「我的弟弟，我的弟妹！」守护人开口了，声音清朗，传遍整座大厅。「我不是来搅扰你们的。你继续当你的皇帝，好好治理吧。因为我已被选为精灵国的国王。明日我便回那里去，不从这里带走任何东西，只除了——只除了——」他的声音颤抖起来，带着说不尽的温柔，默默把手按在依偎着他的两个小家伙的头上。
但他旋即定住心神，招手请皇帝回到席上。众人重新入座——精灵王的座位安排在他两个孩子中间——大法官又站起身来，提议下一轮祝酒。
「下一杯——敬今日的英雄——咦，他怎么不在！」他慌乱地顿住了话头。
天哪！所有人都把乌格格王子忘了个干净！
「宴会的事，当然通知过他了吧？」皇帝问。

===Original===

“Undoubtedly!” replied the Chancellor. “*That* would be the duty of the Gold Stick in Waiting.”
“Let the Gold Stick come forwards!” the Emperor gravely said.
The Gold Stick came forwards. “I attended on His Imperial Fatness,” was the statement made by the trembling official. “I told him of the Lecture and the Banquet﻿—.”
“What followed?” said the Emperor: for the unhappy man seemed almost too frightened to go on.
“His Imperial Fatness was graciously pleased to be sulky. His Imperial Fatness was graciously pleased to box my ears. His Imperial Fatness was graciously pleased to say ‘I don’t care!’ ”
“ ‘Don’t-care’ came to a bad end,” Sylvie whispered to Bruno. “I’m not sure, but I *believe* he was hanged.”
The Professor overheard her. “*That* result,” he blandly remarked, “was merely a case of mistaken identity.”
Both children looked puzzled.
“Permit me to explain. ‘Don’t-care’ and ‘Care’ were twin-brothers. ‘Care,’ you know, killed the Cat. And they caught ‘Don’t-care’ by mistake, and hanged him instead. And so ‘Care’ is alive still. But he’s very unhappy without his brother. That’s why they say ‘Begone, dull Care!’ ”
“Thank you!” Sylvie said, heartily. “It’s very extremely interesting. Why, it seems to explain *everything*!”
“Well, not quite *everything*,” the Professor modestly rejoined. “There are two or three scientific difficulties﻿—”
“What was your general impression as to His Imperial Fatness?” the Emperor asked the Gold Stick.
“My impression was that His Imperial Fatness was getting more﻿—”
“More *what*?”
All listened breathlessly for the next word.
“More **prickly**!”
“He must be sent for *at once*!” the Emperor exclaimed. And the Gold Stick went off like a shot. The Elfin-King sadly shook his head. “No use, no use!” he murmured to himself. “Loveless, loveless!”
Pale, trembling, speechless, the Gold Stick came slowly back again.
“Well?” said the Emperor. “Why does not the Prince appear?”
“One can easily guess,” said the Professor. “His Imperial Fatness is, without doubt, a little preoccupied.”
Bruno turned a look of solemn enquiry on his old friend. “What do that word mean?”
But the Professor took no notice of the question. He was eagerly listening to the Gold Stick’s reply.
“Please your Highness! His Imperial Fatness is﻿—” Not a word more could he utter.
The Empress rose in an agony of alarm. “Let us go to him!” she cried.

===Chinese===

「当然！」大法官答道。「那是值殿金杖官的职责。」
「让值殿金杖官上前！」皇帝正色道。
值殿金杖官走上前来。「臣一直伺候着至尊胖下，」这位官员抖抖索索地陈述道。「臣把演讲和宴会的事都禀告了他——」
「后来呢？」皇帝问：那可怜人吓得几乎说不下去了。
「至尊胖下龙颜大悦，闹起了别扭。至尊胖下龙颜大悦，赏了臣两记耳光。至尊胖下龙颜大悦，说了句『我才不管呢！』」
「『不管先生』下场很惨呢，」西尔维悄悄对布鲁诺说。「我不太确定，不过我*猜*他是被绞死了。」
教授听见了她的话。「那个结局嘛，」他温和地评说道，「不过是认错了人的一桩冤案。」
两个孩子都一脸迷惑。
「容我解释。『不管』和『操心』是孪生兄弟。『操心』呢，你们知道，把猫害死了。人们错抓了『不管』，把他绞死了。于是『操心』至今还活着。可是没了兄弟，他很不快活。所以才有人说『走开，闷人的操心！』」
「谢谢您！」西尔维衷心地说。「这实在太太有趣了。哎呀，这简直把*什么都*解释清楚了！」
「呃，倒也不能解释*一切*，」教授谦虚地应道。「还有两三个科学上的难点——」
「你对至尊胖下总体的印象如何？」皇帝问值殿金杖官。
「臣的印象是，至尊胖下变得越来越——」
「越来越*什么*？」
众人都屏息静听下文。
「越来越**多刺**了！」
「得*马上*去请他！」皇帝叫道。值殿金杖官一溜烟去了。精灵王悲哀地摇了摇头。「没用，没用的！」他喃喃自语。「没有爱，没有爱啊！」
值殿金杖官脸色惨白，浑身发抖，一句话也说不出，慢慢腾腾地又回来了。
「怎么样？」皇帝问。「王子怎么还不来？」
「不难猜到，」教授说。「至尊胖下无疑是有点儿心不在焉。」
布鲁诺一脸郑重其事的探询，望着他的老朋友。「那个词词是啥意思呀？」
可教授没理会这个问题。他正急切地听着值殿金杖官回话。
「启禀陛下！至尊胖下他——」他再一个字也吐不出来了。
皇后惊惶万状地站起身。「我们到他那里去！」她喊道。

===Original===

And there was a general rush for the door.
Bruno slipped off his chair in a moment. “May we go too?” he eagerly asked. But the King did not hear the question, as the Professor was speaking to him. “*Preoccupied*, your Majesty!” he was saying. “That is what he is, no doubt!”
“May we go and see him?” Bruno repeated. The King nodded assent, and the children ran off. In a minute or two they returned, slowly and gravely. “Well?” said the King. “What’s the matter with the Prince?”
“He’s﻿—what *you* said,” Bruno replied, looking at the Professor. “That hard word.” And he looked to Sylvie for assistance.
“Porcupine,” said Sylvie.
“No, no!” the Professor corrected her. “ ‘*Preoccupied*,’ you mean.”
“No, it’s *porcupine*,” persisted Sylvie. “Not that other word at all. And please will you come? The house is all in an uproar.” (“And oo’d better bring an uproar-glass wiz oo!” added Bruno.)
We got up in great haste, and followed the children upstairs. No one took the least notice of *me*, but I wasn’t at all surprised at this, as I had long realised that I was quite invisible to them all﻿—even to Sylvie and Bruno.
All along the gallery, that led to the Prince’s apartment, an excited crowd was surging to and fro, and the Babel of voices was deafening: against the door of the room three strong men were leaning, vainly trying to shut it﻿—for some great animal inside was constantly bursting it half open, and we had a glimpse, before the men could push it back again, of the head of a furious wild beast, with great fiery eyes and gnashing teeth. Its voice was a sort of mixture﻿—there was the roaring of a lion, and the bellowing of a bull, and now and then a scream like a gigantic parrot. “There is no judging by the voice!” the Professor cried in great excitement. “What is it?” he shouted to the men at the door. And a general chorus of voices answered him “Porcupine! Prince Uggug has turned into a Porcupine!”
“A new Specimen!” exclaimed the delighted Professor. “Pray let me go in. It should be labeled at once!”
But the strong men only pushed him back. “Label it, indeed! Do you want to be eaten up?” they cried.
“Never mind about Specimens, Professor!” said the Emperor, pushing his way through the crowd. “Tell us how to keep him safe!”
“A large cage!” the Professor promptly replied.

===Chinese===

于是众人一齐向门口涌去。
布鲁诺立刻从椅子上出溜下来。「我们也能去吗？」他急切地问。可国王没有听见这句话，因为教授正对他说话。「心不在焉，陛下！」他说道。「他准是这样，没错！」
「我们能去看看他吗？」布鲁诺又问了一遍。国王点头应允，孩子们跑开了。不消一两分钟，他们慢吞吞、郑重重地回来了。「怎么样？」国王问。「王子怎么了？」
「他是——就是*您*说的那个，」布鲁诺答道，望着教授。「那个难词。」他向西尔维投去求助的一眼。
「豪猪。」西尔维说。
「不，不！」教授纠正她。「你是说『*心不在焉*』。」
「不，就是*豪猪*，」西尔维坚持道。「根本不是那个别的词。请您来好吗？整座宅子都闹翻天了。」（「你最好带个翻天镜来！」布鲁诺补充说。）
我们急忙起身，跟着孩子们上楼。谁都没有稍稍理会*我*一下，对此我毫不意外，因为我早已明白，我在他们所有人眼中都全然隐形——连西尔维和布鲁诺也看不见我。
通往王子居室的那条长廊里，激动的人群拥来挤去，人声嘈杂，震耳欲聋：三个壮汉抵着房门，想把它关上，却徒劳无功——屋里有一头巨兽不住地把门撞开半扇，趁人们把门推回去之前，我们瞥见一头狂怒野兽的脑袋，一双火眼，咬牙切齿。它的嗓音是个大杂烩——有狮子的咆哮，有公牛的吼叫，还不时来一声巨鹦鹉似的尖叫。「凭声音没法判断！」教授激动万分地喊道。「是什么？」他朝门口的人们大叫。众人异口同声地回答他：「豪猪！乌格格王子变成一头豪猪了！」
「新标本！」教授喜出望外地喊道。「求你们让我进去。得马上给它贴上标签！」
可壮汉们只是把他推回来。「还贴标签呢！你想被它吃掉吗？」他们嚷道。
「别管什么标本了，教授！」皇帝拨开人群说道。「快告诉我们怎么把他关住！」
「一只大笼子！」教授脱口而出。

===Original===

“Bring a large cage,” he said to the people generally, “with strong bars of steel, and a portcullis made to go up and down like a mousetrap! Does anyone happen to have such a thing about him?”
It didn’t sound a likely sort of thing for anyone to have about him; however, they brought him one directly: curiously enough, there happened to be one standing in the gallery.
“Put it facing the opening of the door, and draw up the portcullis!” This was done in a moment.
“Blankets now!” cried the Professor. “This is a most interesting Experiment!”
There happened to be a pile of blankets close by: and the Professor had hardly said the word, when they were all unfolded and held up like curtains all around. The Professor rapidly arranged them in two rows, so as to make a dark passage, leading straight from the door to the mouth of the cage.
“Now fling the door open!” This did not need to be done: the three men had only to leap out of the way, and the fearful monster flung the door open for itself, and, with a yell like the whistle of a steam-engine, rushed into the cage.
“Down with the portcullis!” No sooner said than done: and all breathed freely once more, on seeing the Porcupine safely caged.
The Professor rubbed his hands in childish delight. “The Experiment has succeeded!” he proclaimed. “All that is needed now is to feed it three times a day, on chopped carrots and﻿—.”
“Never mind about its food, just now!” the Emperor interrupted. “Let us return to the Banquet. Brother, will you lead the way?” And the old man, attended by his children, headed the procession downstairs. “See the fate of a loveless life!” he said to Bruno, as they returned to their places. To which Bruno made reply, “I always loved Sylvie, so I’ll never get prickly like that!”
“He *is* prickly, certainly,” said the Professor, who had caught the last words, “but we must remember that, however porcupiny, he is royal still! After this feast is over, I’m going to take a little present to Prince Uggug﻿—just to soothe him, you know: it isn’t pleasant living in a cage.”
“What’ll you give him for a birthday-present?” Bruno enquired.
“A small saucer of chopped carrots,” replied the Professor. “In giving birthday-presents, *my* motto is﻿—cheapness! I should think I save forty pounds a year by giving﻿—oh, *what* a twinge of pain!”
“What is it?” said Sylvie anxiously.
“My old enemy!” groaned the Professor.

===Chinese===

「去搬一只大笼子来，」他普遍地对众人说，「要粗钢条的，还要一道像捕鼠夹那样上下开合的闸门！谁身上碰巧带着这么个东西吗？」
这听起来不像谁会随身带着的东西；然而大家立刻给他搬来了一只：说来也怪，长廊里恰好就立着一只。
「把笼口对着门口，把闸门拉起来！」转眼就办妥了。
「现在拿毯子来！」教授喊道。「这是一场顶有趣味的实验！」
近旁恰好堆着一摞毯子：教授话音未落，毯子已尽数抖开，像帷幕似的四面张起。教授迅速把它们排成两行，布置出一条暗道，从房门笔直通到笼口。
「现在把门甩开！」这本不必动手：三个壮汉只消跳开让路，那可怕的怪物自己就把门撞开了，发一声火车汽笛般的长啸，冲进笼子里。
「放下闸门！」说时迟那时快：眼见豪猪稳稳关进了笼子，众人这才重新松了口气。
教授孩子似的欢喜得直搓手。「实验成功了！」他宣布道。「现在只需要一天喂它三次，喂点碎胡萝卜和——」
「这会儿别管它吃什么！」皇帝打断他。「我们回宴会去吧。哥哥，您带头？」于是一行人由老人带着两个孩子领队，下楼而去。「看看没有爱的日子是什么下场！」回到座位时他对布鲁诺说。布鲁诺听了答道：「我一直爱西尔维，所以我永远不会变得那么刺儿！」
「他确实是刺儿了点，」教授听见了最后几个字，说道，「不过我们得记住，无论多么豪猪气，他毕竟是王室血脉！等宴会结束，我要给乌格格王子送件小礼物——好安抚安抚他，你知道的：住在笼子里可不是滋味。」
「您要送他什么生日礼物呀？」布鲁诺问。
「一小碟碎胡萝卜，」教授答道。「送生日礼物嘛，*我的*座右铭是——省钱！我估摸着这么送礼一年能省下四十镑呢——哎哟，*好*一阵刺痛！」
「怎么啦？」西尔维着急地问。
「我的老对头又来了！」教授呻吟道。

===Original===

“Lumbago﻿—rheumatism﻿—that sort of thing. I think I’ll go and lie down a bit.” And he hobbled out of the Saloon, watched by the pitying eyes of the two children.
“He’ll be better soon!” the Elfin-King said cheerily. “Brother!” turning to the Emperor, “I have some business to arrange with you tonight. The Empress will take care of the children.” And the two Brothers went away together, arm-in-arm.
The Empress found the children rather sad company. They could talk of nothing but “the dear Professor,” and “what a pity he’s so ill!”, till at last she made the welcome proposal “Let’s go and see him!”
The children eagerly grasped the hands she offered them: and we went off to the Professor’s study, and found him lying on the sofa, covered up with blankets, and reading a little manuscript-book. “Notes on Vol. Three!” he murmured, looking up at us. And there, on a table near him, lay the book he was seeking when first I saw him.
“And how are you now, Professor?” the Empress asked, bending over the invalid.
The Professor looked up, and smiled feebly. “As devoted to your Imperial Highness as ever!” he said in a weak voice. “All of me, that is not Lumbago, is Loyalty!”
“A sweet sentiment!” the Empress exclaimed with tears in her eyes. “You seldom hear anything so beautiful as that﻿—even in a Valentine!”
“We must take you to stay at the seaside,” Sylvie said, tenderly. “It’ll do you ever so much good! And the Sea’s so grand!”
“But a Mountain’s grander!” said Bruno.
“What is there grand about the Sea?” said the Professor. “Why, you could put it all into a teacup!”
“*Some* of it,” Sylvie corrected him.
“Well, you’d only want a certain number of teacups to hold it *all*. And *then* where’s the grandeur? Then as to a Mountain﻿—why, you could carry it all away in a wheelbarrow, in a certain number of years!”
“It wouldn’t look grand﻿—the bits of it in the wheelbarrow,” Sylvie candidly admitted.
“But when oo put it together again﻿—” Bruno began.
“When you’re older,” said the Professor, “you’ll know that you *can’t* put Mountains together again so easily! One lives and one learns, you know!”
“But it needn’t be the *same* one, need it?” said Bruno. “Won’t it do, if *I* live, and if *Sylvie* learns?”
“I *can’t* learn without living!” said Sylvie.
“But I *can* live without learning!” Bruno retorted. “Oo just try me!”

===Chinese===

「腰疼病——风湿病——那一类的毛病。我想我去躺一会儿。」他一瘸一拐地走出大厅，两个孩子怜惜地目送着他。
「他很快就会好的！」精灵王快慰地说。「弟弟！」他转向皇帝，「今晚我有些事要同你安排。孩子们就让皇后照看吧。」于是兄弟二人挽着手臂，一同离去。
皇后觉得这两个孩子陪着怪让人发愁的。他们满口只是「亲爱的教授」和「他病得这么重，多可惜呀！」，最后她提了个正中下怀的建议：「我们去看看他吧！」
孩子们急切地抓住她伸来的手：我们来到教授的书房，见他躺在沙发上，盖着毯子，正读一本小小的手稿册子。「第三卷的笔记！」他抬起头来，喃喃道。在他近旁的桌上，正放着我初次见到他时他找个不停的那本书。
「您现在觉得怎么样了，教授？」皇后俯身问候病人。
教授抬起头，虚弱地笑了笑。「对陛下的忠心一如既往！」他有气无力地说。「我这个人，除去腰疼病，全是忠心！」
「多动人的情操！」皇后眼里含着泪感叹道。「这么美妙的话可难得听到——连情人节贺卡上也听不到！」
「我们得带您去海边住住，」西尔维温柔地说。「保管对您大有好处！大海多壮观呀！」
「可大山更壮观！」布鲁诺说。
「大海有什么壮观的？」教授说。「嗐，把它全装进一只茶杯就行！」
「*一部分*嘛。」西尔维纠正他。
「呃，要装*全部*，也不过需要一定数目的茶杯罢了。那还壮观个什么劲儿？再说大山——嗐，只要年头够数，用一辆独轮车就能把它全推走！」
「推在独轮车里的那些碎块，看着是不壮观。」西尔维坦率地承认。
「可等你再把它拼回去——」布鲁诺开了腔。
「等你长大些，」教授说，「你就会明白，大山可不是那么容易重新拼起来的！人活着，人就学乖，懂吗！」
「可不用是*同一个*人呀，对不对？」布鲁诺说。「*我*来活，*西尔维*来学，不就行了嘛？」
「我不活着就没法学！」西尔维说。
「可我不学也照样能活！」布鲁诺反驳道。「你倒试试看！」

===Original===

“What I meant, was﻿—” the Professor began, looking much puzzled, “﻿—was﻿—that you don’t know *everything*, you know.”
“But I *do* know everything I know!” persisted the little fellow. “I know ever so many things! Everything, ’cept the things I *don’t* know. And Sylvie knows all the rest.”
The Professor sighed, and gave it up. “Do you know what a Boojum is?”
“*I* know!” cried Bruno. “It’s the thing what wrenches people out of their boots!”
“He means ‘bootjack,’ ” Sylvie explained in a whisper.
“You can’t wrench people out of *boots*,” the Professor mildly observed.
Bruno laughed saucily. “Oo *can*, though! Unless they’re *welly* tight in.”
“Once upon a time there was a Boojum﻿—” the Professor began, but stopped suddenly. “I forget the rest of the Fable,” he said. “And there was a lesson to be learned from it. I’m afraid I forget *that*, too.”
“*I’ll* tell oo a Fable!” Bruno began in a great hurry. “Once there were a Locust, and a Magpie, and a Engine-driver. And the Lesson is, to learn to get up early﻿—”
“It isn’t a bit interesting!” Sylvie said contemptuously. “You shouldn’t put the Lesson so soon.”
“When did you invent that Fable?” said the Professor. “Last week?”
“No!” said Bruno. “A deal shorter ago than that. Guess again!”
“I can’t guess,” said the Professor. “How long ago?”
“Why, it isn’t invented yet!” Bruno exclaimed triumphantly. “But I *have* invented a lovely one! Shall I say it?”
“If you’ve *finished* inventing it,” said Sylvie. “And let the Lesson be ‘to try again’!”
“No,” said Bruno with great decision. “The Lesson are ‘*not* to try again’!” “Once there were a lovely china man, what stood on the chimbley-piece. And he stood, and he stood. And one day he tumbleded off, and he didn’t hurt his self one bit. Only he *would* try again. And the next time he tumbleded off, he hurted his self welly much, and breaked off ever so much varnish.”
“But how did he come back on the chimneypiece after his first tumble?” said the Empress. (It was the first sensible question she had asked in all her life.)
“*I* put him there!” cried Bruno.
“Then I’m afraid you know something about his tumbling,” said the Professor. “Perhaps you pushed him?”
To which Bruno replied, very seriously, “Didn’t pushed him *much*﻿—he were a *lovely* china man,” he added hastily, evidently very anxious to change the subject.
“Come, my children!”

===Chinese===

「我的意思是——」教授开口道，一脸大惑不解，「——是说——你并不是*什么都知道*，你懂的。」
「可我*确确实实*知道我知道的一切呀！」小家伙执拗地说。「我知道的东西可多可多啦！什么都知道，除了那些我*不*知道的。剩下的西尔维全知道。」
教授叹了口气，投降了。「那你知道布裘姆是什么吗？」
「*我*知道！」布鲁诺喊道。「就是把人从靴子里拔出来的那个东西！」
「他是说『脱靴器』。」西尔维低声解释。
「人是不能从*靴子*里拔出来的。」教授温和地指出。
布鲁诺调皮地笑了。「就*能*嘛！除非他们穿得*紧得要命*。」
「从前有一只布裘姆——」教授开了个头，忽然停住。「这寓言的后头我忘了，」他说。「本来还有个教训可学的。恐怕那个我也忘了。」
「*我*给你讲个寓言！」布鲁诺急巴巴地开了头。「从前有一只蝗虫，一只喜鹊，还有一个开火车的。教训就是，要学会早起——」
「一点儿也不有趣！」西尔维不屑地说。「你不该把教训说得这么早。」
「这寓言你是什么时候编的？」教授问。「上礼拜？」
「不是！」布鲁诺说。「比那近多啦。再猜！」
「我猜不着，」教授说。「多久以前？」
「嘿，还没编出来呢！」布鲁诺得意洋洋地喊道。「不过我*已经*编好一个妙极了的！要我讲吗？」
「得等你*编完了*才算，」西尔维说。「教训就叫『再试一次』！」
「不，」布鲁诺斩钉截铁地说。「教训是『*不要*再试』！」「从前有一个可爱的瓷人儿，站在烟囱架架上。他站着呀，站着呀。有一天他摔跤跤了下来，一点儿也没摔疼自己。可他偏要再试一次。第二次摔跤跤下来，他把自己摔得疼极了，还磕掉了好大一片釉。」
「可他摔第一跤之后，怎么又回到炉架上去了呢？」皇后问。（这是她平生头一回提出一个像样的问题。）
「*我*把他放上去的！」布鲁诺喊道。
「那我恐怕他的摔倒跟你有点关系喽，」教授说。「兴许是你推的他？」
对此布鲁诺一本正经地回答：「没推他*多少*下——他是个*可爱的*瓷人儿嘛，」他又慌忙补上一句，显然急着岔开话题。
「来吧，我的孩子们！」

===Original===

said the Elfin-King, who had just entered the room. “We must have a little chat together, before you go to bed.” And he was leading them away, but at the door they let go his hands, and ran back again to wish the Professor good night.
“Good night, Professor, good night!” And Bruno solemnly shook hands with the old man, who gazed at him with a loving smile, while Sylvie bent down to press her sweet lips upon his forehead.
“Good night, little ones!” said the Professor. “You may leave me now﻿—to ruminate. I’m as jolly as the day is long, except when it’s necessary to ruminate on some very difficult subject. All of me,” he murmured sleepily as we left the room, “all of me, that isn’t *Bonhommie*, is Rumination!”
“*What* did he say, Bruno?” Sylvie enquired, as soon as we were safely out of hearing.
“I *think* he said ‘All of me that isn’t Bone-disease is Rheumatism.’ Whatever *are* that knocking, Sylvie?”
Sylvie stopped, and listened anxiously. It sounded like someone kicking at a door. “I *hope* it isn’t that Porcupine breaking loose!” she exclaimed.
“Let’s go on!” Bruno said hastily. “There’s nuffin to wait for, oo know!”

===Chinese===

精灵王说，他刚走进屋来。「上床之前，我们得小小聊聊。」他领着他们往外走，可到了门口，孩子们松开他的手，又跑回去向教授道晚安。
「晚安，教授，晚安！」布鲁诺郑重其事地同老人握手，老人含着慈爱的微笑凝望他，西尔维则俯下身，把她甜甜的嘴唇印在他的额头上。
「晚安，小家伙们！」教授说。「你们这就走吧——让我反刍去。我一整天都乐呵呵的，除非不得不反刍什么老大难的题目。我这个人啊，」我们离开房间时，他睡意朦胧地喃喃道，「我这个人，除去*好脾气*，全是反刍！」
「他说*什么*来着，布鲁诺？」一等到走远了听不见，西尔维便问。
「*我猜*他说的是『我这个人，除去骨头病，全是风湿病』。那个敲门的是啥呀，西尔维？」
西尔维停下脚步，不安地侧耳细听。听来像有人在踹门。「*但愿*不是那头豪猪跑出来了！」她喊道。
「咱们快走吧！」布鲁诺急忙说。「没啥好等的，你知道的！」

## XXV Life Out of Death / 二十五 死里生
===Original===

The sound of kicking, or knocking, grew louder every moment: and at last a door opened somewhere near us. “Did you say ‘come in!’ Sir?” my landlady asked timidly.
“Oh yes, come in!” I replied. “What’s the matter?”
“A note has just been left for you, Sir, by the baker’s boy. He said he was passing the Hall, and they asked him to come round and leave it here.”
The note contained five words only. “Please come at once. Muriel.”
A sudden terror seemed to chill my very heart. “The Earl is ill!” I said to myself. “Dying, perhaps!” And I hastily prepared to leave the house.
“No bad news, Sir, I hope?” my landlady said, as she saw me out. “The boy said as someone had arrived unexpectedly﻿—.”
“I hope that is it!” I said. But my feelings were those of fear rather than of hope: though, on entering the house, I was somewhat reassured by finding luggage lying in the entrance, bearing the initials “E. L.”
“It’s only Eric Lindon after all!” I thought, half relieved and half annoyed. “Surely she need not have sent for me for *that*!”
Lady Muriel met me in the passage. Her eyes were gleaming﻿—but it was the excitement of joy, rather than of grief. “I have a surprise for you!” she whispered.
“You mean that Eric Lindon is here?” I said, vainly trying to disguise the involuntary bitterness of my tone. “ ‘*The funeral baked meats did coldly furnish forth the marriage-tables*,’ ” I could not help repeating to myself. How cruelly I was misjudging her!
“No, no!” she eagerly replied. “At least﻿—Eric *is* here. But﻿—,” her voice quivered, “but there is *another*!”
No need for further question. I eagerly followed her in. There on the bed, he lay﻿—pale and worn﻿—the mere shadow of his old self﻿—my old friend come back again from the dead!
“Arthur!” I exclaimed. I could not say another word.
“Yes, back again, old boy!” he murmured, smiling as I grasped his hand. “*He*,” indicating Eric, who stood near, “saved my life﻿—*He* brought me back. Next to God, we must thank *him*, Muriel, my wife!”
Silently I shook hands with Eric and with the Earl: and with one consent we moved into the shaded side of the room, where we could talk without disturbing the invalid, who lay, silent and happy, holding his wife’s hand in his, and watching her with eyes that shone with the deep steady light of Love.

===Chinese===

踢门声，或者说敲门声，一阵比一阵响：终于，我们近旁哪儿的门开了。“您方才是说‘请进’了吗，先生？”房东太太怯怯地问。
“哦，是，请进！”我答。“什么事？”
“刚有人给您留了张条子，先生，面包房的伙计送的。他说他打府邸门口经过，府上求他绕个弯把条子送到这儿。”
条子上只有五个词。“请即刻来。穆丽尔。”
一阵骤然的恐怖仿佛冰透了我的心。“伯爵病了！”我自忖。“说不定垂危了！”我急急收拾出门。
“但愿不是坏消息，先生？”房东太太送我出门时说。“那伙计说，好像有位客人不期而至——。”
“但愿如此！”我说。可我心头是惧多过望：不过一进宅门，望见门厅里掿着的行李上打着“E. L.”两个花体缩写，我略略安了心。
“说到底不过是埃里克·林登（Eric Lindon）！”我想，一半宽心，一半着恼。“总不至于为*这个*就打发人叫我吧！”
穆丽尔小姐在过道里迎住我。她双眼发亮——那是欢喜的激动，不是悲恸的。“我有个惊喜给你！”她悄声道。
“你是说埃里克·林登来了？”我说，徒劳地想遮住话音里不由自主的酸味。“‘*葬礼上的冷烤饼，凉凉地摆上了婚宴席*’，”我忍不住暗自念叨。我这样错怪她，何其忍心！
“不，不！”她急切地答。“至少——埃里克*是*来了。可是——”她的声音发颤，“可是还有*另一位*！”
不必再问。我急急随她进去。床上，他躺着——苍白、羸瘦——旧日的他只剩一道影子——我的老友，从死者中归来了！
“亚瑟！”我喊。再说不出第二个字。
“对，回来了，老伙计！”他喃喃道，我攋住他的手时他微笑着。“*他*，”他指指站在近旁的埃里克，“救了我的命——是*他*把我带回来的。除上帝之外，最该谢的就是*他*，穆丽尔，我的妻！”
我默默同埃里克和伯爵攋了手：大家不约而同移到房间的背光一侧，在那里说话不致搅扰病人。他静静躺着，满心欢喜，一手攋着妻子的手，望着她，眼里闪耀着爱那深沉而恒定的光。

===Original===

“He has been delirious till today,” Eric explained in a low voice: “and even today he has been wandering more than once. But the sight of *her* has been new life to him.” And then he went on to tell us, in would-be careless tones﻿—I knew how he hated any display of feeling﻿—how he had insisted on going back to the plague-stricken town, to bring away a man whom the doctor had abandoned as dying, but who *might*, he fancied, recover if brought to the hospital: how he had seen nothing in the wasted features to remind him of Arthur, and only recognised him when he visited the hospital a month after: how the doctor had forbidden him to announce the discovery, saying that any shock to the over taxed brain might kill him at once: how he had stayed on at the hospital, and nursed the sick man by night and day﻿—all this with the studied indifference of one who is relating the commonplace acts of some chance acquaintance!
“And this was his *rival*!” I thought. “The man who had won from him the heart of the woman he loved!”
“The sun is setting,” said Lady Muriel, rising and leading the way to the open window. “Just look at the western sky! What lovely crimson tints! We shall have a glorious day tomorrow﻿—” We had followed her across the room, and were standing in a little group, talking in low tones in the gathering gloom, when we were startled by the voice of the sick man, murmuring words too indistinct for the ear to catch.
“He is wandering again,” Lady Muriel whispered, and returned to the bedside. We drew a little nearer also: but no, this had none of the incoherence of delirium. “*What reward shall I give unto the Lord*,” the tremulous lips were saying, “*for all the benefits that He hath done unto me? I will receive the cup of salvation, and call﻿—and call*﻿—” but here the poor weakened memory failed, and the feeble voice died into silence.
His wife knelt down at the bedside, raised one of his arms, and drew it across her own, fondly kissing the thin white hand that lay so listlessly in her loving grasp. It seemed to me a good opportunity for stealing away without making her go through any form of parting: so, nodding to the Earl and Eric, I silently left the room. Eric followed me down the stairs, and out into the night.
“Is it Life or Death?” I asked him, as soon as we were far enough from the house for me to speak in ordinary tones.
“It is *Life*!”

===Chinese===

“直到今天他一直说着胡话，”埃里克低声解释：“就连今天，也不止一次神志游离。可一见到*她*，对他便是新生命。”接着他讲下去，语调故意装得漫不经心——我知道他最恨流露感情——他怎样执意重返那座瘟城，接出一个已被医生断为必死、他却想若送进医院兴许有救的人：他怎样在那张憔悴的面容上认不出半点亚瑟的影子，直到一个月后巡访医院才认出是他：医生又怎样禁止他声张这一发现，说过劳的脑子受不得任何惊动，一惊即死：他又怎样留在医院，日以继夜亲自护理病人——这一切，他讲得刻意淡漠，像在转述某个萍水相逢者的寻常琐事！
“而这是他的*情敌*！”我心想。“是从他手里赢走心上人的那个人！”
“日落了，”穆丽尔小姐说着起身，领我们走向敞开的窗。“看西边的天！多美的绯红！明天准是个大晴天——”我们随她走过房间，小小聚成一群，在渐浓的暮色里低声交谈，忽然病人的一缕语声惊动了我们，呢喃得太轻，耳朵接不住。
“他又神志游离了，”穆丽尔小姐悄声说，回到床边。我们也凑近了些：可是不，这一回毫无谵语的散乱。“*我拿什么报答耶和华*”，那颤抖的嘴唇正念着，“*赐给我的一切厚恩？我要举起救恩的杯，求告——求告*——”念到这里，可怜的、衰弱的记忆断了，微弱的声音沉入寂静。
他的妻子跪在床边，抬起他一条手臂，搭上自己的臂弯，疼爱地吻着那只毫无气力地躺在她爱握之中的苍白瘦手。我觉得这是悄然退走的好时机，免得她还要行那套道别的礼数：于是我朝伯爵和埃里克点头示意，默默出了房。埃里克随我下了楼，走进夜色。
“是生，还是死？”离宅子够远、能平常开口说话时，我问他。
“是*生*！

===Original===

he replied with eager emphasis. “The doctors are quite agreed as to *that*. All he needs now, they say, is rest, and perfect quiet, and good nursing. He’s quite sure to get rest and quiet, here: and, as for the nursing why, I think it’s just *possible*﻿—” (he tried hard to make his trembling voice assume a playful tone) “he may even get fairly well nursed, in his present quarters!”
“I’m sure of it!” I said. “Thank you so much for coming out to tell me!” And, thinking he had now said all he had come to say, I held out my hand to bid him good night. He grasped it warmly, and added, turning his face away as he spoke, “By the way, there is one other thing I wanted to say. I thought you’d like to know that﻿—that I’m not﻿—not in the mind I was in when last we met. It isn’t﻿—that I can accept Christian belief﻿—at least, not yet. But all this came about so strangely. And she had prayed, you know. And I had prayed. And﻿—and﻿—” his voice broke, and I could only just catch the concluding words, “*there is a God that answers prayer!* I know it for certain now.” He wrung my hand once more, and left me suddenly. Never before had I seen him so deeply moved.
So, in the gathering twilight, I paced slowly homewards, in a tumultuous whirl of happy thoughts: my heart seemed full, and running over, with joy and thankfulness: all that I had so fervently longed for, and prayed for, seemed now to have come to pass. And, though I reproached myself, bitterly, for the unworthy suspicion I had for one moment harboured against the truehearted Lady Muriel, I took comfort in knowing it had been but a passing thought.
Not Bruno himself could have mounted the stairs with so buoyant a step, as I felt my way up in the dark, not pausing to strike a light in the entry, as I knew I had left the lamp burning in my sitting-room.
But it was no common *lamplight* into which I now stepped, with a strange, new, dreamy sensation of some subtle witchery that had come over the place. Light, richer and more golden than any lamp could give, flooded the room, streaming in from a window I had somehow never noticed before, and lighting up a group of three shadowy figures, that grew momently more distinct﻿—a grave old man in royal robes, leaning back in an easy chair, and two children, a girl and a boy, standing at his side.
“Have you the Jewel still, my child?” the old man was saying.
“Oh, *yes*!”

===Chinese===

他答得斩钉截铁，热切无比。“这一点医生们毫无异义。他们说，如今他只需静养、绝对安宁和周到看护。静养与安宁，此地保管有：至于看护嘛，呢，我想倒也*未必没有可能*——”（他竭力让发颤的声音摆出打趣的调子）“在他现在这处居所，说不定还能得到相当不错的看护呢！”
“这一点我确信！”我说。“多谢你特地出来告诉我！”想着他要说的已说尽，我伸手道晚安。他热情一握，又侧过脸去补了一句：“对了，还有一件事想说。我想你愿意知道——我已不——不再是上次相见时的心境了。倒不是——我能接受基督教信仰——至少，还不能。可这一切来得这样离奇。她祷告过，你知道。我也祷告过。还有——还有——”他的声音哽住了，我只勉强听清末尾那几句：“*有一位听祷告的上帝！*如今我确知了。”他再紧握我一回，突然离去。我从没见他这样深地动过情。
就这样，在渐合的暮色里，我缓缓踱向归途，满心欢喜的思绪翻腾如漩：我的心仿佛盛满而外溢，全是喜悦与感恩：我苦苦渴求、恳切祷告过的一切，仿佛如今都成了真。虽然我还狠狠自责——怪自己一度对心地纯正的穆丽尔小姐起过那不体面的疑心——但想到那不过一闪之念，也就得了宽解。
黑暗中我摸上楼去，脚步轻快得连布鲁诺本人也不能更甚——门道里也不停下擦火点灯，因为我知道客厅的灯还亮着。
可我一脚踏进去的，绝非寻常的*灯光*，只觉这屋子蒙上了一层幽微的魔力，生出一种新奇而梦一样的感受。比任何灯盏都更浓、更金亮的光漫满了房间，从一扇我不知怎么从没留意过的窗里流泻进来，照亮三个人影，那影子一刻比一刻清晰——一位披着王袍的威严老人，仰坐安乐椅中，两个孩子，一女一男，立在他身旁。
“那宝石你还带着么，孩子？”老人正问。
“哦，*带着*呢！

===Original===

Sylvie exclaimed with unusual eagerness. “Do you think I’d *ever* lose it or forget it?” She undid the ribbon round her neck, as she spoke, and laid the Jewel in her father’s hand.
Bruno looked at it admiringly. “What a lovely brightness!” he said. “It’s just like a little red star! May I take it in my hand?”
Sylvie nodded: and Bruno carried it off to the window, and held it aloft against the sky, whose deepening blue was already spangled with stars. Soon he came running back in some excitement. “Sylvie! Look here!” he cried. “I can see right through it when I hold it up to the sky. And it isn’t red a bit: it’s, oh such a lovely blue! And the words are all different! Do look at it!”
Sylvie was quite excited, too, by this time; and the two children eagerly held up the Jewel to the light, and spelled out the legend between them, “**all will love Sylvie**.”
“Why, this is the *other* Jewel!” cried Bruno. “Don’t you remember, Sylvie? The one you *didn’t* choose!”
Sylvie took it from him, with a puzzled look, and held it, now up to the light, now down. “It’s blue, *one* way,” she said softly to herself, “and it’s red, the *other* way! Why, I thought there were *two* of them﻿—Father!” she suddenly exclaimed, laying the Jewel once more in his hand, “I do believe it was the *same* Jewel all the time!”
“Then you choosed it from *itself*,” Bruno thoughtfully remarked. “Father, *could* Sylvie choose a thing from itself?”
“Yes, my own one,” the old man replied to Sylvie, not noticing Bruno’s embarrassing question, “it *was* the same Jewel﻿—but you chose quite right.” And he fastened the ribbon round her neck again.
“**Sylvie will love all﻿—all will love Sylvie**,” Bruno murmured, raising himself on tiptoe to kiss the “little red star.” “And, when you look *at* it, it’s red and fierce like the sun﻿—and, when you look *through* it, it’s gentle and blue like the sky!”
“God’s own sky,” Sylvie said, dreamily.
“God’s own sky,” the little fellow repeated, as they stood, lovingly clinging together, and looking out into the night. “But oh, Sylvie, what makes the sky such a *darling* blue?”
Sylvie’s sweet lips shaped themselves to reply, but her voice sounded faint and very far away.

===Chinese===

西尔维异乎寻常地急切喊道。“您想我会把它弄丢，或者忘了*吗*？”她一面说，一面解开颈间的缎带，把宝石放进父亲手中。
布鲁诺艳羡地望着它。“多可爱的亮光！”他说。“活像一颗小红星！能拿在俺手里吗？”
西尔维点头：布鲁诺把它捧到窗前，对着天空高高举起——天色深蓝，已缀满星子。不多时他兴奋地跑了回来。“西尔维！快看！”他喊。“把它举到天跟前，俺能从中间一直看过去。它一点儿也不红了：它蓝得——哦，蓝得可好看啦！字也全变了样！快看看嘛！”
这时西尔维也兴奋起来；两个孩子急急把宝石举向光，凑在一起，一个字母一个字母拼出那行字：“**人人都会爱西尔维**”。
“咦，这是*另一颗*宝石！”布鲁诺喊。“你不记得了吗，西尔维？是你*没有*选的那颗！”
西尔维一脸困惑地接过来，一会儿举向光，一会儿垂下去。“*这一*面看是蓝的，”她轻轻自语，“*那一*面看是红的！咦，我还当它们是*两颗*呢——爸爸！”她忽然喊，把宝石再次放进他手里，“我真相信了，它从头到尾是*同一颗*宝石！”
“那你就是把它从它*自己*手里选出来的，”布鲁诺若有所思地评论。“爸爸，西尔维能把一样东西从它*自己*那儿选走吗？”
“是的，我的宝贝，”老人答西尔维道，没有理会布鲁诺那个令人发窘的问题，“它*确实*是同一颗宝石——不过你选得一点不错。”说着他又把缎带系回她颈间。
“**西尔维会爱人人——人人会爱西尔维**，”布鲁诺喃喃念着，踮起脚尖去亲那颗“小红星”。“你*看*它的时候，它红得、烈得像太阳——你*透过*它看的时候，它温柔地蓝着，像天空！”
“上帝自己的天空，”西尔维梦呓般地说。
“上帝自己的天空，”小家伙跟着念。他们相依相偎地立着，望向夜外。“可是哦，西尔维，是什么把天染成这么*可爱的*蓝呀？”
西尔维甜美的唇禽动欲答，可她的声音听着细微，遥远得很。

===Original===

The vision was fast slipping from my eager gaze: but it seemed to me, in that last bewildering moment, that not Sylvie but an angel was looking out through those trustful brown eyes, and that not Sylvie’s but an angel’s voice was whispering
**“It is love.”**

===Chinese===

幻象正从我渴切的凝望里飞快溜走：可在那最后一阵目眩神迷的时刻，我觉得，从那双信赖的棕色眼睛里望出来的，不是西尔维，是一位天使；那低语的，不是西尔维的声音，是天使的声音——
**“那是爱。”**
